BOOK ONE
ENSEMBLE
T
I
OM HOLMES sat in the front window of Cordiner & Morse's,
with four rows of ailing timepieces screening him from the
outside world. For thirty years he had occupied that post of vantage.
Long before old Cordiner took Frank Morse into the business which
was now the leading jewelry store in Mount Royal, Tom Holmes had
been repairing the watches that kept trains running on time for
the railroad to which everybody in town alluded as "the Q."
He repaired a watch as if he hated it; but he was a good workman.
Now and then, when his eyes tired, he would lay down his pincers,
take the glass out of his right eye, and stare out between the rows of
watches. During these respites his habitual expression of distaste often
deepened to loathing.
Yet the junction of Broadway and Main Street, where Cordiner &
Morse occupied the northeast corner, generally presented some item
of casual interest. Directly across Main Street stood the old Mount
Royal Hotel, with its rows of ancient arm-chairs fronting the wide
windows of the lobby and Dan Mason's shabby hack awaiting busi-
ness at the curb. Diagonally across was the First National Bank: "the
Reynolds bank" it was popularly called, though John Rutherford was
now its Vice-President and owned a third of the stock. Across Broad-
way stood Kortebein's, the town's leading dry-goods store. And at
this corner the street-cars, lately electrified, turned from Main Street
to cross the river. Watching the motorman get out to throw the
switch was employment enough for the loungers in the hotel lobby.
It only sent Tom Holmes back to his pincers.
One fine afternoon toward the end of June, however, Tom took out
his glass hastily; his right hand held a watch spring suspended in
midair while he gazed at a turnout that was approaching down Main
Street. A fat old horse drawing a shabby surrey was driven by a
I
2
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
young-old man whose well-pressed suit and jaunty new straw hat
contrasted markedly with the worn appearance of the turnout.
The young-old man himself, however, and the way he played with
the reins and flourished the whip, were not the cause of Tom
Holmes's sudden interest. Whenever Artie Mills drove one of the
Langworthy rigs, he put on airs enough for a Roman chariot-race.
He didn't get much practice, at that. If Tom Holmes had been in
Colonel Langworthy's place, he'd have let that worthless brother-in-
law of his drive for him all the time, and saved the coachman's
wages.
What was notable to-day was the coachman's presence in the back
seat. In the incongruous splendor of a silk hat, he leaned from the
surrey to lead a horse as ancient as the one between the shafts, but
not quite so fat. The Langworthys drove the horses as a team attached
to the landau, or used them indifferently for the surrey. This was the
first time they had ever appeared downtown leading one horse.
They couldn't possibly be intending to sell old Nellie? "Wouldn't
fetch twenty dollars," Tom Holmes grunted to himself. "Couldn't
get fifty for the team, in spite of the way that family look down their
noses at everybody."
Artie Mills spotted a friend in the hotel window, and saluted with
his whip. He was a tall thin fellow with an aquiline profile and wavy
hair turning gray on the temples. Mount Royal didn't esteem Artie
Mills as highly as he esteemed himself. But there was no denying
that he had a certain dash about him.
In spite of himself, Tom Holmes was sufficiently interested in this
unique turnout to seek light on it. He twisted in his chair and looked
for Mrs. Cordiner. She had tea once a week with a set group which
included Mrs. Langworthy. That circle of revolving parties was the
only social event which Mrs. Cordiner considered of sufficient impor-
tance to take her out of the store.
She was behind the silverware counter this afternoon, rearranging
the show-case and now and then flicking off a stray speck of dust
with her handkerchief. Before she married her employer she had
dusted outright. She still sold more goods than old Cordiner and
Frank Morse put together; lots of people, if they came into the
store when she wasn't there, said they guessed they'd wait and come
again.
ENSEMBLE
3
Tom Holmes kept his back to her nine hours a day, and Mrs.
Cordiner never approached him unless she wanted to call his atten-
tion to a customer. Now when she saw him turn to speak, she simply
lifted an eyebrow in his direction.
But just as Tom was about to point out the excitement in the
street, Frank Morse came from the back of the store. He was a
solidly built man in his forties: young enough to be old Cordiner's
son, and by that same token within a very few years of Mrs. Cord-
iner's age.
"Just a minute, Mrs. Cordiner," said Frank Morse. And so far as
Mrs. Cordiner was concerned, the front window might have been
vacant from then on.
Frank Morse always called her "Mrs. Cordiner," and she called
him "Mr. Morse," though most of Mount Royal called him "Frank."
He lived in an ordinary boarding-house, and led a very quiet life;
people said he was stingy. The Cordiners, on the other hand, had a
big house near the downtown end of Prospect Avenue, and kept up
a good deal of style. Mrs. Cordiner and Frank seldom met outside of
business hours.
During business hours, old Cordiner was generally there. Yesterday
he had gone in to Chicago to buy stock; he was due to return this
afternoon by the four o'clock train. Mrs. Cordiner would undoubtedly
meet him at the train. She was very much the devoted wife. Coming
to Mount Royal a poor girl, she had married a well-to-do man; and
the fact that he was twice her age only entitled him to her care and
vigilance as well as her gratitude.
Just the same on Frank Morse's approach her eyes, which were
of a red-brown shade that almost matched her hair, narrowed and
sparkled; and although she didn't exactly smile, a little dimple
showed at the corner of her pretty mouth. And Frank Morse, though
he came to ask her a simple question about some fans which he
thought might sell as graduation presents, stood looking down at her
with an expresson which he certainly wouldn't have worn if he
had approached old Cordiner with the same query.
"Disgusting!" muttered Tom Holmes to himself, and turned back
to his window. Artie Mills, flourishing his whip but restraining speed
to accommodate the led horse, was now vanishing down the street.
Tom Holmes had just readjusted his eyeglass when down half the
4
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
length of the store Mrs. Cordiner spoke to him. "Remind me when
it's half-past three, will you, Tom? I want to have my hat on when
our coachman comes for me."
For thirty years Tom Holmes had done his intricate work here;
and the Langworthys and the Cordiners had their coachmen, but
Tom hadn't so much as an old horse and buggy to take him out on
Sunday afternoons. Mrs. Cordiner called him "Tom," too; no "Mr.
Morse" business for him. Still, he'd lived to see a lot of people get
their come-uppance in this world. He was going to live to see a lot
more.
He growled a reply over his shoulder. A customer entered just then,
and Morse went to wait on her.
Quite unexpectedly Mrs. Cordiner, approaching Tom's end of the
counter, spoke to his inexpressive back. "Bess Langworthy is coming
home from school this afternoon. I suppose she'll be on the four
o'clock train, too."
So Bess Langworthy was coming home this afternoon! That fact
might well have some connection with the spectacle which had briefly
roused Tom's interest. "She's been away the best part of a year, ain't
she?" he grunted. "Been to one of these fashionable Eastern schools
where they learn a girl not to eat with her knife!"
"I don't know how much Bess would ever learn anywhere," said
Mrs. Cordiner crisply. "But I saw Mrs. Langworthy yesterday, and
she was very much excited about Bess's coming home."
"I sh'd think the Colonel'd be glad to have her," responded Tom,
picking up a tiny screw and dropping it into its hole. "His one chick
away all year, and his house full all the time of his wife's relations."
"His wife's-?" Mrs. Cordiner seemed a little startled. "Oh, of
course they are his wife's relatives! But they've lived there so long, it's
hard to realize that they aren't members of the immediate family."
They all charged their purchases on the Colonel's account, she
might have added. It made her uneasy for a moment to reflect that a
bill for gifts bought last Christmas was still standing against that
account. But then it would be settled some time; Colonel Langworthy
might be slow pay, but he was as honest as the day was long.
She sighed a little, remembering how happy Mrs. Langworthy had
seemed yesterday. And Dora Mills, too, and Amy. There were times
ENSEMBLE
5
when Mrs. Cordiner felt a little lonesome in her big house, having no
woman there to talk things over with.
Habit made her glance along the counter to see how Frank Morse
was making out with his customer. He wasn't doing any too well.
She sidled up, and selected from the show-case a box of silver which
had been there so long that Morse probably wouldn't think to offer it.
She had found that it never did any harm to let customers see every-
thing you had.
Tom Holmes made up his mind once more to seek her opinion on
the fascinating subject of what Artie Mills could possibly be doing
with a second horse led behind the surrey. But by the time he turned
to consult her, she had taken over the customer from Morse; and the
dimple at the corner of her mouth, which had showed so provokingly
for Morse, showed just as clearly for a woman who really wanted to
buy a solid silver wedding-present, but wanted to pay only for plated.
Tom turned back and moodily watched a motorman get out of his
car and tinker with the switch. He couldn't get right down to work
now; he had to keep an eye on the time, so that he could warn Mrs.
Cordiner when the half-hour approached.
Half-past three would mean that the afternoon was looking toward
a close. His rows of watches were ticking the day to its end, as they
had yesterday and the day before, as they would to-morrow and all
the rest of the week. Sunday made a break, but then he didn't care
much about Sunday. He didn't care much about his household, either,
though nobody could ever reproach him with doing anything unbe-
coming a family man. It was just that he felt more at home, and the
time passed quicker somehow, here behind his rows of watches, with
his tools and tiny screws spread out before him on his bench.
B
II
EFORE it ever reached Cordiner & Morse's to excite Tom
Holmes's wrath and disgust, the back wheels of the surrey had
caught in the car-tracks when Artie didn't make a square turn. For a
grinding minute it had looked as if a wheel might be wrenched clean
off. The coachman, grabbing the bridle of the led horse with both
hands, growled over his shoulder, "Both our necks'd be a whole lot
safer if you'd drive down some street where there ain't no tracks."
Artie Mills scorned the reflection on his driving: would have scorned
it even if he hadn't had reasons of his own for wishing to dash
jauntily past the Mount Royal Hotel. As soon as he was sure he
hadn't lost a wheel, Artie began to tool the fat old horse along in a
form that would have done justice to a coach-and-four. The coachman
subsided, fuming. He wouldn't have stayed with the Langworthys
a week if he hadn't happened to like the Missis. The Colonel was a
gentleman, too. But with the brother-in-law the coachman had a
perpetual feud.
Artie fancied his own driving. When not held back by a led horse,
he could get some speed even out of fat old Fritz. But it generally
took Fritz or the mare a week to rest up after half a day of Artie's
driving-not to speak of the extra half hour it took the coachman to
get the dust and foam off the overdriven animal.
The cavalcade swept on down Main Street, crossed the railroad
tracks, and turned north toward the river. There it presently drew up
in front of the last building before you came to the East Avenue
bridge.
The building was an old frame structure, obviously erected at two
different times. On the larger division, which served as showroom,
was a weather-beaten sign that read: LANGWORTHY CARRIAGES &
6
ENSEMBLE
7
WAGONS. The office side had a newer sign: s. H. LANGWORTHY.
Directly in front of the office door Artie thrust his whip into the
socket and twined the reins around it. The door was already opening
to allow the passage of a thick-set man in his middle fifties.
"Hello, Colonel!" called Artie, leaping over the wheel. "Jason was
having the jitters all the way down for fear we'd be late. But there's
plenty of time."
"You can't allow too much time when you have to meet a train."
Colonel Langworthy spoke kindly but anxiously, as one who had
important affairs afoot. Indeed, he was dressed for an occasion: his
pale gray felt hat just matched his freshly pressed suit, and he wore
a piqué waistcoat and a white lawn tie. He had had no pretensions to
good looks even in his youth; but beside his solid strength and ruddy
vitality Artie's elegance looked a little faded.
The coachman descended with dignity, and tied the led horse to the
cast-iron hitching-post. "The new trap is ready and waiting, Jason,"
said the Colonel. "Just inside the door there. Chris will help you
wheel it out."
Chris was already opening the doors of the show-room. Under the
interested gaze of Artie, the Colonel, and Miss Ames from the office,
a smart trap with high red wheels was backed into the street.
Jason set about harnessing old Nellie to the trap. The Colonel went
back into the office and returned with a new whip. Artie took it from
him and cracked it critically. Both the old horses jumped, and the
coachman scowled at him.
"I'm to drive the new rig to-day, ain't I, Colonel?" he demanded.
"No. I want you to drive the surrey. We'll need that to get Miss
Bess's baggage."
"I dunno why Chris can't just as well see about the baggage,"
fumed Jason. "I put on my silk hat special."
"I explained to you before we ever left the house" began Artie.
The Colonel walked away from the argument. He secured the linen
dust-robe from the surrey. Then he climbed into the trap and picked
up the reins.
"Here!" called Artie. "Don't I get to drive the new trap, Colonel?
My cherished niece is returning to the bosom of her family after a
long absence. I ought to be in on the party somehow."
8
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"Your cherished niece happens to be my only daughter," said the
Colonel. "Hurry up, Artie, if you don't want to ride with Jason in the
baggage-wagon."
Artie looked rather put out; but he leaped without further argu-
ment into the seat beside the Colonel. With Miss Ames and Chris
watching, the trap backed, turned, and got away in the direction of
downtown. Jason, jamming down his silk hat, followed in the surrey
half a block behind.
Artie remained somewhat sulky all the length of East Avenue. But
when they approached the corner of Columbus Street, his roving eye
alighted on Eaton's flower-store. Immediately he brightened.
"Pull up here for a minute, Colonel," he said. "We've plenty of
time."
"Now what the mischief-?" the Colonel wanted to know. But he
stopped the horse.
Artie waved to Jason to pass them and continue on to the station.
Then he pranced into the florist's. Through the roses and carnations
behind the plate-glass window the Colonel could see him engaged in
sprightly conversation with the faded spinster who served the Eaton
customers. Artie loved to be the idol of servants and tradespeople;
and since he never had any money with which to promote cordial
relations, his popularity always took time.
He came out presently with two white rosebuds in his hand. "If
you wanted flowers," said the Colonel impatiently, "why didn't you
think a little sooner and bring them from home? There are bushels
of them around the place right now."
"Oh, the girl wouldn't dream of charging for such a small item!"
said Artie airily. He thrust one rosebud in his own buttonhole and
held out the other to the Colonel. "I thought we'd meet Bess in style.
These little touches count with women, you know."
The Colonel was remorseful that he hadn't thought of this "little
touch" himself. And it was thoughtful of Artie to list Bess as a
woman appreciative of such things; lots of bachelor uncles would
have considered her still a school-girl. Artie often got in his fine work
just when the Colonel was losing patience with him.
"I tell you, Art," he said, "on the way home you can drive. I just
wanted to see how the new rig runs."
ENSEMBLE
9
"It runs beautifully," said Artie. "One of your own make?"
"Our new line with rubber tires. I thought it would be good adver-
tising to have it seen around town."
"There ought to be a good sale for pleasure vehicles this summer,"
said Artie in a weighty tone. "The Crandalls have just bought them-
selves a horseless carriage, I heard yesterday. That makes the fourth
in town. But those things are more of a nuisance than a pleasure."
"A passing fad," said the Colonel carelessly. Then he added, "At
least, I hope the fad will pass. Business out at the factory hasn't been
any too good this spring."
"If you'd be satisfied to make farm wagons instead of carriages-"
began Artie. "Now what was funny in that remark?"
The Colonel checked his guffaw. "You wouldn't care to meet Bess
with a farm wagon, would you? Our buggies are always a little too
good: cost us too much to make. We have to charge more than our
competitors. Don't you want to change seats now? That will give
you a little chance to get used to the new rig."
"I wouldn't dream of taking the reins away from you on such an
important occasion," said Artie grandly.
The Colonel was always finding himself on the other side of an
argument just when he had expected to get the better of Artie. He
never quite knew how he landed there, either. Artie had come to
visit at the Langworthy house some years ago, while he "looked
around for a business opening" in Mount Royal. The Colonel had
long ago given up hope that any business opening worthy of his
talents would ever catch up with Artie. Just the same he liked the
lazy cuss-would have liked him even if he hadn't been Retta's
brother.
Now he determined to let Artie drive back from the station even
if he had to bring Jason into the fracas to force Artie's hand. It
would suit the Colonel's program better anyhow if he sat in the back
seat with Bess on the way home, and had a few words with her in
private.
For months he had been looking forward to this day. He had
originally expected to have Bess home at Christmas; but when the
child had a chance to spend the holidays with her room-mate's family
in New York, of course he couldn't stand in the way of her good
10
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
time. But as the long months went by without his darling, his eager-
ness to see her mounted and mounted. When at length the date of
her return was actually set, it seemed to him he couldn't wait.
Then this morning his spirits had been dashed by the receipt of a
letter from the school authorities. He hadn't mentioned it to Retta
at dinner time, when he went home to change into his good clothes.
He didn't believe he would mention it to Retta until he had at least
warned Bess. There must be some mistake somewhere, of course.
All the same it was a shame that such a thing had to happen just
now and take the edge off the happiness of Bess's homecoming.
He swung the new trap into Main Street just ahead of Mrs.
Palmer's victoria, in which she was taking her afternoon airing: a
large, imposing woman who didn't look as if she really needed to
rest her feet on a cushion and shade her eyes with a parasol eight
inches in diameter.
Most passers-by she didn't condescend to notice; but she gave the
Colonel a stately nod. Mrs. Palmer was a good friend of Retta's; she
was a widow and a rich woman, but she hung onto her money. Be-
side the trap her old victoria looked pretty seedy. It wouldn't do
anybody a bit of harm if Mrs. Palmer took the hint.
She had bought her son one of those new horseless carriages,
though-and reduced her old coachman's wages at the same time.
Her son was an idle scapegrace. She had had great plans for him:
college, European travel, and eventually the family business. She'd
never been able even to keep him in school; and there were ugly
stories of things the family lawyer had persuaded the police to hush
up, and of a woman bought off, and lately of a "vacation" that was
really a cure in a sanitarium.
It must be unspeakable to have your only child turn out badly; to
live in a great house alone with servants, and wait for the home-
coming of a son who might better not come, and have all news turn
out to be bad news.
If you were rich, you didn't have to worry about how your bills
would be paid. But did that amount to so much after all? The
Colonel's bills were always paid somehow. And the Colonel, in the
sunshine of a June afternoon, was on his way this minute to meet
the prettiest girl in Mount Royal, who happened also to be his very
own girl, bound for a whole summer at home.
ENSEMBLE
II
He felt like apologizing this minute to everybody who didn't have
the good luck to be Sam Langworthy. Doubtless other people had
their good moments. But who else knew the full richness of life and
how good it could be? At that, his best years were still ahead of him.
M
III
OUNT ROYAL was not the sort of town where the popula-
tion turned out en masse to see the trains arrive. Indeed, to
call it a "town" at all was an offense to civic pride. But the four
o'clock from Chicago was an important train. It made the trip in
three hours and a half, where other trains took four hours or longer;
and it had a parlor-car attached. The important people-the bankers,
the manufacturers, the owners of the zinc mines over at New Dig-
gings-always returned to Mount Royal on the four o'clock.
When Colonel Langworthy and Artie Mills reached the station,
the platform was already well lined. Dan Mason's old hack, which
ordinarily awaited fares in front of the Mount Royal Hotel, was
drawn up in a prominent place. Mrs. Cordiner was leaning from the
back seat of a well-preserved landau to chat with Mrs. Eugene
Reynolds. The Cordiners were not on a social level with the Rey-
noldses, but "young" Mrs. Reynolds passed the time of day where
her mother-in-law would have been content with bare recognition.
While the Colonel was maneuvering the trap into position on the
other side of Mrs. Cordiner, Artie's eye was caught by a turnout at
the end of the platform. It was a buckboard with five seats, drawn
by two horses even more overfed than the Colonel's, and driven by a
man in a loose blue uniform.
"Hello!" said Artie. "Isn't that the buckboard from the Soldiers'
Home? Who do you suppose they're meeting? They come here
sometimes to meet new inmates-but never more than one at a time.
This looks like a large party. Who d'you suppose it can be?"
"Couldn't say, I'm sure." The Colonel looked around for Jason and
the surrey. It hadn't yet arrived.
"Don't you get all the news at your G.A.R. meetings?" persisted
12
ENSEMBLE
13
Artie. "Or at that-what's that officers' thingamujig you belong to?-
the Loyal Legion?"
"The Loyal Legion doesn't meet in summer," said the Colonel
shortly.
The Loyal Legion was one of his own pet objects of derision. The
privates fought the War, he held, but the officers who had returned
from the fray organized the Loyal Legion as convenient backing for
themselves when they ran for Congress. Most of them had run for
Congress as soon as they got their discharge papers; a grateful coun-
try was not allowed to forget its obligations.
Any hint of jocularity on the subject from Artie, however, the
Colonel promptly discouraged. The Millses came from Kentucky,
and when the Colonel wanted to tease Retta he always told her that
her family were "all Johnny Rebs." Artie had carried his partizanship
no farther than carefully to refrain from enlisting in either army.
Just the same, enough was enough.
"I think I'll toddle down the platform and see what I can find
out," suggested Artie.
"Toddle along." The Colonel had discovered Jason over near the
freight-house. He had tied the surrey, and was now approaching to
"hold" old Nellie.
Artie set off briskly on his errand of inquiry. The Colonel looked
at his watch, lighted a cigar, and got out to stretch his legs.
Presently he heard himself hailed from behind, "Hello, Major!"
He knew without looking who that was. He had been breveted
Lieutenant-Colonel at the end of the War; no one called him below
his brevet except Captain Duquesne, who had strayed onto his own
father's staff from the preparatory department of a near-by university
in 1864, and who made up for a certain irregularity in his own posi-
tion by questioning other people's rank.
"Hello, Captain!" said the Colonel cheerfully, and produced an-
other cigar.
Duquesne accepted it and looked critically at the new trap. Jason,
who was now at the horse's head, saluted. Duquesne returned his
salute half-heartedly, and drawled, "Pretty fancy turnout you have
here, Major! Out to show the Board?"
The Colonel had guessed the answer as long ago as Artie's first
16
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
a decided limp. Artie became so fascinated by the spectacle of the
Board of Managers being welcomed to the platform that he stopped
to enjoy it.
But the Colonel passed the private car without a glance. The
whole train had drawn into the station-indeed the whole Q railroad
existed to-day-simply in order to set down one small person, one
important and unique person, the youngest of the Langworthys.
She was not immediately visible. Eugene Reynolds descended from
the parlor-car and was wafted off by his smiling wife. Mr. Cordiner
followed somewhat creakily, and Mrs. Cordiner took his arm gra-
ciously, as if his arm were something she needed to lean on; that
was her way of guiding him. Eddie Palmer, unshaven and looking
as if he hadn't slept for a week, found a haven in Dan Mason's hack.
Then at last the climax appeared. Young Doug Rutherford, a tall
ruddy youth in his twenties, got out carrying two traveling-bags, a
large open box of candy, and several magazines. He set his burdens
down hastily, and turned to give a hand to a girl who carried nothing
but a handsome leather dressing-case bound with silver and con-
spicuously monogrammed.
She was a brunette of nineteen, with a carnations-and-cream com-
plexion and thick dark hair wound in a knot at the back of her head.
She wore a long pale tan coat, smart gauntlet gloves, a little hat that
was raised at the back to allow a soft tan feather to rest against her
hair. At her waist was pinned a bunch of violets, drooping a little in
the heat. She was a creature born and trained to attract attention, and
to enjoy attracting it.
She sprang down the steps with a laugh and looked about her
eagerly. Senator Roberts paused in his ministrations and stared at
her. The reverend heads of the Board were every one turned in her
direction. The station-master walked around his baggage-cart to get
a better view, and then pretended he hadn't done anything of the
kind.
The Colonel was conscious of all these admiring glances, though he
looked only at her. Bess threw her arms around him and planted a
quick kiss on his cheek. The Colonel hugged her to him and didn't
care who saw him do it. Jubilant but a little husky, he whispered to
her, "Home at last, darling!"
"Home at last!" Bess echoed in a pretty though rather high-pitched
ENSEMBLE
15
cially by persons who have gone out of their way to call a Colonel
"Major." Anyhow, the Colonel didn't covet any appointment. He
was a business man, and had no intention of going into semi-
retirement as officer in a Soldiers' Home. But Duquesne never said
anything without a purpose; under a surface languor which often
degenerated into rudeness, he took a keen delight in shoving human
beings around the chess-board. Well, it didn't matter much. But
naturally it made a man curious.
Artie arrived full of his subject. "The Board of Managers is coming
this afternoon, and they're taking them to the hotel. Inspection out
at the Home to-morrow. I've a good mind to drive out and see the
parade."
"There won't be any parade. Just the men drawn up for inspec-
tion."
"Well, they'd let me in, wouldn't they? They let everybody in on
Decoration Day."
"Decoration Day was three weeks ago. They'll have another next
year." To get Artie off a subject on which he always found him irri-
tating, the Colonel started to offer him a cigar.
But at that moment he heard a train whistle far off down the
track. Instantly the platform was in motion. Mrs. Reynolds and Mrs.
Cordiner advanced side by side to welcome their husbands. The
station-master wheeled up a baggage-truck. Artie began to calculate
where the parlor-car would stop, and Captain Duquesne collared the
one porter.
The train rolled in and came to a standstill with the brakes shriek-
ing and the bell clanging. It had scarcely stopped when Senator
Roberts swung himself off the private car at the end.
He was a tall erect man of sixty, with a snow-white Vandyke beard,
a frock-coat, and a broad-brimmed black hat. In those days profes-
sional men took pains to look their parts; and, in addition to a man-
ner naturally authoritative, J. Cadwallader Roberts had been so long
in the public eye that generally wherever you encountered him you
could fairly hear the Vice-President announcing to the chamber and
galleries, "The Senator from Illinois!"
On the present occasion, however, he was weighted down with
two large knobby valises; and the second he had deposited them he
turned to assist the descent of a bulky bearded man who walked with
16
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
a decided limp. Artie became so fascinated by the spectacle of the
Board of Managers being welcomed to the platform that he stopped
to enjoy it.
But the Colonel passed the private car without a glance. The
whole train had drawn into the station-indeed the whole Q railroad
existed to-day-simply in order to set down one small person, one
important and unique person, the youngest of the Langworthys.
She was not immediately visible. Eugene Reynolds descended from
the parlor-car and was wafted off by his smiling wife. Mr. Cordiner
followed somewhat creakily, and Mrs. Cordiner took his arm gra-
ciously, as if his arm were something she needed to lean on; that
was her way of guiding him. Eddie Palmer, unshaven and looking
as if he hadn't slept for a week, found a haven in Dan Mason's hack.
Then at last the climax appeared. Young Doug Rutherford, a tall
ruddy youth in his twenties, got out carrying two traveling-bags, a
large open box of candy, and several magazines. He set his burdens
down hastily, and turned to give a hand to a girl who carried nothing
but a handsome leather dressing-case bound with silver and con-
spicuously monogrammed.
She was a brunette of nineteen, with a carnations-and-cream com-
plexion and thick dark hair wound in a knot at the back of her head.
She wore a long pale tan coat, smart gauntlet gloves, a little hat that
was raised at the back to allow a soft tan feather to rest against her
hair. At her waist was pinned a bunch of violets, drooping a little in
the heat. She was a creature born and trained to attract attention, and
to enjoy attracting it.
She sprang down the steps with a laugh and looked about her
eagerly. Senator Roberts paused in his ministrations and stared at
her. The reverend heads of the Board were every one turned in her
direction. The station-master walked around his baggage-cart to get
a better view, and then pretended he hadn't done anything of the
kind.
The Colonel was conscious of all these admiring glances, though he
looked only at her. Bess threw her arms around him and planted a
quick kiss on his cheek. The Colonel hugged her to him and didn't
care who saw him do it. Jubilant but a little husky, he whispered to
her, "Home at last, darling!"
"Home at last!" Bess echoed in a pretty though rather high-pitched
ENSEMBLE
17
voice. She kissed him again. Then she looked beyond him, and her
smile vanished.
"Why!" she said in a tone of sharp disappointment. "Where's
everybody?"
Artie came down the platform with long strides, leaned over to
kiss her, and thought better of it. Years ago Artie had carefully made
up his mind to stop kissing his niece Hortense because she was too
grown-up for avuncular embraces. When Bess went away to school
he had kissed her good-by. But now Bess, too, had reached the mys-
terious and disturbing age of young-womanhood. It was too bad,
in a way. But he recognized the fact which was thrust upon him.
"Nobody here but you and Papa?" asked Bess, more pleasantly
now.
"The ladies of the family are at home killing the fatted calf," ex-
plained Artie. "Where's your trunk-check? Oh, hello, Doug! Kept
Bess company on her journey, did you?"
"I didn't expect to come out on this train," answered Doug. "Glad
I did, though. I see Eddie Palmer has cornered the station-hack.
Well, he needs it worse than I do."
"You don't need it at all," said Bess. "You can ride up with us.
We've plenty of room, haven't we, Papa?"
"We have two rigs down here, as a matter of fact," said the
Colonel. "You won't mind riding in our surrey, Doug? Jason is
driving."
But Bess wouldn't hear of Doug's riding in the surrey. She handed
over her lesser baggage to Jason, retaining only her dressing-case.
Then after a single look at the trap she climbed nimbly in, passed
the raised seat, and established herself, pulling her skirts aside to
make room.
Doug promptly got in beside her. And the Colonel had promised
Artie that he could drive! Reluctantly, but without protest, he
climbed into the one remaining seat.
Artie started off uptown with a flourish. Over his shoulder he
called, "Some style to the new trap, eh, Bess?"
"-and are very anxious to have me come to them again next
Christmas," Bess was saying to Doug. "What's that, Uncle Artie?
Oh, the trap! Yes. It's new, isn't it?"
18
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"The East is fascinating," Doug volunteered. "Especially New
York. How long did you say you were there?"
"The Christmas holidays, and again at Easter. My room-mate is
a Sheldon girl. Her family have a house in the East Thirties. They
know everybody and see everything. We sat in a box at the Metro-
politan Opera, and lunched at Sherry's and Delmonico's. Mrs. Jerome
didn't like to have us dine in restaurants even with her along, be-

Doug!"
"You're going to find Mount Royal dull after all that," murmured
Doug.
Bess looked at him archly. "Oh, I don't expect to find it too dull!"
"That's why I'm making you a present of the trap." Colonel Lang-
worthy screwed around in his seat and addressed his daughter di-
rectly. "I though you and Hortense needed some way of getting
around this summer and seeing your young friends."
"You mean to say we'll be allowed to drive old Nellie ourselves?"
Bess giggled. "Well, Nellie is safe enough. I'll say that for her."
"That's only temporary. I plan to get a new horse especially for
you, if I can find one that's gentle." The Colonel hadn't planned pre-
cisely that; he had planned to let her have Nellie and hire a livery
horse on a monthly basis in case it seemed necessary to take the trap
and the landau out at the same time. But he was the person who had
brought up the subject in front of Doug; and after all a horse of her
own was little enough, surely, when she would have the whole sum-
mer to beguile at home.
"I'd like a riding-horse," said Bess eagerly. "I've learned to ride
quite well, and I love it."
"Some girls in the East are beginning to ride astride," volunteered
Doug.
"Not the nicest girls," said Bess decisively.
There was an old side-saddle in the barn; it had been brought
years ago from Kentucky. As a little girl Bess used to ride the
carriage-horses. Of course she couldn't be expected to do that now.
But what made her say she had now "learned to ride"? She was a
born rider: had always taken to it.
Old Nellie's hoofs clattered up Main Street; the rubber tires of the
ENSEMBLE
19
new trap rolled soundlessly over the granite pavement. From behind
his rows of dangling watches Tom Holmes looked out at the new
Langworthy rig, and the deep lines at the corners of his mouth drew
down sardonically.
Just beyond Tom Holmes's perch of observation Artie twitched
on the reins. The Colonel looked at him sharply; but Artie had
already changed his mind.
"I don't believe they've gone there," he muttered. "They naturally
wouldn't. The Government pays all their expenses."
"Who hasn't gone where?" demanded the Colonel.
"The Board of Managers. They always used to put up at the
Mount Royal. But I suppose this year they've gone to the Manning
Arms."
"It's a good thing if somebody goes there," said the Colonel.
"Charley Manning bit off a lot, building a hotel the size of that in a
town the size of this."
"Oh, he doesn't expect to make money by it!" said Artie airily.
"He says he built it so he wouldn't have to drive out into the country
on rainy nights."
The Colonel shot him a warning glance. Where Charley Manning
spent his nights wasn't a question he cared to have discussed in front
of Bess.
"Personally," Artie went on, "I don't care for the Manning Arms.
I think it's flashy. The solid people here have always found the old
Mount Royal good enough for them and their friends."
The Colonel preferred not to answer that last observation. Was
Artie fool enough to suppose that the Colonel didn't know that his
brother-in-law had been warned away from the bar of the substantial
old Mount Royal Hotel because he couldn't pay his bills there? He
wasn't welcome now even in the lobby, whence he used to bring
home all the town gossip. The Colonel respected his pride, but he
wished Artie wouldn't bother to keep up pretenses in front of him.
It made him uncomfortable.
Two blocks farther on they came abreast of the new Manning
Arms. It was a massive stone pile seven stories high; through the
swinging doors could be glimpsed a lobby with luxurious arm-chairs,
Oriental rugs, and the rows of potted palms which shut off sight of
the elevators and the entrance to the bar.
20
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Artie pulled the horse down to a walk. "I've a good mind to go in
and see whether the Board really is staying here," he muttered.
"Do you expect us to wait while you inquire?"
Artie handed over the reins. "No, oh, no! It's a fine afternoon. I
won't mind the walk home. Or I can take the street-car if I'm de-
layed."
"Very well," said the Colonel, moving into the driver's seat. If the
two of them had been alone, he wouldn't have minded standing

Artie might have gone on home with them.
A substitute was already offering himself. From the back seat

coachman understood that one of those grips belongs to me. Perhaps
I'd better go on home with you and claim my baggage."
"If it shows up at our house, I'll send it over to you," said the
Colonel. He turned the horse up Prospect Avenue. Bess giggled.
They passed the William Reynolds' house, one of the largest in
Mount Royal, with Eugene's mansion, only slightly smaller, occupy-
ing the rest of the block. Then came the Pattersons'; a group of
young people were playing croquet on the side lawn. Bess waved to
them eagerly, and Elsie Patterson waved back.
The Alexander Kirkland house looked shut up, as usual. Just be-

ours has a sharp turn in it."
"A very sharp turn," agreed Bess. "That's where you tipped me
out of a cutter winter before last. Remember?"
"I'd just as soon not be reminded. It was no way to treat a girl."
Doug tilted the front seat of the trap, swung a leg out, and turned
to take his leave of Bess.
She looked up at him under her eyelashes. "You can't think how
glad I was to see you get into that parlor-car. I was afraid Eddie
Palmer might make a nuisance of himself."
"I know. A girl traveling alone!" murmured Doug.
The Colonel looked at her sharply. A teacher from Sheldon School
had accompanied her and three other girls as far as Chicago. Was
she going to leave Doug with the idea that she was allowed to stray
around the country all by herself?
ENSEMBLE
21
Bess passed from demureness to cordiality. "The candy was lovely.
Bill Jerome used to buy me candy from some place in New York
that everybody there thinks the world of; but I'm sure Gunther's is
every bit as good."
"Who's Bill Jerome?" demanded Doug.
"My room-mate's brother."
"Oh, just your room-mate's brother?"
"Well, of course he took us around New York while I was staying

room-mate, I must have seemed very young to him. But he couldn't
do less than be nice to a guest, could he?"
"I don't suppose he hurt his own feelings." Doug sprang out of the
trap and waved a hand. "Good-by for the present. Good day, Colonel.
Thanks for the lift."
The Colonel kept the seat tilted for a moment, but Bess made no
move to join him in front. He slammed the seat down and mut-
tered, "I didn't know you could buy five-pound boxes of candy on
the train."
"Oh, he had it with him!" said Bess casually. "I suppose it was
intended for some one else."
The Colonel jerked sharply on the reins. He reminded himself that
Bess was nineteen now. At that age the Colonel's mother had been
married and had produced a baby. He mustn't allow himself to be
jealous of Bess's young men; he wanted her to have the same "good
time" that other girls of her age expected. Just the same, he some-
times wished she could have stayed a little girl; the time had been
when her father was the big person in her life.
Funny, he had never heard about Doug's tipping her out of the
cutter! And this Bill Jerome was something more to worry about;
the Colonel didn't want Bess to get any ideas of settling in the East.
It had been a wrench to let her go so far away even for a year at
school. But Retta had thought Sheldon School could do a lot for
Bess. It was too bad Sheldon hadn't had the same idea.

H
E drove on along Prospect Avenue. This was as short as any
other way home, and much the showiest.
"The McDonald place looks nice this year," he commented.
"They've put in a lot of shrubbery. Another year I suppose Mrs.
Crandall will have to have shrubbery, too. Then the next thing you
know there'll be a patch of bushes on every lawn on this street.
What one of this crew does, the others all hurry to imitate."
Bess yawned a little. "Just the same, I wish we lived here. I like
Prospect Avenue."
"We could just as well have built here," the Colonel apologized.
"On a smaller lot, perhaps, to equalize the expense; the house itself
would have cost the same wherever we built it. But at the time I
thought that the north side of town was the coming section. We
have the only hill for miles around, and the only trees except these
confounded soft maples."
"Yes," said Bess. "But look at the district you have to drive through
to get there."
The Colonel looked at it: tried to see it through her eyes, when
they had turned off Prospect Avenue and started north. There were
a lot of double houses in here: roomy frame structures put up by
people who lived in one half and rented the other. Under that ar-
rangement your property carried itself. Molly Travers lived in one
of those houses, a house not distinguished above its neighbors; and
Mrs. Travers was one of Mrs. Langworthy's little circle of intimates.
"These houses show that they belong to the people who live in
them," he apologized. "The flower-gardens are pretty right now.
You can smell the roses as you go by."
"It gets much worse farther on," said Bess. "Did you think I'd been
away so long that I'd forgotten what the old town looks like?"
22
ENSEMBLE
23
It was quite true that north of the Q track began a much less
desirable district: it was given over to workmen's cottages, many of
them built a row at a time as a speculation, and rented to a poor
class of tenants. This wasn't the sort of building which the Colonel
had hoped to see go in between his property and downtown.
But luckily by the time they reached the poorest streets Bess's mind
had passed off her surroundings. She leaned on the back of the seat
beside him to ask, "What's all this business about Jason marrying
Louise? Aunt Amy wrote me something about it, but I couldn't
see why it should cause such a furore in the family."
The Colonel guffawed. "Oh, yes, he married her all right! It was
your mother's doing, really."
"Mamma's doing? I didn't know she thought so well of Jason."
"Your mother always has a feud with the coachman," agreed the
Colonel. "I tried once having a houseman and a coachman both.
But we didn't get along any better. Jason takes a good deal of pride
in his work."
"But why should Mamma make him marry the cook?"
"She spoke pretty sharply to him about hanging around the
kitchen so much. He was supposed to get his meals there, and then
leave. But first he took to spending his evenings talking to Louise.
Then he was in there having her get him snacks when he was sup-
posed to be working. Well, when your mother scolded him about it,
he up and asked Louise to marry him."
"She's old as the hills!" commented Bess.
"She's a splendid cook, though. It seemed to me like a good ar-
rangement. I had a bathroom put in on the third floor when Jason
moved in from his room over the stable. Louise and the second girl
had always used one of the second-floor bathrooms, but I could
imagine the fracas if Jason had to wash off the stable smell in any
room Hortense and Dora used."
"Heavens, yes!" agreed Bess. "Servants are a nuisance, anyhow.
But Mamma has always prided herself that her 'girls' left her only to
be married. Now she can boast that they don't leave even then."
"Your mother has been very busy all spring," went on the Colonel,
"having things planted and transplanted and then replanted. The
grape-arbor is about the only thing she doesn't root up every year.
She could keep six men and a boy busy carrying out her orders."
24
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"Uncle Artie is supposed to keep sort of an eye on the yard and
garden, isn't he? Surely I've heard him say, 'That's my depart-
ment!'"
"I've heard that myself a hundred times." The Colonel groaned a
little. "Next year I believe I'll get a man over from the Soldiers'
Home to help around the place for a few months. He can have
Jason's old room over the stable."
"Mamma keeps well?" inquired Bess. "She didn't get around to
write to me very often. If it hadn't been for your letters and Aunt
Amy's, sometimes I'd hardly have known that I had a family at all."
"Your mother has a lot to attend to, running such a big house-
hold," said the Colonel. Loyally he suppressed the reflection that the
household ran itself, aside from Mrs. Langworthy's casual orders to
Louise. Mrs. Langworthy cut and arranged flowers, began large
pieces of embroidery which she generally tired of long before they
were finished, and read any book that she didn't have to leave her
chair to get. She was a heavy woman with exceptionally small feet
and hands; under her lazy good humor she had an iron will. The
Colonel had learned years ago that the way to get on with her was
not to cross her.
"I've been away from her for a long time," said Bess. "Couldn't
you drive just a little faster, Papa? Seems to me it's taking us forever
to get there."
The ugly rows of workmen's cottages were scarcer now. There
were many vacant lots, grown up to burdock and mullein. The old
mare smelled her supper, and began to pick up her heels. The sun-
light was dazzling when they turned into Washington Avenue.
This had been a perfect June day; but to-morrow would be hot.
Washington Avenue had been laid out with an eye to the future.
It was a wide street, with elm trees planted at regular intervals along
the parking. The lots here had originally been held at good prices;
many of them were still being held.
But that gave open space on the town side of the Colonel's house;
and beyond it to the west open country began, and a little brook
ran north to the river.
The Colonel's grounds occupied half a block. Stone gate-posts had
been erected when the house was built, and for a year or two they
had waited forlornly for the fence which should have flanked them.
ENSEMBLE
25
Then a lilac hedge had been planted clear across the front of the lot.
The bushes were ten-foot trees now, gorgeous in their bloom about
the middle of May, and always furnishing a substantial screen be-
tween the grounds and the street. The vacant half of the block was
shut off less loftily by a row of bridal-wreath bushes.
From the gate-posts a gravel drive led to the right of the house and
turned in a spacious sweep to enclose a little island of verdure, where
between two tall trees there still hung the swing that Bess had used
as a child; the same swing, the family always considered it, though
the ropes were renewed every year. Between the drive and the lot
line the side yard was thickly spotted with flower-beds, some large
and some small, put in at random. There blossomed Oriental pop-
pies, a solid mass of nasturtiums, square yards of sweet peas staked
up with brush. To the north of the flower-beds was a rose-trellis,
obviously new, and just beyond it at the back of the Colonel's
grounds a grass tennis-court, carefully rolled and marked.
The wide veranda which ran almost across the front of the house
was set out with wicker chairs. When they turned into the drive a
slender woman in a frock of delicate "dusty" pink rose as if she had
been watching for their approach. The old mare started deter-
minedly for her stable, which lay between the tennis-court and the
house. But the Colonel jerked her head around into the small spur
of drive which led directly to the front steps.
"There's Auntie Dora on the watch for us!" said Bess in some
excitement.
The Colonel corrected her. "That's Amy. Dora's hair has grown
so gray this past year that you could see it from here."
Amy had advanced to the screen-door and spoken to some one in
the house. She was promptly joined by a smaller woman with gray
hair, who wore a frock of pale lilac. That was Dora. She was fol-
lowed in turn by a tall girl in cornflower blue, whose bright yellow
hair was wound in a great braid around her head.
The Colonel cramped the wheel hastily and stood up to tilt his
seat. Bess got out, holding her pale tan coat carefully away from the
wheel. She had just reached the foot of the steps when the screen-
door opened once more. The three women, who had started down
the steps to greet Bess, stood aside to let Mrs. Langworthy be the
first to kiss her.
26
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Then they engulfed her. The Colonel dismounted slowly, not
looking where he stepped. His full attention was on the perfume and
rustle and femininity that swirled about Bess. Mrs. Langworthy, as
became her size and matronly dignity, was wearing a thin dress of
mixed black and white. With the lilac and pink and blue of the
others, it formed a perfect combination.
Women-and summer-and welcome home! It was for sights and
sounds like this that a man lived through the working days and
somehow managed to meet his pay-roll at the end of the week. The
Colonel tied the mare and got Bess's dressing-case out of the back
seat. He had intended the new trap as a surprise for the whole
family, of course. But there would be time enough later for them to
notice that.
They were grouped on three or four steps, and all talking at once.
Mrs. Langworthy had her arm around Bess again, and on the other
side Hortense was admiring her hat, with support from Dora.
Rather abruptly Amy detached herself from the group and came
down the steps toward the Colonel.
Amy was the youngest of the Mills "girls": one of those slim, age-
less women to whom nothing ever happens, and who never change
from one year's end to the next. She was a person of rather terrific
reserve; the Colonel had always stood a little in awe of her, though
she had been an inmate of his household since the year Bess was
born. Many people were shy with Amy; perhaps she was shy with
them to begin with. Or perhaps it was largely her trick a trick that
seemed to hint mystery and veiled power-of speaking without lift-
ing her eyes.
She did not lift them now, though she came close to the Colonel
to ask in a low voice, "Where's Artie?"
"He stopped at the Manning Arms to find out whether the Sol-
diers' Home Board was staying there," explained the Colonel. "We
ran into them at the station. They're in town for the night."
"He stopped at the Manning Arms?" The delicate color faded
suddenly from Amy's cheeks. Her blood betrayed her, though she
was trying to sound careless. Back in Kentucky not even the grown
son of the family had ever been allowed a latch-key; it had always
fallen to Amy's lot to lie awake and listen for Artie and try to
smuggle him into the house without his father's knowledge.
ENSEMBLE
27
"He knows supper is at six in this house, and we don't wait for
anybody. He'll be along all right when the bell rings," the Colonel
assured her.
"Oh, of course!" Amy looked up then, but not at him. "This is the
new trap, isn't it? Louise told Dora after dinner that you were tak-
ing one out of the store."
Louise had learned the news from Jason and told it to Dora.
Dora told Amy, and probably Retta and Hortense, too. The Colonel
supposed a good deal of information traveled into the family by the
backstairs route. Vaguely he wondered whether he himself ever
heard some of it.
"It's about time we had something newer than the landau, isn't
it?" he asked.
"This is very smart. So gay and-and light-looking. I've always
admired a trap." Amy raised her eyes to his for a moment. They
were dark blue eyes, very clear and flashing; they spoke of a deep
vitality, and rather belied the thinness of her skin and the fragility
of her wrists and ankles.
"We won't use the surrey any more, except maybe for errands,"
explained the Colonel. "Retta will still find the landau easier to get
into, I suppose. But you other girls make Bess take you in the trap.
Cut a dash around town when you go to see your friends."
Amy smiled, shook her head a little, and turned away. But in-
stead of rejoining the chattering group on the veranda, she walked
to the corner of the house and whistled. The dogs had been off on a
prowl, or they would have greeted the arrival of the "rig." They
came bounding around the house in answer to Amy's whistle.
There were three of them. A gray collie, middle-aged and very
dignified, led the procession. Then came a Russian poodle, nearly as
large as the collie, but elaborately clipped like a French poodle. He
was a deep tobacco brown. Behind him trotted a black cocker spaniel,
which looked very small after the other two.
The dogs jumped all over the Colonel. He shouted "Down!" at the
collie and the poodle; they got down, after they had both licked his
face. But he picked up the little spaniel.
Bess turned at the commotion. Then she shook herself free of the
women and came running down the steps. She hugged both the big
dogs and scratched them at the base of the skull, where a dog loves
28
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
to be scratched. Then she threw a stone for the collie to fetch, and
put out a hand for the poodle to give his paw.
The Colonel, still holding the spaniel in reserve, grinned under
his mustache. There had been moments this afternoon when he
feared that Bess might not like her home quite the way she used to.
He had noticed the thing happening in other people's families. The
children, after they had grown up and gone away to school, never
seemed to belong again in just the old way.
A
T the sound of the supper-bell Bess came running downstairs,
followed by Dora, who had been helping her unpack. The
rest of the family assembled from the veranda and the library. Only
Artie had not yet appeared.
The dining-room was the largest room in the house. It occupied
the first floor of the wing which had been built on about fifteen
years ago, and could be entered through either the parlor or the
library.
It would have accommodated forty people easily; but the walls
were so crowded with china closets that there was scarcely room for
the heavy sideboard, and the serving-table had to stand in front of a
window. Mrs. Langworthy liked dishes, and was always buying new
ones: always introducing them, too, with such a sensible explanation
of the necessity for their purchase that there was no escaping the
conclusion that if it had not been for her constant vigilance the
Langworthys would shortly have been eating off the floor.
Four tall windows opened to the west and the open country. Three
looked to the south, where the lawn was broken by a grape-arbor
and a not-entirely-successful pond, which had been created under
Artie's direction by diverting some of the flow of the brook that ran
just outside the Colonel's premises. At this hour on a summer after-
noon the room was full of warm bright light. Hortense sometimes
said Aunt Retta didn't realize that not everybody liked to eat in a
glare; but Mrs. Langworthy retorted that if Hortense preferred "a
dim religious light," she was always at liberty to pull down the
shades in her own room.
Mrs. Langworthy sat at the north end of the table, with her back
to the spacious "butler's pantry." In front of her stood a tea- and
coffee-service on a huge silver tray. The tray had been the Colonel's
29
30
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
present to her on their twenty-fifth wedding anniversary, but the
service had come with her from Kentucky. Beside the tray stood a
small silver bell. When the house was wired for electricity the
Colonel had had a push-bell installed in the floor near her chair; but
after a week or two of groping with her foot for the elusive button,
Mrs. Langworthy had had the old bell restored to use.
The Colonel sat at the other end of the table, with the carving
things before him. He carved well, and he liked to do it. He kept
the carving-knife sharp as a razor, and never used it without giving
it a few preliminary whets on the steel.
Dora and Bess sat at Mrs. Langworthy's right; Bess, of course,
was next to her father. Amy sat at Mrs. Langworthy's left, and Hor-
tense at the Colonel's right. Between them stood a chair which was
vacant to-night until the rest of them had been seated and served.
Then Artie entered with careful precision and took the vacant
place. The Colonel served him promptly, and Artie devoted himself
to his food. Only his unusual dignity betrayed the fact that he had
been drinking; he'd be all right when he had had something to eat.
They had porterhouse steak for supper: three thick steaks, dark
brown on the outside and richly red within. When the Colonel had
served everybody generously and laid aside a portion in reserve, he
sent the platter out to the kitchen. He liked the servants to have
their steak hot and tender.
There were baked potatoes, young onions, and tiny crisp radishes,
and a large bowl of new peas. Most of Mount Royal thought it was
doing well to have fresh peas by the Fourth of July. But the Lang-
worthy garden specialized in early peas, and in a variety of purple
corn which no one else in the town grew.
Amy dished lettuce already dressed, and Dora stewed fruit. Ida,
the second girl, waiting on table in a cap to emphasize the special
nature of Bess's homecoming, passed around hot rolls. Aside from
Ida's cap, the meal was not more elaborate than any other supper
in that household. Officially the Langworthys dined at noon; but
dinner was distinguished from supper only by the appearance of
soup at dinner.
During the early part of the meal Bess chattered about her jour-
ney and asked a few questions about the garden. An immense bowl
of roses stood in the middle of the table. Mrs. Langworthy had
ENSEMBLE
31
filched them, practically at sword's point, from under the guardian-
ship of Jason, who always held that flowers were grown to "make
the yard look nice."
"I like flowers on the table," said Bess. "The Jeromes in New
York have fresh ones delivered every day by a florist."
"Haven't you always been used to something on the table?" de-

"You liked your school, didn't you?" asked Amy.
"No normal person likes school," said Dora. For all her gray hair
and the exquisite perfection of her dress, Dora could look at times

"A year at boarding-school was the most Papa could do for us, he

"I didn't like even what I had," said Dora. "I didn't relish having

"If you don't like having females around, why do you organize

the woman's suffrage bee so hard, you wouldn't take 'No' for an

"Oh, I retired from that crowd in April! Handed back my gavel,

had come from the local w.c.T.U. He was relieved to hear that Dora

"If you don't like women in meetings, Dode," said the Colonel,
"it's probably due to the fact that you don't like meetings. If you

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
had seen more of men in meetings, you'd find that they're just as

She helped herself to more peas.
"We really ought to have a better library in a town of this size,"
conceded Mrs. Langworthy. "The rooms are all right: upstairs above

"The Public Library is supported out of the taxes," went on the

33
noon. The Library fund is barely enough to pay rent to the G.A.R.

The Colonel regarded his small sister-in-law with interest. Dora
wore a three-and-a-half shoe and was the special pet of the neighbor-
hood children; she entertained them on the "island" in the drive in
summer, and in winter gave them cocoa and told them stories in her
bedroom. Dora walking into a meeting of the town's crustiest citi-
zens was a piquant notion.
"What was your new idea?" he asked. "Not a larger appropria-
tion?"
"That would take too long," said Dora. "I suggested instead that
we get up a fund by popular subscription, and use every cent of it
to buy new books."
The Colonel guffawed. "You suggested that to old Reynolds? He
probably fainted on the spot."
"He didn't dare faint. He was afraid I'd go through his pockets
while he was unconscious. So he hung onto himself, and told me
he had given every cent he could afford to the new hospital when
they raised a subscription for that."
"The new hospital was built three years ago," said Mrs. Lang-
worthy dryly.
"Exactly. But all our manufacturers and bankers practically bank-
rupted themselves to build it. Or at least, after Dad Reynolds
thought of that and spoke up, they all breathed freer."
"Didn't any of them give you anything?" demanded Hortense.
"The Rev. John Warren Meade did. He followed me into the hall
and pressed five dollars on me. He was afraid to come out in front
of the rest; but I'm sure he couldn't afford as much as he gave."
"Then the one subscription you did get hurt your conscience?"
pressed Hortense.
"It hurt my conscience, but I took his money. It's a beginning,"
said Dora.

Artie looked up suddenly. "If you really wanted money, you

"I wouldn't brag too much about such a friendship," said Mrs.

be such a goose as to make that remark about a "chum" of Charley

Retta's little group of intimates got busy with interesting tidbits.
But you'd think she must hear something. And even a tenth part of
what people said about Charley Manning was enough to entrench
Mrs. Langworthy in her position that his very existence was in the
nature of an insult to chaste matronhood.
Ida cleared away the first course and set down in front of Bess a

cut the cake as if this were her birthday. The Colonel passed his cup
for more coffee. Dora, after one glance at the cake, said she'd have a
little more stewed fruit instead of dessert.
Mrs. Langworthy liked to linger at the supper-table, pouring her-
self additional half-cups of tea and sipping them slowly. But when
Bess had finished her cake she asked to be excused. Dora left the
room with Bess.
The Colonel followed the two of them upstairs. Bess's room was
at the front of the house, next to the Colonel's and Retta's, which
occupied the entire space above the dining-room.
At present the room looked as if at least three girls were moving

considered "very artistic."
"Well, it's all nice and new," said Bess. "Aunt Dora, we'll have to

"I'm moving downstairs, thank you," said the Colonel. "You'll be

"Oh, Papa, not to-night!" pouted Bess. "I'll sing for you some

"Come down anyhow, and listen to the rest of us. No hurry, of

He withdrew to the library, which lay to the left of the entrance-
hall and just beyond the solid walnut staircase. It was called "the
library" because the house plan had originally designated it that way.
There was one glass-fronted book-case here, as against two in the
sitting-room and a lot of open shelves beyond the sitting-room in
what had been the dining-room before the wing was added.
The so-called library was furnished with deep leather-covered
chairs and a vast, inviting leather couch. There was a desk, in case
the Colonel ever cared to work at home. There were also Hortense's
easel and big color-box (the windows of this room opened to the
north), Artie's fishing tackle, and in one corner a table piled with
mending.
Artie joined the Colonel presently, accepted a cigar, and settled
himself for a little man-to-man talk. Since they were two men en-
tirely surrounded by women, Artie always took it for granted that
his society had special charms for his brother-in-law.
It was in this very room that Artie had talked the Colonel into
the manufacture of a patented fishing reel: the only business ven-
ture in which the two of them had ever been associated. They still
made the best reel on the market, the Colonel was convinced. The
only trouble was that aside from the two or three a year which the
Colonel managed to dispose of at the store, their reel had no sale
whatever.
But Artie was one of those unsuccessful men who always know
just how the successful men could have done better. He settled him-
self to-night to explain that the First National Bank, under the
direction of William Reynolds and John Rutherford, was conduct-
ing its business on lines altogether too conservative. "They believe
in keeping the money in the bank, just the way Dora's old girls in
the Public Library believe in keeping the books on the shelves," said
Artie lucidly.
