37
He was just warming nicely to his subject when he was inter-
rupted by loud and singular noises. A continuous discharge of fire-
crackers, accompanied by odd squeaks and squeals, seemed to be
approaching the front of the house.
Artie went to look. A minute later he called in some excitement,
"Oh, Colonel! Doug Rutherford has just driven his father over in
their new horseless carriage. Come and have a look at it."
Just as the Colonel reached the hall Bess came rushing down-
stairs, followed more decorously by Dora. Even Mrs. Langworthy
sallied out to the drive to have a look at the strange vehicle-a car-
riage deprived of its horse, and looking very unfinished and mourn-
ful under the deprivation-which was puffing and snorting at the
Langworthy door-step.
When the inspection was over, Bess led Doug to the chairs on
the veranda. She was at her most vivacious now, and didn't seem at
all tired. Perhaps she had expected him to offer her a ride in the new
contraption; but when Doug had his father along, he was under
orders.
The Colonel invited John Rutherford into the library for a glass
of port. The port was kept under lock and key, in a cabinet deco-
rated with "poker work" by Hortense. The Colonel never had
whiskey in the house, though he took his drink at a hotel bar with
the rest of them. He had seen whiskey enough drunk during the
War, when it took the place of medicine, of anesthetic, and too often
of food.
Artie, too, was accommodated with a glass of port; but John
Rutherford ignored him and plunged into the errand which had
brought him here. Old John never paid a social call unless he ex-
pected to gain something by it; but there were some matters which
could be discussed over a glass of wine more amicably than in his
dreary little office at the bank.
At the bank he had often renewed a loan for the Colonel. Two
or three years ago the bank had actually held a mortgage on the
Colonel's factory; but a later loan had cleared that off, using as col-
lateral the Colonel's stock in the newly reorganized and ambitiously
retitled "transit company." The new note would fall due in July.
Rutherford had come to suggest that the Colonel "clear up the whole
business" by turning that stock over to the bank.
38
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
The Colonel was amused by the suggestion; but he was a little
indignant, too. He could see as far through a hole in a stone wall as
John Rutherford.
"That stock is as good in my pocket as it is in yours," he said
defiantly. "Better, perhaps. I intend to leave it to Mrs. Langworthy
when I die. A widow needs something she can turn into ready cash.
Don't they always take their money and go to Europe?"
"It's just as you say, of course," growled Rutherford. "You can
get another loan, I suppose, if you prefer to keep on borrowing
money. But I thought perhaps you'd like to go ahead with a clean
slate. The carriage-business isn't what it used to be."
"It never will be again." Artie spoke up suddenly. "Mr. Ruther-
ford, why don't you back the Colonel and have him start manu-
facturing horseless carriages? He's got the factory already, if he
wanted to use it that way. Or if you can't talk him into it, why don't
you start some one else in at that business? It would be better than
loaning money on farm mortgages and loading up the bank with a
lot of property it can't resell."
"Artie," said the Colonel, "don't you want to go and see if Jason
can't find a few peas left on the vines? He could still see to pick 'em,
and I'm sure Mrs. Rutherford would like some."
Artie, with a lingering glance at the port bottle, took himself off.
The Colonel poured a second glass for John Rutherford, but the con-
versation languished. John probably expected the Colonel to back
down in his brother-in-law's absence. The Colonel had no such in-
tention. Artie didn't embarrass him that much.
Presently John Rutherford looked at his watch and rose. Doug
was enjoying himself on the veranda and was in no hurry to leave.
Just the same he left.
Bess stood watching the car churn its way down the drive. The
Colonel slipped an arm through hers, and she made no protest when
he drew her into the sitting-room. On his way past the parlor-door
he called to Mrs. Langworthy, who, conscious of Rutherfords in the
house, was sitting under the great central chandelier which lighted
mahogany upholstered in sprigged satin and set off by the new rug,
a wide expanse of rosy garlands on a pale background.
The sitting-room was less magnificent but far more comfortable.
It had easy-chairs, rocking-chairs, low lights. Bowls of flowers stood
ENSEMBLE
39
on little tables, and a large jardinière filled with tall stalks of holly-
hocks masked the empty fireplace. Beside the upright piano stood an
open cabinet overrunning with sheet music; and on top of the piano,
among family photographs in heavy silver frames, lay a banjo tied
end to end with a broad red ribbon.
"Come on in here, Dora!" called the Colonel. "Hortense, tell Artie
not to mind about those peas; our company has gone home. Amy!
Where's Amy? Amy, didn't you get some new music last week?
Now's your chance to try it on the dog."
Two or three times a week he constituted himself master of cere-
monies, and inaugurated these musical evenings for the family. He
held that people who lived under the same roof were in duty bound
to entertain one another sometimes, not simply meet for meals.
They came trooping in answer to his call, the gracious women in
their pretty summer frocks. They disputed laughingly over their
music. Retta held things up because she couldn't find her thimble.
Retta always embroidered while she was being sung to; she insisted
that she could listen just as well if she kept her fingers busy. Artie
fussed with chairs, and opened windows, and moved the piano a
little so that the player wouldn't be sitting in her own light.
When the audience was finally settled, Bess stood beside the piano
and sang to them. She had taken piano-lessons at Sheldon, but she
kept her instrumental exercises for another time. Amy played her
accompaniments. Hortense played the piano very well, much better
in fact than Amy. But Hortense had never been allowed to accom-
pany Bess's singing at the musical evenings; she was too sharp with
the singer.
Bess sang until she was hoarse. Then Dora took the banjo from
the top of the piano and accompanied herself while she sang
"Georgia Camp Meeting" and "Whistling Rufus, the One Man
Band."
That got Artie started on his darky stories. He seldom told his
stories around town, having discovered that audiences in Mount
Royal tended to confuse the teller with the thing told. But the fam-
ily, though they had heard his repertory a hundred times, laughed
in all the right places. His two-part stories, in which he had Dora's
coöperation, always made a nice climax.
40
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
The Colonel himself did not perform; when he had got the rest
of them started, he withdrew into the background. From a chair near
the door he looked over the room: all the household so busy, so
happy, so-so all together.
Amy had come to stay with the Langworthys before Bess was
born. Retta, who had got through ten years of married life without
children, was nervous about her approaching ordeal, and the Colonel
had thought that the company of her sister would cheer her up.
When the baby arrived Amy had become its slave; and beside the
baby there were the dogs to be seen to, and a succession of cats, and
generally a parrot or a cage of canaries. Amy had always been quiet,
and even a little strange. But surely if she hadn't fitted in here from
the first, she wouldn't have stayed.
When Bess was still very small, "Papa" Mills had died back in
Kentucky, leaving mixed memories of himself and little of a more
substantial nature. Naturally Retta had offered a home to her sister
Dora and her orphaned-her worse than orphaned-niece. Artie had
drifted to Mount Royal with them, in search of that elusive business
opportunity.
It wasn't the sort of household the Colonel would ever have
planned in advance. He would have liked three stalwart sons and
three lovely daughters. He wasn't quite sure how he hadn't happened
to have more of a family. Retta was the type of woman commonly
described as "motherly," and she had been in her twenties when she
was married.
But this oddly mixed household was also oddly satisfactory. In
spite of certain drawbacks-Artie's shiftlessness and Dora's spurts of
rebellion and Hortense's fits of temperament-they always had good
times together. Moreover, they fitted into something of a coherent
whole. In fact as well as in name, the household was "the Lang-
worthys," though more than half of its constituents were of the Mills
family. (People commonly thought of Hortense as "a Mills," though
her name was actually Gibson.)
This was an especially enjoyable evening. Even Retta presently
laid aside her embroidery to assist in a burlesque of the duets she and
Dora used to play at home, with Artie imitating their late father's
stentorian shouts of alternate encouragement and disapproval. This
was one of the tricks which Dora and Artie had worked up between
ENSEMBLE
41
them. It was new to Bess, and she giggled over it until she was a
little hysterical.
She sprang to help, too, when Amy appeared with a tray of claret
lemonade and the rest of the cocoanut cake from dinner. "This is
quite a party," she said. "Quite a party, really!"
But then life at the Langworthys was always like a party. All that
they had needed lately was to have Bess home again. She was with
them now, with them joyously and completely. Clearly, she loved
being here.
The Colonel was glad now that he hadn't got around to tell her of
the cloud on her homecoming. It might have cast a shadow on her
evening.
BOOK TWO
DORA
D
I
ORA MILLS had been dreaming that she walked alone in a
strange city. Twilight was creeping on, and ahead of her lay
a street of tall houses, dark, shuttered, apparently deserted. A myste-
rious excitement filled her: she knew that if she could get to the end
of this street she would turn into the lighted square where something
very wonderful was about to happen. It was a fête of fêtes, quite
unlike anything she had ever witnessed. If she could only hold on
until she turned the corner, she would see a coronation or a royal
wedding, or perhaps some high medieval festival. She wanted to go
on dreaming.
But Dora always waked wide, as a child wakes. Under the half-
drawn shades the sunlight of a July morning was making the room
very bright. She sat up in bed, stretched, and wrinkled her nose at
the glare.
In the bed across the room Amy still slept. An open book lay face
downward on a chair at her bedside, on top of her stockings and
chemise. Amy was a poor sleeper, and generally read late at night.
Often even after her light was out, if Dora chanced to wake she
would hear Amy stirring.
Amy had turned on her side now, away from the light. Under the
sheet her slim body lay relaxed; lying so she looked very young, and
somehow queerly defenseless.
She and Dora had never roomed together until they came to the
Langworthy house to live. But Dora wanted Hortense to have a room
to herself. Dora could have roomed with anybody; and it didn't
bother her to hear herself lumped with Amy as "the aunts." Dora
liked being an aunt, and encouraged the use of the title outside of
the family circle. Amy was carried along, acquiescent though never
quite fitting.
42
DORA
43
Dora got into her slippers and stole across the room, being careful
to make no noise. From the dresser she collected her brushes, from
the closet two towels and a sponge bag. In her right hand she man-
aged to assemble her clothes. She could dress in the bathroom, so as
not to disturb Amy.
The Langworthy establishment was run on what the Colonel con-
sidered a "comfortable" scale. Compared to the genteel poverty that
Dora had known as a girl in Kentucky, the style of living was lavish.
But the house was in many ways decidedly inconvenient.
All the bedrooms opened off a corridor that ran across the house.
Artie's door was opposite Amy and Dora's. Farther along, beyond
the hall which traversed the house from front to back, Hortense's
door was opposite Bess's. At the end of the corridor was the door into
the bedroom which occupied the wing: the Colonel's room and
Retta's.
But all the bathrooms were on the north-and-south hall: one at
the front, between Dora's room and Bess's, two at the back, over the
kitchen. It was impossible to get from any bedroom to a bathroom
without going into a corridor and turning a corner; and to reach
one of the bathrooms at the back, Dora had to travel through two
corridors and pass the front stairs, the back stairs, and the door which
concealed the stairs to the third floor.
"The aunts," Hortense, and Artie used the bathrooms at the back.
Until this last year, the servants had used them, too. That was how
Dora had got into the habit of carrying her towels to her bedroom.
Artie was still a possible hazard. When it came to a question of neat-
ness, Dora trusted no one.
She took her bath standing in the boxed-in tin tub; she squeezed
cold water down her spine from a huge sponge and soaped and
rinsed from a running faucet.
Drying herself with a big Turkish towel, and then patting her skin
with linen, she got into her crisp white underwear, which was fin-
ished simply with a row of hemstitching. Her long stockings fas-
tened to a "garter belt"; Dora had never worn a corset. Her morning
slippers were plain, with low heels; her dress was of pale gray per-
cale, with a collar and cuffs of white embroidery. Since her hair
began to turn gray Dora had dressed with increasing demureness.
But she had always liked simplicity, and felt that it became one of
44
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
her small stature. She was only four feet eleven in her morning slip-
pers; when Hortense was a little girl, she had often played that Dora
was her doll.
No other member of the family was stirring when Dora stole
downstairs. In the parlor, which was never used for family evenings,
a sleepy and somewhat sulky Ida was picking up lemonade glasses.
"Have you let the dogs out, Ida?" asked Dora.
"Yes'm, Miss Dora. Louise let 'em out a while ago."
Dora eyed a sticky ring on a small highly polished table. "It makes
extra work for you, having young people in the house nearly every
evening. But Miss Bess's callers use mostly this room and the
veranda."
"I don't mind the extra work, M'm. But Miss 'Tense's callers was
always more careful about the way they left things."
Hortense and Bess had entertained together last night, as a matter
of fact. Mrs. Langworthy had received their young men and chatted
with them for a few minutes; then, with a sigh of thankfulness for
duty done, she had joined the family in the sitting-room. But con-
science compelled her to stay downstairs as long as there were young
men in the house; Dora had left her, at eleven, stifling yawns over
her embroidery.
Dora went out the front door and whistled to the dogs. They came
running from the direction of the stable, straight across the vegetable-
garden, and around to the front of the house. The little spaniel was
last, as usual. She stooped to scratch its head.
A big tom-cat came from the sunny end of the veranda and rubbed
against her legs. Dora patted the cat, too. Then she went back to the
library for a small braided whip. At sight of it the dogs began to
bark, and the little spaniel frisked. The whip meant that they were
going to be taken for a walk.
It was a gorgeous morning. Dora stepped briskly down the drive
and turned to her right, toward the open country. The macadam
street gave place here to a dusty road; after she had crossed the
bridge she walked in the grass, up the bank of the brook.
It was a tiny brook, and often went dry late in August. But in the
morning light it was very pretty. It flowed over moss-covered stones
to a bed of watercress; and presently Dora reached the trunk of an
old willow which overhung the stream.
DORA
45
This willow was the identical one, Hortense had supposed as a
child, from which Ophelia flung her garlands. Dora crawled out on
it now and seated herself, swinging her feet.
Behind her the country rose to a hill crowned by a solitary elm.
Dora liked the location of the Langworthy house because it was so
close to the open country. She loved that hill-the only one for miles,
unless you counted the little knoll on which the house itself was built.
Often she walked straight up the slope to the elm, and then into the
fields beyond.
To-day she felt more like sitting here and listening to the murmur
of the brook. She was still a little under the influence of her dream.
She had been born with a lust for travel. On the rare occasions
when dreams broke her rest, she usually thought herself in some
strange foreign city, as she had been this morning, or among craggy
mountains, or fronting limitless fields of ice with storm-clouds
banked on the horizon.
A great city at twilight! There had been an illumination in the
public square, and people dancing on the pavement to the sound of
wild peasant music. If only she could have managed to turn the
corner, joy was waiting for her beyond.
"I'd like to go to Alaska sometime," she thought. "And to Aus-
tralia. That's a strange country, clear away at the edge of things.
Yet it's a white man's country. Africa is a black man's country, full
of ivory and elephants and jungle. And China: there'd be cemeteries
for ancestor worship, and they'd pull me around in a jinrikisha.
And Japan! It would be like stepping into the pattern on a porce-
lain plate, if I could just go to Japan."
Twice only she had managed to satisfy that itch for travel. "Papa"
had given her a trip to Europe when some family friends were going.
She hadn't especially enjoyed trailing about with Mr. Arbuthnot and
his wife and sister-in-law; there had been too many shawls and foot-
warmers and cups of tea on that journey, and Mr. Arbuthnot was
the worst old woman of the three. But at least Dora had seen the
Rhine and Westminster Abbey and the Champs Élysées.
When Papa died, too, she had taken her money from his "estate"
and stolen a few weeks in Europe. What she should have done was
stay at home and turn the old Mills place into a really good boarding-
house, and thus secure a future and a living for herself.
46
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
But Retta wouldn't have liked that; and of course there was the
question of Hortense. When Margaret died she had left her baby to
Dora. But the sisters had all taken a share in bringing up Hortense.
At first they had worried for fear the child's good-for-nothing father
might come back and make them trouble about the little girl. But
they needn't have worried; Alec Gibson had been only too glad to
stand from under.
"I'm so happy," Papa had said, "to feel that you and Amy are
satisfied to stay on at home with me, Dode. You've seen in Mar-
garet's case what a husband can be like. Isn't it better to be safe and
contented, even if people do call you 'an old maid'?"
Dora had called herself an old maid oftener than anybody else had
ever applied the term to her. Just the same she felt a little stab at the
calm way Papa had ignored what he knew so well: what they all
knew, and refrained from speaking of because they hesitated to
remind her.
Dora herself had been in love, that faraway summer when all the
Mills girls were in the flush of the mating season. All but Amy,
that is; Amy had been a serene untouchable even then.
But Margaret had taken the wretched Alec: walked through the
woods and picked up a crooked stick, she had. Retta had married a
Northerner, and had done well enough at that; for a brother-in-law,
Sam Langworthy was fairly satisfactory. Dora had fallen in love with
a dark-haired boy who could have laughed his way into a stouter
heart than hers. Three weeks after she became engaged to him, he
died of typhoid fever.
Loving once, she had loved always. But she had to show everybody
-her family, her too sympathetic friends, herself, everybody-that
life couldn't do such things to her. Couldn't do them and get by with
the doing! Dora had been thumbing her nose at life ever since.
She screwed up her face now. To-day's sunlight would mean to-
morrow's crop of freckles. At fifty you weren't supposed to freckle;
but perhaps her skin was more sensitive because of the care she gave
it. She couldn't imagine letting herself go the way Retta had. Retta
had been the most conspicuously good looking of the family when
they were young. Now she was purely a slave to comfort: break-
fasted in bed, and let her household saunter along at its own sweet
DORA
47
will, though she was inclined to resent aid which seemed to trespass
on her authority.
A farm-wagon rattled along the road. Dora was out of the dust,
but the noise disturbed her. She really should have gone farther afield
if she needed quiet. But exactly why did she need so much quiet?
She had never been a person to sit down and develop "nerves."
She crawled back to land and whistled to the dogs. She led them
along the road and into the yard. But she didn't feel like going into
the house just yet. She turned aside to the patch of sweet peas and
began to gather the pink ones only, running her thumb down each
stalk to the end and snipping it off clean with her thumb-nail.
This side yard was a mess. A bed of phlox here, sweet william
there, Canterbury bells between the asters and the mourning bride:
everything flourished in its own right, but it was all set out hap-
hazard, too far apart for unity, too close together for separate beds.
The one attempt at proper grouping had ended in disaster: the lilies-
of-the-valley had originally been planted inside a border of perennial
forget-me-nots. The lily-of-the-valley, for all its fragile look, is a
hardy flower; it had crowded the forget-me-nots out of existence.
For three successive summers the Colonel had planned to plant
more forget-me-nots. He had a scheme, too, for regular paths, and
for filling in between them. With a little systematic planting, this
whole haphazard growth could be connected up into a unified and
very lovely flower-garden.
"But he doesn't go ahead and do it!" thought Dora. "Why doesn't
he do it? For that matter, why don't I? Retta would interfere and
try to boss the job; but I'm not scared of Retta's bossing. I might
much better plan this garden than waste my time the way I do waste
it. Why don't I ever do anything that I might just as well do? For
that matter, we don't any of us do the things we might just as well
do. I wonder why not?"
The sweet peas wouldn't last much longer if the hot weather kept
up. But they were lovely to-day, like little pink butterflies that had
lighted on the vines. They smelled sweeter than roses, too. The roses
were gone now, all the kinds that Dora liked best: the round tidy
"thousand leaf," the loose-petaled white with its heart of gold, the
new "La France" that lots of people claimed couldn't be grown
except in a green-house. Retta was lucky with her flowers. It was
48
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
too bad she had ever got the idea of a trellis for the roses. The
climbers were all new; it would take two or three years to cover that
trellis so that it wouldn't stick out like a sore thumb.
The old tom-cat, picking his way daintily among the flower-beds,
found Dora and rubbed against her. The dogs had galloped off
somewhere in search of fresh amusement. She'd better get her flowers
in water before they wilted. Her head was beginning to ache a little.
Perhaps she shouldn't have stayed out so long in the sun without her
breakfast.

II
T the back door she stumbled over Amy, who was setting out
two pans of table scraps for the dogs. Bob the collie and Czar
the poodle had their heads in the larger pan before it was deposited
on the ground. Then Czar decided to sample from the small pan,
which was designed for the spaniel. Amy pushed him away.
"Good morning, early bird," she said. "Been stealing yourself a
nosegay, haven't you? How many times has Mrs. Langworthy told
you never to approach her sweet peas unless you are armed with
scissors?"
"If Mrs. Langworthy can find a single uprooted vine where I've
been, Mrs. Langworthy is welcome to say the worst she likes about
me." Dora picked up the cat. "Any meat left for Fuzzy? He's
hungry, too."
"Set him inside the door," said Amy. "I'll see to him in a minute."
"It would be so sweet if you could teach him to eat with Beppo,"
mocked Dora. "Lion and lamb style!"
"Yes, wouldn't it? Poor Bep has a hard enough time as it is, with
the big dogs always crowding him." Amy lifted with a light hand
the long hair of the spaniel's ears.
"Bep eats too much. He's getting fat. Want to put my flowers in
water for me, honey? I never can make them look the way you do."
"Leave them on the kitchen table. I'll see to them right away."
Amy remained at her post to be sure that the dogs' breakfast was
eaten properly. Dora slipped past her into the kitchen.
At a table spread between the two windows Jason and Louise were
sharing their connubial breakfast with Ida. Louise, a scrawny woman
of sixty with her scanty hair screwed into a hard knot on top of her
head, scowled into her oatmeal. Ida, who was in her forties and
smooth and plump, simpered at Jason. Jason had just said something

50
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
half under his breath when Dora entered; he was caught in the
middle of a guffaw.
"Drama belowstairs," thought Dora.
With a polite greeting, she walked past the tableau and laid her
flowers on a second table. There two canaries, under the wire super-
structure of their cage, were splashing in their porcelain bath. Dora
walked on through, and deposited Fuzzy out of mischief's way in
the library.
She lingered to pick up a book on Yellowstone Park which Hor-
tense had given her for her birthday. Dora would like to see the real
West before she adventured again to Europe. She had about as much
chance of one as of the other. Meanwhile, however, she could read;
and now that they printed from photographs on this new shiny
paper, the pictures were almost as good as a visit in person.
In the dining-room the table was set for a family breakfast; but no
known power had ever been able to make that household keep to a
breakfast-hour. The Colonel, with a heap of unopened letters before
him, was reading the morning paper and breakfasting in solitary
state.
Dora slipped behind the coffee-tray, which was duly set forth at
Retta's end of the table though Retta always breakfasted in her room.
"A house full of women, Colonel!" she said. "And you had to pour
your own coffee!"
"I'm quite capable of pouring a cup of coffee. But I do like com-
pany." The Colonel laid down his paper and began to go through
the envelopes. "I think there's a letter here for you, Dode."
Dora slipped into her own chair and reached across Bess's vacant
place for the letter. She glanced at it, made a face, and thrust it
unopened between the pages of her book.
"A bill?" asked the Colonel.
"How did you guess?" mocked Dora.
He held out his hand. "Let me have it."
"No, thank you, Colonel. I'll face the bad news later, when I've
had my coffee."
"Let me have it, and there'll be no bad news to face."
The bill, as Dora knew without looking, was for three pairs of
new shoes that Hortense had bought in the spring. Hortense was
extravagant in her tastes; when she had exhausted the tiny income
DORA
51
from her share of her grandfather's estate, Dora was in the habit of
saying, "Oh, just charge it to me!" Dora's own share of the Mills
money had been cut in two by that long-ago journey; but she could
do without things better than Hortense.
The worst of it was that they none of them did without things.
The Colonel ran the household, and the women had their little
incomes for spending money. When they charged on his account-
so much more convenient!-they were supposed to settle with him
at the end of the month. Sometimes he took their money; oftener
he waved it aside. But that was different from their coolly planting
their bills on him.
"No, no, and again no!" said Dora. "Save your breath to cool your
porridge, Colonel. I wonder whether I'm going to get any porridge,
by the way. Ida saw me come through the kitchen, but the fact
doesn't seem to have impressed itself on her."
"Better ring," suggested the Colonel.
But Dora had already opened her book. She was a light eater at
the best of times; she already had her coffee, and the pain in her
head was a little better.
Suppressing a grunt-he was a heavy man, and didn't move with-
out feeling it-the Colonel shoved back his chair and edged around
to Retta's end of the table. At the sound of the bell Dora looked up.
"You shouldn't have done that. Gals who are too lazy to ring a bell
should be made to do without."
"I suppose you think ladies aren't waited on except in Kentucky."
The Colonel turned to Ida, appearing in the pantry door, and or-
dered, "Miss Dora's oatmeal and my eggs, please. And tell Jason I'm
taking the street-car this morning, but Mrs. Langworthy wants the
landau at half-past ten, and he is to drive her."
"Retta's awake, then?" said Dora as soon as the pantry door closed.
"Retta is half awake, and grumbling," said the Colonel with a
chuckle. "She was kept up late last night. I don't know why she
didn't send for me. I'd have shaken hands with the young men, and
wished them a pleasant walk home."
"Retta lets them stay until they go of their own accord," said
Dora. "I can't figure why, exactly. Papa used to shoo our young men
out at ten precisely."
"Don't I remember that? He used to shoo me first of the batch.
52
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
But then he had a flock of daughters. I suppose Retta feels that an
only chick is entitled to special privileges."
"Whether or not she's entitled to them, she's getting them," said
Dora rather grimly. "Retta was always much stricter with Hortense."
"You don't think Retta favors Bess over Hortense?" This was an
unpalatable notion to the Colonel-so unpalatable that it just wouldn't
go down. "The girls are quite different. Isn't that it? Hortense is so
brainy, she scares the boys away."
"Do men really admire stupidity in a woman?" asked Dora.
"Boys do, sometimes. Old codgers like me know better, of course.
Why, look here, Dode! Look at the women under this roof right
now. Do you suppose Art and I rolled together could hold a candle
to any one of you?"
Dora wrinkled her nose at him. "Artie and you rolled together
would make a funny compound. I wouldn't recommend you to have
it done."
The Colonel wasn't to be sidetracked from his anxiety. "It seems
to me Hortense is having a lot better time now that Bess is home.
Don't you think so? She sits in on all the giggling in the evenings,
and she and Bess are on the tennis-court with two boys nearly every
afternoon when I get home."
His facts were quite correct. For the last month Hortense had
helped to make four at tennis, had entertained the overflow of Bess's
callers, had occupied the back seat of the trap with the less desirable
of two young men. If Bess and Hortense entertained together, Bess
wasn't required to drag her mother or an aunt about with her.
Freedom from chaperonage meant more fun: Dora sympathized
with that.
But more than once lately it had struck Dora that Bess was not at
all averse to showing up her own freshness against the background
of an older girl. Hortense was only twenty-eight, and would still be
handsome at fifty, with her proud aquiline profile and abundant hair
and distinguished carriage. But like all blondes, she looked ghastly
when she was tired; and at the best of times, the carnation red of
Bess's lips and cheeks, and the clear white of her forehead and neck
against her black hair, tended to make any less vivid girl look a

trifle faded.
All this, however, Dora didn't even dare hint to the Colonel. His
DORA
53
treatment of such a situation would have been to call Bess on the
carpet and order her to be more considerate of her cousin.
Right now he was waiting anxiously for her to get on with the
subject. Dora preferred to dismiss it. "Well, summer will soon be
over. In September Bess will be away again at Sheldon School, and
the rest of us can sit back once more and take things easy."
"Humph!" said the Colonel. "Dode, if I tell you something, can
you keep it to yourself?"
Dora was startled. The Colonel didn't ordinarily confide in her.
On the other hand Dora never told Mrs. Langworthy anything that
she wouldn't just as soon have get to the Colonel; and she had
supposed it worked both ways. Often of a winter evening, indeed,
if she and Amy took their books into the parlor for the sake of quiet,
she would hear his voice going on and on to Retta, and Retta reply-
ing always at length and sometimes with heat. Dora had often been
driven to wonder how people who had been married for nearly
thirty years could possibly find so much to talk about.
But apparently there were things he didn't tell Retta, at that. And
his need of some one to confide in now must be pretty real. His
eggs were hardening on his plate, and his usual hour of departure
for the store was long past.
"Of course I'll keep it to myself," she said. "What's the trouble,
Sam?"
Her calling him by his first name was a touch he appreciated.
Ordinarily no one but Retta actually called him by name. "Colonel,"
said the family, just as the town said "Colonel." Sometimes Artie
shortened, it to "Cull," but only when he was in his cups.
"The fact is," he blurted out, "Bess isn't going back to Sheldon
next year."
"But she thinks she is! She was saying only yesterday-"
"I know, I haven't told her either. But the truth is that the day
she got home I had a letter from the Principal asking me not to send
her back."
"Some silly scrape!" said Dora. "I'm surprised you didn't have it
out with her at once. I thought you believed in having things out
with people."
"It wasn't a scrape exactly. In fact, it doesn't seem to have been a
scrape at all. Bess had very poor marks all the year-"
54
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"That's the Mills influence," interrupted Dora. "We all of us like
to read, but we're none of us students. The day school reports came
home was always a day of storm in our household. I can still hear
Papa thundering."
"The poor marks alone I wouldn't have minded. And neither
would they, I think."
"I'm sure they wouldn't. Those fashionable schools don't, so long
as the tuition is paid." Dora stopped suddenly and bit her tongue.
It was the custom in that household to regard the Colonel as an
inexhaustible source of supply. But Sheldon was expensive, and
school bills couldn't be allowed to run. Was this?
No, she hadn't touched the sore point. The Colonel went on,
"What they didn't like was what they called her 'attitude.' It seems
she was naturally something of a leader in the school."
"Yes, I can easily see that she would be."
"And she used her influence with the other girls, especially the
younger ones-well, against authority."
"Is that all?" said Dora. "I thought you were really going to tell
me something, Sam."
The Colonel looked immensely relieved. "It doesn't sound so bad,
does it, now that it's out? But I've put off and put off breaking the
news to Retta. You know how she fumes."
"Retta will go East and dynamite Sheldon School when she gets
the glad tidings. Or if she gets them. Would it be possible for you
to take your own measures without letting Retta know the worst?
Have you thought of that?"
"But I'll have to tell Bess some time, and Bess will run straight to
her mother. You can't blame her for that, Dode."
No, Dora couldn't fairly blame Bess, for that or for anything else.
She blamed Retta; Retta and the Colonel between them had made
Bess a thoroughly spoiled, selfish brat. ""Brat' is the word," thought
Dora. But she wouldn't say it aloud, of course. She loved to provoke
a storm by her use of bad language; but she had no business to
pronounce judgment against other people's children.
"There are other schools," the Colonel went on. "Plenty of other
schools."
"Oh, plenty!" agreed Dora. "Your idea is to send Bess to another
school, and manufacture some excuse for the change?"
DORA
55
"What else can I do? Retta doesn't want Bess to make her début
until she's twenty."
"If Bess tears around any harder after she makes her début than
she is tearing this summer, Heaven help us all!" said Dora piously.
"However, the début is a fine old Mills custom. We all made ours;
and Retta put Hortense through the mill in her turn. She can't very
well do less for Bess."
"Until Bess makes her début, isn't she supposed to stay in school?
I honestly don't know, Dode. I never had any sisters, and my
mother never talked to me about such things. If she had, I don't
suppose I'd have listened."
Dora measured him with her eye, and calculated her chances. If
she took Bess and traveled all next winter, that would not only put
in the time, it would also give the girl prestige. A year in Europe
would mean a lot in Mount Royal.
But that was one word for the niece and two for the aunt. If Dora
made the suggestion, the Colonel would feel in duty bound to put
up the money. It was just too much to ask. (But oh, how Dora
would have loved a year of crammed interest and ever-changing
horizons!)
She controlled her grasping impulse and said slowly, "I don't
believe another school would do any more for Bess than Sheldon has.
In fact, I'm sure another year away from home would be just that
much more time wasted. Bess will get married one of these days;
and the sooner she sets about it the better, if you ask me."
The Colonel was horrified. "Don't you start putting any such ideas
into Bess's head. She won't get married for years and years yet."
"You don't have to put that idea into a girl's head, Colonel. It
grows there of itself. Anyhow, what else would you expect her to do
with her life?"
"I suppose she will get married some time. But she's such a child!
Why can't she be happy for a while, just here at home with us?"
"It isn't a question of being happy right now. It's a question of
how she is going to spend her life."
"But that's a long look ahead, Dode."
"Not too long," insisted Dora. "She's being brought up to get
married, isn't she? Of course I think girls should be brought up as
boys are: trained to go to work or into business."
56
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"But there's no need for Bess to go to work. Why, if she should
even think of such a thing, I'd feel as if I hadn't done my duty by
her."
"She won't think of any such thing," Dora assured him. "The
present system isn't so bad, either, if the girl does get married. But
if she doesn't-"
"Good God, Dode, are you thinking of yourself? Aren't you happy
here?"
Dora winced. She had been thinking of herself. But she denied the
idea indignantly. "When you get to my age, Colonel, you realize how
much worse off you might have been. It's the years in between that
cut and wound. I'm not very happy about Hortense. It's seven years
since she made her début; and when she has a cousin who is 'out,'
that's going to put her right in the class with Amy and me."
"Hortense should be tickled silly to be in any class with you and
Amy. Has Hortense been complaining to you?"
"She'd be too proud to make that complaint. Anyhow, I'm not
just sure that Hortense realizes she is being stared in the face by the
prospect of being 'an old maid. "
"You're always calling yourself 'an old maid.' But you're a good
deal of a humbug. And as for Amy-" The Colonel began to grin.
"Why, it wasn't more than a year ago that Doc Simpson wanted to
marry Amy. She nearly took his head off when he suggested it. But
any girl who still gets proposals isn't an old maid, even if one of the
proposals happens to come from a fat old widower who is looking
for his third."
"Dr. Simpson was worse than nothing. I don't suppose I'd ever
convince you how galling it is when an amiable old fool like that
puts a woman into his own class. Why he should ever have sup-
posed that his attentions would be welcome to Amy-!"
"Well, they weren't." The Colonel sobered suddenly. "I'd have
hated to see Amy marry him. But I still think she was complimented
by being asked."
"Men always think a woman should feel complimented, even when
she is practically insulted. I don't suppose," said Dora slowly, "that
there is a man alive who doesn't believe at the bottom of his heart
that he could get any woman he wanted, if he just went about it the
right way."
DORA
57
The Colonel chuckled. "Still, we have our good points. Aren't you
going to ring for more breakfast for yourself, Dode? You don't eat
enough to keep a canary alive."
Amy must be waiting on the canaries this minute. Amy always
had to be reminded, or she wouldn't have breakfasted at all. She
was one worse than Dora, who ate sparingly but regularly. Dora's
headache was gone now, after a cup of coffee and two spoonfuls of
oatmeal.
"You haven't eaten your own breakfast," said Dora. "Let me ring
for some more eggs for you. Those on your plate must be stone cold."
"I must get along now. I'll make up to myself at dinner." The
Colonel shoved back his chair. "See you then, Dode."
Dora watched him out of the door. Should she have offered to
speak to Bess for him? It was all right to expect a man to provide
for a lot of women; he expected it himself, and it made him feel like
a little tin god to realize that he looked out for all these pretty
creatures. But perhaps somehow among them the pretty creatures
might arrange that the provider should get what he paid for.
She shoved back her chair, with an impulse to follow him and at
least make the offer. But she heard him speak to somebody in the
hall; and a moment later Hortense came through the parlor. She was
immaculate in chambray of a robin's egg blue which set off her hair
and eyes to perfection. The Mills ladies might be tardy in making
their morning appearances; but they always took as much pains then
as later in the day to appear to advantage.
At the sight of Hortense Dora decided to let the Colonel fend for
himself. She rang for Hortense's oatmeal, and poured her coffee and
sweetened it. "I didn't hear you come upstairs last night, honey," she
said. "Did your callers stay late?"
"They did. Quite late."
"Did you have a good time?"
"Yes and no. Oh, yes, I suppose I did! It seemed like a good time
last night. But all those hours of silly conversation!" Hortense
shuddered.
"Who was here?"
"Doug Rutherford and Roger Willett. The same two who were
here Monday night."
"Are they coming for tennis this afternoon?"
58
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"No, thank Heaven they can't. Doug works for his father, and
that means he often can't quit even at five. Bess tried to get Roger
to promise he'd bring some other fellow. But I don't believe they
settled anything."
"How about those two boys who played here yesterday?"
"Bess didn't ask them for to-day. I think she was planning ahead
to Roger."
"Then you actually have the afternoon free?" Dora's eyes spar-
kled. "I'll tell you what let's do. Your Aunt Retta has ordered the
landau for half-past ten. Let's put up a picnic lunch, and you and
Aunt Retta and Aunt Amy and I will drive out to the Soldiers'
Home. It ought to be cool there under the trees, and this afternoon
there's a band concert."
"I'd love that, if it were possible." Hortense made a long arm for
the Colonel's newspaper, the Chicago Record, without which he
could scarcely have begun the day. "Even the sight of an old soldier
would be a treat after so much of Bess's beaux. But 'The Bee' is
meeting here this afternoon. Had you forgotten?"
Dora had forgotten. So apparently had Retta and Amy. "The Bee"
was Retta's little sewing-circle, which met every week at the different
houses in turn. Mrs. Cordiner, Mrs. Palmer, Mrs. Chase and Mrs.
Travers made up the roster: all intimate friends. But even intimate
friends know whether the dusting has been done; and furthermore,
since "The Bee" always partook of a bountiful tea in the dining-
room at five o'clock, the entire domestic arrangements had to be
altered to conform.
Dora rushed out to the kitchen. Amy was just putting the canary
cage together. She adjusted it with deft touches of her fine long
hands. Amy's hands were as delicate as porcelain; and whatever task
she undertook she handled only with her finger-tips.
"Amy, did you tell Louise-?" began Dora.
But Amy was leaning over the cage and murmuring to the cana-
ries. When they chirped back at her, she smiled. Amy very seldom
smiled at people. For all their long association, there were times when
Dora felt that she didn't know her youngest sister very well. Amy's
remoteness was one thing which convinced Dora that if one of these
days she did somehow manage to get the bit between her teeth and
set out for foreign parts, she wouldn't be seriously missed.
THE
III
HE Langworthys, while they were not precisely in the Prospect
Avenue set, entertained a good deal and were entertained in
turn. And as the various members of that mixed household all had
their own special friends, a tide of company commonly flowed
through the house even when Bess was not at home to attract the
younger people.
It was a very different life from that in the double houses which
Bess disdained, or in the neat blocks of frame "homes" south of the
Episcopal Church or east of the river. In these unfashionable neigh-
borhoods people went to church on Sunday, and had a heavy mid-
day dinner, and walked it off. Once in six months or more they
invited the minister and his wife in for supper. Thanksgiving and
Christmas they entertained their relatives. Summer evenings they
went for a ride on the street-car, "to cool off." The races-Mount
Royal was on a racing circuit-and the circus waked the whole town
up, but only temporarily. The theater, however, was fairly well
patronized and drew good attractions, which played it as a "one-
night stand" between Chicago and Peoria.
The Colonel liked diversions which could be made to include the
entire family. He always bought a box for the annual concert which
was heralded weeks in advance on the billboards: COMING SOUSA AND
HIS BAND. When he had a good year, he took the family into Chicago
to see Mansfield or Sir Henry Irving; it was an excursion which
involved staying the night at a Chicago hotel, and a morning's shop-
ping afterward for the women.
It was noticeable that the Colonel always organized these diver-
sions. In the course of a year Mrs. Langworthy generally accumulated
a good deal of unreturned hospitality; in fact, unless the Colonel
kept an eye on her, she accumulated a good many unanswered invi-
59
60
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
tations. In her more jovial moods she always explained that she
could "have a better time right here at home than running all
around town eating other people's angel cake." When she felt sorry
for herself-as near as Dora could make out, all married women had
those spells of feeling sorry for themselves-Mrs. Langworthy was
wont to declare plaintively that she had "given up everything but
The Bee."
"The Bee" was always spoken of that way, in capital letters. It had
evolved itself years ago when several young married women, all new-
comers to Mount Royal, had fallen into the habit of inviting one
another to "spend the afternoon and bring your sewing."
Now it was a recognized institution; it met every week with fair
regularity, though the day of the week was shifted to fit in with the
members' other engagements. Mrs. Cordiner stayed away during the
Christmas rush at the store. Mrs. Palmer attended somewhat irreg-
ularly; she was a member of the Prospect Avenue set and just a
little condescending to her old friends, though she had never exactly
dropped them.
Mrs. Ellicott Chase always came, and Mrs. Travers. Amy and Dora
had been adopted into The Bee when they came here to live. Hor-
tense attended the sessions at the Langworthys', but never considered
herself a member of the charmed circle.
The Langworthy table was run on a liberal scheme. There were
rows of Mason jars in the basement store-room, and at the proper
season barrels of apples and bins of vegetables. A cookie-jar, open to
all comers, stood in the pantry, and the cake-box and bread-box were
never quite emptied between successive bakings.
Nevertheless, when Louise received the news that The Bee was
"coming here this afternoon," she attacked the preparations as if the
house had been in a state of famine, and sent Jason downtown with
a marketing-list that covered both sides of the paper. Amy counted
out embroidered doilies and silver. Hortense took out and dusted
one of the many sets of dishes, and moved up others so that the
china-closets should show no gap. Mrs. Langworthy established her-
self in the kitchen to arrange the flowers which Dora was told off to
cut. Ida, the second girl, was sent upstairs to give Hortense's room a
special dusting, so that the ladies could deposit their hats and parasols
there.
DORA
61
Dinner was always a punctual meal, and more hurried than either
breakfast or supper. To-day it was cleared away with unusual speed.
Hortense came into Dora's room to dress. Down the corridor Mrs.
Langworthy called for some one to come and hook her.
At a quarter before three Mrs. Langworthy established herself in
the largest chair on the veranda, disposing her ample flowered skirts
carefully, and making sure that her work-bag, her handkerchief, and
a large palm-leaf fan were all within easy reach. Her niece and sis-
ters joined her just as Mrs. Travers turned in from the street.
Mrs. Travers lived in one of the double houses which spread their
unfashionable atmosphere just north of Prospect Avenue. The
Traverses had not changed their scale of living when they acquired
money. They owned a "rig," a single buggy; but the man of the
family used it to get back and forth to his factory. Mrs. Travers
always claimed that she "liked the walk." Nevertheless, she took
pains to arrive before any of the guests who had themselves driven.
She was a pleasant wholesome woman in her middle forties. Dora
liked her the best of the crew.
Mrs. Palmer presently arrived in her victoria, and ordered the
coachman to return for her at six. Then came Mrs. Cordiner, in a
lace gown and a flower-covered Leghorn hat which was roguish
rather than matronly. Mrs. Cordiner loved pretty clothes; consciously,
too, she played before women friends the rôle of "old man's dar-
ling."
Mrs. Chase brought her daughter Agnes with her. Agnes was the
middle one of the Chase girls, "the quiet one." Mrs. Chase had early
despaired of marrying her off as easily as she knew she could marry
off Marie and Geraldine. She was now grooming Agnes for the
part of mother's companion.
Hortense took the guests upstairs when they arrived. On their
return to the veranda Dora found chairs for them.
"It's such a hot day," explained Mrs. Langworthy, "I thought we'd
sit out here-unless, of course, you'd rather sit in the house."
The house was cooler than the veranda, which had a southern
exposure. Nevertheless, the guests all protested that they preferred
to sit outdoors. Ida immediately appeared with a trayful of tall
glasses of iced tea decorated with sprigs of mint. The wicker furni-
62
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
ture creaked a little when the ladies stirred; and Mrs. Palmer, who
had been accommodated with the only rocking-chair, managed to
get it over a loose board in the veranda floor.
Small noises, however, were speedily drowned in the flood of
speech which the iced tea unloosed. Three or four women talked at
once, without waiting for an opening and apparently without listen-
ing to one another. Just the same, each of them managed to hear
most of what went on; and it was an unsuccessful Bee that didn't
bring forth several items which would later prove of interest at the
members' scattered supper-tables.
Dora produced from her work-bag a large centerpiece with an
elaborate design of bunches of grapes. She never worked on it be-
tween Bees; it had served her as "sewing" for several years, and with
care would serve some time longer. She waited for an opening
before she put in her remarks. Sometimes, indeed, an opening was
made for her; Dora had a wicked gift for touching a situation off,
if she thought the situation deserved it.
Amy sat a little withdrawn from the group. She had adopted an
unfinished "piece" of Mrs. Langworthy's: a square linen table-cover
which was to be adorned with a deep border of cross-stitch in various
shades of blue. Against the embroidery Amy's hands were lovely; a
little lace frill drooped over each, and didn't seem to get in her way
as it would have in Dora's.
"Isn't Bess coming down this afternoon?" Dora asked under cover
of the chatter.
"I don't know. She went out the back way about two o'clock. But
she didn't say she wasn't coming to The Bee." Amy cast a worried
look in the direction of the grape-arbor, which stood on the shore of
the half-filled pond. A masculine laugh had just caught her ear above
the hubbub behind her.
Dora looked surprised. "Is that Artie out in the arbor?"
"Yes. He generally goes downtown when The Bee meets here.
I'm sure I don't know why he stayed to-day."
Dora knew why Artie had remained on the premises-or more
likely had come back here and brought a boon companion with him.
Right after dinner he had wanted to "borrow" a dollar or two from
her. She wished now she had let him have it instead of keeping her
money to pay her bills. Artie hadn't any credit downtown; and if
DORA
63
he had provided the shelter of the arbor and the mint for juleps, he
wasn't likely to desist until his friend's flask was empty.
"I'll shoo Artie in the back way after the ladies go into the dining-
room," she whispered. "I'm sure after Eddie Palmer poor Artie is
mild enough. But when he has been drinking he's so dignified it's
a little embarrassing."
Amy gave her a smile of gratitude. Dora, who had now showed
her good will by covering about half a grape, laid aside her embroi-
dery to pick up Fuzzy. She sat stroking him and listened to the argu-
ment which had just developed out of the babble of tongues.
"Yes, I was there all right," Mrs. Langworthy was saying. "The
Colonel always takes the whole family to the races. I remember
seeing the tally-ho, too. Charley Manning always drives his tally-ho
to the races on the big day. He loves doing things that make people
look at him."
"I'm sure I don't know how he manages to fill the top of the
tally-ho year after year," said Mrs. Palmer. "Of course, the men can't
snub him. But it's worth any woman's reputation to be seen with
him in public. That is, of course, if she has any reputation to lose."
"The men don't want to snub him," said Hortense. "He always
carries two or three cases of champagne with him, and the ice right
in the cases. In between races they all sneak around to have a drink
with him."
"Do the boys you know do that?" demanded Mrs. Cordiner.
They did it, and they bragged about it. But Mrs. Cordiner must
be able to enumerate pretty accurately the "boys" whom Hortense
knew, and Hortense wasn't giving them away. "I don't blame any-
body for wanting a cool drink on a hot day," she said sweetly.
"I'm going to have Ida bring out some more iced tea."
Her departure left the older women alone except for Agnes Chase,
whom it was always easy to forget. Heads drew together, and Mrs.
Cordiner went on with her story.
"I had supposed the girls were from the chorus of a Chicago
show. That was what I heard at the time. But in the store this
morning some one told me they all came from a 'house' down near
Creston. It seems a madame from Chicago has set up there on a big
scale, and Charley Manning took all her girls with him to the
races."
64
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Charley Manning has done more for this town than anybody else
ever did, Dora thought to herself. I don't mean just the money he
has given away and small thanks he ever got for that. I mean the
way he has always given the town something to talk about. A big
establishment down near Creston! It must depend for its patronage
on the cows and the chickens. The youths of Mount Royal went into
Chicago when they wanted a real bender.
"I can't quite believe that story," said Mrs. Travers. "We used to
hear things about the stag parties he gave out at his country-place."
"Stag parties!" said Mrs. Palmer scornfully.
"People said they weren't," Mrs. Travers went on. "But Mr.
Travers went to one of them last year. He felt he had to go for
business reasons. I hated to have him. But he reported that it was a
very nice party. Lots of quail and frogs' legs and other things Mr.
Travers doesn't care about eating; and wine with every course, as
well as whiskey and brandy right on the table all the time. But there
wasn't a woman in sight-let alone a tally-ho full of them."
Mrs. Palmer pursed her lips primly. "Well, I imagine the con-
versation at that table wouldn't bear repeating."
Why should it? thought Dora. You like to get away with just
women around, and then let your tongue wag and wag. Why
shouldn't a man like to talk to other men with no women around?
They probably talk about women a plenty. But I suppose that
wouldn't suit you either, Mrs. Palmer.
"Bess heard us all at the dinner-table," worried Amy to Dora.
"She must have known The Bee would meet here this afternoon."
"Oh, she'll be along when she gets around to it! I can't blame her
if she doesn't let herself in for the whole session."
Dora tucked her cat under her arm and moved around to where
Agnes Chase sat at her mother's elbow, her eyes on the narrow lace
and insertion which she was "whipping" together for the adornment
of underwear. "Let your work go for a few minutes, Agnes, and
come out in the garden with me," she suggested.
Agnes looked at her mother. Mrs. Chase smiled and said, "Yes,
run along, dear." With a sigh of relief, Agnes laid down her work
and followed Dora.
The house shaded much of the garden at this hour; and on the
island in the drive the two tall trees sheltered the old swing. "Get
DORA
65
in, and I'll swing you for a minute," said Dora. "The ropes are re-
newed every year, just as they always were when the girls were
little."
Agnes seated herself obediently. "I used to be afraid of swinging,"
she confided in her shy little voice. "But you taught me not to be,
right here in this very swing. You don't remember, Miss Dora. But
I remember."
Dora gave the swing a gentle shove. "Of course I remember.
Marie and Geraldine used to pump up so vigorously that they'd
scare anybody. Bess and Hortense did the same thing. When I was a
little girl, we weren't supposed to exercise so rudely. Little girls have
more freedom now than they did in my time."
"Being a little girl was fun, Miss Dora. Marie and Geraldine were
always wild to grow up and do grown-up things. But I wanted to
stay a little girl."
"Did you? I'd just as soon have stayed a little boy. But I never had
that much luck."
At the moment Dora looked like a little boy: a naughty little boy
who was playing hooky. Playing hooky: that was her ideal in life.
Unfortunately, when you grew up you learned to take your school
with you. Unless, of course, you were like Artie: Dora wished she
had lent Artie five dollars instead of the two he asked for.
"You used to give us stove parties up in Bess's room. Do you
remember?" went on Agnes. "We three had a little stove, too, but
we weren't allowed to build fires in it. Neither were the Langworthy
girls, I suppose. But you used to build a fire for us, and make us
dolls' pancakes. Then sometimes you'd make cocoa, too, and that was
a regular party."
"Imagine your remembering all that!" said Dora. She wanted to
add, You shouldn't remember it. At your age you should be look-
ing forward, if indeed you aren't too busy and excited and cocksure
to look any way at all.
"I don't suppose you give stove parties any more, Miss Dora."
"I had one only yesterday, on this very island," said Dora. “I
brought home three children with me. They live a block or so
toward town; the father of one is a motorman. They were not very
clean children, but they loved my party." She laughed a little, re-
membering the looks that Bess had given her when, immaculate in
66
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
white piqué and followed by two swains carrying their rackets, she
had come around the house bound for the tennis-courts and had
found Dora slumming on the home premises.
"You always did know what children like, Miss Dora."
"They like the same things grown people do, scaled down, of
course," said Dora. They even had their own social distinctions, she
had been reminded yesterday: the policeman's two had looked down
from a height on the motorman's child.
Hoofs in the driveway made her turn her head. "There's Bess
now!" said Agnes, and hastily got out of the swing.
It was indeed Bess, driving the trap. Beside her sat Doug Ruther-
ford-Doug who was supposed to be working at this hour. She
nodded very coolly to Dora and Agnes. Doug took the horse around
to the stable, and Bess went in the back door for her racket.
"I think we'd better return to the veranda," said Dora hastily.
"It's cooler here, but back in Kentucky we got into the habit of sit-
ting on the veranda afternoons."
She was aware of some constraint when she rejoined the party.
All the embroidering ladies had seen the arrival of Bess and her
escort; they were all busily pretending they hadn't seen a thing. All
but Mrs. Palmer, that is. She boomed, "Isn't that Bessie who drove
up with a young man?" Everybody pretended not to hear her, and
Mrs. Travers said hastily, "Mrs. Langworthy, won't you show me
how you make a French knot? You make such beautiful fat French
knots; mine always pull askew."
Hortense's face was scarlet. Dora wouldn't have minded what
Bess had just done, if it hadn't affected Hortense. Bess was fulfilling
Dora's own desire and playing hooky. It wasn't sensible of her to
show Doug off before a crowd of gossipy women unless she had him
securely hooked. But that was her lookout. What stuck in Dora's
crop-and she knew this as well as she knew her own name-was
that Bess had deliberately schemed to "shake" Hortense this after-
noon.
Dora wished acidly that Bess would marry a millionaire: marry
somebody from the Eastern seaboard, where according to her own
story she was genuinely appreciated and go away and let the rest
of them be happy just as they used to be. But this was Bess's home,
after all. Oughtn't the rest of them to get out of Bess's way?
DORA
67
Oh, dear! Stove parties for children were so simple; and library
trustees could insult you, but then you forgot about it. Only when
women shot across the bows of other women, you always found
trouble. And generally it was the sort of trouble that you couldn't,
however much you might desire to do so, bring out into the open
for a good airing.

T five o'clock The Bee went into the dining-room for what had
originally been, and was still called, "a cup of tea." Years ago,
however, it had evolved into a substantial supper, and the members
vied with one another in the quality and novelty of the meal they
provided.
The table was set with doilies embroidered with sweet peas in
colored silk; a low glass bowl filled with sweet peas to match stood
in the center of the table. They were using Retta's pink-sprigged
Haviland china to-day-her third-best set-and heavy cut-glass tum-
blers which Dora would have liked to take both hands to lift.
The second girl, in her cap of state, passed creamed chicken and
slices of toast for the chicken to rest on. This was Mrs. Langworthy's
improvement on chicken patties, which were most hostesses' great
stand-by. Saratoga chips, which had cost the cook an hour over the
deep frying-pan, were accompanied by small hot rolls and large pats
of butter molded in the shape of roses. Jason's peas were duly
honored; and a novelty was offered in cucumbers cut lengthwise
and designed to be eaten with salt. Home-made caramel ice-cream
and a large frosted cake formed the dessert. The guests had their
choice of tea or coffee; large breakfast cups were used for both, be-
cause this wasn't a formal party, simply The Bee.
At ten minutes before five Dora had taken a look at the tennis-
court. Bess and Doug were playing hard, and had just begun a new
set. Dora slipped into the dining-room and removed one "cover"
from the table.
She saw Mrs. Langworthy give a sharp glance around after they
were all seated. But the party proceeded as usual through second and
third helpings to a climax of repletion. Even Dora allowed ice-
68
DORA
69
cream to be served to her. Dessert was nothing in her life; but man-
ners were manners.
The session was broken up by Mrs. Cordiner; a second later Mrs.
Travers realized that she, too, had a husband.
"It's my turn to entertain next," said Mrs. Chase. "What day next
week will suit everybody?"
Out of a considerable babble it appeared that Thursday would be
the best day. Agnes saved her mother a trip upstairs, and Hortense
brought down Mrs. Palmer's black silk coat and jet-trimmed toque.
They had talked themselves out on the veranda when Bess came
around the corner of the house, brandishing a racket in each hand.
Her linen blouse was turned in at the neck, and the sleeves were
rolled above her elbows. Her hair, loosened by exercise, was slipping
from its firm knot; but her bright eyes and heightened color made
her disorder attractive even to Dora's tidy eyes.
She made the rounds and greeted all her mother's guests. A mo-
ment later the Colonel appeared on the front walk; the departure of
The Bee was once more delayed, while he chaffed Mrs. Cordiner.
Mrs. Chase, steering Agnes toward the drive, turned to speak to
Bess. "The Bee is meeting at my house next Thursday, Bessie.
You're especially invited."
"Oh, thank you, Mrs. Chase!" said Bess. "I'll come if I can. But
don't count on me."
"We should enjoy having you. Marie and Geraldine will be with
us, of course." There was a faint hint of reproof in Mrs. Chase's tone.
When The Bee met at their house, all the Chase girls were sup-
posed to put in an appearance.
"I really have another engagement," insisted Bess. "I made it, of
course, before I knew where The Bee was meeting next week."
"I'm sorry. We shall miss you." Mrs. Chase clambered into her
victoria, opened her parasol, and gave it to Agnes to hold. Agnes's
future would be holding parasols and hunting for scissors. Her
family would be very kind about it, though, and pretend that Agnes
liked just staying at home with Mamma.
Mrs. Chase had scored off Mrs. Palmer in getting started first.
Mrs. Palmer turned to say to Mrs. Travers, "May I drive you home?
It's right on my way."
Before Mrs. Travers could explain that she "liked the walk," Mrs.
70
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Cordiner put in, "I'm driving Mrs. Travers." Mrs. Travers lived in
an unfashionable part of town. But Mrs. Cordiner was in the store
every day, and owned a landau instead of a victoria. There was no
love lost between her and Mrs. Palmer. Anyhow, that dig about its
being "right on my way" was too much to put up with.
The third carriage rolled down the drive. The party was over.
Mrs. Langworthy turned to Bess. In an aside which was probably
louder than she had intended, she said, "What's your important en-
gagement for next Thursday? I didn't know you were dated up so
far ahead of time."
"I will have an engagement by Thursday," said Bess. "You know
I will. I always do."
"I don't see why you should," returned her mother. "There are
days enough in the week. I think it was lovely of Mrs. Chase to in-
clude you especially. I'd like it if you went with me."
"What's that?" demanded the Colonel. "Your mother wants you
to go to Mrs. Chase's next week, Bess? Then you'll go with her. Of
course you will."
Bess pouted. "The Bee is fun for Mamma, maybe. But I don't see
why I should be like Agnes Chase, dragged around from one old
ladies' party to another."
"Agnes Chase is a nice girl," said the Colonel. "What's the matter
with Agnes?"
"She's such a nice girl," retorted Bess. "That's all that's the matter
with her. But it's enough, goodness knows."
Hortense lost her temper. "There's no reason you should speak
that way about poor Agnes. You enjoyed flourishing Doug Ruther-
ford in front of her. You know you did. You showed her your life
teems with young men, while she has to be satisfied with what you
call 'old ladies."
"Doug Rutherford? What's all this about Doug Rutherford?" de-
manded the Colonel. "Will somebody please tell me what is going
on here anyhow?"
Mrs. Langworthy explained, "In the middle of the afternoon Bess
brought Doug here in the trap. That was all we saw of him. Or of
her either, until just before you got home."
The Colonel turned to Bess. "Did you have Doug Rutherford with
you in the trap-alone?"
DORA
71
"I called for him, and brought him here for a game of tennis.
What was wrong about that?"
"Everything was wrong about it. You know that as well as I do.
In the first place, I don't want you calling for a young man. The
Rutherfords have plenty of rigs of their own, not to mention the
new benzine buggy. If Doug wanted to come here, he could get
here without being called for. In the second place, I've expressly
forbidden you to go riding with any young man alone."
"But it was just from the bank out here," pleaded Bess.
"Nobody who saw you could tell that. They might very well sup-
pose you and he were going for a drive together."
"But nobody special saw us."
"Your mother's friends all saw you, didn't they? Anyhow, that
doesn't make any difference. I don't want you to ride alone with a
young man, even if you don't go five feet. Is that clear?"
Bess looked around angrily. Amy had gone into the house at the
first sign of conflict, and Dora was collecting work-bags on the
veranda. But Hortense, who had been a witness of Bess's triumphant
entry that afternoon, was now a witness-and a rather flushed and
eager witness-of her humiliation.
"I don't see why you have to scold me in front of everybody," Bess
said furiously.
Dora found a glass of iced tea which had not been touched; it was
still cold, too. She brought it down the steps to the Colonel. He must
have had a bad day downtown; ordinarily he would have been
careful not to speak to Bess in that tone at all, let alone so publicly.
"What you did was done in front of everybody. Oh, thanks, Dode!
I'm dry as a bone. It was hot downtown this afternoon."
He buried his face in the glass. Something was bothering him,
and he would have liked to confide it to a sympathetic listener. But
he never seemed to succeed in catching any listener alone under this
roof except Retta; and this was something he especially didn't want
to tell to Retta.
"This is awful," stormed Bess. "When I'm at home for such a
short time, I don't see why I can't be allowed to have a little fun.
But if I do, it seems everybody is indignant. Oh, well! It won't last
long. School will be a relief after this."
"School?" The Colonel handed his empty glass back to Dora, and

took Bess by the elbow. "You run along in the house, Hortense, and

Hortense obediently disappeared. Mrs. Langworthy looked hard at

Mrs. Langworthy had been seriously put out at Bess that afternoon;
but her own attempts at discipline were too likely to end in peace-at-

Retta's broad back disappeared into the house. Dora started to fol-

Dora took four work-bags upstairs and distributed them to bed-

"Supper in just a few minutes, Bess," warned Dora.
"I don't want any." Bess's voice was close to tears. She slammed
the door of her room, and the key turned in the lock.
Dora went on down to the dining-room. After The Bee, the
ladies of the family didn't need any more refreshment that evening.
(Dora generally felt that she didn't want to eat again for a week.)
She helped the second girl reset the table for three persons.
Supper to-night was more of the party food. The Colonel sat
down alone, and a minute later Artie joined him. But Artie wasn't
very hungry. He didn't even wait to be offered an after-supper cigar.
He went outdoors again.
Retta and Hortense had established themselves in the sitting-room.
Dora was starting to get her banjo a little music would relieve her
feelings without hurting anybody else's when Amy beckoned her
into the hall.
"Bess's door is locked," she whispered. "I heard her crying inside,
but she called to me to go away. She said she had a headache and
didn't want any supper."
"Well," said Dora, "I never believed in punishing naughty chil-
dren by making them go to bed without their supper. But if they do
it voluntarily, how are you going to stop them?"
"I'm afraid the Colonel hurt her feelings, Dora."
"Bess's feelings are easily hurt. They always have been. But she
never stops to think that other people may have feelings, too."
DORA
73
Amy looked embarrassed and at the same time relieved. Dora
often said the things Amy just couldn't bring herself to say. Under
her breath Amy confided, "Sometimes Bess isn't very kind to Hor-
tense." Then she added hastily, "But I don't believe she realizes
quite how she sounds."
All the afternoon Dora had been itching to take up the cudgels
for Hortense. But when Amy brought the whole thing into the
open, Dora's wrath was suddenly appeased. After all, Hortense had
her own share of adulation: Amy and Dora both adored Hortense.
Bess was their niece, too; but in a special sense Hortense belonged
to them.
But the Colonel was now eating a solitary meal in a room which
had rung for an hour with talk and laughter. He ought to have
something out of this ménage beside the honor of paying the bills.
"You go help Retta sort that embroidery silk," Dora advised. "She's
making a mess of it. If the girl's own mother can sit down to her
embroidery now, Amy, I don't see why you should worry your
head off."
Reluctantly but without audible protest, Amy joined Retta and
Hortense. Dora slipped through the library to the dining-room.
"Want me to keep you company, Colonel?" she asked. "How are
you making out with the ice-cream? Everybody else has given us
fresh strawberry lately. But I think Louise's caramel can't be beaten."
"The ice-cream is fine. Sit down, Dode." The Colonel snatched
his napkin in his hand; but before he could get farther with his ges-
ture of rising, Dora had slipped into her own place one away from
his.
He looked wretched. He had quarreled with his darling; and the
fact that he was on the right side of the argument, though it had
strengthened him during his outburst, now added to his remorse.
To talk about the weather wouldn't distract him. The gossip she
had heard that afternoon might not interest him. But a counter-
irritant should do the business.
Dora glanced at the doorway, to make sure that Amy hadn't fol-
lowed her. Then she lowered her voice. "Artie was in the grape-
arbor all afternoon. I saw Pete Fleming sneak out of there just
before The Bee went home. Pete Fleming drinks his share. Amy
doesn't like to have Artie hang around with him."
74
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"Artie is welcome to select his own company. That's about all the
fun he gets out of life." The Colonel looked at her sharply. "Does
Artie ever ask you for money, Dode?"
Dora grinned. "A brother's confidences are sacred. I suppose that
applies to his touches, too, doesn't it?"
"I hope you don't ever give him anything?"
"So little that I don't see why he bothers to come to me. Probably

"Dode, there's no reason why you girls, out of your little incomes,

"I think Pete stood treat to-day," said Dora. "And neither Amy
nor I owes money the way Artie does. In fact, I don't believe Amy
ever owed a bill in her life."
"I wish you'd promise me, Dode-" began the Colonel.
"I'll promise you nothing," she interrupted. "But Artie has never
got around me yet, and he's been trying for a long time. Longer
than I've known you, Sam."
The Colonel sat musing for a moment. What had disturbed him
that afternoon was the discovery that Artie not only owed a large
bill in the bar of the Mount Royal, but was in the habit of touching
friends and acquaintances of the Colonel's for the loan of "a fiver."
In fact, at supper just now the Colonel had said to him sharply,
"When you feel that you must have money, Art, I wish you'd come
to me."
Artie had withdrawn in a huff-which wouldn't prevent his tak-
ing advantage of what might, when he cooled off, seem to him less
a reproof than an opportunity. Oh, well! Artie didn't matter so
much. But indignation at him had certainly sharpened the Colonel's
temper for Bess. To be sure, she had been disobedient-yes, and then
saucy to her mother. She had been so taken aback, though, when she
heard that she wasn't to be allowed to return to Sheldon! A door
closed in her face: and from the moment she was born she had
always been used to welcome everywhere!
It was too severe a punishment for the offense. If the school-marms
at Sheldon couldn't keep the girls in order, they had no right to
turn around and blame Bess. When Retta heard of the expulsion,
too, she'd be harder on the girl than he had been. She'd be afraid
DORA
75
that her friends would get hold of the story and magnify it.
Yet Retta was actually fond of every one of those friends of hers,
even Mrs. Palmer; and for Mrs. Travers or Mrs. Chase she would
willingly have gone to the stake. Women were funny, very funny.
Hard on each other, they were. Retta would be hard even on Bess.
The greater worry had drowned the lesser. Artie passed from his
mind. "Did Bess eat with you women?" he asked.
"No. She hasn't had any supper."
"D'you think she'd eat off a tray if you took one up to her? You
do make a tray look tempting, Dode. I remember the ones you used
to fix for me when I had grippe last winter."
"Servants don't take enough pains with anything like that. I be-

finished."
"Oh, I'm practically through now! Those sweet peas are pretty
on the table, aren't they? Don't you want to put a little glass of them
on Bess's tray?"
Bess ought to live on bread and water for a while, thought Dora,
and be fed off a tin plate. Aloud she said, "No hurry, Colonel. The
longer she's kept waiting the more anxious she will be to make
peace with us."
The Colonel chuckled. "You splutter a lot, Dode. But you're as
soft-hearted as the next one. I'll bet you've already been up at Bess's
door begging for admittance and trying to heal the breach."
He'd lose his bet, then; but Dora didn't see any reason to enlighten
him too far. "I'll have Louise make fresh coffee," she said. "This has
been standing."
He shoved back his chair and felt for a cigar. In his relief he no
longer looked like the same man.
Dora was half-way to the kitchen when he called her back. "Tell
Bess it's high time we were looking for that new horse for her. If
she wants to be ready when I leave the house to-morrow morning,
we'll see what we can do about it then."
Dora looked around at him, and had difficulty in controlling her-
self. She wanted so awfully to thumb her nose at him. But the
Colonel had his standards of behavior for a lady; and in his eyes
the Millses were not only ladies but ladies of quality. Anyhow, she
mustn't let him realize that she thought he was soft. She had taken
76
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
some pains to soothe him; and, of course, the minute his indigna-
tion evaporated he must do his best to make everybody happy.
Bess would be fed off the Haviland china instead of a tin plate.
And to-morrow she would be allowed to select a special horse to go
with her trap, though she had just proved she couldn't be trusted
with the trap at all. It was lucky the Colonel couldn't afford solid
gold service-plates. That would be his idea of what Bess was really
entitled to.
