V
HE new horse was delivered at the Langworthys' two days
later. It was a handsome bay with a black mane and tail and
one white foot: a young horse, but not too sprightly. The dealer
had guaranteed him "gentle enough for ladies to drive."
"Let's try him out this afternoon," suggested Artie.
"It's Bess's horse, Artie," warned Amy.
But Bess was in an expansive mood. "Of course we'll try him out
this afternoon. We've been talking all summer about driving out to
the Soldiers' Home. Let's go to-day. I was brought up on those
Soldiers' Home band concerts, and I haven't heard one for ages."
Mrs. Langworthy liked the idea, but she couldn't see herself climb-
ing into the new trap. She preferred the landau, which had a step
only eight inches above the ground and a wide back seat. In the
landau she finally set forth, with Amy beside her and Jason driving.
Bess drove the trap, with Artie for help in case of emergency; Dora
sat in the back seat with Hortense.
The Soldiers' Home was situated four miles north of Mount
Royal; it was reached by a road which at this season of the year lay
deep in dust. As far as the race-track, however, it was a wide road.
The young bloods from Prospect Avenue, driving their sulkies out
to the track for the Sunday afternoon races of the Driving Club,
often had a preliminary heat or two on the way.
It was hot in the trap, and a strong south wind made it hard for
Hortense to keep her parasol up. Dora, in a hat that shaded her eyes
and a neat veil, sat with her gloved hands in her lap, a picture of
propriety. When occasion demanded, Dora could look much more
the perfect chaperon than either Amy or Mrs. Langworthy.
Just beyond the race-track they met a single buggy with a driver
in the loose blue uniform of a veteran. Beside him sat an oldish man
77
78
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
of military bearing, who took off his hat to the occupants of the trap.
"Wasn't that Governor Wiley from the Home?" demanded Bess.
"Yes," said Artie. "I had thought we might drop in at his house
and pay our respects. But of course there's no sense in going when

"Too bad he saw us, or this would be the ideal time to call," said

"She's fat and she's dull," snapped Bess over her shoulder, "and

"Humph!" said Artie. "She has a fine chance, stuck out there in

"They ought to have Civil Service in the Home: give those young

Presently the road began to be lined with saloons bearing good
Civil War names, "Sherman," "Sheridan," "Grant." Farther on the
saloons were all crowded to the left of the road. To the right was a
high iron fence, beyond which lay fields and an orchard. At a wide
gate Bess turned in; a guard, who had just been examining a vet-
eran's red pass, stared indifferently at the trap. Artie glanced around,
but the landau was nowhere in sight.
Ahead of them lay a winding road bordered with trees. The new
horse's hoofs clicked sharply on macadam. Here and there through
the trees could be seen a brick building: the laundry, the bakery, the
guard-house. What the arrangement lacked in convenience it made
up in picturesqueness; the place had no look of an "institution."
When they approached the center of the grounds Dora looked
DORA
79
around with interest. This was just as she remembered it from last
year and the year before. Here all the grass was shaved close, the
trees were trimmed, the fences and benches were painted every
spring. Off at the right was a pond-artificial but of fair size-on
which two pairs of swans floated and flat-bottomed rowboats were
drifting under the willows on the bank.
The grounds had the cared-for, parklike look that Dora had ad-
mired on country estates in England. Time meant little here, and
attention was lavished on details. None of the show-places back in
Mount Royal contrived to get quite this effect. "Our leading citizens
know how to make money," thought Dora. "But they haven't yet
learned how to spend it."
Bess drove around the Main Building and the hospital, and circled
past the wide lawn where Julia Wiley sat in solitary splendor to
listen to the distant strains of the band concert. They grew louder as
the drive approached a sort of central plaza. There benches grouped
about a band stand were occupied indifferently by veterans and

and children were drawn up.
"Not so many people out here as there used to be," commented
Bess.
"People tear around so much nowadays," said Artie. "When you

town. But now the mammas all want to dash into Chicago and spend
their money as fast as possible, or else take rooms for the season at
a summer resort where you change your clothes three or four times
a day."
"Poor old Artie!" teased Dora. "You think things are running
down since you were young?"
"I'm not so old," protested Artie. "But I do see a vast change.
Leisure and dignity-"
"Leisure and dignity nothing!" Bess interrupted saucily. "When
Papa came West with his father they had very little leisure and no

"That sounds like Colonel," said Hortense. "He never plays up
what a great man he was when he was young. And talk about

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
frankness! I remember once when I was a very small girl, I asked

kept meeting veterans, all clad alike in straight blue coats, trousers
of a lighter blue, and stiff little McClellan caps.
"Do you notice, no two of them walk together?" muttered Artie.

When they had to sit side by side, they didn't seem to have much to
say to one another."
"Why should they?" said Dora. "This isn't a veterans' reunion.
They see one another every day and all day. Enough is enough, I
agree with them."
Ahead loomed a building which housed a theater, a library, and a
small store. In front of the store an old soldier was holding forth to
three somewhat abashed civilians. With wide gestures of his cane
DORA
81
he was explaining how a certain battle was won, largely through
his personal exertions.
"H'm!" commented Artie. "It's different when they get an out-
sider to talk to."
They strolled on. Beyond the library was a row of houses assigned
to subordinate officers, the Treasurer, the Adjutant, the Commissary
of Subsistence. Each house had its private lawn and flower-garden.
"I wonder what's going to become of all this when the last old
soldiers die?" speculated Artie.
"You and I won't live to find out," said Dora. "These men don't
look more than forty or fifty years old, most of them."
"They aren't. The War was fought on both sides by an army of
boys. Let's see. It closed in '65. Say these men were twenty-two when
the War ended-"
"The Colonel says some of them sat on the fence and waited for
the Home to be built," interrupted Dora.
"He shouldn't say a lot of the things he does," said Artie with
offended dignity. "I'm sure most of them have earned their reward
from a grateful Government."
Dora thought of a lot of answers, and didn't use any of them. The
Colonel disliked professional old-soldiering, and hadn't been at all
discreet about declaring his dislike. He could have headed the Mount
Royal chapter of the Loyal Legion any time these past twenty years,
but he seldom even went to meetings. Maybe, though, he figured
that his record would speak for him. Artie had to shout down his
record.
"Hadn't we better be turning back?" she suggested. "Why, there's
Bess now!"
The trap had followed them north. Bess drew up to explain that
the flies bothered the new horse, and he didn't want to stand.
"Is your mother ready to leave?" demanded Artie.
"She won't leave before the Star Spangled Banner. You can go
back with her, if you want to. But there won't be room in the landau
for both you and Aunt Dora."
Artie decided to stay for a time, “that is, if you think you'll be
safe with a new horse and just women in the rig." Dora took his
place in the front seat.

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Just outside the grounds Bess turned and winked at her. "Now
perhaps we can get a little speed," she said.
Bess drove somewhat better without Artie's superintendence. She
kept them out of their own dust on the way home, and turned into

She had rather expected to have Hortense go with her; but Hor-

Small as she was, she had strong wrists; and the horse, feeling her
hand on the reins, showed more speed than he had all afternoon.
Dora drove downtown by a back street. But by the time she reached
Prospect Avenue she had acquired confidence. She turned west and
swept along in fine style.
This was rather mean, taking Bess's horse down the fashionable
street where Bess herself hadn't yet had a chance to drive him. Still,
it was fun. Dora shifted the reins to her left hand; her right, clutch-
ing the whip at a jaunty angle, rested on her knee.
She had intended to turn back at the city limits. But when she
reached them, she decided to keep right on and look for a country
road that would take her north. This was a lark, to be out alone,
and not even have to hear advice on her course. She had never been
fond of taking advice; right now she didn't even have to listen to it.
She skirted a farm-wagon returning home. Ahead of her lay a
clear stretch of road. She touched up her horse and felt the wind
whistle in her ears. To go on and on like this on and on until you
and the horse both dropped-that would be an ending! Things so
seldom ended properly in this world. They just stopped; and if you
wanted an answer, you were left still looking for it.
Ahead of her, on her side of the road, loomed an obstacle.
Dora pulled on her left rein. But instead of swerving, the horse shied.
"Pretty boy! Nice boy!" said Dora, and looked to see whether she
had room to turn.
She hadn't. The horse shied again, and she set her teeth. Perhaps
an ending was closer than was at all comfortable. She could warn
Bess of this horse's bad trick, if she got back to warn anybody of
DORA
83
anything. She wasn't in the least frightened, but she had an idea that
the trap would tip easily. Would prayer do any good? Or should
she risk using the whip?
She decided on the whip. The next moment she all but laughed
aloud. She had just made out what it was that the horse was
shying at.

VI
HE had started to laugh at the moment of rescue. A man in
white flannels and a Panama hat, who had been standing beside
the stalled vehicle, sprang to the horse's head with an agility which
would hardly have been expected from any one of his bulk.
"It's all right!" he called to Dora. "You can't blame the horse a
particle. I'll lead him past."
"Oh, hello, Mr. Manning!" said Dora. "Just hold the horse a
minute, and I think he'll feel better. He's new, and I don't suppose
he's broken yet to automobiles."
"Neither am I," said Charley Manning. With his free hand he
stroked the horse's neck. "I had the first automobile in Mount
Royal. This is a new one, but all the old troubles seem to be right
here with it."
The horse was standing now. Dora took a real look at the new
automobile. Under its coating of dust its red sides gleamed proudly;
from beneath the front stretched the prone legs of what had once
been the smartest groom in Mount Royal.
"Did you run over your friend?" she asked.
"No. He's tinkering with the engine. This is a French car, and it
ran well enough in France. But these Illinois roads seem to be more
than it can negotiate. There, baby! There, baby!"
Dora looked around sharply; but as she might have guessed, he
was addressing the horse, not her. Charley Manning liked horses,
and always drove good ones. What had possessed him to take up
with these new-fangled contrivances you were beginning to hear so
much about?
"You haven't far to walk home," she said politely.
Charley Manning's eyes, set far back in his head and pouchy
84
DORA
85
underneath, twinkled at her joke. "That's right, but it's a point of
honor not to walk."
"Have you been here long?" asked Dora.
"About an hour. But an hour is nothing in this game." He cast a
disgusted look at the car. "We had two punctures getting here. The
farmer between my place and town bought a special paper of tacks
for my benefit, I think. Now the engine stalls. I don't suppose I'll
ever get a car to suit me unless I make it myself. This is just a nice
way of passing the time."
"Very nice." Dora looked at the dusty road ahead and reconsidered
her decision to drive home by a long way around. "Do you think I
have room to turn here? That would be simpler perhaps than
going on."
"I'll keep hold of the bridle while you back the horse. That's the
idea. Steady a minute! Not quite so far. There we are!"
She was headed back toward the city, and the offending car was
cut off by the horse's blinders. She could have twitched on the reins
and gone on. It was what she should have done, if she intended to
relate the adventure at home.
But Heavens! People always made her sick, the way they talked
about Charley Manning. The women could be excused, perhaps;
women had to be on the defensive in an unjust world. But the men
were simply hiding their jealousy; Charley Manning did more or
less openly what other men were secretly hankering to do, if they'd
just had the nerve.
She thrust her whip in the socket and began to wind a rein
around her wrist. "I'm not sure whether you remember me," she said.
"I'm Dora Mills, Mrs. Langworthy's sister."
"I remember you perfectly," said Charley Manning. "How's the
Colonel? I just got back from Europe last week, and I haven't seen
him yet."
"He's well," said Dora.
"He's a fine man," said Charley. "I always enjoy talking to him.
And how is Miss Amy?"
Dora was startled. If he had asked about Mrs. Langworthy, that
would have been mere politeness. If he had inquired about one of
the girls, anybody in Mount Royal would have known what to think.
86
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
But Amy! Why on earth should Charley Manning have even re-
membered Amy?
"Amy's well," she said rather breathlessly. "We're all quite well,
and enjoying the summer. This is a new horse, which the Colonel
just bought for us. I'm glad I didn't get into serious trouble the first
time I had him out by myself. I've you to thank for my-my rescue."
"You have me to thank for your danger." He stepped back and
stood with his hat in his hand. Dora didn't know how long it was
before he put his hat on again. She was careful not to look back.
But when she was rolling again past the substantial respectability
of Prospect Avenue, her panic not only subsided but became ridicu-
lous. She was a good one to laugh at the hide-bound conventions of
other women! She had been actually frightened because Charley
Manning asked in the most casual way about her sister Amy.
He probably asked about Amy because she was the only member
of the household whose name he happened to remember. He was
awfully nice with animals. He had quieted the horse in a jiffy.
Dora would like to see him with a dog. You could tell everything
about a man-nearly-by the way he handled a dog.
He had enterprise, too: she remembered that first automobile of
his, and how the small boys had lined up along the curb and shouted
when he puffed and clattered down the street. Now he had bought
a new auto which was no better than the first. But he'd keep right
on until he discovered one that would go.
He'd keep on, or- What was that he had said? He'd "make one
himself." Well, he was quite capable of doing just that. He had
engineered a dozen enterprises here in Mount Royal: foolhardy
enterprises, too, some of them. Yet he had had a high average of
success, while more self-consciously sensible people sat back and
envied him.
He would make an automobile himself. Yes, and get in on the-
didn't they call it "the ground floor"? Somebody was going to get in
on the ground floor, if the idea of a "horseless carriage" had anything
at all to it.
Somebody would-! Dora jerked on the reins so sharply that the
horse stopped dead still, which wasn't what she had intended. Why,
this was the chance Colonel Langworthy had been waiting for all his
life!
DORA
87
He had a factory already; he made the carriages. All he needed to
do was put some works in them: an “engine,” Charley Manning had
called it. If people were going to buy those crazy contrivances, the
thing to do was start in and sell them.
"I'll put the idea up to the Colonel to-night," she promised herself.
"Maybe it isn't such a good idea as I think it is. Maybe it's really
quite a rotten idea. But I'm going to give it to Colonel for what it's
worth. If he can make something out of it, he's welcome."
She reached home, found the spur drive to the veranda occupied,
and drove on into the stable. Luckily Jason was already there.
Luckily, too, she hadn't so much as broken a strap in what after all
didn't amount to an accident: it was just a horse shying.
She came around the house to the front door and made sure that
the rigs tied near the horse-block were a buggy and Dan Mason's
hack. On the veranda Bess, in a new grass linen dress, was yawning
over a magazine.
"Who's here?" demanded Dora.
"Governor Wiley and two other men. They're in the library with
Papa. Senator Roberts and that awful Duquesne man got here only
a little while ago. But the Governor must have been headed here
when we met him early in the afternoon."
Dora felt dashed. If four men were shut up together for a pow-
wow, they might take from now until midnight. She wanted to
spring her new idea while it was still fresh. It would keep, of course,
if it was a good idea. But why did things always have to "keep"?
In spite of her veil her mouth and nose were full of dust. She took
a bath and put on fresh clothes. She could still taste dust, but she
looked cool and neat when she came downstairs again.
She had just reached the foot of the staircase when the Colonel
came around from the library door, showing his visitors out. Dora
wondered what Captain Duquesne was doing here at all; he always
made such a point of being disagreeable to the Colonel. Senator
Roberts, of course, was an important man, and the Wileys were
family friends. Men sometimes came here to see the Colonel about
this and that; the "business" they talked over was supposed to be
too intricate for female comprehension. Still, she had an idea that
what she was about to say might in the end amount to a whole lot
more than whatever it was the conclave had met here to discuss.
88
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
She waited until the Colonel came back from seeing his visitors
off. Then she followed him into the library. The air was thick with
cigar smoke; she left the door open to get a draft through.
The Colonel had walked straight across to the bay window, and
stood looking out. "Did you have a good time this afternoon?" he
asked over his shoulder.
Dora perched on the arm of a chair close to his elbow. "The new
horse is fine." (What was the use of talking about an accident that
hadn't happened?) "I took him for a little drive after we got home.
What are we going to call him, by the way?"
"They called him 'Mac' at the stable. But you women can change
his name if you want to. That's your affair."
"We'll leave that to Bess. Personally, I think 'Mac' is a very good
name." Dora smoothed down a fold of her lilac organdy. "Sam, do
you think you and I will live to see the day when nobody will keep
horses any more?"
He looked down at her and smiled. "Hardly. Bess may live to see
it, though. Stranger things have happened."
"Then, Sam, if people are going to get along without horses, why
don't you sell instead of buying? I mean, turn your carriage-factory
into a place to manufacture automobiles."
"Funny you should speak about that to-day, Dode."
"You mean the same idea had occurred to you?"
"Not at all! Almost the dead opposite, in fact. Governor Wiley
was here just now, and Senator Roberts and Captain Duquesne."
"I know. I saw them leaving."
"Well, they came to sound me out about going to the Soldiers'
Home as the new Governor."
Dora was startled. "How come? The job never really seeks the
man, so far as I've discovered. And I should think for that job there
would be at least a thousand willing Barkises."
"This is supposed to be a profound secret, Dode, but Governor
Wiley is thinking of resigning. It's on his daughter's account, I
gather, though he didn't say that in so many words."
"Oh, yes! We were speaking of Julia Wiley just this afternoon.
I suspect Mamma Wiley is at the bottom of the move."
"I shouldn't wonder. Well, when the Board of Managers was here
last month, the idea was to let the Treasurer succeed; he's second in
DORA
89
command anyhow, you know. But opposition has developed because
the Treasurer is 'a civilian.' On the other hand, if they ever start
to look for a new Governor in the ranks of the Loyal Legion, they
will find too many willing candidates. There's only the one job, and
there'd be twenty unsuccessful applicants left with-"
"With a flea in their ear?" suggested Dora coolly. "I see! So they
hit on the idea of using you as a compromise."
"That's about it. How does it strike you, Dode?"
"You should feel complimented, Colonel. But beyond the compli-
ment I don't see much in the idea. I don't know just what salary the
Governor gets-"
"It isn't large. Perhaps I could jack them up a little, but not much."
"Then you couldn't arrange to take the position. It would mean
that you'd have to retire from business, and of course you couldn't
afford that. Isn't that just the way things would have to happen?
A hundred men want the job, so it's offered to the one who doesn't."
"I don't want it, no. I tried to make them see that I couldn't take
it."
"You 'tried'? You mean you couldn't make them see that?"
The Colonel smiled faintly. "Oh, they're all politicians more or
less! So they gave me credit for maneuvering the way they would
have in my place."
"Then the matter is still undecided? For how long?"
"Oh, a month or two, I suppose! There's no tearing hurry about
Wiley's leaving."
There was a curious wistfulness in his tone. Dora was puzzled.
Did the idea really attract him? If so, he must be held back by the
sense of his responsibilities. Dora didn't relish that notion. She was
close kin to all the responsibilities.
"It would be a financial sacrifice," she said slowly.
"Not so much of a sacrifice as it might sound," muttered the Col-
onel. "In business you make money, and then again you lose it.
Half the time you don't even know where you stand. A small salary
is at least clear. And of course a house goes with this job of Gov-
ernor."
"Sam Langworthy, you really want to take that position!"
"Oh, no, Dode! No. You mustn't get that idea."
90
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"You want to take it," insisted Dora. "But you won't. You won't
take it, and that's as may be. But you're trying to fool yourself when
you claim you don't want it. None of us do the things we really
want to do. And we all fool ourselves more or less."
The Colonel looked down at her again, and this time his eyes
focused sharply. She was such a tiny creature, so demure, so soft-
voiced, as finished as a miniature on ivory. With her lilac gown and
silver hair, she was the perfect picture of a sweet spinster. Yet under-
neath she was seething with rebellion. Her rebellion was chronic,
and he was often conscious of it.
Ordinarily, however, he dismissed it as "just Dode's way," like
Amy's reserve or Retta's procrastination. To-day, however, he was
more than usually sympathetic with any square peg in a round hole.
"Has somebody been shooting across your bows, Dode?" he de-
manded.
"I wasn't speaking of myself especially."
"Maybe not. But I was. Oh, of course that idea of yours about my
manufacturing automobiles is very likely a good idea, Dode! It
would take capital, quite a lot of capital. But perhaps that could be
managed. I'll think it over. Thank you for mentioning it."
To Dora the idea didn't sound nearly so marvelous as it had
seemed when it popped into her head. But she had had her say,
whether or not anything came of it. She slipped to her feet. "All
right, Sam. You're welcome. I won't detain you any longer. You'll
want to wash before supper."
"Not so fast!" The Colonel got between her and the door. Thrust-
ing his hands into his pockets, he stood barring her way. "There's
something I'd like to ask you, young woman."
"Ask away. I won't promise to answer, though."
"I wish you would answer. You ought to, really, since you brought
the matter up. Dode, if you could do exactly what you wanted to do
-now, to-morrow, any time soon-what would it be?"
"You sound like a game of forfeits, Colonel. All right, I'll pay. If
I could do exactly what I want to do, I'd pack my bag to-morrow
and go off for a trip through Yellowstone Park. They take you
through in a stage coach, and there's a hotel at each point of interest."
"How much does the trip cost? Have you any idea?"
DORA
91
"Several hundred dollars. It might as well be thousands, though,
so far as I'm concerned. You have to get from here to Montana
first-"
"If you can do the whole thing for five hundred dollars, that can
be managed. Find out to-morrow, and let me know."
"Sam Langworthy, if you think I'm going to let you send me

"I'm not," said the Colonel. "Use your own money as far as it
will go. But count on me for the rest."
Use her own money? She could let her bills run; let Hortense get
along without a few things, too. Geysers and painted rocks: early
morning starts, and halts to drink from clear mountain streams!
It was tempting, but of course- She shook her head.
"If you really wanted to do it, you'd do it," insisted the Colonel.
"Don't tell me, Dode. I wasn't born yesterday."
The Park season was short. She had missed too many things in
her life, and she wasn't getting any younger. Why didn't people use
their brains on their problems, instead of using their prejudices and
their habits?
"I suppose you wouldn't care to go alone," he pressed. "But if you
took Amy with you, or one of the girls-"
"Oh, stop, stop!" she interrupted. "I'd love to go alone. I'd like
nothing better. And I'll find out to-morrow just what it would cost.
You ought not to do this for me."
"But you're going to let me?"
"I'm going to let you. And I'm going to get the value out of your
money by enjoying every single minute while I'm away."
"That's more like it," said the Colonel. "Send me a postal while
you're away, Dode. And don't try to walk through Yellowstone Park
in patent leather slippers. Buy whatever you need in the way of an
outfit."
He stood aside to let her pass. Dora sped through the hall and out
into the flower-garden, lazy and fragrant at this hour. She needed
the open air to give vent to her sense of release.
A month's trip, such as thousands of people took every year, but
through scenes entirely new to her: she valued the trip itself, because
travel was the thing her heart was really set on. But in another sense
the trip itself was only a symbol of the way life should be lived:
92
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
without hesitation or scruple, freely and simply taking all that was
offered.
Her head buzzed with the delights in store for her; in no time at
all now she would be seeing with her own eyes those sights at which
she had been staring for months in the photographs. She began to
calculate time and prices. And could she manage with just one bag?
Luggage was always such a problem!
It wasn't until she reëntered the library on her way to supper that
she realized there was another side to her going. These last few days
the Colonel had been confiding to her things he hadn't yet told
Retta. One or two of them, perhaps, he never would tell Retta.
That sort of thing couldn't be allowed to go on. Dora realized
with dismay that merely by listening she was in a sense dividing
the household into two camps. No, she mustn't let him get into the
habit of turning to her with his perplexities.
If she stayed here right along, she might find it hard to head him
off. A month's absence now would make a great difference.
Oh, dear, was there always some annoying reason for doing even
the thing you most wanted to do? She would enjoy her trip to the
utmost. Of course she would! But already the gold of the idea was
a little tarnished.

ETTA MILLS had been named "Henrietta." Papa, who had
been quite clear all along that his first-born must be a boy on
whom he would bestow his own name, had minimized his disappoint-
ment in the child's sex by refusing to admit defeat in the business of
baptism.
It had proved a good name for him to bellow in his seasons of
wrath; but it was years now since any one had called her "Henrietta."
Indeed, it was now difficult for Retta to realize that she had ever
borne the Mills name either. She felt as if she had been married all
her life. When Dora and Amy got to reminiscing about the old days
back in Kentucky, Retta could recall many of the events of her girl-
hood; but the person to whom they had happened eluded her
strangely. Dark-eyed, dashing Retta Mills might almost have been a
beloved younger sister or elder daughter; Mrs. Langworthy had lost
all sense of identity with her younger self.
With the years she had put on a good deal of weight: far too much
for her small feet to carry. (All the Millses had pretty feet; it was a
sign of good blood, as Papa had always been careful to point out.)
She got around less and less.
But while she was seated she was always careful to keep her fingers
busy; that gave her a sense that she wasn't wasting any time. She
began many more "pieces" than she finished; if her enthusiasm lapsed,
her conscience was not likely to hold her to a task that had become
tedious.
Yet her work was always beautifully done; and in The Bee she was
the recognized authority on satin-stitch and French knots, on honiton
and Brussels point. This summer she had learned the form of Swedish
drawnwork called "hardanger" from Mrs. Travers, who had acquired
the art from her hired girl. The exquisite exactness of Mrs. Lang-
93
94
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
worthy's hardanger put to shame not only Mrs. Travers but the girl
who had learned the art in Sweden as a child.
On a morning early in August Mrs. Langworthy finished her break-
fast with a sigh of repletion and set the tray aside on the unoccupied
half of the great walnut bed. Then, after a lengthy pause, she fumbled
around on the chair at her bedside for the case in which her pincenez
always spent the night. Increasing short-sightedness had made it
impossible for her to take a step even in her own bedroom without her
glasses; but until she had had her substantial breakfast she preferred
to have the world remain a pleasant blur.
Her glasses brought into sharp focus not only Sam's night-shirt
flung across the footboard, but the piqué vest which he had discarded
that morning for a clean one, and a collar which he certainly should
have taken time to put into the laundry-box in the corner.
Retta's own possessions were piled in chairs, heaped on the wide
couch that stood in front of the south windows, stowed in boxes which
filled the entire space under the bed and all had to be hauled out on
sweeping day. She had moved into this room when "the addition"
was built the year after Dora and Hortense came to the Langworthys'
to live. Mrs. Langworthy hadn't got the room really settled when she
had to tear it up again for the paper-hangers. The Colonel insisted on
new wall-paper every two or three years; getting it added to the
horrors of housecleaning, but he believed in "keeping things up."
He was careless, however, about putting his possessions away; and
after nearly thirty years of his untidiness, Mrs. Langworthy was still
annoyed by it. She was quite convinced that the day would come
when she herself would get everything straightened out.
"When cooler weather comes," she assured herself, "I'll begin by
clearing out my closet and all my drawers. It would help if I had some
shelves built between my closet-door and the window; I could hang
a curtain over them. Yes, I really need more places to put things. Sam
hasn't a thing here except his clothes, but he makes them go pretty
far. I have material and sewing-implements and my fancy-work and
all those magazines I'm going to look over when I have time; but at
that my part of the room isn't much worse than his. I'll have to get
those new shelves on his account, really. He needs a good example
set him."
Meanwhile it was a hot day, though the bedroom with its windows
RETTA
95
on three sides was always airy. Mrs. Langworthy sat up and reached
for her stockings; she couldn't bear the feeling of slippers on her bare
feet. But the idea of a corset and corset-cover and two starched petti-
coats was distasteful. From her closet she fetched a clean white dimity
wrapper, and put it on over her nightgown. Amy and Dora never
appeared downstairs unless they were completely dressed; and they
had infected Hortense with their sense of propriety. But after all, this
was Retta's house. She could do as she pleased on a hot morning, so
long as she didn't stand between anybody and the light.
She washed in the bathroom at the front of the house and combed
her hair there so that she could rinse the feeling of hair off her fingers.
With Dora away, she had hinted to Bess that the back bathrooms
had less than their share of users. But Bess had retorted that Aunt
Dora would be home before she herself left for school in the fall, and
she wasn't going to all the bother of moving her things twice.
Bess was using an expensive imported soap which had been a
present from her room-mate's brother, Bill Jerome. She talked a lot
about the Jeromes and the way they lived in New York. Mrs. Lang-
worthy was glad Bess understood the art of getting in with the right
people. But soap struck her as an odd gift from a young man to a girl.
Candy and flowers, books of course, or a few pieces of sheet music:
well-brought-up girls limited their acceptance of gifts pretty rigidly
to those. Roger Willett had brought Bess a new tennis-racket the week
before, and Mrs. Langworthy had made her send it back to him. Bess
pouted, but she obeyed. Her father bought her a new tennis-racket the
next day. A girl's father should be able to buy her everything she
needed. That was part and a very binding part of the code.
Mrs. Langworthy helped herself to a use of Bess's soap. The Lang-
worthy bathrooms were always provided with pure Castile, which
was bought in large bars and divided with string. Dora or Amy would
have gone dirty before she lowered herself to use scented soap.
But Mrs. Langworthy rather liked it for a change. She hoped, how-
ever, that Bill Jerome's next present to Bess wouldn't be some superior
sort of toothbrush.
Tidy on the surface at least, and very much refreshed, the lady of
the house descended the front stairs, shut the outside door to keep
out the hot morning air, and turned toward the kitchen. A strong
smell of paint as she passed the library door told her where Hortense
96
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
was. Hortense had undertaken to decorate a set of panels which were

In the kitchen the second girl was ironing. A woman came every
Monday to do the washing; but the ironing in that household, with
five women to be kept in summer dresses and white petticoats, often
lasted almost through the week.
At the back door Louise was giving an order to the grocer's boy.
Mrs. Langworthy always trained her cook to do the ordering. Dora
had more than once hinted that the grocer's and butcher's bills would
have been smaller if the lady of the house had done the ordering
herself. But Mrs. Langworthy held that it took half the pleasure out
of a meal if you knew in advance just what you were going to have
set before you. Wives like Mrs. Travers and Mrs. Chase considered
that dampening of zest one of the necessary evils of the wifely state.
But Mrs. Langworthy believed that she was as much entitled to the
pleasures of the table as Sam was.
"Good morning, Ida. Morning, Louise," said Mrs. Langworthy. She
had greeted Ida of course when the girl brought her her breakfast-
tray. But this was her official appearance as head of the household.
"You be sure those sardines are in oil, Benny. Last time you brought

living through the year to greet all the different good things that came
RETTA
97
with the revolving seasons: melons and sweet corn, early peas, straw-

Of course it was better to have a husband who took an interest in
his home than one of those husbands who acted as if they simply
boarded there. But there was never any telling when Sam would

This week, for example, he had insisted on hiring an old soldier to

scarcely be considered urgent.

no time to transplant: no time to plant, either. And it was a shame

"They're making the old boy eat by himself after the rest of them

The Colonel looked relieved. He thought she held him responsible
for the damage done by his hireling. Of course she had held him
responsible during the first few minutes. But he ought to know by
this time that her bark was worse than her bite.
Amy ought to know that by this time, too. Amy had scarcely
touched her dinner. It always gave her a shock when people raised
their voices and thundered; and somehow at one time or another a
good many voices were raised in that household.
She seemed happy enough here, though. She had never been one to
put herself forward even when they were girls. Retta herself had been
a dashing girl; Hortense's mother was really beautiful, and Dora had
been small and saucy and entertaining. But when Amy was among
her sisters, no one would have bothered to take a second look at her.
The odd thing was that she would have repaid a second look better
than the more striking sisters. Amy was exquisite. She had changed
very little with the years; there were times when she actually looked
younger than Hortense. Sam liked her, too. He had been known to
102
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
say, especially after a brush with his wife or Dora, that he wouldn't
care who heard him say it, Amy was a lady.
"I'm driving downtown to do a few errands this afternoon, Amy,"
said Mrs. Langworthy. "Do you want to go along?"
"I had thought I'd try to get a nap this afternoon," answered Amy.
"But of course if you really went me to go with you-"
"Oh, no! You go ahead and have your nap." Mrs. Langworthy's
tone was faintly condescending. She herself might breakfast in bed,
but naps were no part of her schedule. When she finally put in an
appearance, she was "up for the day."
"I'll go with you, Aunt Retta," said Hortense. It would mean an-
other postponement of her painting, but she knew Aunt Retta liked
company. Things would be simpler for Hortense when Dora got back.
More enjoyable, too. Officially Hortense lumped all "the aunts" to-
gether as the people who brought her up. But privately she felt that
she belonged especially to Dora.
"Is there any corn beside what was cooked?" asked the Colonel.
"Some of your friends would enjoy a present of a dozen ears, Retta."
"There isn't an ear."
"Take 'em some flowers, then. There isn't a house in town has such
cartloads as we have, even if the arrangement of the garden is some-
what-er, irregular."
That was Sam for you. Generous with everything. Generous to a
fault, really. But he never stopped to think how often his generosity
involved trouble for other people.
However, Hortense helped with the flowers. They put together a
tall bouquet of mixed blossoms, and then filled a fruit-basket with
pansies. Mrs. Langworthy stooped to lay her cheek against their cool
and fragrant softness. "Aren't these lovely, Hortense? Isn't it too bad
flowers don't last?"
"Perhaps we wouldn't like them so well if they did last. Who is
going to get these, Aunt Retta?"
"Oh, we'll decide that later on! I want to get my errands done first.
Cover the bouquets with tissue paper, and they'll keep."
In her pale gray summer silk she filled more than half the back seat
of the landau. The bouquets were laid between her and Hortense
at first, but Hortense soon took them on her lap. Hortense was one
of those cool blondes; she didn't wilt flowers. Mrs. Langworthy felt
RETTA
103
like a furnace herself, driving along the downtown streets on that
breathless August afternoon.
In front of the grocer's, the butcher's, and a small fruit store Jason
drove the landau close to the curb, and the proprietor came out to
serve the ladies. At Kortebein's they alighted; Mrs. Langworthy had
some embroidery silk to match, and new kitchen towels had been
urgently needed for weeks.
Hortense helped with the silks, but she yawned in the linen de-
partment. Girls like Hortense seemed to think that household supplies
grew on trees and houses could be trusted to maintain themselves. On
the other hand, old maids like Dora were inclined to hint that such
matters could be attended to promptly with no special trouble. Mrs.
Langworthy had a good mind to teach Dora a lesson some day: let
her take over the running of the household for a few months, and
maybe she'd find out it wasn't quite so simple.
"Seeing we're right down here, I think we'll leave all these flowers
for Mrs. Cordiner," said Mrs. Langworthy. "That will save getting
out of the carriage a second time."
"Mrs. Travers always loves your flowers," hinted Hortense. "If the
Cordiners really like flowers, there's no reason why they can't grow
them."
"Molly Travers is one of my best friends," said Mrs. Langworthy a
little savagely. "But I hate to think of that husband of hers profiting
by anything we have taken the pains to grow."
Hortense slipped her hand inside Mrs. Langworthy's arm as they
crossed the street. When they reached the curb she muttered, "I heard
just yesterday that Mr. Travers was chasing after some woman in
Chicago. Quite a young woman, the story said. But what young
woman would want him, Aunt Retta?"
"She wouldn't necessarily want him. But she might want his
money."
"Do you think she has much chance of getting his money? He isn't
very liberal with his wife and son."
"I think he's an old fool," said Mrs. Langworthy. "But you're right,
Hortense; Molly needs a little cheer all the worse, if she has to stand
him and his goings-on. We'll take just the pansies to Mrs. Cordiner."
Mrs. Cordiner wasn't in the store, however. Business was dull on an
August afternoon, and she had gone with her husband for a ride
104
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
in the country. Frank Morse took the basket of pansies to keep for her.
If she didn't come downtown before closing time, he would walk
around by the Cordiner house on his way home.
"Frank Morse is still half an errand boy in that store," commented
Mrs. Langworthy, clambering back into the landau, which had fol-
lowed them across the street from Kortebein's. "He lives in a boarding-
house, too. I don't see why some nice girl doesn't get hold of him
and marry him."
"Aunt Retta, you said that louder than you intended," whispered
Hortense.
Mrs. Langworthy sniffed. She was always saying things louder than
she intended. But if Jason cared to pass on her casual remarks, he was
welcome; these coachmen always knew more about the affairs of the
families they worked for than the families knew themselves.
Hortense was looking back over her shoulder at Tom Holmes, who
sat motionless behind his rows of dangling watches. He hadn't even
glanced in their direction. But Hortense knew he had seen and recog-
nized them; she was sure, too, that he had overheard Aunt Retta's
remark.
Mrs. Langworthy followed the direction of her eyes and laughed.
"I'm sure I don't mind Tom Holmes, if that's what's worrying you.
He's just painted on the scenery. He was sitting in that window when
I came to Mount Royal as a bride. I've never seen him move away
from it. He hasn't changed a hair in thirty years, either. Main Street
wouldn't be the same without him there."
"It would be much improved," said Hortense dryly.
Hortense was young, and liked change. Well, her Aunt Retta didn't.
She was perfectly happy in her daily round. Of course she would have
liked to be a little better abreast of her responsibilities. But that she
firmly expected to be. This had been a full summer, what with Bess's
young men around the house so much and then Dora getting that
idea of chasing off West.
But when the weather cooled off, things would be different. Bess
would be away at school again. For better or worse, the old baboon
would have ceased to function in the Langworthy garden. Sam might
sell that new horse he had bought for Bess. It was a needless extrava-
gance with Bess away at school most of the year; and Sam was so
fussy about having her drive it, too.
RETTA
105
Anyhow, it made an extra horse for Jason to take care of. Sam
hadn't thought of that when he bought the horse. He liked to see
people happy. He never paused to realize that when you dropped a
stone into a pond, you had no way of telling just how far the ripples
would spread.

THEN Mrs. Langworthy came downstairs the next morning
she found Amy bending over the mail, which was spread
out on the dining-room table. "Nothing here for you but a school
catalogue," reported Amy. "Oh, pardon me, Retta! We always told
Hortense and Bess to hand over the mail without commenting on it."
"We also told them not to say, 'Will you have some more?' when
they offered second helpings. But I catch myself saying it every now
and then."
"I suppose that's the general experience. People bring children up
well by telling them not to do things that the grown-ups do all the
time."
"Well, they've been told anyhow," said Retta comfortably. "Did
Sam get away from the house this morning before the mail was de-
livered?"
"I don't know. I didn't see him." Amy lifted startled eyes; her glance
always seemed to tell more than her speech did.
But she wasn't a close observer. A school catalogue was more mail
than Mrs. Langworthy expected. It was also more than she should
have received. Amy had read "Mrs." for "Mr."
But why should the Colonel be getting a catalogue from St. Agnes,
if Bess was going back to Sheldon in a few weeks? It might be un-
solicited, of course. But he had originally insisted on Sheldon, when
Retta would have been satisfied to send Bess to the small Southern
school where she herself had spent a year, and had been followed in
turn by Dora and Margaret and Amy, and then after a long interval
by Hortense.
There was no sense in getting excited over nothing. But Mrs.
Langworthy had a notion that Sam had something up his sleeve. If
he had, there was no point to letting him take his own time about
106
RETTA
107
shaking it down. Sam was the kindest man in the world, and the most
generous. But he wasn't half-or a quarter-as clever as he thought
he was.
That evening she joined him in the library. Bess and Hortense were
entertaining two young men on the veranda, and Artie had taken his
cigar into the sitting-room because he wanted Amy to try over with
him some new light opera songs which he had bought that afternoon.
When Artie clipped one of his infrequent coupons, he often bought
little gifts for the women of the family. He had just presented Mrs.
Langworthy with a tiny pair of silver-handled embroidery scissors.
"Sam," said Mrs. Langworthy abruptly, "have you any idea of
changing Bess's school this fall?"
The Colonel looked startled. "Did Bess say anything to you about
it?"
"No. She knows of your scheme, then?"
She had outflanked him in her first move. He had already admitted
too much, yet he hadn't made a clean breast of what was bothering
him. The Colonel sald uneasily, "I had thought some of putting her
into a church school. They're supposed to teach very nice manners
at those places."
"They were supposed to teach all sorts of wonderful things at
Sheldon, I remember. But Bess didn't learn a thing there except ex-
pensive tastes," said Mrs. Langworthy bluntly. "What makes you
think St. Agnes would do any better for her?"
"St. Agnes? How did you know-?"
"I guessed. And it seems I guessed right. But if you're asking me,
Sam, the child would be just as well off here at home as she would be
gallivanting off to school for another year. I suppose you intended to
consult me sooner or later?"
"Retta, do I ever do anything important without consulting you?"
"Don't change the subject," said Mrs. Langworthy. "How much
does Bess herself know about your plans for her?"
"She doesn't need to know anything, does she, until you and I
have made up our minds?"
"Let's make them up right now, and get it over with. And let's
call the young lady into the consultation." Mrs. Langworthy started
toward the door.
108
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"Retta, please! Bess has company to-night, and there's no such
hurry."
He wasn't in time to intercept her; when Mrs. Langworthy was
roused, she could move fast. She was roused right now; she was on
one of her tears. The Colonel resigned himself, and tried to think
rapidly. He had been trying all along to spare Bess's feelings. But had
Bess gone behind his back to enlist her mother's sympathies?
Mrs. Langworthy didn't look very sympathetic. She called Bess in
from the veranda, and shut the library door after her. "Sit down,
young lady," she snapped. "Your father has something he wants to
say to you."
"That sounds like the Day of Judgment," said Bess, not sitting
down. "I haven't been up to anything lately, Papa. Honestly, I
haven't."
"We won't go into what you've been up to," said Mrs. Langworthy
grimly. "He has some new plans for you, Bess. He's thinking of
sending you to a church school next year. We've never been strong
church people; but he seems to think you need a little discipline for
a change."
"Retta, I never said that!" exclaimed the Colonel.
Bess shot a glance at him, and decided that he hadn't intention-
ally given her away. However, Mrs. Langworthy when she pleased
could turn the Colonel inside out like a glove. Bess had a feeling
that they were both holding out on her. She had ideas of her own as
to how she would like to put in her time this next year; but she
wasn't sure of her ground.
She sank into one of the roomy leather chairs and crossed her
ankles daintily. "Is this going to take very long? I'm leaving Hor-
tense with two men to talk to; and sometimes she isn't awfully good
at keeping up with their chaff."
"Don't you worry about Hortense," advised Mrs. Langworthy. "And
don't worry about your young men. Hortense won't steal 'em. Now
then, Sam, what was it you wanted to say to your daughter?"
"You've already told her. I simply wanted to ask her what she
thought she'd like to do about entering a different school this fall."
He couldn't go back on Retta in front of Bess; but on the other
hand he disliked the idea of letting Bess think he had exposed the
whole case to her mother. What Sheldon had done to Bess was
RETTA
109
between Bess and him. Retta was sometimes harder on the child
than she meant to be.
Bess looked from one to the other of her parents, measuring her
chances. "We're waiting," said Mrs. Langworthy.
Bess took the plunge. "Fran Jerome isn't going back to Sheldon.
She's making her début in New York this fall. She has written ask-
ing me to come down there for a long visit. I'd like to go, if I may."
"A long visit? How long a visit?" demanded her mother.
"A month or two. I could learn more with the Jeromes than I
could at any old school, Mamma. I'm not much of a student, you
know. It's the Mills in me."
"You mean you'd like to drop out of school for a year?" asked the
Colonel.
"If you dropped out now, you wouldn't go back," said Mrs. Lang-
worthy.
"She could if she wanted to," argued the Colonel. "They won't
close the schools just because she stays out. That's not such a bad
idea, Bess. You might loaf along here for a year, visiting your friends
if you wanted to, and in betweentimes just having a good time with
your mother. She'd enjoy your being home again."
Retta looked disgusted, but Bess leaned forward eagerly. "We
could give some nice parties. I owe heaps of people. You can't expect
young men to keep on calling and calling here, unless we do some-
thing to show them that-"
"Young men are very important to you, Bess," interrupted Mrs.
Langworthy. "Very well! But let me remind you that the attentions
must come from their side."
"In theory they must. They do! You can't say I don't have my
share of 'attentions,' Mamma. But, after all, you might do something
to let people see that I have a family back of me."
"That's quite right," said the Colonel hastily. "We can give a little
party for you almost any time, Bess. It's nice here when the flowers
are all in blossom. What sort of party had you in mind?"
"A dance, of course. A dance is much the nicest thing."
"It's a lot of trouble," objected Mrs. Langworthy. "And anyhow
you're not supposed to go to dances until after you 'come out."
"Oh, Mamma! That will be all right, if it's understood that I'm
coming out next winter."
110
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"I don't see any hurry about that coming-out business," the Colo-
nel struck in. "You have time enough ahead of you to be a young
lady."
"That's where you're wrong," said Bess sharply. "I've seen what
happens to girls who flatter themselves that they have time enough
ahead of them."
"Why, Bessie!" ejaculated the Colonel.
"Don't 'Why, Bessie!' me, Papa. There are old maids enough in
the family now. I don't intend to make one more."
The Colonel stared at her in bewilderment. "Enough old maids in
the family!" But if Dora and Amy hadn't been old maids, the whole
family wouldn't have been having those good times together all these
years. And if in her list she included Hortense-well, that was simply
cruel. As long as the Colonel could keep a roof over his own head,
Hortense was welcome to share it.
He was hurt by Bess's vehemence. But Mrs. Langworthy was
amused. "It's nice to know your own mind, Bess," she said dryly.
"You needn't be quite so saucy to your father, though."
Bess slipped toward the edge of her chair. "I didn't mean to sound
saucy. But girls are brought up to get married. I don't see what the
sense is in making any bones about that."
Girls are brought up to get married! Dora had said that same
thing to the Colonel not long ago. Dora felt that way of bringing
them up was all wrong; she thought something ought to be done
about it.
Retta didn't feel that it was wrong; it was just not quite sufficient.
She said again what she must have said to Bess a hundred times
before. "There's only one real reason for getting married, Bess. That
is love, of course."
"Of course," said Bess. "Now if I may be excused-"
Something stirred dimly at the back of the Colonel's mind: some-
thing he hadn't thought of in years, and would rather not have
thought of just now. Retta had married him for love, surely; yet in
their first year together, it had seemed to him that she preferred to
think and speak of almost anything else.
Oh, well! perhaps men and women always differed in their ideas
of love. He was very fond of Retta. She had been a splendid wife to
him; and if she hadn't been entitled to consideration on any other
RETTA
III
count, the fact that she was Bess's mother would have been enough
in itself. What on earth had reminded him so sharply just now that
his early feeling about his marriage had been disappointment?
"Run along, Bess," he said quietly. "Don't bother your head about
the things older people say to you. We were young once ourselves.
It's too easy for us to forget that fact."
Bess dropped a light kiss on his forehead and slipped out of the
room as quickly as she could. Mrs. Langworthy sat staring at the
closed door.
"Now just what did we accomplish by calling our daughter on
the carpet to-night?" she demanded.
"I didn't think of it as 'calling her on the carpet.' But I believe we
accomplished a lot."
"Just what, Sam? You hold me responsible for Bess's bringing up.
Oh, I'm not complaining about that! A daughter is the mother's
special responsibility. But the minute I try to do anything with Bess,
you always step in and spoil her."
"I don't call it spoiling to let her know that we love her, and that
we are always glad to have her around. What's the matter with this
plan for keeping her right here next winter? She can have a good
time, and maybe she can help you a little with the housekeeping."
Any help with the housekeeping that she might get from Bess
weighed but lightly with Mrs. Langworthy. But she had missed the
child terribly last winter. They'd be just a lot of old fogies shut up
together if it were not for Bess.
"Do you think we might manage a party for Bess some time
before cold weather sets in?" she asked abruptly.
The Colonel smiled under his mustache. It was Retta's habit
periodically to charge him with spoiling Bess. But she always stepped
forward promptly and did her share of the spoiling. If Retta had had
more children, or even if she hadn't had to wait so long for Bess, it
might have been better for the girl in the long run. But there again,
that wasn't Bess's fault.
"We could put down canvas and have a dance right here in the
house," he said. "We've done it before. But the rooms aren't really
very well arranged for dancing. What would you think of building
a platform out on the lawn? We could string Japanese lanterns for
light. It would make a very pretty party."
112
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"That's a good idea, Sam. Then the dining-room could be used
for refreshments, and the mammas could play whist in the sitting-
room and the room behind it. But that would run into money."
"We can raise the money, if you think Bess would like it. See to
it that she has a new dress for her party, too. A girl always likes a
new dress, doesn't she?"
"Bess has more clothes now than the four of us put together had

a new dress. She always does."
She helped herself to a sheet of paper from the Colonel's desk and
began to jot down names of possible guests. "Include some of your
own friends for the card-room," suggested the Colonel. "It's a long
time since you've given a real shindig, Retta."
"Big parties are a lot of fuss and bother," said Mrs. Langworthy
indifferently. But she turned over the sheet to begin a list of refresh-
ments. Lemonade and cake might be all that the young people would
expect at an evening party in most houses where they were enter-
tained; but Mrs. Langworthy was conscious that she had a reputation
for serving good food.
Seeing her so happy and absorbed, the Colonel wondered what he
had been agitating himself about just now. Retta sometimes had to
be worked up in order to get her started. But in a pinch she had
never failed him yet. Retta was splendid. It must be a nuisance to be
tied to one of those women who spent their middle life reproaching
their husbands for not loving them "the way you used to when we
were young." Nobody stayed young forever. Retta didn't expect it of
him. She was surely entitled to respect and admiration in her com-
fortable middle age.
His worries were those suitable for a man of his years and position.
Perhaps, though, he ought not to try to spare Retta her share of his
anxiety. He had tried to "spare" her lately in this business of school,
and his consideration hadn't been entirely successful.
He needed reassurance often. He needed it right now. He deter-
mined to ask for it. Clearing his throat nervously, he began, "Retta,
you don't think Bess's anxiety to stay at home next year-"
"She didn't seem anxious to me," said Mrs. Langworthy without
looking up. "We can get three pieces for the music: violin, piano,
RETTA
113
and a drum. It's a funny combination, but that's what the young
people are using this year, Mrs. Palmer told me."
"You're not thinking of asking Eddie Palmer to Bess's dance?"
"Certainly not! Not that he'd come if we did ask him. Nice girls
are the least of Eddie Palmer's troubles."
"Retta, I was going to ask when you switched me off-"
"Excuse me, Sam. I've always had a horror of interruptions. Papa
was most particular about that."
"I can easily believe that you didn't interrupt your papa, especially
not twice in a row." The Colonel grinned at her, but he didn't allow
himself to be sidetracked. "I started out to ask you, Do you think
Bess wants to stay here just to be near Doug Rutherford?"
"Bless you, no! What ever put that idea into your head?"
"He's been around here a lot this summer."
"Not any more than Roger Willett or Phil Tracy. There have been
half a dozen others in and out, too. What makes you pick out

"He's older than Roger or Phil. They'll be sent back to college in
the fall. He's already in the bank and drawing a fair salary."
"There's nothing against him that I know of except that I can't
stand his father." Retta looked up at last, and seemed to meditate.
"No, I've no feeling that he has been especially attentive to Bess.
And as for her getting ideas first, put that right out of your head,
Sam. Bess may be my daughter, but she's a thoroughly nice girl.
She will wait until she's asked."
Nice girls did wait until they were asked. He knew that as well as
Retta did. But whether there was any chance of Bess's being asked,
Retta either couldn't or wouldn't guess.
She could, of course; probably she had. But she wouldn't tell him
what she guessed. Women all stuck together in such matters; a
father was as carefully excluded as if he had been the merest out-
sider.
Yet surely a little frankness on the subject wouldn't have done
anybody any harm. John Rutherford was a tyrant and a skinflint;
but in his day he, too, had been young. Who knew whether at John's
age Doug wouldn't turn out a second John? Granted that family
resemblances often crossed over-nothing flattered the Colonel more
than to be told that Bess looked like him-you could judge a lot
114
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
about the chances of a matrimonial alliance by looking carefully at
the girl's mother and the young man's father.
But undoubtedly the Colonel was borrowing trouble. Bess had
talked a lot that summer about Bill Jerome, the marvelous young
man who had made the delights of New York an open book to her.
That talk, too, had worried the Colonel; he winced at the mere possi-
bility that Bess might marry an Easterner and settle down a thousand
miles away from home.
Still, he'd see to it that Bess made that Eastern visit this fall. It
didn't hurt a girl to let it appear that she had more than one string
to her bow.
Probably that was Bess's chief idea in regard to Doug Rutherford.
A girl her age always liked quantity. Scalp-hunters, that's what girls
were. Another year the Colonel would probably be worrying his head
about Roger Willett. Fathers were that way, he guessed.
Mothers were different. If Bess were to come to Retta to-night and
announce her engagement, Retta would weep over her, of course; but
inside of half an hour she'd be planning the trousseau and the wed-
ding. Probably her way was better in the long run, too, as well as
easier in the beginning.
Retta had fewer illusions than anybody else he knew. She believed
she was a hard-working domestic slave, and she believed that some
day she was really going to get her room completely settled and keep
it neat from then on. But aside from those minor considerations she
was not merely clear-sighted; under her easy-going exterior she was
rather hard. Probably she needed to be hard in order to get along with
the men among whom her destiny threw her. Dear Papa-and Artie
-and now for all these years the Colonel himself.
She knew how to deal with the Colonel. She had proved that afresh
to-night. But there were times and this was one of them-when he
felt that her very competence in dealing with him sprang from a
fundamental deadly indifference to him.
Pshaw! He was getting notions in his old age. Retta was used to
him, that was all. He wouldn't have enjoyed it if she had gone on
expecting him to act like an eager bridegroom. If he insisted on hav-
ing something to worry about, he'd better go back to his business
affairs. Business meant worry; he was used to that, and considered
it a man's share in the transactions of the world.
RETTA
115
The finer shades of feeling should be left to the women to manage.
He had women enough to take that load off his shoulders.
Sometimes it seemed to him that women, even the nearest and
dearest, were awfully hard on one another. But there again, it was
none of his concern. From the hints and flashes which were all that
was allowed to seep through to a man, he couldn't fairly judge.
