III
RS. LANGWORTHY used her visiting cards for the invita-
tions to Bess's dance. Hortense wrote most of them, spacing
her flowing penmanship so carefully that the finished cards became
works of art. Jason was sent forth to deliver them by hand, in spite
of the Colonel's hint that the United States Mail was still functioning.
Mrs. Langworthy couldn't often manage to get herself squared around
to the serious business of entertaining. But when she did entertain,
she saw no reason for not doing the thing properly.
There were several days of spirited debate as to where the platform
for dancing should be built. The space between the flower-beds and
the tennis-court was long and narrow; the lawn in front of the house
and to the left of the drive was a better shape, but hardly large
enough. Finally it was decided to use that front lawn and carry the
platform right over the pond, which hadn't been functioning very
well lately anyhow.
The reorganization of the flower-garden was definitely suspended,
and the temporary gardener sent back to the Soldiers' Home. He had
succeeded only in making confusion worse confounded. Next spring
perhaps Mrs. Langworthy could induce the Colonel to begin the job
sooner and plan it more carefully.
Her original idea of hiring three local musicians was not carried
out. Bess held that if they were doing the thing at all, they couldn't
be satisfied with the kind of music which might have been all right for
a high school dance.
"You're right, Bess," said the. Colonel. "There's no sense in trying to
economize on your luxuries." He engaged a ten-piece orchestra from
Chicago.
That had to be his principal contribution to the preparations. He
was tied up with business right now. For several years he had been
116
RETTA
117
manufacturing farm implements in a half-hearted way, ordering the
metal parts from one foundry or another. Now the plow people
down at Moline had offered to take his stock of farm implements
off his hands. He jumped at the idea of getting rid of that special
sideline, but he wanted to sell his equipment and good-will, too.
The negotiations had been dragging along for several weeks. Mrs.
Langworthy was vague about the details, though she knew people
came up from Moline, and the Colonel made two trips down there.
Molly Travers knew more about her husband's business than he knew
himself; and Cordiner & Morse would have had to shut up shop if
Mrs. Cordiner had ever decided that she belonged at home. But Retta
Langworthy prided herself on never meddling in Sam's affairs.
To be sure, he had spoiled her consistently about money matters.
Whenever she suggested, "It would be nice if we had-" his ideas of
proper behavior for the head of the family would not suffer him to
reply, "We can't afford-" And eight times out of ten suggestions
for interesting new expenditures came from him. If anybody was
driven to act prudent, it had to be Retta.
The way he would spread and spread, if unchecked, was shown
now before Bess's dance. It had been agreed in advance that Bess was
to have a new dress for the party, and that meant Hortense must have
one, too. Mrs. Langworthy was always careful to see that whatever
Bess had, Hortense had something to correspond.
But when he suggested that Mrs. Langworthy and Amy also get
new dresses for the party and send the bills to him, she drew the line.
She had more good clothes now than she ever got around to wear;
and the only way you could ever get past Amy's rigid little standard
of independence was to persuade her that this was her Christmas
present.
Two party dresses under construction in the house were enough,
anyhow. The dressmakers had to be fed and waited on; and fittings
and matchings always required Mrs. Langworthy's superintending
eye. Then there were extra waitresses to be engaged for the party; the
downstairs rooms had to be rearranged, and a substantial supper
planned.
"Now if it rains on the big day!" Bess worried audibly.
But the big day dawned fair and hot; that August was a month
without rain. Artie and Jason strung the Japanese lanterns from tree
118
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
to tree, and thence to the rose-trellis and the grape-arbor. The dance
floor itself had been provided with posts at the corners; when their
wires sagged between posts, Artie did miracles with clothes-poles. He
was up and down the ladder a hundred times that day. This was just
the sort of job at which he shone.
Mrs. Langworthy, with a veil tied around her head to conceal her
curling-pins, rotated like a weathervane in a cyclone. She was glad
she wouldn't be expected to dance to-night; already at noon her feet
were killing her. Amy had taken over the arrangement of the card-
tables in the east rooms; Hortense was counting out silver and nap-
kins, dusting dishes, superintending Louise and Ida while they set up
a punch-table outdoors. Bess had offered to "help with the flowers,"
but Amy finally did them. She said Bess mustn't get too tired on the
day of her party.
At half-past four the Colonel came driving up in the trap, which
he had borrowed for the afternoon. He found Mrs. Langworthy in
the dining-room. "It's all right, Retta!" he said gleefully. "Break a
bottle of champagne over the party, and shove off!"
Mrs. Langworthy, who was shoveling salted almonds into tiny three-
legged dishes, frowned abstractedly. "What's all right, Sam? Here,
don't do that!" He had helped himself to almonds from one of the
dishes she had just filled so exactly; he had a sure instinct for getting
in her way whenever she was attempting to hurry.
"The plows and harrows are sold, madame! In other words, the
party is paid for."
"Oh, yes! Well, it must be a relief to you to have the thing finished
up." She turned to speak to Ida, then unexpectedly turned back. "I
suppose that means you sold the name and the good-will, too."
"The good-will? I talked about good-will until I found they could
talk louder than I could. I'm lucky they didn't just break me instead
of buying me out. I rushed into that business without realizing what
competition I'd have to buck."
She was tired, or she wouldn't have said it to him. But, then, where
had she heard that about "good-will" except from him? Her voice
was steely when she remarked, "You always do get in at the wrong
minute, don't you?"
He stared, not quite believing that he had heard right. Then, as the
sense of her words sank in, blank astonishment was succeeded by a
RETTA
119
bewildered hurt. "I didn't know you felt that way about me, Retta.
You always have everything you want, don't you?"
She hadn't said a thing about him that she hadn't grounds for
saying-not a thing indeed that he hadn't himself hinted from time
to time in a jocular way, or regretted aloud in one of his infrequent
fits of dejection. This very house had been built in expectation of a
real estate boom which didn't materialize. He had taken a flyer in
zinc mine stock just about the time zinc ceased to be profitable. His
fishing reels were a drug on the market. But all the same he expected
to be treated as a little tin god here at home. The head of the family;
the provider; the business man whose days were spent in mysterious
employments past which the women folks ought to tiptoe with
averted eyes.
But she hated to see him look like a baby whose hands have been
slapped. "I didn't mean to hurt your feelings," she said hastily.
"My feelings aren't so important." He backed away from her stiffly.
"You're right, of course. I'm not the business man I wish I was. But
do me this justice, please: I'm not one of those men who live for
making money. Perhaps if my heart had been more in what I was
doing, we'd all be better off this minute."
"You're putting words in my mouth, Sam. I simply said-"
"I heard what you said. I heard every word of it." He turned on
his heel and walked out of the dining-room.
Mrs. Langworthy had a fleeting impulse to follow and offer him
some nuts; salted almonds were his favorite delicacy. But she, too,
was angry. She had apologized, and her apology hadn't been accepted.
He had no right to twist her words to suit himself: no right, either,
to come bursting in and getting his feelings hurt when she had a
thousand and one things to attend to.
After all, this party had been his idea in the first place; but he hadn't
done a thing about the arrangements except to insist on an elaboration
that made work for somebody else. Doubtless he salved his conscience
by telling himself that she had plenty of help. He had been a family
man long enough so that he should have realized, the more help you
had, the more people you must watch. Trying to manage a big party
in your own household, you were inviting comparison with hostesses
who simply hired a caterer from Chicago. That Prospect Avenue crew,
many of them had ballrooms in their third stories, too, This morning
120
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
she had felt that an outdoor dance was not only prettier but more
luxurious than a dance which had to be taken upstairs right past the
servants' bedrooms. Now she burned with revolt against makeshifts.
Amy came in from the kitchen with a bowl of flowers. "All finished
now," she announced cheerfully. "Is there room for these on the
sideboard?"
"There isn't room for anything anywhere," said Mrs. Langworthy
bitterly.
Amy shrank. You always had to be careful how you spoke to Amy.
She started out of the room now with her rejected offering.
"Where are you going?" demanded Mrs. Langworthy.
"I thought perhaps there might be a place in your room."
"All my room needs is a few flowers. It's a perfect Bedlam! I think
I'll just lock the door of my room to-night, so it won't be all over
town to-morrow that I'm a disgraceful housekeeper."
"Oh, dear!" said Amy under her breath. "I hate this atmosphere just
before a party."
"Well, don't go into my room now anyhow. Sam's up there, and I
hope he's dressing."
Sam, of course, seized that moment to appear with a suit which
should have been pressed for that evening, but wasn't. Probably he,
too, hated the atmosphere just before a party. Probably Hortense, who
was telling Ida to keep an eye on the temporary waitresses to-night,
hated this atmosphere, too. Probably Bess was sulking over something
upstairs; she had been quiet altogether too long.
They all had their nerves and their grievances. But what led any of
them to suppose that Mrs. Langworthy liked all this fuss and hurry
and upset? They didn't suppose anything about it, of course. They
just took her for granted.
At half-past five the house was a scene of total confusion. At six the
family had a hasty meal in the pantry; the servants ate standing in
the kitchen. At seven everybody withdrew to dress; everybody, that
is, except Artie, who was lighting his lanterns, and the Colonel, who
was waiting in the kitchen for Ida to press his suit.
At eight o'clock the confusion miraculously resolved itself. Mrs.
Langworthy couldn't very well lock the door of her room, because
Sam was still in there struggling with his tie. Speechless but dutiful,
he had hooked her into a mauve silk with trimmings of Brussels point.
RETTA
121
In the lower hall she found Amy revolving around her two nieces.
Bess wore white, of course, very soft white, with a great flutter of
filmy skirt that would whirl around her as she danced. Hortense had
taken advantage of her greater age to indulge in a little train; her
gown was of a delicate green, and she had wound a twist of matching
green through her thick fair hair.
Even to a mother's eye, the superiority to-night was not on the side
of youth. Hortense was one of those women who are born to wear
evening clothes; for every quarter of an hour that passed after the
lights went on, Hortense appeared to lose a year. Her figure, her car-
riage, her superb hair, were all at their best right now. But perhaps
her very queenliness would set off Bess's fluff and vivacity, especially
in the eyes of the younger boys.
As Mrs. Langworthy came down the stairs Bess was saying, “Oh,
surely not gloves, Aunt Amy! For an informal party, in summer es-
pecially, they just aren't needed."
“Gloves will be worn," said Mrs. Langworthy, flourishing her own.
"You can take yours off later in the evening if you get too warm. But
a little dignity is becoming to a hostess. Indeed, it is what her guests
have a right to expect."
Backed by the Colonel and Amy, Mrs. Langworthy stood in the
parlor to receive her guests. Her feet ached clear up to the top of her
head; she was hot, she was tired, she felt that entire responsibility for
the success of the party rested on her shoulders.
Yet she was actively and excitedly happy. To be a good hostess was
a woman's crowning achievement in life. Greeting her guests, sorting
the dancers from the card-players, hearing the dance music begin out-
doors, Retta Langworthy swam on the crest of the wave. An evening
like this was worth months of contented calm-was worth, even, the
hours of agony that had preceded it.
By nine o'clock the party was in full swing. Amy remained in the
card rooms, helping the mammas and papas who didn't play whist to
organize a table or two of euchre. Mrs. Langworthy, after a visit to
the dining-room to make sure that all was going well there, went
outdoors to watch the dancers.
Above the dancing floor the paper lanterns swayed in a faint breeze,
little bubbles of colored light. Over the beat and body of the music
came the sound of youthful laughter. In the whirl of the waltz the
122
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
girls' skirts, colored like summer flowers, eddied and sank. From
each gloved left wrist depended a program card. Doug Rutherford,
whirling Bess toward the edge of the platform, hummed in her ear-
loud enough so that Mrs. Langworthy heard him-"Save the last
waltz-for me."
The Colonel was doing his duty dancing with the wall-flowers.
There was no wall for them to sit against; but a row of benches over
beyond the grape-arbor was only too easy for the plain girls to reach.
Artie was supposed to be filling in, too; he was a better dancer than
the Colonel. But by reading Artie's program you could have told
which were the prettiest girls at the dance. He might not be so young
any more, but he still had eyes in his head.
Mrs. Langworthy sank on the one bench which had been placed
near the musicians' chairs. Mrs. Chase was already there; she had
three daughters here to-night, so the freedom of the card-room was
not for her.
Mrs. Langworthy followed the direction of her eyes. Yes, to be
sure, the Colonel was dancing with Agnes Chase. What a tragedy it
was to have a daughter who didn't catch on with boys! Mrs. Lang-
worthy's proud glance went back to Bess. She had thrown back her
head to laugh up into Doug's face. The echo of her laughter was like
a silver bell, borne upward on the throbbing music of the violins.
Mrs. Langworthy caught the Colonel's eye and nodded encourage-
ment. He didn't acknowledge her nod; perhaps he didn't see it. He
was very serious about this business of guiding his partner past more
nimble leaders. When the music stopped he took his lady to the
punch-table; the instant he had seen her supplied his anxious glance
roved again in search of programs with too many blanks on them.
Mrs. Langworthy went back to the dining-room to tell Ida that
they would begin serving supper at half-past ten; the card-rooms
must be taken care of first, and then a signal would be given for the
dancers to come into the supper-room.
While she was there, she helped herself to some lemonade and a
buttered roll. She had scarcely eaten a thing at meal-time, and sud-
denly she was feeling famished. She had some salad, too, and a few
salted nuts. It was almost ten when she rejoined the dancers.
Waltzes alternated with two-steps all through the evening. The
schottische and the gavotte, which she had danced in her girlhood,
RETTA
123
were still taught to girls in dancing school; but as ball-room dances
they were "out." The cotillion, too, which had always followed sup-
per at big dances in Mrs. Langworthy's youth, had never really
established itself in Mount Royal for lack of a suitable leader; it was
danced only at the annual Charity Ball.
A two-step was in progress now. The Colonel was doing his man-
ful best with it, though in his disgusted opinion, "The two-step is
nothing but a hop." Hortense, a little paler than she had been at the
beginning of the evening, but still lovelier than any other girl on
the floor, moved smoothly and graciously; Hortense could have kept
on dancing all night. Bess, with a clear space just ahead, was trying
a little double-time with Roger Willett.
Mrs. Langworthy, alone now on her bench, again tried to catch the
Colonel's eye. Agnes Chase was over beyond the grape-arbor now,
trying to keep up an animated conversation with Julia Wiley, who
had been driven in from the Soldiers' Home and was staying that
night with a girl friend, and had had a new dress made and had
spent an hour over her hair, all for the sake of a heart-breaking
disappointment.
But the Colonel still had his hands full. When the music stopped
he mopped his forehead and ran a finger around inside his collar.
But already he was looking for the next girl he must dance with.
Retta felt a wave of acute sympathy. This made a terrible evening
for him, at the end, too, of a hard day. And in the interval between
his business and his social duty, just to put a finishing touch to his
discomfort, there she must go and take his head off!
Now she couldn't for the life of her see why she had been so sharp
with him. Yet substantially the same thing happened over and
over: had happened before, and would undoubtedly happen again.
The Colonel ought to be used to it by this time. He ought to be
used to her. But how cowardly it was to take refuge in habit and
custom, you who had once been young yourselves, and had whirled
with a glad kind world as these young people were whirling to-
night! If she could only go up to him this minute, and touch him
on the arm, and look into his eyes with laughter in her own! If she
could only have felt that there was a chance he would look back at
her with anything but the unseeing stare of a husband who had felt
124
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
his own youth melt between her hard hands-oh, dear, it was thirty
years ago!
She had been young and impulsive and headlong once, like these
untroubled children whirling before her now under the swaying
bubbles of light. She, too, had felt youth's careless certainty that she
could make draft against the future for limitless possibilities, and her
draft would always be honored. Where had youth gone, and trust-
fulness, and rash sweet hopes? Right now she was glad to sink down
for a moment and get her weight off these torturing high heels.
Moving back and forth between the house and the lawn, she had
undoubtedly caught a little cold and would have a stiff neck to-
morrow. Why didn't she go back in the house this minute, instead
of sitting here choking with envy and regret?
But the next dance ended and another began, and still she kept
her post. The scene before her no longer set up a glow at her heart
to answer to its own gaiety. The music was like a file drawn over
her raw nerves. Even the sharp contrast with youth had faded now;
she could no longer believe that she had ever been young. Automati-
cally she smiled at Bess and nodded to Ned Barnes, Bess's present
partner. She saw Hortense's proud head glide smoothly past; and
Hortense woke in her only a tinge of pity.
Suddenly she heard her name spoken close at hand. The Colonel
had stopped in the middle of a dance; he had left his partner at the
edge of the platform and approached Retta without her seeing him.
"Can't you speak to her or something?" he was saying. "She's
dancing every dance herself, without bothering whether the rest of
the girls have partners."
"Oh, you mean Bess?" Mrs. Langworthy tried to collect her wan-
dering thoughts. "Have you spoken to her yourself, Sam?"
"No. I didn't want to sound reproving. I wasn't sure how to go
about it. I think possibly she doesn't understand."
Bess understood perfectly. The Colonel was simply refusing to
face the truth about her. It was a rather grim truth, if you insisted
on reducing it to words: Bess was a selfish, spoiled, pitiless wretch,
who noticed the sufferings of other girls only because they set off
her own triumphs.
Retta adored Bess, but she had never had any illusions about her,
and had pretty well ceased to cherish any hope of changing her.
RETTA
125
But that didn't make a particle of difference, really. Not now, any-
how. What really mattered was that the Colonel, finding himself in
a difficulty, had turned to Mrs. Langworthy for aid.
He still believed that she could make Bess see reason. Well, she
wasn't going to let him down.
"I'll tell Artie to announce supper," she said. "And I will speak to
Bess before they begin dancing again."
A roll on the drum called the dancers' attention to Artie, mounted
on the leader's chair. He announced supper. The rush began for
the dining-room.
Mrs. Langworthy stopped Bess on her way in. "I thought I'd
brought you up in the first principles of being a good hostess. When
you see a girl without a partner, you're supposed to stop dancing
at once and offer her your own partner."
"Oh, Mamma!" cried Bess. "The boys don't like that!"
"Whether they like it or not-" Mrs. Langworthy started to insist.
But Bess interrupted, "I tried twice early in the evening. Honor
bright, Mamma, I did. First with Roger Willett and then with Chuck
Fairbanks. And they both got it in for me, and the girl was embar-
rassed. And that was all the good it did!"
That was all the good it would do, probably. Duty was duty; but
perhaps the younger generation had standards and ideas of its own.
Anyhow, what was the sense in clouding that bright face just to lug
an unwilling youth up to Agnes Chase or Julia Wiley, who couldn't
keep a fellow even after he was tied up and delivered to them?
Bess was a wretch; but still Retta wouldn't have exchanged her
for anybody else's model daughter. And Sam had spoiled her; but
that was all right, too. "If you've honestly tried, you've done your
part," said Mrs. Langworthy. "Don't bother your head any farther.
Run along and have a good time. There are lobster-patties for supper.
You like them, don't you?"
She had her own supper in the card-room, in the company of her
contemporaries: the society which she could really enjoy, and which
made her feel comfortable. Mrs. Rutherford complimented the
chicken-salad, and Mr. Patterson drank three cups of coffee and
wondered audibly why his cook couldn't be taught to make coffee
like that.
After supper Mrs. Langworthy took Amy's place at the euchre-
126
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
table. Amy of course was tired out; but Mrs. Langworthy was getting
her second wind. The disappointed middle-aged woman who had
moped outdoors an hour ago was only the ghost of a vanished hap-
piness. In her proper person Retta Langworthy could take her hap-
piness here and now.
She was actually sorry when the first carriages began to call for
guests. She moved back to the formal parlor to receive farewells.
The musicians were being paid off now and taken back to their
hotel. The Colonel had gone out to help Artie extinguish the lan-
terns. Jason would take them down to-morrow, with considerable
growling. Meanwhile, they flapped with their glow gone; but they
looked to Mrs. Langworthy not so much desolate as triumphant.
She loved this feeling at the end of a party, exhaustion mingling
with satisfaction. Why didn't she get around to give more parties?
It was easily the best thing she did.
She left the locking-up to the Colonel, and followed Bess upstairs.
In the pretty room where the bed now looked a little empty because
the scarfs and slipper-bags had all gone home she stooped, puffing
a little, to unhook Bess's dress.
Bess turned a radiant face over her shoulder. "Oh, Mamma! It
was such a nice party! I'm afraid I was a naughty girl and had too
good a time myself. But you'll forgive me, won't you? It was such
a good time!"
Forgive her? Mrs. Langworthy would have despised Bess if she
hadn't enjoyed herself. If you didn't look out for your own good time
in this world, whom could you expect to do it for you, pray?
This was what Retta and the Colonel lived to bring about: Bess's
good time. Or no, perhaps not that altogether. Retta lived for her
own modest ends; but Bess's good time stood high among them.
It all worked in together, this complicated pattern of daily life and
special occasions, these crossing and often conflicting destinies. Mrs.
Langworthy was a little bit sorry for herself sometimes. But she'd
do herself justice: it didn't happen often.
Generally she not only liked life as she found it: she even took
credit for the way it came to her. What had receded in time or de-
clared itself impossible she could ignore without too much effort.
Each day as it came could be met on its own terms; and in these
RETTA
127
mellow middle years she looked neither forward nor back. Indeed,
without stronger provocation than she had had lately-or was likely
to have soon again-she was unable to imagine her life much dif-
ferent from what it was right now.

IV
FTER the outdoor dance, it seemed no time at all until a grate
fire in the sitting-room of an evening drew them all about it
for comfort as well as cheerfulness. There were fireplaces in the
sitting-room, the library, and Retta's bedroom, thanks to the fact
that the house had three chimneys. But the sitting-room fire, at first
of wood and then a week or so later of cannel coal, was always the
first sign of the setting-in of autumn.
Then the two furnaces had to be started. Ten bags of winter pota-
toes were put into the cellar, and three barrels of apples: Spitzenbergs
and Northern Spies for eating, and Greenings for cooking. Dora
came back from the Yellowstone and other western points-she had
prolonged her trip until her money and her traveling suit both gave
out. And Bess left for a long visit with her friends in New York.
Getting Bess ready to leave was an undertaking which took weeks.
The sewing-machine was moved into her bedroom, and the dress-
maker and an assistant installed there. Amy "whipped" together
yards of narrow lace and insertion for Bess's underwear, and Mrs.
Langworthy made it up; every stitch must be done by hand. Hor-
tense ran ribbons through beading, cleaned gloves, manufactured
dozens of tiny sachet-bags to be filled with dried lavender flowers.
"All this fuss for a few weeks' visit!" commented Dora. "What
are you going to do when Bess gets married? I tell you, Retta, girls
are shoved along too fast nowadays. By the time they're twenty, they
have everything that used to be saved for a trousseau."
Secretly Mrs. Langworthy agreed with Dora; but she wasn't going
to have Bess go to the Jeromes' looking like a country cousin. At
least, the rush took Mrs. Langworthy's mind off the advance of the
season. She always had a sense of tragedy the morning she woke to
find that the flowers had been touched by frost. She hated to see
128
RETTA
129
the screens come down and the storm-windows go up; that was the
sign that you were imprisoned for another winter. It wasn't just that
she was getting old, either. She never had liked cold weather.
"You Southerners!" scoffed the Colonel.
As a boy he had waded to school through drifts that would have
kept the entire Mills family not only snowbound but stupefied. He
still enjoyed the tang of cold weather; he liked coming home to the
coziness of a house heated to eighty degrees, liked having the lights
go on early, liked knowing that the pantry and cellar were so well
stocked against the winter that the house could have stood a six
months' siege.
In winter, of course, the sale of carriages fell off to nothing. He
spent more time now out at the factory, a rambling one-story brick
structure which always smelled enticingly of wood-shavings and var-
nish. Orders at the factory ought to pick up after New Year's; he
had had to lay off two men lately, but he had managed to find one
of them a job elsewhere, and he hoped that he could take them
both back later on.
His intensified feeling of the joys of home, however, was marred
by missing Bess around the house. He missed her worse than he had
last year, though now she had gone only for a visit. The summer's
gaiety had spoiled him; it threw the present quiet into deadly relief.
For it was quiet now around the Langworthy house. Hortense
might still shine at a dance, or make a useful fourth when Bess was
entertaining young men. But Hortense no longer had evening callers
when Bess was not there. She was sticking too close to her painting
this fall, to make up for lost time; often of an evening there were
tired lines under her eyes, and you could see all too clearly how she
would look when she was forty.
Artie had thought up a new occupation for himself. He had con-
verted the produce of the grape-vines into wine; and many of his
evenings were now spent down cellar in a space which he was board-
ing off and equipping with racks. Perhaps he sampled his product
rather freely; at any rate the Colonel didn't believe that all his time
in his new retreat was spent in carpenter work.
That left a diminished crew for the sociable evenings which the
Colonel loved; and half the time now Dora, too, was unavailable,
being buried in time-tables and guide-books. She was planning in
130
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
detail trips that she never expected to take. She would work out a
complete itinerary of a month in Europe, and then two months,
and then six, noting when each boat or train left, deciding on hotels,
supplementing guide-book information by the perusal of Ruskin's
Mornings in Florence and Mrs. Oliphant's studies of Italian cities.
"What's the idea?" Mrs. Langworthy inquired more than once.
"You're stuffing your head as if you were going to give lectures or
something."
"It's a form of old maid's insanity," Dora explained crisply. "Some
spinsters take to pet animals, like Amy. Some of them take to charity
or church work. I'd take to travel, if I had the money. Not having
it, I get my thrills anyhow."
"It's all right, I suppose," Mrs. Langworthy said uncertainly.
"It's splendid," said the Colonel. "It keeps Dode out of mischief."
It did not, however, keep her altogether out of mischief. He should
have guessed that, knowing Dora.
Christmas passed without Bess, though the Colonel had hoped to
have her home by that time. Hortense duly presented the painted
panels for Aunt Retta's room; they were rather charming in a pale
way, and were set in a row along the mantelpiece until Retta could
get around to having them regularly installed behind a molding.
New Year's afternoon the Langworthys always kept open house,
with egg-nog served to all comers, and an oyster supper afterward
for any one who chose to remain. Mrs. Langworthy, presiding in
the dining-room when the family were at length free to eat, overheard
Dora ask the Colonel, "When do they decide what's to be done with
the year's taxes?"
"This week," answered the Colonel. "Not thinking again of raiding
the treasury, are you, Dode?"
"I want fresh figures," said Dora, "on how little this town actually
spends on its library."
Mrs. Langworthy looked at her sharply. "I thought that was your
last summer's craze."
"It was," said Dora demurely.
Mrs. Langworthy felt relieved; she should have known better.
Tiny Dora shared the characteristics of the largest of the land mam-
mals; like the elephant, she never forgot. She hadn't dropped her
idea of a subscription to buy new books for the Mount Royal Public
RETTA
131
Library; she had simply been biding her time. The event for which
she was waiting she would never have dared to disclose in advance.
Nobody but Dora, indeed, would have dared to speak about it after-
ward.
Late in February Bess came home, full of accounts of the good
times she had been having, yet pleased and eager to be home again.
Mrs. Langworthy had dreaded that home would seem cramped to
her after theaters and Grand Opera and meals at restaurants with
wonderful names.
Now she was both disappointed and relieved that Bess felt as she
did. Something had obviously gone wrong toward the end of her
visit: something, Mrs. Langworthy surmised, which had to do with
the fabled Bill Jerome, for on that subject a sudden silence had
fallen. Well, let the child have her own secrets. Anyhow, she was
back now; the household was itself again, complete, self-sufficient,
and unassailable.
Early in April Dora received a letter with a local postmark. That
day she left the house immediately after dinner, without a word as
to where she was going. She got back just a few minutes after the
Colonel returned from the office; and her eyes were sparkling with
amusement and mischief.
"Where have you been all afternoon?" demanded Mrs. Langworthy.
"I was thinking I'd better send down to the police station to ask
whether a small woman in a gray coat had been picked up wander-
ing around the streets and unable to give an account of herself."
"What would you like to bet that I can't give an account of my-
self?" asked Dora.
She drew a chair up to the sitting-room register and planted her
tiny feet on it. Then from her little embroidered reticule she drew
a neat gray purse, and from the purse she pulled a folded paper.
"What's that? A check?" asked Mrs. Langworthy.
Dora extended the check toward her, then drew it back and gave
it to the Colonel instead. He examined it and looked surprised.
"That's a neat sum, Dode. That will buy your library a lot of new
books."
He handed the check to Mrs. Langworthy. Her horror over the
signature kept her from exclaiming over the sum. Yet it was a signa-
ture which would have been good for a much larger sum.
132
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Hearing Retta's exclamation, Amy rose and looked over her shoul-

a woman, he is simply exercising his constitutional right to pass

honestly seems to enjoy giving money away. After the check was

fool schemes than anybody else in this town. I've heard you say that

and Dora had an understanding or an undertaking-from which

RETTA
137
But that could be gone into later-if indeed it had to be gone into

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"There are the other officers' families. Oh, of course if you dont

She had spoken much too bluntly, she was afraid. But hadn't he

Now he had seriously proposed to make a living as an old soldier
looking out for other old soldiers at the Government's expense. She
was sorry if he felt disappointed. But really, he should have known
better than to consider that offer for a second.
She was half-way to the door when she remembered that a minor
point still remained unsettled. She turned back to say, "Of course
we'll drive out there on Decoration Day. I haven't gone with you

if you're going to make the speech this year, I'll go along for once."
He looked pleased, and fairly touched. "You will, Retta? You're
sure you don't mind going?"
"Not a particle. You so seldom make speeches, Sam."
"Never made one in my life. Not a real speech, that is. I leave
that to my patriot friends in the Loyal Legion. But this once-this
once I feel I'd like to."
"We'll all be on hand, Sam. Just let me know what time you want
to start."
It would be a nuisance, of course, to be ready so early; the Home
was four miles away, and the exercises would begin at ten o'clock.
She yawned in advance, too, at the idea of sitting through the Chap-
lain's prayer and a sermonlike address and all the firing of salutes
and blowing of bugles that the morning would see.
Sam would limp a little, too, going up to the speakers' stand. He
always limped a little on Decoration Day. Oh, well, she mustn't
grudge him his small peculiarities! On important points he gave her
her way-or rather, perhaps, she took it. Victory this afternoon had
been easy enough; but her nerves were still on edge over the per-
ception that perils could lie in wait for her in a life which seemed
so well organized and secure.
She would give Dora a piece of her mind for putting this last
RETTA
139
idea into Sam's head. An ambitious new business enterprise was just
as bad on the one side as retirement to the Soldiers' Home on the
other.
Why couldn't they all stay safe and happy, as they were right now?
They could. They were going to. Mrs. Langworthy would see to
it that they did.

ILACS and peonies all in blossom, and long plumes of bridal
wreath. The sunshine of late May over all, and a curious hushed
air of expectancy. Little flags bordered the principal paths, as if for
a gala day; but the great flags on the staffs were flying at half-mast.
No passes were being honored this morning at the gate of the
Soldiers' Home. There were no uniformed loiterers beside the pond
or around the band-stand. The paths were full of people, but they
were all civilians; they were bound north toward the cemetery, which
lay just within the boundary of the Home.
The Langworthy landau and trap had to be left near the Gov-
ernor's house; the road to the cemetery would be closed to vehicles
until noon. Governor Wiley promptly took Colonel Langworthy in
charge; they were to march at the head of the procession. The Lang-
worthy ladies and Artie Mills, who after considerable debate had
joined the expedition at the last moment, were met on the Governor's
lawn by Mrs. Wiley and Julia.
"There are seats saved for us close to the speakers' stand," ex-
plained Mrs. Wiley. "But I thought we'd better start at once. Then
we won't have to hurry."
Just north of the band-stand they began to pass the formations
which were soon to parade: long ranks of veterans in blue cloth
uniforms with shining brass buttons were drawn up according to
the companies into which they were organized in the Home.
"Do they all have to turn out for the parade to-day?" asked Artie.
"They are glad to honor their dead comrades," said Mrs. Wiley
crisply.
But Julia turned to answer with a little smile, "It's an order, yes."
Governor Wiley began to pass along the ranks and to shout some-
140
RETTA
141
thing brisk and unintelligible; he wouldn't have felt that an order
sounded properly military if a word of it could have been under-
stood. His order was repeated by each company captain; the long
ranks wheeled and caught step. Some of the spectators paused to
see the parade form; others redoubled their pace to get a good
position at the cemetery.
"It's quite a show," said Artie.
Mrs. Langworthy slipped her arm through his, and motioned to
Amy to take her place beside Mrs. Wiley. In spite of the warm
sunshine, Mrs. Langworthy was shivering a little.
She would have liked to murder Artie for saying out of turn what
she herself felt only too acutely. It was a show. Yet it was bitterly and
strangely like a funeral, too-and not like the quiet huddled funerals
which were the only ones Mrs. Langworthy considered "in good
taste."
She had attended a funeral here at the Home once; a former house-
man of hers had died in the Soldiers' Home hospital, and she had
driven out with Sam to see him buried. The vivid memory of that
day was adding to her discomfort now.
Sam had felt that their duty was done when the service in the
chapel was over; but Mrs. Langworthy insisted on following the
funeral procession to the cemetery. The military pomp was correct
in every detail. The firing-squad marched with rifles reversed, an old
army caisson bore the flag-draped coffin, the fife-and-drum corps
played "How Firm a Foundation." The sentence of committal had
already been read in the chapel. At the cemetery the ceremony
consisted only of three volleys fired across the open grave, and then
the final bugle-call, "Taps."
That was the proper ending for a soldier's life, she had felt deeply
and thrillingly: to be laid for his last long rest with the strains that
had closed each day of his soldier's life. "Lights are out, go to sleep,"
the bugle call had whispered to her, too; and she had turned to say
to Sam, "That's the way he should go. A soldier should! It's just
right. It's beautiful."
Five minutes later she was too shocked and bewildered for further
speech. The procession had re-formed. The fife-and-drum corps struck
up a quickstep. The driver of the caisson touched up his horse. Duty
was over for the day. It was a duty they performed nearly every day.
142
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Soon enough their own turn would come. The dead man's friends
didn't bother to go to his funeral; the "mourners" were a detail from
his Home company, ordered out as a matter of routine to attend the
services, just as on another day they might be told off to rake leaves
or peel potatoes.
"It's cold-blooded. It's cruel," Retta thought. "Another one planted.
And nobody cares!"
When the sting of tears in her throat had cleared a little, she said
something of the kind to Sam. "Many of these men haven't any close
relatives," he explained. "Relatives could claim the body if they
wanted to. But you shouldn't expect the members of the Home to
make much fuss over funerals here. How could they stand it if they
did?"
That was just enough, she supposed. But to-day, when the ceme-
tery came into view around a bend in the road, she experienced the
same revulsion which she had felt after that routine funeral.
In the center of the cemetery towered a tall granite shaft, at the
foot of which stood the flag-draped speakers' stand. To right and
left beyond the clear space, and for hundreds of feet straight ahead,
were rows of graves all just alike. Each was marked by a granite
headstone bearing the man's name and his company in the War;
to-day each grave had been "decorated" with a small cotton flag
and a red geranium in a pot. It was a soldiers' cemetery; and the
effect of discipline in death hurt Retta to-day as if she had come
upon it totally unprepared.
Near the speakers' stand were several rows of benches; Home
policemen were keeping the crowd of spectators away from them.
A sergeant saluted as Mrs. Wiley led her party to the front rank of
benches.
"Aunt Retta, can't I go back a little way and stand?" asked Hor-
tense. "I like to see the men march."
"You can see from here," Mrs. Wiley assured her.
But Mrs. Langworthy said impulsively, "I want to see your uncle
march. I never have, in all the years that I've been married to him.
I suppose we'll just have to elbow into the crowd if we want to see
the procession turn into the cemetery."
But there were prescribed ways of doing things, if you belonged to
the small ruling clique. In church the officers sat apart, occupying
RETTA
143
special pews in the nave. In the small theater they had boxes. Now
Mrs. Wiley beckoned to the sergeant. He escorted Mrs. Langworthy
and Hortense back to the edge of the cemetery, and made a place
for them close to the side of the road.
Guards had cleared all the spectators off the road itself. It was
exactly ten o'clock: time for the parade to leave the center of the
grounds. Mrs. Langworthy closed her parasol and leaned on it; there
was shade here, doubly welcome after the glare back yonder where
all the trees had been cleared away to make room for graves.
Strains of music began to be borne toward her on the May wind;
the full Soldiers' Home band had been turned out to-day, and they
were playing Chopin's funeral march. Presently the band came into
view around the turn in the road; they were preceded by the Lieu-
tenant of the Guard and the color squad. Behind the band came the
officers of the Home. Colonel Langworthy, conspicuous by not being
in uniform, marched at Governor Wiley's left.
Sam still remembered how to march. And why wouldn't he?
There wasn't one of them who had forgotten. Eight abreast, filling
the narrow road from gutter to gutter, one company after another
swung into view. They marched, not stragglingly and apologetically
like civilians, nor smartly and self-consciously like cadets on parade,
but with the long swinging step that they had learned when they
did their twenty and thirty miles a day. Many of them turned out
now only twice a year, for Decoration Day and for an annual in-
spection; but they wouldn't have improved perceptibly if they had
drilled every day. The march was in their blood.
Sam seemed a little strange to her with that solemn set look on
his face. This meant so much to him. It meant something she couldn't
understand, let alone sympathize with. He jeered at the idea of
living the rest of a man's life on the strength of the service he had
given in his youth. But how could he ever forget those four years?
His was a record to be proud of: enlisting as a private at Lin-
coln's first call for volunteers, and mustered out as a field officer
after the War was over. Retta hadn't known him at all in those days.
It was a piece of his life that was already a bygone when she first
met him. But was it also a piece of his life from which she must
be forever excluded?
The exclusion had been her own choice. Sam hadn't wanted to
144
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
put himself forth as a hero. Was that any reason why she should
have carelessly brushed the whole experience aside? When they were
children Bess and Hortense used to ask him questions about the
War. He had been brusque, jovial, offhand on the whole affair. He
had taken pains to shatter the myth of military glory for them. If
Retta had asked those same questions years before, would he have
felt compelled to teach the children that there was nothing in it?
The last of the marchers had been added to the ranks drawn up
in a hollow square around the monument. At the end of the parade
came the five-seated buckboard and a long open bus filled with men
from the Home hospital: cripples and sick men, who couldn't march,
but who were well enough to share the glory of the day. The firing-
squad was close to the speakers' stand by this time, and the buglers.
The sergeant who was escorting her touched Retta on the arm.
She and Hortense were led back through the crowd, which was now
shifting toward a new center of attention.
She scuttled to the place that was being kept for her between Bess
and Hortense. The Protestant chaplain stepped to the edge of the
speakers' platform and opened his prayer-book. Retta closed her eyes
and bent her head. The scene was swimming around her.
The chaplain prayed too long, of course. Still with her head bent,
Retta managed side-glances to see how her nearest neighbors were
taking it.
Bess was the picture of decorum, and was undoubtedly thinking
about anything rather than the matter in hand. This meant nothing
to her except as a family ceremony, one of those things which you
went through to please Papa and Mamma, just as you endured a
family dinner on Thanksgiving and a family opening of gifts at
Christmas.
Amy looked a little paler than usual. Amy was the sort of spinster
who kept lost causes alive if she were given half a chance. The
great Lost Cause, however, had passed Amy by: Papa hadn't been
a Johnny Reb really. And on the other hand Retta had seen to it
that neither Amy nor any one else under her roof should have an
inkling that sometimes there are causes gained.
But are there ever? In the nature of things, is not every cause
sooner or later a lost cause? Retta turned farther, to look at the rows
of bared heads in the solid blue ranks. With the best of life behind
RETTA
145
them, these men were all here in the Home to drag along somehow
until they died; and to-day they had been marshaled forth in sol-
emn ceremony to remind them, "Your turn next, old man! Your
turn next!"
The prayer ended at last, and Retta tried to get back to her hard
every-day common sense. They weren't such very old men, really.
It was over thirty years since the War ended, to be sure. But with
care these men had another twenty years ahead of them; and care
was what they were getting here in the Home. Not sentimental
doom, but good care. Good quarters, good food, medical attention to
their slightest ailments. Their very uniform and discipline were a
sop to their pride: this so clearly wasn't a charitable institution, it
was a military organization.
Granted that they didn't get much easy sympathy-well, they
probably weren't accustomed to that, anyhow. They were not a very
sentimental lot of men: she'd heard Sam say more than once that
the reason this National Home was crowded while the State Home
reported itself only half full was that married men were allowed to
take their wives with them into the State Home. They preferred to
break the tie and come here.
Perhaps they weren't wildly happy. Who is, in this workaday
world? They were contented enough. And if facile sympathy was
in the least dear to them, on Decoration Day they generally had a
chance to hear enough to carry them through until next year.
Not to-day, however. When Sam rose to speak he was husky;
and he began rather falteringly about the occasion which they were
here to mark. They were joining the rest of the country in a tribute
to their dead comrades. The united country was itself the work of
those who lay here and on distant battle-fields, and of the living
soldiers for whom this was a day apart.
His voice cleared as he went on, and in his earnestness he forgot
to be embarrassed. Perhaps he wasn't saying a thing beyond what
other speakers were saying this very moment at "exercises" all over
the country. The difference was that he meant every word he said.
There was nothing perfunctory here; he was speaking from the full-
ness of experience and emotion.
Did his hearers realize that all this came from depths within him-
self? Most of the civilian crowd were out of range of his voice, and
146
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
were waiting with what patience they might for the parade to re-
form. But the blue-coated marchers to whom this should mean so
much-how were they listening?
She turned to look. There in the front rank stood the old man
who had made her so much trouble about her violet plants. He
didn't see her. He wasn't even looking at Sam. (Sam had been kind
to him, though. Surely he could forget the hasty things she had
said to him, and remember only that Sam had been kind.)
The old man- He was an old man. A forlorn, forgotten old man,
cared for by strangers as a matter of routine, here in the hands of
strangers until he should die. By this time the strangers must be all
too familiar to him; but right now they were outside his conscious-
ness. He was looking far away beyond them and beyond the idle
crowd of spectators, looking back into the past that he had shared
with so many, yet in a sense could share with no one. He was dream-
ing of the hard days that were now luckily gone forever. But mustn't
it seem to him that they were great days, too?
Sam paused and dropped his voice a little. His next sentence came
piercing clear, in a somewhat different strain.
"We gave them back a united country. The deed is done. It will
not require doing a second time. Our country is now united so se-
curely that the result is half lost in oblivion. Those of our fellow-
citizens who have come up since the War can scarcely believe that
the very existence of our nation was ever seriously threatened.
"Perhaps it is better for them to go on and forget. Perhaps it is
our misfortune that we cannot help remembering. But to-day at
least, it is fitting that we should look back upon our deed."
He was rounding now toward his conclusion. No one had stirred
or looked uncomfortable for that minute or two when he had seemed
to welcome oblivion. But for Retta those words were like a stab in
the heart.
You meant me, Sam. Whether or not you knew it, you meant me.
I couldn't be troubled to go back into the ashes of a past you knew
before you met me: it was none of my concern that somewhere
under those ashes might linger a spark which could be blown into
new flame to light our joint path. I've taken our life together too
easily. I know that now. Do you know it, Sam?
She sat with her gloved hands folded in her lap: the wife of the
RETTA
147
speaker, the picture of matronly pride and decorum. She would will-
ingly have died this minute, if by her death she could have given
him back what she had all unwittingly filched from him. Could she
make it up to him now, in the little time that still remained to them?
Nothing in this world can ever be made up. Her own hard common
sense rang the knell in her ears: what is gone, is gone forever.
VI
OLONEL LANGWORTHY had finished and stepped back.
The Catholic chaplain was advancing now to give the Bene-
CO
diction.
"The Colonel was good," Artie breathed on the back of Mrs.
Langworthy's neck. "Short, too. The whole business didn't take him
twelve minutes."
Under cover of the Benediction Mrs. Langworthy turned to glare
at Artie. If he felt compelled to time his brother-in-law's utterances,
he might at least have kept the results to himself. Twelve minutes?
She had lived through thirty years in that time; and it was a thirty
years which she now wished back in her hands to live over, and to
live acutely this time, and unselfishly.
The firing-squad were answering the sharp word of command
now. "Load! Ready! Aim! Fire!" Then twice more, "Load! Aim!
Fire!" Their three volleys were followed by "Taps"; two buglers
blew the final call. Then there was a second's deep silence.
And after that, of course, it was time for the quickstep. Behind
the select benches the parade was re-forming. Mrs. Wiley turned to
shake Mrs. Langworthy's hand, and to repeat in substance what
Artie had just said: the Colonel was good.
"We expect you all at our house for lunch," Mrs. Wiley went on.
"Just a simple lap meal. We expect Senator Roberts to join us. He's
there on the stand next to my husband. Major Atchison was to have
been here, too. He's our Local Manager, you know, and a member
of the Board. But he was detained at home."
They were gathering, the local great and near-great. They were
gathering to greet Sam, and to greet him in a special sense and with
a special purpose. Sam had just made his own triumphant intro-
duction to the veterans. This little interested circle attached a par-
148
RETTA
149
ticular meaning to these proceedings. This was to have been the
opening day of Sam's entry on the business of being Governor of
the Home.
Well, Sam would have harsh news for them to-day. His decision
had been overruled; and he wouldn't even be free to let the powers-
that-be know exactly what had happened. He would have to cover
it up, as he had been covering so many things for so many years.
Generally perhaps he succeeded in fooling himself. But to-day for a
little while he had faced the bitter truth. His life was not his own,
and it was often at cross-purposes with the lives to which it was
supposed to be united.
"If you'll just wait until the road is clear, the carriages can come
over here for us," Mrs. Wiley was saying. She had to make all such
arrangements for official company, of course. She'd been doing it
for years. Doubtless she had often been bored, and her private plans
upset, by Managers and inspectors and junketing politicians. Had it
ever occurred to her, though, that she was moving in step with some-
thing a little larger than herself?
No, it hadn't, or she wouldn't be on the point of leaving here now.
She wanted to marry off her fat, kind-hearted, stupid daughter; and
then she wanted to go off somewhere and live quietly with the
General, just as they had lived when they were first married. "If
Julia ever leaves us-" she was in the habit of saying, barely able
to keep out of her tone the eagerness with which she waited for
Julia to leave her, for life to quiet down, for old age to complete
the circle and join onto selfish young happiness.
"The carriages won't be here for some time," said Mrs. Langworthy
to Hortense. "You girls run along toward the Governor's quarters.
It will be fun for you to watch the crowds. Perhaps Julia will go
with you, so that you won't get lost on the way."
Dora joined the young people. Amy sat quiet, satisfied as usual
to be disposed of. Mrs. Langworthy resolutely screwed her attention
to what Mrs. Wiley was saying. Mrs. Langworthy knew from her
own experience what it was to fish up topics; any hostess knew
that. But at least her guests were of her own choosing.
Mrs. Wiley, on the other hand, had spent the last ten years work-
ing hard at her husband's job. Only think of all the yards and yards
of "made" conversation that she had reeled off since she first came
150
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
here as Governor's lady! Surely she could be excused if she was
relieved-infinitely relieved that the end was now approaching!
Surely Retta Langworthy ought to be congratulating herself this
minute on her own instinctive good sense: without hesitation she
had decided that she would not succeed to the tedium and the arti-
ficiality-you might almost say the hypocrisy-inseparable from any
"official life."
But Retta wasn't happy in her own wisdom; she wasn't easy in
her mind. The storm of emotion which she had experienced that
morning had brought about a sharp reaction now. She was listless
and depressed. She wanted to be comforted.
In the dining-room at the Governor's quarters, while lunch was
being served, she tried to edge up to Sam. But he was surrounded
by all the officers of the Home. Even the young Assistant Surgeon
whom Julia Wiley was supposed to favor was there; they were all
putting in a good word with the Colonel. The rumor had undoubt-
edly got around that he would be the next Governor. His subordi-
nates were already currying favor. This was another sample of what
Retta had luckily escaped.
She couldn't even catch Sam's eye; and she got tired of listening
to Mrs. Wiley and trying to talk to Julia. Her attention wandered
to the house.
It was a good house, and sizable. The grounds, as she had noted
on her way in, were better cared for than Retta's own in Mount
Royal. There was a downstairs bedroom and bath, too; and Retta
hated stairs. But that was all settled. Why did she have to go peck-
ing uneasily away at the situation when it was all settled, and set-
tled to suit her?
The party had scattered after luncheon; the men were smoking
out on the lawn, the older ladies sat on the veranda. Bess and Hor-
tense and Julia were back in the parlor, grouped around the piano
with the young doctors. After the morning's solemnity and strain,
the present hour seemed leisured, endless, golden. Summer after-
noons were what they lived for, this little group of officers and
officers' wives set down here to make the best of one another.
It was cooler here all summer than in Mount Royal. When there
was no official company, life was slower-yes, and much easier. The
Governor left his office at four every afternoon. Of course all the
RETTA
151
time he was on the Home grounds he was actually on duty. But
his duties and his powers were both defined. This wasn't like being
in business and having to go out and create your own undertaking
at your own risk.
Oh, what difference did it make to Retta that the drawbacks were
balanced by advantages? Or, on the other hand, what did it matter
that the drawbacks were the kind which naturally irked her? Sam
had wanted to come out here: that was the important thing. He
had wanted to come, and she had refused to let him. She had re-
fused to leave her own house, and her little circle of intimates, and
the round of duties and pleasures which she had gradually built up
for herself in Mount Royal.
She didn't like old men. But she hadn't been asked to deal with
the old men. She had sometimes boasted that she could be happy
anywhere with her family and a garden and her needlework. For
all her boasts, she had sunk into a rut so deep that for years she
had scarcely glanced over its edges.
The big flag on a tall staff in the center of the Governor's lawn
had waved in the breeze that morning at half-mast. Raised to the
top of the staff at noon, it now hung limp in the afternoon calm.
The band-concert was beginning; the Governor's family always lis-
tened from their own lawn, and to-day they could hear perfectly.
It was only the end of May, but summer was in sight. Out here they
lived for their summers.
The young party around the piano found their music interfered
with by the band-concert. They passed through the veranda on their
way to the lawn. Mrs. Langworthy stopped Bess.
"Ask your father to come here a minute, will you? Just for a min-
ute. I want to speak to him."
She followed Bess down the veranda steps and stood waiting. The
Colonel excused himself and joined her. Doubtless he thought she
was going to give the word for starting home. Mrs. Langworthy had
only to give that word, and her life would go on just as it had been
going.
But over the edge of her rut she had to-day caught a glimpse of
Sam: Sam whom she knew so well that she could often tell what
he was going to say next before he uttered a word of it, Sam who
had lived alongside her for so many years that the distant days
152
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
before she knew him now seemed like some one else's life, not her
own at all. And that one glimpse had revealed Sam to her as a
stranger: a man cut off by a high barrier largely of her raising, a
man whose secret life she not only did not share, but until to-day
had never suspected.
"Did you tell Jason-?" he was beginning as he approached her.
Yes, he thought she wanted to leave. He probably wanted to stay
a little longer; that was a common passage-at-arms between them.
Let him go on, and sink back into the old easy imperfection; or
interrupt him, and keep peace with herself. Right now Retta might
have chosen the easy and accustomed way and doubtless have found
a thousand good reasons afterward for justifying her own indolence
-she might have let him go on, if the queer awakening of her
senses hadn't interfered. She looked at him now, not casually as she
looked twenty times a day, but shrewdly, as if she were seeing him
for the first time.
And she did see him-saw him acutely-for the first time in years.
He was tired from his morning's exertion; he was carrying his hat
in his hand. The bright sunshine of May showed the fine lines under
his eyes, the deep folds of his jowl. For the first time he looked to
her like an old man.
"Never mind Jason," she said crisply. "Sam, have you told the
people here anything about your plans for the future?"
He flushed a little, and hesitated. "I didn't think I had to. You
see, the actual offer hasn't been made to me. It won't be for another
month. I thought I could deal with it when it came."
"That's all right, then." Retta drew a long breath. "When the
offer does come, Sam, take it."
Obviously he didn't believe his ears. "Take it? But Retta, you
said only the other day-"
"That was the other day. Since then I've changed my mind. I
didn't realize that you actually wanted to come out here. I thought
it was just a-a notion. I know better now."
He looked faintly quizzical. "I'm glad you're square with your
conscience, Mrs. Langworthy. I won't take advantage of your rash
offer. Thank you just the same for making it. I'm glad you did."
"But Sam, I mean it! If you don't take the Governorship now, I'll
RETTA
153
never forgive myself. I won't forgive you, either. Oh, can't you see
anything as plain as this, Sam Langworthy? I mean it!"
He was still bewildered. "What on earth made you change your
mind?"
"It was your speech, Sam. Your maiden effort. You'd better let
your first speech be your last, if you know what's good for you.
You'll never equal that again."
"Oh, my speech! My speech couldn't have had anything to do
with it. Not much, anyhow. I suppose it was the emotion of the
day. But this is only one day in the year. To-morrow you'll feel quite
different."
To-morrow she might lapse a little. Later on she might even re-
gret her impulsive decision. Sooner or later a person regretted every-
thing. But only a very young person considered her own regrets
important.
"I'll talk to you to-morrow," she said smiling. "You'll see!"
Past his shoulder her eye caught a level patch of lawn between
paths: a patch that would make an ideal site for a flower-garden.
There was a lot of help available on these grounds, such as it was.
It would be great fun to begin again from the beginning, and have
a proper flower-garden this time, after her long experience with a
conglomeration.
She could make the place blossom as Mrs. Wiley had never made
it. The house, too; a lot could be done with the house. New plans
and a new life: she was equal to them. So many women her age,
and thirty years married, took their circumstances and their husbands
calmly for granted. She herself had come altogether too near to doing
just that.
The oppression at her heart was gone now. She looked forward
with eagerness to the change that she had lately refused even to
consider. She would have liked to move out here to-morrow and
begin on her new life.
That very word "to-morrow" must sound to Sam like a fatal
postponement. Well, she'd show him whether she meant what she
said. To-morrow she would begin to practise in private, so that she
could say it coolly when the time came: she would begin to practise
alluding to "Governor Langworthy."

VII
HEIR bedroom had been the scene of long domestic narra-
tives, when Retta recounted the misdemeanors of the house-
man or the goings-on of Artie, and the Colonel promised to see
what he could do about it. There, too, he was wont to consult her
about the things that lay manifestly within her province: "Retta, do
you know what I did with my piqué vests last fall?" "Retta, can't
you make Bess-?" "Retta, how would it be if we moved the peonies
farther back in the lawn, and set out a row of bushes in their place?"
But now for the first time the room was the stage of a long-drawn
stubborn battle. The Colonel was not only strongly attracted by the
idea of going to the Soldiers' Home as Governor: the appointment
would solve difficulties which Mrs. Langworthy scarcely suspected.
But he felt that in consenting to go along she was sacrificing herself
to his wishes; and he hesitated to accept her sacrifice.
"I've never had any idea in life except to do what was best for
my little family," he argued. "It would be interesting to see what
I could make of the Home. I like the men, and I think I could make
them like me. But it's too big a change for the rest of you."
Quite consistently Retta held to her impulsive decision. It was
strange to find herself on this side of the argument. She had been
consulted about some of Sam's other undertakings; but then it was
always the Colonel who wanted to go ahead anyhow. This time he
was the one who argued against change, and she urged it.
She hated the idea of pulling up stakes here. Sam would never
know how deeply she hated it. Indeed, in her low moods she felt
that it would be easier and more satisfactory to be carried out of
here feet foremost than to demolish the household which after all
had been her life-work.
But she had read the writing on the wall; and under her indolence
154
RETTA
155
and easy compromises, she felt an imperious necessity for keeping
on good terms with herself when the matter was so important. Sam
had never grudged her anything she could possibly have. For Bess
he would allow himself to be ground in small pieces. That might
all go by the book; a man was supposed to look out for his wife and
child. But Sam had also for many years kept a roof over the heads
of Retta's relatives. That, too, had passed with him for a matter of
course. If the Millses hadn't come here to live, what would have
become of them?
At the bottom of her heart Retta felt that if the Millses hadn't been
able to take refuge here, they would have made out somehow. Oh,
she liked having them here! But Sam had a sense of responsibility so
keen that he was inclined to dull that faculty in other people. It was
high time that the rest of them turned around now and began to
consider what would be best for Sam.
He viewed "the rest of them" as possible allies. Right now he
would have called a family council when he felt his resolution weak-
ening before Retta. But she would have none of that. She insisted
that the whole matter be kept a secret until it was definitely settled.
The subject must never be mentioned downstairs.
Things ran on in the family with no idea of impending change.
Dora had insisted on having her say about the new library books
that were to be bought with the money she had raised; this month
her time-tables were laid aside in favor of publishers' catalogues.
Amy spent hours every day over the sweet peas, which were par-
ticularly lovely this year. Artie had drained the pond with an idea of
improving the dam.
Hortense was now going around less with Bess's young crowd:
they were too young for her, and far too silly. She had wearied also
of her oil painting in the library; she was now much engaged with
water-colors in her room. Her pictures had a certain pale charm;
but she spent too many hours shut up there alone. Viewing Dora
and Amy, Mrs. Langworthy often felt that there was a great deal
to be said for the spinster life. But Dora and Amy were the finished
product. An old maid in the making was not a cheerful sight; and
although Hortense at twenty-nine was still the handsomest person
under that roof, Mrs. Langworthy was beginning more and more to
fear that Hortense was headed for the single life.
156
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
That was one reason the more, really, why Sam ought to go out
to the Soldiers' Home. A business man often saw his business
dwindle as he grew older; but as Governor of the Home Colonel
Langworthy could stay on, with a house and a salary, until he died.
Of course he ought not to take up the governorship just for the
sake of the Millses. But it all seemed to work in very nicely to-
gether. Dora and Amy could take a room in a boarding-house for a
few months next winter, if they actually found the Soldiers' Home
impossible as a steady thing.
This house could be sold or rented. With whatever he owned
turned into cash, the Colonel would have a neat sum above his
salary. It was clearly best to make the change. Only just at first the
idea of such a complete upset was a little hard to get used to.
The administrative year at the Soldiers' Home would end the last
day of June. On the twentieth of the month the Colonel came home
in the middle of the afternoon and found Mrs. Langworthy up in
their bedroom.
"It's here," he said solemnly. "A letter making the formal offer of
the appointment; it came by the afternoon mail."
"Then that's all settled." She straightened up from the bed, where
she had just dumped the contents of a chiffonier drawer. "I thought
we'd have a little more time. I wanted to get some clearing up done
before I had to start in packing."
"You can have all the time you need. The Wileys will leave the
first of July. But I can live out at the Home until you're ready to
move. Or perhaps the Treasurer can be left in charge for a month

"That's right. You need to clear up a lot of things, too, don't you?"
Mrs. Langworthy sank on the edge of the bed and fanned herself.
"If you decide to sell out your business and dispose of the factory,

"I can manage somehow. This won't be the first time that I've ever
run two jobs. But Retta, are you quite sure you want to make the
change? It isn't too late yet to back out."
"Are we going to have that all over again?" demanded Mrs.

"There are so many people to be considered. Retta, have you-
have you said anything to Bess about this?"

"Certainly not. Bess would tell Dora, and Dora couldn't keep it

Down in the library she sank into a chair beside his desk and

He handed over the letter of appointment. She sat studying it

Looking up at her, he grinned. "As easy as that?" she asked.

They had actually started down the drive when she heard her

hung. Hortense sat alone in the trap, holding the reins.
"Isn't Bess coming out?" she demanded. "We haven't finished
our drive."
Jason pulled up, and Mrs. Langworthy called back, "She's in the
house talking to her father."
"She asked me to wait," said Hortense. "She had picked up some
news that burned a hole in her pocket. But I don't see why it should
take her this long to tell."
"That there Mac don't stand the way the old hosses do," muttered
Jason. "Miss 'Tense better drive him around a bit 'stead o' fretting
him the way she's doing."
"Drive into the stable and tie the horse there," Mrs. Langworthy
called to Hortense. "Jason, is the stable-door open?"
"Course it ain't. Colonel's told me time and again never to leave
the stable-door open. He hates the looks of it."
"Well, you'll have to go help Miss Hortense. While you're about
it you may as well take a little more time and unharness Mac. Find
out first though if Miss Bess has changed her mind about wanting
him again. You'll find her in the library with the Colonel."
Jason tied his team and went to the bridle of Hortense's horse. He
was much better with the horses than around the yard. Mrs. Lang-
RETTA
161
worthy wondered whether they could take him with them when they
moved out to the Soldiers' Home. The officers' families all had old
soldiers for drivers; but if the Langworthys let Jason go, that would
mean parting with Louise, too.
"I shall hate breaking in all new servants," thought Mrs. Lang-
worthy. "I wonder whether Ida could manage with the cooking. I'm
afraid not."
She turned in her seat to look at the side yard. This past spring
Sam had made one more effort to get it organized into a planned
garden. The eastern third didn't look so bad now; she felt en-
couraged.
Then with a sinking of the heart she realized that she wouldn't
be here to see the job finished. Haphazard as her flower-garden was,
she loved it. How long would it be before the flower-garden at the.
Governor's quarters ceased to seem to her like "Mrs. Wiley's gar-
den"? Would she ever, indeed, get the feeling that a garden on
Government land belonged to her in any real sense? Her possession
of it would never be disturbed, of course, so long as Sam remained
in office. Perhaps after the first wrench was over she would get used
to living in a house that went with the job. But for a time "quarters"
were going to seem very strange, after all these years of ownership.
She glanced down at the fateful letter in her gloved hand. Right
now she wished it didn't have to go. The sharp break with her past
made her realize what a good life hers had been. A happy marriage,
a circle of congenial friends, health, comfort: she had had every-
thing.
The change was her own choice. She didn't regret making it. She
was taking her loved ones with her. In a sense she would enjoy
showing people-showing everybody, herself included that she
could build afresh under different circumstances.
She could build afresh, and better. Too often in the past she had
simply drifted on from day to day, not accomplishing half the things
that she really wanted to undertake. She ought to welcome the chal-
lenge. This change would be a splendid new beginning. Only she
wished that right here and now it didn't seem so frightfully like
dying.
She would be glad when this afternoon was over. That is, if it
ever was over. Jason had been gone a long time. It was hot sitting
162
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
here in the stationary landau. When she was in a carriage, she
wanted to ride.
She would have sent Hortense to see what was keeping Jason, but
Hortense had disappeared, too. Amy was upstairs lying down, and
Dora had gone for one of the long country walks which she often

household you never could find anybody if you really wanted some

have to caution him against being too sociable with the old soldiers.
Mrs. Langworthy hated unnecessary motion. But rising temper
was getting the better of her inertia. She had actually put out a hand
to brace herself for the descent from the landau when she saw Jason
returning at last.
She settled back in her seat. But instead of untying the horses,
Jason came up to the side of the carriage. "Colonel says for you to
come in the house a minute, please."
This afternoon never would end. Should she send Jason to mail

on mailing it herself. She had wanted to make the act a sort of
symbol of how completely she had gone over to Sam's side. But
there was no such hurry about it, either.
Of course Sam had got in deeper than he intended with Bess. His
attempts at discipline or even enlightenment were all too likely to
end in an appeal for help. "Can't you make Bess-?" How often
had she heard that in the last twenty years!
"You wait right here, Jason," she said. "I don't expect to be long."
The house was cool after the graveled drive. A nice quiet place:
the rooms to right and left looked peaceful. But the library was the
scene of tension. Bess, standing in the middle of the room, was an
image of hurt feelings and wounded pride. The Colonel, hot, anx-

"It's all right, I tell you," she was insisting when her mother en-

You're my father, and I have to accept your decision. But you're
asking a good deal when you ask me to like it."
"That's all settled, then," said Mrs. Langworthy briskly. "Perhaps
RETTA
163
we should have told you sooner, Bess. But the delay has been my

"It wouldn't make any difference when you told me. I should

hadn't taken your happiness into account. Maybe we can manage
