"Yes, I suppose I can get away sometimes, even from the Soldiers'
Home. I'll have to get away sometimes. I can see that already."
"It will be a great change for all of us," said Mrs. Langworthy
coolly. "You might stop and consider that for a minute."
"A great change for all-?" Bess started to echo the words with
rising fury. But before they were half out the meaning penetrated.
Her eyes widened, and a quick surprised flush mantled her cheeks.
Up to now she had been too angry to stop and consider that any one
except herself was involved in the situation. The realization bewil-
dered her.
"Why, Mamma!" she stammered. "You're losing your home, too,
aren't you? Your own dear home, where we've all been so happy.
How can you bear that? Oh, how can you?"
Mrs. Langworthy bit her tongue to keep from crying aloud. A
164
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
moment ago Bess had seemed to her simply a stubborn child who
ought to be sent to bed without her supper. Now here was her own
darling taking her part. Oh, but taking her part against herself! This
mustn't go on.
"I can't endure the thought that I'm taking your home away from
you," the Colonel was saying slowly.
"You're not," Retta tried to say. Through her dry lips the words
came unintelligibly. She clenched her hands in their hot tight gloves,
and tried again.
Suddenly Bess's lips began to quiver. Shaking with sobs, she turned
to run from the room.
But the Colonel was ahead of her. He seized her and drew her
down on his knee in a big chair. There he sat comforting her as if
she were a three-year-old. Whatever Retta had managed to say to
him at that moment he wouldn't have heard.
He turned to glance at her, however, when Bess's sobs quieted.
Over the tired young head that rested on his shoulder his eyes met
Mrs. Langworthy's in a long solemn look. Then the hand that lay
on Bess's waist moved slightly. He was pointing to the envelop in
Mrs. Langworthy's lap.
She lifted it, and looked the question that she knew he didn't want
her to put into words. He nodded. Often and often had they tele-
graphed that way when Bess was little.
Mrs. Langworthy would have given a good deal that moment for
the courage to pretend that she did not understand. She understood
very well. The renunciation which had cost her so much was being
hurled back in her teeth.
Slowly she tore the letter across. The tough paper resisted her. She
had to look down at her own hands when she tore each half. She
did not look up again after she had crossed the room and dropped
the fragments into the waste-basket.
Men often did things for their daughters that they wouldn't do
for their wives. Mrs. Langworthy had lived long enough to see that
happen over and over. She hadn't actually wanted to move out to the
Soldiers' Home anyhow, sternly as she had steeled herself to the
resolve. If this had happened in a different way, she would have
greeted the about-face as a full pardon on the very steps of the
scaffold.
RETTA
165
Later on common sense would come to her rescue. She would
never breathe a word of her secret rebellion to Sam, either.
But at this moment she hated him. The warmth and strength of
affection which she liked to feel always about her-the atmosphere
which was so native to her that she breathed it constantly whether
she thought about it or not the feeling of being cherished and
important-had all deserted her now. She was alone in the cold. And
it was those two, who should have been closer to her than anybody
-it was those two who had excluded her.

TORTENSE GIBSON had passed her seventh birthday, and
had been going to school for several months, before she ever
wakened to the realization that other little girls were cared for by
parents, not by aunts.
Other little girls, carefully selected, sometimes came by arrange-
ment to play with her. Small Hortense, conducted by an aunt or by
a darky servant, went in turn to play with her little friends at their
houses.
But all this was by grown-up contriving. Hortense's verdict was,
"It's nicer at our own house." And it was nicest at her own house
when she didn't have some small Elsie or Mildred there to be
allowed the use of her best doll and to be given the choice whether
they would play house or tag.
Hortense's favorite playmate was Dora. Auntie Dora could do
most fascinating things with colored paper or with wet sand. She
and Hortense acted out stories: "Cinderella" and "Aladdin" and
"Bluebeard." "Bluebeard" was Hortense's special pet, largely on ac-
count of the tense scene in which Dora stood over her booming in
a strange bass, "Say your prayers, Fatima, say your prayers," and
Hortense, filled with delicious terror, would squeak, "Just a minute!
I'm saying 'em. Sister Anne, sister Anne, do you see anybody com-
ing?"
They let Amy act with them sometimes, when they needed an
extra character. "But we better let Aunt Amy be the Princess Bal-
roubadour," Hortense would say with childish frankness, "because
the Princess hasn't so much to do, and Aunt Amy really isn't very
good."
Aunt Amy was the one you turned to when you bruised a knee
or somebody had hurt your feelings. Aunt Amy had the gentlest
166
HORTENSE
167
hands in the world, and the softest voice. Her lap was a refuge in
time of trouble. Aunt Amy knew all about animals, too; and she
was the one who always thought that another cup-cake wouldn't
hurt a little girl-and who selected for Hortense the cake with the
thickest frosting, which good manners would have forbidden Hor-
tense to snatch for herself, because you must always leave the best
for other people.
Uncle Artie and Grandpapa filled smaller parts in her life, but
they rounded out her circle. "Mamma" meant only the picture of a
beautiful young woman with flowers in her hair.
Her other parent was mentioned to her for the first time after she
learned to read. Then Aunt Amy had taken her to a tree worked
in faded embroidery which hung on the wall in Grandpapa's library,
and had pointed to her own name on one of the lower branches.
"There you are, Hortense, you see. This is your mother's name.
That was your father."
"Alec Gibson," a name on a silken background. "That was your
father." She knew her mother was dead. Hearing now about her
father, she asked, "Is he dead, too?"
"No," said Aunt Amy.
It was years before Hortense felt any farther stirring of curiosity
about her father. Indeed, the little scene, which Amy had approached
with a sinking heart, failed to interest Hortense for more than a
moment. Reading her own name so carefully embroidered was nice;
but her attention that afternoon was centered on a toy piano with
which Uncle Artie had just presented her. Its tinkling tones were
not especially grateful to her ear; she had drummed too often on
the grown-up piano to be satisfied with a single octave, and that
octave tinny. But the toy piano was provided with a key, and the
key worked. Hortense spent hours of bliss locking and unlocking her
instrument.
Her small circle was broken when Aunt Amy went West to visit
Aunt Retta, and didn't come back. Hortense missed her acutely at
first; but Aunt Dora took Hortense into her room to sleep while Amy
was away, and that was lots of fun. Aunt Amy was fussy about having
her things touched; but Aunt Dora knew that little girls liked to
rummage. She would allow Hortense to spend a whole rainy Saturday
168
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
turning over the contents of grown-up drawers, with the promise
of course to put everything back as she found it. But when the hour
of putting back came, Aunt Dora generally joined in the straightening.
She realized that little girls mean to do what they say they will, but
they sometimes don't remember.
So it was like the end of the world when Grandpapa died and the
house had to be sold. Hortense was sent to stay with Elsie's mamma
at the time of Grandpapa's funeral; and on her return she sensed the
strain and uncertainty which had come over everything. Uncle Artie
talked more than ever, and Aunt Dora didn't talk at all. And then one
day Uncle Artie told her they were all going West to live; and Auntie
Dora, who never cried when she cut her finger or even when one
of the puppies died-Auntie Dora burst into tears and ran out of the
room.
Packing things up was fun, though; and they gave her her choice
of which puppy she would take with her. Hortense was wildly ex-
cited during the long train ride. She was thrilled to get to the new
city where she was to live: a city so much larger than the sleepy
Southern town where she had lived up to now. Uncle Artie promised
her that she could have a ride on the horse-car every day for a week
if she'd be a good girl; and Hortense went to bed that night perfectly
contented.
But she waked in the night and couldn't remember where she was.
Her frightened screams brought Dora at once; but even Aunt Dora
didn't look right to her somehow. Hortense cried herself to sleep; and
when she woke in the morning, she woke to a dragging misery and
loneliness that failed to pass with the passing of time.
"I don't see how such a small child can be so homesick," said her
Western Aunt Retta, both worried and irritated.
Her new uncle, whom they all called "Colonel," tried to distract
her by showing her the baby: her little Cousin Bess, who was going
to grow up and play with Hortense, and love her very, very much,
because they were cousins. Uncle Artie took her for the promised rides
on the horse-car, though Hortense herself wasn't sure that she was
being good enough to earn them. Aunt Amy admired the puppy,
and tried to interest Hortense in the problem of getting the puppy to
live on friendly terms with the two dogs and the big cat who already
lived in the house.
HORTENSE
169
But Hortense was interested in nothing. She grew hollow-eyed and
wan. She couldn't eat; she didn't feel like playing. At night she
would lie in her strange bed and think, "If I just wish hard enough-
if I just wish hard enough, maybe to-morrow I'll wake up and find
myself back home."
"It will wear off, if we're only patient with the child," said the
Colonel. "You have to be very patient with children."
But at the end of a month, which had seemed to Hortense like ages
of wretchedness, her homesickness hadn't worn off; it was worse if
anything. "She'll make herself sick, if she keeps on this way," said
Aunt Amy in deep distress.
"She is sick. And I can't think what more we can do for her," said
Uncle Artie.
"We'd better send for the doctor. Perhaps he can do something,"
said the Colonel.
But Dora's lips tightened. "We'll send for no doctor," she said. "I'm
sick of such foolishness."
She had spanked Hortense once for telling a lie, and once for being
saucy to Grandpapa. This made the third time. Dora took off one
of the tiny slippers which were among the things that made Hortense
regard her as half a little girl herself. For an aunt who had had so
little practice, she spanked thoroughly.
Artie said he had never heard of anything so cruel; and Amy
couldn't eat for a day afterward. In later years Dora herself had
apologized to Hortense for that spanking.
But the queer thing was that it worked. After the punishment
Hortense had climbed into Dora's lap and sobbed herself to sleep;
but when she woke, she felt better. And Dora, who had been her
playmate and her doll up to now, but not especially dearer to her than
the rest of the circle, was henceforth established as Hortense's special
grown-up.
When Hortense stopped being homesick, she began to realize that
the household in Mount Royal was much nicer really than the first
home she remembered. There was no Grandpapa thumping with his
heavy cane and growling. The Colonel bought her a pony and taught
her to play dominoes. Aunt Retta, whom she had remembered only
vaguely, turned out to be a hearty person with a warm laugh, who
never bothered when little girls happened to step into the flower-
170
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
beds, and who always let you have some of her own best perfumery
on your handkerchief when you were dressed up for Sunday.
When Hortense grew older she realized that her Aunt Retta was
that rarest phenomenon in either sex, a person essentially just. Hor-
tense was a polite little girl, very much inclined to give up her own
things to other little girls. Especially she handed them over to her
tiny Cousin Bess.
But Aunt Retta taught Bess from the time she could toddle that
whenever Hortense handed her a plaything, she must return the favor
with one of her own toys. Bess was rough with her toys, and the loan
often proved embarrassing. She was rough with Hortense's toys, too.
Hortense would have let her smash them all to atoms, however,
sooner than raise a row about it.
For every toy that Bess had, Hortense had a toy to correspond; and
because she was older than Bess, her things were larger and cost more.
When Bess had a new dress or new shoes, Hortense likewise was
supplied. Because Bess always had a party on her birthday, Hortense
was awarded a birthday party, too. Being a nice little hostess was
one long-drawn agony for Hortense. But she knew the grown-ups
were being kind to her; and anyhow she couldn't see any way of
getting out of those parties.
But being a playmate for her little cousin was an arrangement that
never really worked well. Luckily as they grew older it naturally
fell into abeyance. Hortense was busy with school and music-lessons.
Then she was sent away for a year to be "finished" at the same school
where her aunts and her mother had gone before her. When she
came back she made her début; and after that the hours which were
not devoted to her own "good times" went to her music and her
painting.
There had been one season along in her teens when she developed
a romantic affection for her missing father. After all, in a sense she
was an "adopted child." It was only reasonable then for her to suppose
that some day her missing kin would appear to claim her. And of
course when he appeared he would come in splendor. Long-lost
fathers always turned out to be the king's son or something. She had
read about such things in the paper-covered novels which she bor-

always shoving Scott on her these days, and Fenimore Cooper. Dora's

own idea of romance at the age of thirteen had been lurking Indians

big Russian poodle, Hortense's first gift from an admirer, and the

When her true love came, of course she would marry him. She

future then would take care of itself.
The marriage which was to solve everything for her never connected
itself in her dreams with the marriages which she saw in practice

The first year that she was "out" Hortense had two proposals. The
second year she had two more. She kept track of them, just as she
saved all her dance-programs: dance-programs proudly full of
scribbled names. It all counted in the score which she supposed every
girl kept. But the proposals themselves Hortense rejected promptly
and almost scornfully.
"I don't love you," she would say. And although, once or twice,
dancing late at night or walking under a summer moon, she had felt
a warm sweet tremor which was at least akin to love, the full rapture
and intoxication had never been hers. She would know it very surely
when it came.
The third year after her début she had only one proposal. Since
then she had drawn blank. Oh, well, to be asked by the wrong man

might be a compliment, but it was also an annoyance! In this clear

Bess was still officially a school-girl, of course, even if she wasn't

She shaded the truth about her conquests. Let a boy who had hung

that other people were noticing his defection, she would flutter her
dark eyelashes and murmur, "Poor Jack!" She must always give the
idea that the youth had offered himself and been rejected. By that

Bess chirped a great deal at one time about Bill Jerome in New
York. But of course Bill had been simply a brother rung in to be nice

silent about Bill. He was older than she, and must have played
around with a good many girls from time to time; doubtless Bess had
amused him at first, but now the novelty had worn off.
The boys Hortense rejected had married other girls. The boys whom
Bess's suitors brought to the house were younger than Hortense; and
when Bess wasn't at home, they never came of their own accord to
call on Hortense. She wouldn't have welcomed them if they had
come. She wasn't interested in them personally, and she cared less and
less about racketing around simply to fill in the time.
But sometimes lately the sick realization had come over her that
she was building her life on a single chance. She was born to love

him one of these days. Of course she would! Only-when would she
meet him, and how, and where? She didn't meet many strangers in

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
If he didn't appear, what was to become of her? Hortense was

If anything should happen to the Colonel, Hortense and Amy and

from school, Hortense couldn't get the idea out of her head.
Along in the spring she painted some place-cards for a luncheon-
party Mrs. Palmer was giving, and Mrs. Palmer insisted on paying her
for them. At a quarter apiece, the dozen come to three dollars. Hor-
tense put the three dollars into new paints, and let it be known among
her friends that she was taking orders for "hand-painted" cards.
Sometimes she sold a dozen cards a week. Then she might not sell
any for a month. Once, and once only, she had three orders the same
week.
She took exquisite pains with her work; her place-cards were
always carried away from the table as souvenirs. It took her two days
to earn her three dollars; and except when she put it into new mate-
rial, she never knew what became of the money. But it was the first
she had ever earned; and the very fact of earning made her feel less
helpless.
To be helpless-to be forced to sit and wait until something was
HORTENSE
175
offered: that was the tragic part of being a woman. Until just lately,
however, Hortense had never seen any tragedy in being a woman.
The women in her family were so superior to the men! Then, too, they
took life so casually and amusingly.
Aunt Retta, of course, had things just the way she wanted them.
Luck was always with Aunt Retta. This summer, for instance, when
there was a chance that they might have to move out to the Soldiers'
Home, which Aunt Retta would have hated-why, the thing had
just blown by without her having to lift a finger.
Dora was happy enough, too, with her travel-books and her monkey-
tricks and her parties for the neighbors' children. (And such children
as she managed to dig up! Bess's face was always a study when she
brought company home and happened to encounter Dora's troop of
vagabonds.)
But Amy-Amy was always so quiet that Hortense had grown up
taking it for granted Amy was the most contented of the sisters. With
the sharpened vision which had come to her lately she saw that Amy
was actually wistful and unsatisfied, that under her deep reserve lay
longings of which Amy herself was perhaps scarcely conscious.
Like Hortense, Amy had never compromised. But there was in her
an arrested youth that hinted its own tragic story.
If you refused to take anything beneath your standard and ideal,
perhaps you were fated to end this way with nothing. Or not with
nothing exactly; Amy might have been worse off. But surely when
she was a girl she had not looked forward to a middle age of taking
care of everybody's pet animals and finishing the ambitious table-
covers and fire-screens that Retta began and then wearied of.
Hortense was growing sentimental toward the generation older than
hers. Perhaps the added sympathy there would help her a little if she
had to encounter what she was beginning to fear: if she actually
found herself shoved out of her girlhood, and yet love still lingered
on its way.
She would accept the worst, if she had to. Indeed, a practical com-
promise was at present beyond her reach: the only proposal which
had been made under that roof in the last five years was Dr. Simpson's
offer to Amy, which Amy had so hotly resented. If Doc Simpson had
asked Hortense instead of Amy-but then, he hadn't.
Hortense's pride was making her suffer these days. Yet it strength-
176
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
ened her, too, in a bitter but necessary resolve. If she had to pass from
Bess's class to "the aunts," she would do so without ever letting
anybody see what the transition cost her.
Bess's début reception might mark the line very definitely. The date
was set for late in November. Into the preparations for that reception
Hortense threw herself with much more than her usual eagerness.

II
OR the last two years she had spent more and more of her time
in her own room. It lay just across the transverse hall from
Bess's. Like Bess's, it had originally been furnished in curly maple;
but there the resemblance ceased.
Bess's room remained a girl's bedroom-oh, very, very girlish!
Hortense's was now definitely a sitting-room. She had discarded
dimity flounces for curtains of plain pongee. She sported a low wicker
table set forth with a charming blue-and-white tea-set. Near the bed
was a well-furnished book-shelf of her "favorites." A large engraving
of Lord Byron and a framed photograph of Richard Mansfield told
the history of two somewhat evaporated enthusiasms. The rest of the
pictures were her own work: many water-colors of flowers and land-
scapes, a few canvases, and two wood panels done in oils.
On a table in front of the north window Hortense's color-box and
plain cards lay carefully protected with tissue paper until she should
get another three-dollar order. She had a little desk; but the table
offered a wider and solider surface when she had a lengthy job of
writing to do. She had one the afternoon she sat down to write out a
list of the guests who were to be invited to Bess's début reception.
Mrs. Langworthy was sending to Chicago to have the invitations
engraved; but Hortense had offered to address the envelops. Every-
body whose acquaintance the family desired to retain must be on this
list. For Hortense's own début eight years ago Aunt Retta had been
mailing out invitations for a week after her list was supposed to be
disposed of. Well, this time she would remember everybody in the
beginning. Hortense would see to it that she did.
She opened the drawer under the little row of pigeonholes in her
pretty desk. The list for Hortense's own début reception was put
away there. Most of her dance-programs, still carefully saved, were in
177
178
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
a box on her closet-shelf. In this little drawer she continued to keep
the program of the one "hop" she had attended at West Point (How
full the moon had been that night above the Hudson!), two theater
programs with her escort's name scrawled on the margin (She had
liked Stanley James a good deal that one winter), a sample of the
material of her first grown-up "ball dress," and a curl that Dora had
sentimentally saved the year Hortense got scarlet fever and they had
to cut her hair. Hortense didn't know why she still kept that.
She spread out the old list and began to copy names on a fresh sheet
of paper. Heavens, what changes eight years made in a list of this
kind!
To Mrs. Reynolds and Mrs. Eugene Reynolds, add Mrs. Paul. Old
Fairbanks was a widower now and enjoying it; strike out "Mrs. Fair-
banks." Copy "Mrs. Stephen Durand," but realize that the old list
meant the first Mrs. Durand, and this was her too-juvenile successor.
Should the Chase girls' names go below their mother's on the one
envelop, or should they have an envelop to themselves or perhaps an
envelop apiece? And must they really include Mrs. Alexander Kirk-
land just because everybody else did? The old harpy wouldn't come;
but she owned the largest house on Prospect Avenue, and this list was
supposed to be inclusive.
Hortense had preferred to be alone when she started to draw up
this list; later on she could meet the objections which Bess was sure
to raise. But she hadn't realized how many difficulties she would en-
counter from the very outset. Half-way down the second sheet she
resolved to appeal for information. There was no good in going to
all this trouble if everything she put down was wrong.
In any quandary Hortense always turned to Dora. She laid down
her pen now and found Dora before a fire in the library, toasting her
tiny feet and taking a first read off a new travel-book before she
turned it over to the Public Library.
From this congenial employment Dora refused to be distracted.
"Just put down every one," she advised. "Ask the Colonel to bring
home the City Directory, if you're in any doubt."
"Oh, don't be funny!" said Hortense. "If I make a slip in this
business, I'm going to hear about it."
"It's not your responsibility, honey. Write out the list the best you
can, and then take it up with your Aunt Retta."
HORTENSE
179
"Where is Aunt Retta?"
"In her own room, I think. She said after dinner that she wanted
to get it straightened out before the great day." Dora grinned mis-
chievously, and went back to her book.
The début reception was still six weeks off. However, it would take
Aunt Retta that long to go through her piles of boxes and her over-
loaded drawers, even if she disposed of things as she went. Expecting
to find a scene of confusion worse confounded, Hortense knocked
on Aunt Retta's door and went in without waiting for her knock to
be answered.
Nothing in the room had been touched, and Mrs. Langworthy was
lying on the broad leather couch which stood across one of the west
windows. Hortense was startled. Mrs. Langworthy made such a point
of never lying down in the day-time; she couldn't take naps, and she
didn't see why anybody else should want to.
"Auntie, are you sick?" demanded Hortense.
"Just resting. Hortense, have you ever noticed how beautiful that
one elm looks against the sky? You can see it from here so much
better than from the lawn."
"Yes. You're higher here." Hortense stood beside the couch and
looked at the solitary elm, the only one for miles around. Then she
glanced down anxiously. "You're sure you feel all right?"
"I felt a little tired after dinner. So I thought I'd just come up here
and lie down for a while."
"I wouldn't have disturbed you, if you'd told me you were going
to rest." Hortense was inclined to stress the point. Aunt Retta's habits
were so rooted that any change in them was vaguely alarming.
Mrs. Langworthy smiled. "I think it makes me tired nowadays to
watch your uncle work."
"He has a lot to see to, hasn't he, starting that new business?"
"The new business is going to make us all rich, Hortense. That is,
it will, if it does." Mrs. Langworthy's tone was a little dry.
"I wish I understood a little more about it," said Hortense hesi-
tantly.
"Ask your uncle, my dear. I'm sure he'll be grateful for your
interest."
This wasn't the answer Hortense had expected. She was accustomed
to being told to run along and not bother her head about business.
180
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Not only was that Aunt Retta's attitude toward the girls: Hortense
knew it was Colonel Langworthy's habitual answer to Aunt Retta
herself. The business side of life was a man's concern; the women of
the family were supposed to enjoy the fruits of his activity without
concerning themselves about processes.
Something had gone crosswise now, however. Or was it simply that
Hortense herself was beginning to notice undercurrents which had
been there right along, but had hitherto escaped her?
Aunt Retta and the Colonel had always presented a united front to
the younger generation; Hortense had been twenty at least before
she found out that they ever differed on points of policy with respect
to "the girls." Did they differ more fundamentally, behind the mask
of a secure and successful marriage? It was a perplexing idea, and
somehow very distasteful to Hortense.
She took refuge in her list. Mrs. Langworthy woke to immediate
interest in the problem. Presently she rose from her couch and led the
way to Hortense's room. There they labored for an hour over the
list of guests.
Bess interrupted before they were quite through. "Oh, Mamma,
do we have to have her?" she groaned again and again as they went
through the sheets together.
"We're asking everybody whose acquaintance we wish to keep up,"
said Mrs. Langworthy decisively.
"But all those old pelicans!"
"Old pelicans, as you term them, are the people who run society.
They can do a great deal for you, Bess."
"Does Bess need what they can do for her?" asked Hortense.
"She may. Anyhow, it's time we let these people know that the
Langworthys are still on earth. Formal affairs don't interest me
much. They never have. But sometimes lately I've thought that as a
family we keep ourselves a little too much to ourselves."
"But we're all such nice people in this family!" said Bess gaily.
Hortense agreed that they were all very nice people in this family.
When Bess was at her smiling best, as she was this afternoon, she
was quite adorable. Hortense mustn't allow herself to become jealous
of her cousin just for being younger and fresher than herself. It
wasn't Bess who was shoving Hortense up on the shelf; it was cir-
cumstances. Indeed, Bess might have had some grounds for com-
HORTENSE
181
plaint about the way she was supposed to include Hortense in her
arrangements. If Hortense had done her duty by the younger girl,
she would have been married and out of Bess's way by this time.
"What color dress are you going to get for my début?" Bess asked.
"I suppose I'll have to have white; the dowagers would all faint if
a débutante appeared in color. But you can take your choice, Hor-
tense."
"I can wear something that I have," said Hortense. "Maybe that
green I got for the dance last summer."
"Oh, no, 'Tense! Everybody has seen that. You might take pink.
Some blondes can't wear pink, but I know it would be becoming to
you."
It might be becoming, thought Hortense a little bitterly; it
wouldn't be suitable. But then what would? Mrs. Langworthy could
wear lavender or gray. Dora would be charming in thin silk with
delicate sprigs of flowers all over it. Amy would wear a little good
lace at her throat, and lace ruffles over her beautiful hands. But what
was the correct costume for a débutante of eight years' standing?
One year had been so much like another. They had been happy
years, in spite of that sense of unfulfilment at the heart of them all.
If this year could just have gone on like the others, Hortense would
have had courage to wait, and to believe. But Bess's début was
going to make Hortense, in the critical eyes of the assembled dow-
agers, nothing except a girl who had waited too long: a girl who
had been put up as marriageable many seasons ago, and who was
now set forth for all the world to view as a person who had missed
her market.
She would be happier if she could do something about it. Things
always irked you less if you could do something about them. Only
in this case what was there to do except acknowledge that the shoe
fitted, and put it on?
After Mrs. Langworthy and Bess had left the room, Hortense tried
to settle to her water-colors. If she could paint a dozen place-cards in
advance, that was almost as good as money in her pocket; sooner or
later she was certain to get an order, because "hand-painted" place-
cards added a touch of distinction to a party.
Perhaps if she were wise she wouldn't wait for orders; she'd make
up a stock of cards and put them on sale downtown. Mrs. Cordiner
182
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
would be glad to handle them for her, if she didn't feel like going to
Watson's the stationer's. There was nothing to be ashamed of in
earning money; but if she seriously intended to go ahead with any
such ambitious undertaking, she'd better not say anything about it
until she had some results to show. If you came out and said, "I
think I'll do thus and so," you were all too likely to be satisfied with
thinking about it.
She decided to keep her own counsel. But a fever of accomplish-
ment was on her. That evening she shut herself up in her room and
painted until bedtime. To paint by artificial light was against her
better judgment; and sure enough when she examined the product
next morning the leaves were all too blue and had to be retouched.
She worked along for the better part of a week, doing flowers at
night and filling in the leaves the next morning. Finally one evening
she finished her last blank card and laid it to dry. She had done
five dozen now: enough to start with downtown.
She pressed the backs of her hands against her tired eyes; they felt
hot to her touch. Then she went to the bathroom for fresh water, and
set about cleaning up her brushes. Her painting hand was cramped;
she must have been pushing herself this evening, driving along
toward the finish. Her eyes wouldn't stand such hard use continu-
ally, either.
Perhaps she needed glasses. Glasses would be a long step on the
way to spinsterdom; with her type of face, they would make her look
exactly like a school-teacher. Artie sometimes wore glasses for read-
ing; and in her more discouraged moments Hortense always realized
how much she looked like Artie.
She glanced in the mirror and thought bitterly, to-night she didn't
even look like Artie. She was a haggard blonde with too much hair.
Ten years from now she would be one of those old maids who tend
carefully a mop of hair that has lost its color and luster, and who try
to make themselves believe that mere quantity is still a distinction.
She turned back to her cards and thought, "I'll never take those
downtown. I'd die sooner than ask a favor of Mrs. Cordiner; and
that's what she would consider it if she took my cards, a favor. 'Poor
Hortense,' she'd think, 'trying to earn a little pocket money!' Prob-
ably she'd buy one set of cards herself, just to help me out. And
Watson's wouldn't sell many at three dollars a dozen. They have
HORTENSE
183
only the poorer class of trade; the rich crew buy in Chicago. Even
if they should happen to sell some cards, they'd have to take their
own commission out. What would that leave for me?"
She swept the cards into a box, though the last of them were not
perfectly dry: anything to get them out of sight. Then she set the
box on her closet-shelf and turned out her light. It was only nine
o'clock; she might as well go downstairs and give her family a little
of her society.
Bess was spending the evening with Elsie Patterson; Elsie had a
grown and eligible brother. Mrs. Langworthy and Amy were at their
embroidery; across the sitting-room from them, Dora was reading
under a lamp of her own. Artie sat at the piano, picking out with
one finger the melody of a sheet of music open on the rack.
Hortense started toward him; piano-playing, unless it was correct
in time and had all the chords to it, always drove her distracted. But
half-way across the room she paused and asked, "Where's Colonel?"
"In the library, working," said Mrs. Langworthy. "But go right
ahead. Your piano-playing won't disturb him."
Often now the Colonel brought work home; he shut himself up
with it, while the rest of them enjoyed the evening. You might have
thought that when he was so busy with his new undertaking, Artie
would at least have offered to help him out.
Artie wouldn't have been of great assistance, though, even if he
hadn't been sunk in sloth. And why should it be especially up to
Artie? The rule was, of course, that the men of the family worked
for money; the women might keep busy, but their activities should
be impractical or even useless. Like painting place-cards to make idle
talk at "pelicans " parties, for instance. Or better still, painting place-
cards to be stuck away on a closet-shelf. Hortense thought bitterly
that she had at last discovered the perfect activity for a woman.
Yet she had her share of brains. She liked to be busy. She would
have enjoyed feeling that her energies counted for something. To-
night she somehow couldn't sit down and play for Artie. She went
out of the room and circled the stairs to the library door.
The door was open. The Colonel sat at his desk with a heap of
papers before him. He was copying some figures; but when he heard
Hortense's step he looked up and smiled. She rather got the idea
that he was not sorry to be interrupted.
184
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"May I come in for a minute, Colonel?" she said cheerfully.
"There's something I'd like to ask you."
She had intended to ask whether he wasn't about ready to call it
a day, and perhaps play a little backgammon with her. But when
she reached the side of his desk, she suddenly realized that getting up
and leaving now wouldn't be much relief if his work still lay there
waiting for him. She said impulsively, "I believe I have an idea,
Colonel. But if I'm just making a nuisance of myself, don't be
bashful about telling me so. Do you promise?"
"You're never a nuisance," he assured her.
"Oh, yes, I am! Even to myself sometimes. Often to other people.
But look here, Mr. Langworthy, I write a good hand, and I can
add a column of figures and get it right the first time, which is more
than lots of people can do. So if you care to read off figures to me,
I'll put them down for you." She seated herself beside his desk and
reached for a pencil.
"That would save time," he admitted. "But I don't see why you
should be bothered with my work, Hortense. Haven't you something
you'd rather do?"
"Not a thing," said Hortense flatly. "What is all this, by the way?
I thought Miss Ames did your bookkeeping for you."
"This is an estimate of what an engine will cost me to install in
an automobile. I'm trying to find out whether I'd save much if I
bought the separate parts and had them put together in my own
factory. I might be just as well off in the end, you see, if I had the
complete engines delivered to me."
"I see," said Hortense. She didn't, quite; but she knew enough to
do what she was told. "Go on from where you left off; and when
you want me to begin another column, just tell me."
In half an hour she had covered three sheets with figures. She
added each sheet separately. "Now do you compare these, or do you
want the grand total?" she asked.
He took them to compare, and frowned over them. "This doesn't
seem right, somehow. I wonder whether I've got the thing straight."
"Let me see," she began, and stopped abruptly. An idea had just
come to her: an idea which took her breath away by its strangeness.
Yet there was no real reason why she shouldn't have a try at the
HORTENSE
185
thing. At least she might put it up to the Colonel and let him do his

"You're tired to-night," she went on. "Put it all away for the pres-

"You mean you think my help wouldn't be worth much?"

factory is a horrid place. It's dirty and it's noisy and it's cluttered.
There isn't a woman on the premises, except when Miss Ames goes

If I'm more bother than I'm worth, I'll promise not to annoy you a

"They're horrible," said Hortense. "Come with me, and we'll soon
put a stop to them."
He yielded, smiling. He was tired; right now he was glad to be
dragged away from his work. Yes, and he considered the dragging a
suitable thing for Hortense to do. Women were a legitimate distrac-
tion; the women of his own family should never be put even tem-
porarily in the same class with queer dried-up workaday specimens
like Miss Ames and Charley Manning's Mrs. Knowles.
To-morrow, of course, he intended to let the subject drop of itself;
in the ordinary routine he would be out of the house before Hortense
came downstairs to breakfast.
But Hortense had other ideas on the matter. She was careful to
avoid it for the rest of the evening. But at bedtime she borrowed
Louise's battered tin alarm clock, and set it for seven.
She hadn't got up at a stated hour since her school-days. The mere
act of winding the clock made her feel that she was embarking on
something new and fascinating.

III
FTER her first day in the Colonel's factory Hortense went to
bed at eight o'clock, and then found herself too tired to sleep.
Strange words danced before her eyes, words that had to do with
machinery; she had looked some of them up in the dictionary after
supper, and the dictionary definitions had muddled her worse than
ever.
But she had no intention of letting herself be licked right at the
beginning. She went back a second day and a third. Luckily she had
begun on a Tuesday; she managed to get through the shortened
week, though she felt that one day more would have finished her.
"Good Heavens!" said Mrs. Langworthy. "I'll be glad when this
new fad of yours wears out, Hortense. You look a wreck."
Privately Hortense wished that she had never started this "fad."
It was, she saw, silly to the verge of insanity. She had actually sup-
posed that she could step in and learn a whole business as easily as
she could learn a new stitch in embroidery. Her respect for Colonel
Langworthy went up by leaps and bounds. Seeing him bossed around
at home by Aunt Retta and deluded by Bess, Hortense had been
gradually forming a rather low estimate of his intelligence. She knew
better now.
She wasn't committed to this new undertaking except in her own
mind. She had only to sleep late any morning, and then take up her
ordinary pursuits; no one would be either surprised or disappointed.
But the mere fact that escape was so easy roused an obstinate
demon in Hortense. She would stop when she could feel that she
had accomplished something.
The second week was a little easier than the first; and after that
she began to settle toward a new routine. In the little office parti-
tioned off in one corner of the factory she banged out letters on the
186
HORTENSE
187
old typewriter, kept track of supplies, made endless estimates of costs
and possible profits. Mornings weren't so bad; it was rather fun
leaving the house every day with the Colonel. The second start after
dinner was harder; and along about four in the afternoon, Hortense
always found herself gritting her teeth and watching the clock.
But the family, who had been either puzzled or derisive at first,
now began to be impressed. Artie, who had spoken vaguely of going
into the new business as soon as the Colonel got it established, was
now forced to set up a second line of defense.
"I'd be more interested," he asserted, "if the Colonel were going
into the manufacture of a different type of auto. To get any real
power you have to have a steam engine. Steamers are the best car
made. And don't you see, you'd have a fine name ready and waiting
for you? 'The Langworthy Steamer.' Sounds great, doesn't it?"
"An auto doesn't run on its name, Uncle Artie," argued Hortense.
"The type Colonel is figuring on making won't run at all, unless
they're pushed. How can you expect 'em to? A gasoline-stove on
wheels!" Artie had borrowed that last phrase from a musical comedy
song he had just bought; he seemed to feel that it clinched the
argument.
Dora, on the other hand, was extremely proud of Hortense.
"You're doing just the sort of thing I've always wanted to do," she
told her niece.
Hortense wasn't doing the sort of thing she herself wanted to do.
For the first time in her life, she was deliberately killing the hours.
If she was of some small use to the Colonel-though she didn't flatter
herself she had taken much off his shoulders-she was glad of that.
It was about time that one of the woman who profited by his exer-
tions should at least show him a little appreciation.
Busy as she was, and at times absorbed, Hortense was always con-
scious of her underlying dissatisfaction. Going over to the factory
every day was all right at present. But suppose they got the new
business started, and Hortense managed to obtain a foothold in it-
would she be satisfied then to go on spending her days in this rou-
tine? Go on until she dried up like funny little Miss Ames-go on
until she lived only for her job, like Minnie Knowles?
Hortense shuddered at the very idea. But she ground away so per-
sistently at her self-imposed task that she scarcely took time to have
188
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
her new dress fitted. She had chosen a plain peacock blue silk, and
was having it made very long and severely simple; a great cluster of
artificial violets pinned at the girdle was the only relief. It really was
a stunning turnout; the time when Hortense wouldn't "pay for
dressing" (her Aunt Retta's phrase) was still far in the future.
Being involved in business, of course, let Hortense out of the prep-
arations for the début party. Mrs. Langworthy had hired a caterer
from Chicago, and Eaton's were doing the decorations. But having
so much come in from outside seemed only to add to the confusion.
Aunt Retta's parties were always successful; but to get one under way
involved creating a new world out of chaos.
The day of the reception Hortense did not go back to the factory
after dinner. Her room as well as Bess's would be required for the
guests' wraps. She took her things in to dress with Dora. But Dora,
already finished, persuaded her to lie down for a few minutes; and
to her own surprise she went sound asleep.
Beautifully rested and beautifully dressed, she came downstairs
after the reception was in full swing. She descended slowly, one hand
holding her little train, the other fingering the violets at her waist.
Her head was held high, and her nostrils quivered a little. To take
the stage at a party was always Hortense's great moment. Right now
she was not bothered by the fact that this particular party wasn't
being given for her. She wasn't a superfluous and aging cousin; she
was a woman who could hold her own with the best of them.
It was a reception to justify the gushing report it would have at
the head of the Society Column in to-morrow's Beacon. Bushels of
hothouse flowers were disposed through the lower rooms: every
mantle was "banked," every light fixture wreathed in Alabama
smilax. Vast quantities of chicken in aspic and mushrooms in hot
cream were being served in the dining-room. In the library was a
constantly renewed bowl of champagne punch.
Mrs. Langworthy, tremendous and imposing in black satin, and
Bess all in fluttering white, received their guests in the formal parlor.
Dora and Amy mingled with the guests and asked time after time,
"Have you been served yet?" And all the impressive ladies from
Prospect Avenue were here; the caterer's man who was receiving
cards had to empty his silver tray again and again.
Along toward five o'clock the Colonel came in to see how the party

was progressing. At six Artie appeared, in a cutaway coat with a
white carnation in his buttonhole. Bess, who had been carrying an
armful of flowers all the afternoon, gave them to him to hold while
she limbered up a little.
"Having a good time, young person?" asked Artie.
"Oh, wonderful! I only hate to think it's almost over."
"Over? Why should it be over so soon?"
"How can it help being? Most people have gone already."
"But look here, honey, we won't let it be over." Artie took fire
with a great idea. "There's enough food left to feed a regiment.
Why don't we dig up some of your young friends and have a second
party this evening? Get our money's worth out of all these decora-
tions!"
"Artie, that's an insane idea," said Mrs. Langworthy. "The cater-
er's men will have to clear up, and then the house still has to be put
back in order."
But the Colonel had overheard Artie's suggestion: had seen Bess's
face light at the idea, and then cloud over at her mother's disap-
proval. "Nonsense, Retta!" he said. "We can tip the caterer's men,
and they'll be glad to stay a little longer. Bess can get busy on the
telephone, and Artie can go around town with the landau and collect
a few young men to make the girls happy."
Bess's special friends were still lingering, as special friends often
do at the end of a big party. They were called in to help with the
arrangements; two of them found that they could use the family
carriages to call for masculine guests.
Then Artie covered himself with glory by remembering that in the
attic was some canvas which had once been used at a dance for Hor-
tense. He and Jason lugged it down and covered the carpets for
dancing.
"We can't dance without music," said Bess doubtfully. She had
wanted a small orchestra for the reception, and had been put off with
Miss Prendergast and her harp. Miss Prendergast, a languishing lady
of uncertain years, played the harp at far too many parties in Mount
Royal; but there really was no space for an orchestra.
"A piano is enough for an impromptu dance," said the Colonel.
"Can't you girls take turns playing?"
"I'll play, of course," said Hortense. The afternoon had been intoxi-
190
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
cating; such good times didn't last. Once she had sat down at the
piano, she would go on playing. Amy, of course, would offer to
relieve her; but Amy's playing never caught a proper dance rhythm.
Hortense had actually begun to play for the dancing when Doug
Rutherford and Roger Willett came in. "What's the idea of this?"
demanded Roger. "We can't spare our best partner."
Hortense started to explain, but he cut her short. "I'm going to pass
the hat, and we'll get a piano-player. Here, Doug, how much money
have you?"
There was delay, confusion, telephoning. But finally they found a
pianist who was accustomed to play for dancing. Doug called for
him, to hurry things up. After his arrival, Roger claimed Hortense
for the first waltz.
"The first waltz, and the third and fifth," he whispered as they
slid into the dance. "As many after that as you can spare for me.
Hortense, you're simply beautiful to-night. I hope you don't mind
my telling you, I've never seen you look so well."
Why shouldn't she look well? She felt like a woman who has
come home from exile. The long candles burning down, the rich
scent of hothouse roses opening to the full in the warmth, the sound
of laughter and gay voices rising above the music: this was the
atmosphere that Hortense was born to.
She went from Roger's arms to Doug's; back to Roger, then on to
Everett Stacy. Roger and then Arch Patterson. Roger again.
They paused to eat. Roger claimed her once more. He had been
only one of Bess's boys when Bess came back from Sheldon. But
since then he had graduated from college, had gone into the family
business; he seemed to have grown up at a single bound. He had
grown up to Hortense, perhaps. Well, why not? She had stood still
and waited for him.
The morbid impressions of the last few weeks had all blown away,
dissolved by the magic of the hour. "You like a double reverse?"
whispered Roger against her hair. "I'm going to give you a double
reverse, and watch over your shoulder. It's the prettiest thing in the
world to wind a girl up in her dress and then unwind her. That's
what I'm going to do to you."
She was making herself conspicuous by dancing so many times
with one man. But then, why shouldn't she make herself conspicu-
HORTENSE
191
ous? Who was she to sit on the sidelines? She wasn't formed to
provide a background for other young women. She was the born
center of a scene like this.
She had danced again with Everett Stacy. Crashing chords asked
for attention. From beside the piano Artie called, "Next dance will
be ladies' choice."
Roger tried to get Hortense's eye; he even took a step in her
direction. Hortense smiled provokingly, and walked over to Arch
Patterson.
Bess had chosen Doug Rutherford; there was no surprise in that.
She was dancing every dance, her cheeks glowing, her eyes sparkling.
What difference did it make to her how good a time the other girls
were having?
It always made a difference to Bess. The piano-player paused for a
glass of punch. Bess pirouetted into a clear space in front of Roger.
"I saw the prettiest opera in New York last winter," she said
gaily. "At the Metropolitan, of course. Hansel and Gretel, it's called.
Gretel has a little song when she asks her brother to dance with her.
I wonder if I can put it into English." In her fresh clear voice she
began to sing,
"Brother, come and dance with me,
Dance with me, dance with me."
The pianist set down his glass hastily, caught the air from her, and
played it through. "That's right!" Bess called over her shoulder.
"Can't you put some chords to that? Just any chords."
He played the air accompanied, and she sang it, taking short steps
in front of Roger,
"Right foot here, la, la, la!
Clap the hands, tra, la, la!"
It was a pretty little performance, and she didn't keep it up a
minute too long. Slipping her hand under Roger's arm, she drew
him out on the floor with her. Everybody applauded. Hortense, too,
of course. Yes, Hortense applauded. She couldn't have taken the part
of a little girl, even if she had been to the Metropolitan Opera in
New York last winter.
Roger had to dance the next waltz with Bess to keep her in coun-
192
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
tenance. Hortense danced it with Arch Patterson. Roger was nothing
to her, nothing at all. She was determined to go ahead and have a
good time.
It was a determined good time, however. The magic was suddenly
gone; she felt tired and dizzy. This was the first time she ever
remembered feeling tired at a dance.
It was growing stuffy here, as well it might with an evening party
after the reception, and all those candles going. Hortense kept on
through another dance. Then she whispered to Everett Stacy, "Let's
slip out on the veranda for a minute. I need some air."
Amy saw them start toward the door and handed Hortense a
scarf. Wrapping it around her shoulders, Hortense stood aside to let
her escort struggle with the heavy front door. Then she passed him
into the outer air, and stopped abruptly.
"I think we'd better go the back way, to be out of the wind," she
said.
He followed her through the crowded kitchen. Heaven send he
hadn't seen what made her change her mind. She was afraid he had
seen it, though. If he had, would he keep the revelation to himself?
Fellows weren't supposed to tell a thing like that. But if they really
never told, would there have been all this anxiety to keep things
from them?
Hortense forced herself to turn to Everett, to fling a saucy remark
at him, to laugh at his rejoinder. But when she came back from her
brief airing it seemed to her that she could hardly set one foot before
the other. If she went on dancing after this, it was only because she
could always dance.
Roger came back to Hortense for another waltz. She let him have
it, of course; there was nothing else she could do. Oh, she didn't
blame him especially! If girls were like that, men would always take
advantage.
At the moment when Hortense started to pass out of the front
door, she had seen Roger on the veranda kiss Bess. Hortense had
sometimes suspected that Bess allowed men to kiss her. Hortense had
never allowed it; she was saving her lips for the one man.
But she wouldn't have blamed Bess greatly, if she hadn't felt her-
self soiled by the incident. Roger was passing from one girl to an-
other, enjoying himself; like a bee passing from one flower to
HORTENSE
193
another, perhaps he figured it. There again, she couldn't blame
Roger.
But Hortense had enjoyed and encouraged his attentions: had
actually felt that his singling her out made her evening a success.
Hortense should have known better: should have realized that in
his eyes she was simply a daughter of a hospitable house, and a
pleasant armful so long as the music went on.
Midnight was approaching. Cinderella might not return to rags
when the hour struck; but at the Mount Royal power-plant, the
dynamo was always shut off at twelve o'clock.
"I'm going down to the power-house and bribe the men to let us
have lights a little longer," Doug Rutherford sang out.
"You don't need to," the Colonel called back. "All our downstairs
fixtures are combinations. When the electricity goes off, we can
simply light the gas."
"Let's light it now," said Doug. "Come on! Let's have a real
illumination!"
It was half-past one when the guests finally left. The downstairs
rooms were a confusion of dropped flower petals, dog's-eared canvas,
furniture out of place.
"This is the most successful début party anybody has ever given in
Mount Royal," yawned Bess. "Mamma, so many of your friends
asked me to come and see them, I can't keep track."
"We'll call on all of them soon," said Mrs. Langworthy. "You
have had a happy day, honey?"
"Oh, gorgeous!" sighed Bess. "The dance was the best part of it,
too. I was so keyed up at the end of the afternoon, I don't see how I
could ever have got through the rest of the evening if we hadn't
danced."
Dora followed Hortense upstairs to unhook her dress. "You're both
of you my nieces," she said quietly. "I'm fond of you both. But if
you did some of the things that Bess does, I should simply fall on
you and pound you."
Dora and Hortense had always been uncommonly close together.
Bess hadn't that knack of getting on with older people. She hadn't
Hortense's resources against loneliness, either. Perhaps Bess had bet-
ter snatch all that she could right now; in another ten years her
pretty little tricks would pall on any audience.
194
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Still, snatching is a nasty habit. It would serve Bess right if Hor-
tense turned the tables on her some day.
But Hortense couldn't see herself doing it. Being Hortense, she
simply couldn't. And anyhow, she realized how such an act would
affect those near and dear to her, how it would appear to the eyes of
Dora and Amy, and the Colonel and Mrs. Langworthy.
"Wait till I get on my dressing-gown, Auntie Dora," she said. "TII
come to your room and unhook you."
For answer Dora reached over her own shoulder, then around
under her arm. Before Hortense could get to her she had unhooked
her dress and let it slide off. She stood there in her chemise and her
tiny high-heeled slippers, grinning impudently because she was so
independent of help.
Hortense picked her up bodily and bounced her down on the bed.
"Be careful of my chicken salad!" moaned Dora.
"Auntie Dora, have even you had too much to eat to-day?"
"To-day was nothing," said Dora. "We'll have to eat aspic for a
week. When Retta gives a party, she certainly gives one. No wonder
she doesn't often feel that she can make the effort."
Hortense got a blanket and draped it around Dora. Herself in a
dressing-gown, she sat down beside her on the bed. Dora could have
gone on for another two hours, and then been fresher in the morning
than Hortense was after a solid eight hours' sleep.
It flashed through Hortense's mind, "I don't see how I could get
married, even if I wanted to. I'd miss Dora too much."
Yet from her childhood there came back to her some dim echo of
a story which would indicate that Dora's being as she was hadn't
come about through her own choice. Oh, dear! Was nothing in this
world ever perfect or complete?
She could have made the complaint aloud, and Dora would have
met it with some impudent sally. But why bother Dora? Hortense
was tired, that was all.
She wanted to get over to the factory in good season to-morrow,
too. It would be rather fun to walk out to-morrow right after break-
fast. Little Bess wouldn't be walking out to-morrow. She'd be stuck
here like a fly on fly-paper, in the aftermath of a long and complicated
party.

IV
Y any one of a dozen signs, the household was settling down
for the winter. The Colonel had shifted some time ago from
his pale gray suit and white piqué vest to dark gray alternating with
navy blue. Artie had rigged up a still in his basement nook and was
turning this year's wine into brandy. Amy had finished the table-
cloth with the blue cross-stitch; she was now busy with the hardanger
of which Mrs. Langworthy had wearied.
Bess, in a charming new brown outfit with a muff and neckpiece
of beaver, had made the round of calls with her mother. Now she
attended a luncheon or a tea-party almost every day. Sometimes Hor-
tense was included in those day-time invitations. When she was
invited, she went. In between times she was at the factory right
along. It was a good thing she had made the shift when she had.
Thanksgiving week Roger Willett's engagement was announced.
He was marrying Elsie Patterson.
"Poor Roger!" said Bess. Hortense looked hard at her, but man-
aged to hold her tongue.
Two days later Doug Rutherford told Bess he was leaving to spend
the rest of the winter in Detroit. The Reynoldses, his father's part-
ners, had connections there; John Rutherford thought Doug could
get some valuable experience in a larger city.
"He'll be back in the spring, I suppose," said Amy.
To Hortense she worried, "I wonder whether Doug and Bess are
engaged. Surely though, she'd let us know if they were. I'm afraid
perhaps she cares for him, and he hasn't said anything yet."
"Oh," said Hortense lightly, "Bess has more than one string to her
bow!"
That was Bess's idea, of course: to show just how many strings
she had to her bow. She began to talk once more about Bill Jerome.
195
196
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
She had heard from him lately; he might be coming West in the
spring. If he did, he would stop in Mount Royal for a few days.
"He'll find us quaint," said Dora.
Hortense didn't believe he was coming. She was almost sure that
Bess had asked him; that didn't prove anything. He had shown Bess
some attention when she first visited his sister. But the signs indicated
that since then he had ditched her. Bess spoiled her chances through
overeagerness.
Oh, well! Hortense had taken pains to be dignified and aloof; and
she, too, was stranded. Indeed, she was much more definitely washed
up on a reef than Bess. Bess was a débutante, and a reasonably pop-
ular débutante. Hortense had reached the place where she felt that
she needed to make herself useful.
How completely she had passed over into a different class she
realized when the Colonel offered her a salary if she was staying on
at the factory. Hitherto he had handed her money several times,
remarking with a smile that it was "for her services." Hortense had
taken the money, had felt indeed that she earned it. But a regular
salary would make her definitely an employee.
"In the spring, when we actually get to manufacturing, we can
settle that," she said.
"We're manufacturing right now," the Colonel insisted. "There are
three cars on the floor this minute, and a crate of machinery came in
to-day."
"I know. But so far the money has all been going out, Colonel.
It will be a long time before anything comes in. Are you sure you
were right to start in with only ten thousand dollars' new capital?
Mr. Manning wanted you to take more, you said."
"We can branch out later, when we've proved that we can make
a good automobile. Borrowing even from Charley Manning isn't like
finding the money growing on a tree, Hortense. Every cent we take
now will have to be paid back some day; and the more we take, the
more interest will pile up right along."
Hortense wasn't competent to advise him, of course. Anyhow,
nothing different could be done just then. Charley Manning had left
for a winter in Italy. To all inquiries Minnie Knowles replied coldly
that she would forward his letters when he sent her a permanent
address. Of course she had an address for him right along; but in
HORTENSE
197
his absence she enjoyed the feeling that she was protecting him.
Hortense had to feel her way forward by inches. Often she wished
that she had never left the safe shelter of home life. Then money
had actually seemed to her to grow on trees: not a great deal of
money, to be sure, but enough. Her time had been her own, too.
And her hair hadn't smelled of varnish; and she hadn't had to
stumble from bed every morning before she had her sleep out.
Still, she had never appreciated home when she was there right
along. She knew the difference now.
One day early in January she left the factory before the Colonel
did. All the way home in the street-car she was looking forward
eagerly to her arrival. She would have a cup of tea in her own room,
and stretch out on her bed afterward with a magazine. Rest, shelter,
quiet: her own room was a haven.
She let herself in at the front door and stole upstairs. Just for an
hour now she didn't want to meet even Dora. But when her hand
was on her own knob she heard somebody start to open Mrs. Lang-
worthy's door at the end of the hall, and then pause for a moment
to speak to another person inside.
Hortense's impulse was to get inside her door before she was seen
and spoken to. But habit prevailed, and courtesy. People in that
household simply didn't sneak away from one another.
It was Amy who had caught her. Amy was frowning a little, as
if she had something on her mind. But when she saw Hortense she
smiled and said, "Home early, aren't you, honey?"
"Where's everybody?" asked Hortense.
"Dora has gone for a walk. Retta is lying down in her room."
Something passed across Hortense's mind like a tiny cloud: passed
and was gone. "That leaves just you and me, doesn't it?" she said.
"I suppose Bess is off somewhere?"
"She went to a luncheon-party. She hasn't come back yet."
"I'm going to have a cup of tea," said Hortense. "Won't you join
me?"
Somewhat to her surprise, Amy followed her in. While Hortense
was taking off her wraps, Amy filled the tea-kettle in the bathroom
and set it going over the spirit lamp. Then she went down the back
stairs and returned in a minute with a plate of cookies.
"Nice and easy for me," commented Hortense.
198
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"Why don't you slip into something comfortable and just lie down
on your bed? I'll make the tea." Amy turned back the bedspread
and piled the pillows.
Hortense stretched out luxuriously. "Don't you want to take tea
to Aunt Retta when it's ready? She isn't asleep, is she?"
"No, she isn't asleep. But I just offered her tea, and she said she
didn't want any."
"She's better off without the extra meal," said Hortense. "But it's
a nice custom."
With her cup half-way to her mouth, she remembered what it was
that had flickered across her mind a few minutes ago. "Aunt Amy,
since when has Aunt Retta got into the habit of afternoon naps? I
caught her taking one not so long ago; and you know she used to
be so scornful of other people's naps."
"She has been lying down every day lately," said Amy just above
her breath. The worried frown had come back to her forehead.
"Do you think she doesn't feel well, Aunt Amy?"
"She says she's all right. 'Just tired.' But it seems to me that I
notice a difference in her lately. She hasn't her old energy."
"Do you suppose she's growing old?" Hortense was startled by the
notion. In her eyes Mrs. Langworthy had never been young. As far
back as Hortense could remember, Mrs. Langworthy had been stout,
had worn glasses, had showed gray streaks in her thick dark hair.
But her comfortable middle age had never seemed to alter by a
hair's breadth. The idea of Aunt Retta "old" was ridiculous.
"We're none of us getting any younger," said Amy. "But no, I
don't think it's that. I suppose it's all this chasing about that she has
to do with Bess nowadays, and on Bess's account."
"It would be nice for all of us if Bess got married," said Hortense.
"I wouldn't say that to anybody but you, Aunt Amy."
She wondered actually why she had said it to Amy. Amy never
repeated things; Dora was sometimes provoked with her for the way
she didn't pass on perfectly legitimate news. But disagreeable facts
and unkind comment she was seldom in a position to pass on even
if she had wanted to.
The tendency in the family was always to spare Amy. Anger hurt
her. Even small unkindnesses disturbed her. She spent her days in a
HORTENSE
199
world of her own; even in the intimacy of that household, Amy
always managed to remain a little bit withdrawn.
She let Hortense's remark pass. In silence she sipped her tea.
Presently she relieved Hortense of her cup. Then she sat down by
the windows, a little out of Hortense's line of vision.
"Close your eyes, and perhaps you can drop off for ten minutes,"
she said quietly. She had a beautiful rich voice, but she habitually
talked in an undertone. If she had ever spoken up like Retta, or
looked you gaily and defiantly in the eye like Dora, she wouldn't
have been Amy at all.
Hortense closed her eyes obediently. When she opened them, Amy
was still there. Hortense turned on her pillow and looked at her for
a time without Amy's knowing it.
Being away from the house so much gave you a different angle on
the people you had once been so used to that you took them for
granted. Amy had always slipped between the lines, so to speak. Now
it suddenly struck Hortense that the most interesting and important
part of a letter was often what you could read between the lines.
Early dusk was falling; but Amy faced the light. She was very
lovely, and infinitely sad. And supposing herself unwatched, she had
at this moment the look which Hortense had surprised in her before:
the look of wistful youth, just beginning life and afraid of what it
might find ahead.
But Amy wasn't young. Actually, she was older than Artie; and
for all his drooping distinction Artie fitted without incongruity into
the "retired" class. The look of youth in Amy meant simply that
life and experience had passed her by.
That was the real tragedy: not grief and trouble, which you could
endure or at least make a stand against, but emptiness. Years and
years of disappointment so quiet and gradual that you scarcely real-
ized how all the possibilities of life were slipping away from you.
Nothing to make you flame into joy and eagerness, however briefly.
Nothing even to make you want to hold on when your time came
to die.
And this was precisely what might lie ahead of Hortense. Because
in a sense even being the third wife of rotund little Dr. Simpson was
better than this emptiness.
But Amy, like Hortense, had held out for everything or nothing.
200
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Those flashing deep eyes of hers were meant to gaze into a man's,
with all life in their depths. Those dexterous beautiful hands were
meant to meet other stronger hands, or to lie lightly on children's
heads. Now her hands lay loose in her lap, palm upward; and the
eyes that never met yours even in the common cheerful affairs of
every day were gazing far away into the deepening dusk.
Or was that accurate and just? It was too easy to be sympathetic
with a woman simply because she hadn't married. A beautiful unat-
tached woman of Amy's age was always open to facile curiosity. "Why
do you suppose she never married?" How many times that must
have been said about Amy in the last fifteen years!
But what did Hortense really know about Amy? Until to-day, it
had never even occurred to her that there was anything to find out.
She ought not to make the forthright attempt, either. The first prin-
ciple of her upbringing had been, "Never ask personal questions."
Just the same, Amy's experience might help Hortense. To stay on
at the factory or lapse back into domestic routine: that wasn't a
crucial problem. It would take care of itself; and in a deep sense it
didn't much matter which way such a problem was settled.
But some day the real issue might rise. An eligible widower might
come Hortense's way; these mornings when she rose before she was
rested, she looked like easy game for a middle-aged man who wanted
a woman about the house. Such a marriage would be a way of life,
at least. It would offer a definite position, security, some one to grow
old with. Might not a sensible compromise be better after all than
the weary waste of one's years?
"Aunt Amy!" whispered Hortense.
Amy turned her head. "I thought you were asleep. I was trying
to be quiet."
"You were quiet. You always are."
"Perhaps you could rest better if I left you alone?"
"No, don't go. Aunt Amy, do you mind if I ask you something?"
A faint smile flickered across Amy's face. "Ask away. Just don't
ask anything that's too hard for me to answer. Save the real stickers
for Dora."
"This is about you, honey. Have you always been so quiet?"
"Now that's a thing you'd better ask Dora. I know I've never been
HORTENSE
201
noisy. But neither was your mother-nor Aunt Dora herself. Retta
was always the robust member of the family."
"It's funny she never had more than the one child, isn't it? She's
the sort of woman everybody calls 'motherly.”
Amy sighed. "I'm afraid our family is running out. There were
five of us, you know, beside one baby who died young. Now in your
generation there are only you and Bess."
"Just why is it running out, Aunt Amy? Are we the sort of people
who don't grab things? Do we allow ourselves to be shoved aside?
Is that the trouble?"
"Mercy, what a deep problem! You're beginning to take the family
very much to heart, Hortense."
"I won't go on if you'd rather I didn't."
"Do go on, dear. We see so little of you lately; I don't know when
you and I have sat down together."
They had never sat down together just like this. Hortense couldn't
fathom why she had suddenly decided that Amy could tell her what
she so passionately wanted to know. But her eagerness drove her to
ask in so many words, "Aunt Amy, when is a person justified in
getting married?"
If she had thought she would startle or trouble Amy by the ques-
tion, she was mistaken. Amy gave her a clear, searching look. Prob-
ably that was the reason Amy so seldom met your eye; she was afraid
she might see too much. Afraid also, perhaps, that she might tell too
much.
But under her reserve was a flaming directness. With passionate
conviction she said, "There's only one thing justifies marriage, Hor-
tense. You know that just as well as I do."
"I've always thought I did. 'Love,' I've said, and kept on saying
it for years, as if that were the only possible answer. But most people
don't seem to feel that way about it."
"The more shame to them, then, if they don't." Amy had turned
her back to the window now; but her voice was clear and deep as
she spoke from that inner fastness of which Hortense had never
before been allowed so much as a glimpse.
Hortense lowered her own voice a little. "I suppose it depends a
good deal on what you mean by love."
"There's no mistaking that, child. Unless you care so much for a
202
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
man that you feel your life would be quite worthless without him-
unless he means more to you than everything else put together-you
don't really love him." Amy drew a quick audible breath. "I've never
cared that much for any man. I've never-cared that much."
She was sentencing Hortense. Yet the issue that had meant so
much to Hortense a few minutes ago, the picture of Amy's life fore-
shadowing her own, had slipped into oblivion.
She felt nothing but a passion of pity for Amy, and a sudden fury
against Fate. That this should have happened to Amy! It was worse
by far than what had happened to Dora all those years ago. “I've
never cared that much for any man." And Amy could have "cared,"
so deeply and passionately and unselfishly!
If you were not capable of loving like that, of course, deeper issues
didn't make much difference. You went ahead and looked for a com-
fortable life; and if you had health and common sense, and enough
money to live on, no doubt you might make out very well.
But what did making out well amount to, compared to preserving
your own integrity? Amy had a tragic courage. Hortense had lived
her entire life at Amy's side, and had never once suspected the exist-
ence of that quality.
Now just for a moment she had been suffered to see what it was
that Amy concealed so sedulously. Hortense must never presume on
that revelation. This evening when they met downstairs under the
eyes of the family she must be careful to let Amy see that the rela-
tion between the two of them had not altered in the least.
Instinctively the family had always adopted the policy of "sparing"
Amy. Oh, but they could never spare her enough! She was a fine
instrument on which no one had ever played. And it was too late
now.
But hadn't it always been too late? The trouble wasn't just that
hope had died in Amy. She had always been a little too fine, and too
remote, to expect fulfilment or even content in a world made up so
largely of clash and compromise.
