HE sunshine was so clear it dazzled; and low in the sky tiny
white clouds raced before a south wind. This was one of those
matchless spring days which sometimes intervene even in the treacher-
ous climate of the Middle West: days big with a promise which is
seldom fulfilled.
In a little hollow down the creek and hidden from the high road,
Amy Mills stooped to gather hepaticas. She came here every day in
spring; this had been her custom ever since the long-ago afternoon
when as a stranger homesick for Kentucky but held here by ties there
was no breaking, she had wandered forth simply to be by herself for
an hour, and had come upon a stray anemone.
Now that a tide of cheap building had crept so close to the Lang-
worthy property, the hollow was often devastated between her visits.
The neighbor children with whom Dora was on such good terms
generally awoke in Amy only a distant pity. But she was furious at
them whenever she found violet plants uprooted, or bloodroot thrown
down to die only a few yards from where it had been picked.
Every day in the season, however, her patience was rewarded
by some flower that had managed to hide itself from vandal ap-
proaches. Before the snow was off the ground she knew where to look
for the tiny striped mayflowers that withered in your our hand. Then
came three kinds of violets: the purple, the leafy yellow, the dimin-
utive blue that was just the color of a baby's eyes. Later she would
find the trillium, lovely enough for a bride's bouquet, and the man-
drake with its heavy odor that always made Amy think of Cape
jasmine.
Often enough she stooped to push aside the fallen leaves and find
the flowers; then after a lingering look she would pass on, searching
but not plucking. To-day, however, she had picked a handful of
203
204
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
hepaticas. Perhaps Hortense would like to paint them. Hortense had
scarcely painted at all this past winter; but for the last week a cold
had kept her home from the factory, and she had stayed in her own
room all day, busy with her water-colors and some music she was
copying.
The ground was damp here in the hollow, or Amy would have sat
down for a few minutes before she started home. She loved the peace
of this nook; you might have been a thousand miles from anywhere.
Back at the house she saw people a little dimly, as if a fine mist floated
between her and them. But she could never quite forget that they were
there. Even her bedroom was shared with Dora: shared willingly, of
course, because that gave Hortense a room to herself. But an hour
alone was always a treat to Amy.
This afternoon she had even managed to get away without the
dogs; Dora took them for a run along the high road. Old Bob had
died during the winter, but Czar and Beppo were still in fine form.
Officially Czar belonged to Hortense and Beppo to Mrs. Langworthy.
But whoever was the titular owner of a dog in that household, the care
of the animal fell mostly to Amy, and the dog's allegiance went to the
Colonel. He was the sort of man every dog adores.
Amy picked up the care of the pets just as she picked up the pieces
of "fancy-work" that Mrs. Langworthy always began with such zest
and ambition, and then so promptly wearied of. She had taken a
good deal of the care of Bess, too, when Bess was a baby. She loved
handling a baby; but when it grew into an urchin-such as Dora
found fascinating-Amy shrank away from it.
Dr. Simpson, seeing Amy's deftness with very small children, had
once told her that she would make an admirable nurse if she were
only stronger physically. Amy hadn't the slightest desire to be a nurse,
and she detested allusions to the fact that she tired easily.
Dr. Simpson had also told her that part of her trouble must be that
she took life too seriously; there couldn't be much the matter with
her, or she wouldn't have looked so much younger than she was. He
had been leading up to something, of course, when he began to flatter
her about her looks. Since she had refused his hand and some one
else had taken him, Amy hadn't seen the rotund doctor. She would
have felt awkward if she had met him; yet surely he was the one who
should have been uncomfortable.
AMY
205
She must put her hepaticas in water before they wilted. Amy came
up the bank of the creek, walked a few yards along the high road, and
turned into the Langworthy grounds.
The trees were putting out their first tender green. Jason was raking
a flower-bed in the side yard. The window-screens were stacked on
the lawn, to be painted before they were put up. How secure and
satisfying it all looked! In that substantial cream-colored house she
had already spent twenty summers-no, twenty-one. This coming
summer would not be vastly different: a great pile of flowers to be
arranged every day and distributed through the rooms, afternoon
guests on the veranda, young voices and laughter drifting across the
hall of an evening. The members of that household had all gone on
together for so many years: Amy had come to dread the very idea
of change.
She let herself in at the front door and walked through to the
kitchen to put her flowers in water. As she passed the library door
she heard voices: the Colonel's and Hortense's. Amy identified voices
in that household just as she identified steps.
It flashed through her mind, "Perhaps Colonel wants Hortense to
go back to the factory to-morrow. It's been pleasant this week having
her at home. I've scarcely seen her except at meals, but I've known
she was here. I thought this noon she was looking lovely The rest
has done her good."
She was returning with her wild flowers in a little bowl of thin

came out so abruptly that she almost collided with Amy. Inside the

more time, that's all."
Hortense closed the door and stood with her back against it. She
seemed on the verge of tears.
"Hortense dear, what has happened?" demanded Amy.
"Oh, nothing! Nothing at all, really."
"Something, surely, or you wouldn't be so upset. I don't want to be

Arms was one thing that forbade hotels to unescorted ladies. Amy had

licly repudiated: subjected to town gossip, and worse still to officious

Real suffering never could be hidden from her, in spite of the bar-

so close, Amy felt ashamed to be sitting here so safe and comfortable.
One of her days was so much like another, one of her years the
counterpart of the rest.
Sometimes she rebelled secretly against a life which for all its
pleasant smooth surface seemed to lack depth and point. But her
rebellion never even flared into saucy speeches like Dora's; and
Hortense's readjustment, which had filled Dora with admiration,
struck Amy as obscurely not right.
Perhaps her underlying rebellion kept her from becoming too
smug. Her contented life was no thanks to her: Retta and the Colo-
nel gave her a home, and Dora and Hortense shared the simple
interests which broke up the day for her. But sometimes Amy

she thought of the rest of them.

Artie must have been shuffling with his eyes closed. The three sisters
and Artie and the Colonel could have a pleasant evening together
even if the young people didn't choose to join them.

MY and Hortense both realized that the Colonel needed cheer-
ing up. But if the family evening was gayer than usual, the
result was largely due to Dora.
For the past two weeks she had been working up an imitation of
a "public reading" to which she had dragged the reluctant Amy.
The Mount Royal Woman's Club had given King Lear under the
supervision of Mrs. Meade, who was the town's leading teacher of
elocution as well as wife of the Episcopal rector.
Mrs. Meade had done the mad monarch herself; and if King Lear
had happened also to be the wife of a rector, he might have been
very much like that. Mrs. Saunders, who was The Fool, had mislaid
her eyeglasses twice at crucial moments. Miss Jenkins, the town's
severest spinster, who had been President of the Altar Guild for
twenty years, impersonated the Duke of Gloucester, one of whose
sons was-ahem! They had cut the text where it explained about
that son.
It was all meat for Dora, who had sat through the performance as
if butter wouldn't melt in her mouth, while Amy suffered. Now the
high points were reproduced at some length. Presumably it was the
most cheerful reading that King Lear ever enjoyed.
When Dora finally ran out of breath she took down her banjo and
announced that she was going to play an obbligato for one of Artie's
darky stories. Amy was trying to put chords to her "obbligato" when
the door-bell rang.
Hortense, who was nearest the hall, answered the door; unless
especially important company was expected, the second girl went off
duty when the supper-dishes were finished. Hortense could be heard
replying to a young man's voice. Then she turned and called, "Bess!"
But the young man walked right past her into the sitting-room.

They could have used the street-car, Amy thought; it wasn't such

The meal was delicious: oysters on the half-shell, a clear soup,
quail on toast, and Nesselrode pudding. They had an embarrassing
amount of attention, however, from waiters who were thrilled to
see any lunchers appear in that vast cavernous dining-room.
The room itself chilled Amy. The huge expanse was broken by
great square dark pillars, and all the doors were shut off by rows
of potted palms which reminded her too distinctly of a funeral. The
bright spring daylight turned here to a greenish gloom like the light
far below the surface of the sea; and what lamps were turned on
AMY
217
might keep the waiters from bumping into things, but diffused no

Trying to be merry here was like eating a good meal on the eve

immensity; and after one or two polite murmurs Amy took refuge

expected of an older woman lunching with two youngsters.
It must have been the room which affected them all. On the drive

Jerome? You must make the most of your visit in Mount Royal,
and we'd love to have you."
Bill assured her that he would love to come, but he didn't want
to be a pest. Bess cut in, "If there's anything else you'd rather do,
Bill, say so."
There was nothing else he'd rather do, he said. He was going to be
here until Friday. Thursday he understood that there was to be a
play at the Opera House; he had seen it in New York, and it was
fairly funny. He'd get tickets, if Bess and Miss Amy could come with
him. To-night he would trespass on their hospitality, if they'd let
him. He was a stranger in Mount Royal, and he didn't relish the idea
of spending an evening alone in his hotel bedroom.
"Oh, you could come around this evening anyhow!" said Bess.
"But supper it is. Pot luck, you understand."
Amy rather expected Bess to get some of her young friends to-
gether that evening. Impromptu parties were always fun; and it was
natural enough that she should want to show off to the other girls
the fascinating young man who had come clear from New York to
see her. (He said he had been attending to business in Chicago;
but Amy suspected that a serious business week does not end on
Tuesday afternoon, and he had arrived in Mount Royal Tuesday
evening.)
The interval between homecoming and supper, however, Bess em-
ployed in hearing all the news of her friends in the East. She did
not telephone anybody, and Amy made no suggestion that she should.
218
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Asking Bill here to a family meal was officiousness enough for Amy.
Supper was a merry meal, and as substantial and well cooked as
meals under that roof always were. After supper the Colonel invited
Bill to join him and Artie in the library for a smoke. This was the
first time that the Colonel had ever extended that special courtesy
to any of Bess's young men. Decidedly, Bill was making a hit with
the old folks.
When they left the library along toward eight o'clock, the Colonel
stood aside for Bill to pass into the formal parlor. But Bill walked
around him and joined the ladies in the sitting-room.
"I'm going to turn music this evening for whoever plays," he an-
nounced. "That will be a special help to you, Bess. You really need
the services of a capable music-turner."
He passed himself around that night as if he enjoyed extending
his services to every one of them. The next day, too, when he came
with his theater tickets, he had bought a whole row. He ordered two
carriages from the livery stable, and wouldn't take "No" for an an-
swer. When he finally sat down next to Bess on the aisle, he had
shepherded in all the Langworthy family.
"Really," Dora whispered to Amy behind her program, "it looks
to me as if that young man had designs."
Amy liked the play, a Charles Hoyt farce in which an impossibly
beautiful woman spent half an act trying on hats before a full-length
mirror, and making a whole series of bewitching pictures. Amy al-
ways enjoyed the sight of things happening on the stage; and be-
tween the acts she liked to look over the audience and see whether
any of her friends were here.
As the evening drew toward a close, however, she began to reflect
uneasily that she would soon have to make some sort of polite speech
to Bill about being so kind to her especially. She liked him; she
thought she detected a genuine kindness where another young man
would only have been doing the proper thing by his hosts. But
Amy never adjusted herself easily to new people; and at the idea of
a set "Thank you" to Bill she felt an oncoming shyness which she
knew would develop into agony when she actually began to speak
to him.
She nerved herself to begin while they were leaving the theater.
He cut her short, however, at the end of her first strangled sentence.
AMY
219
"Don't sound so valedictory, Miss Amy, unless you want to embarrass
me. I'm staying on until Monday."
Amy short a quick glance at Bess. Bess was smiling triumphantly,
and well she might. It was one thing for a young man to be nice
to his sister's school friend when she was a guest under his roof.
But to seek her out in her own home, and then to postpone the day
of his departure-well, this was more than casual attention, at least.
The impromptu party that Amy had expected earlier was actually
held Saturday night. They spread down Artie's canvas once more,
and got in a piano-player, and had a dance.
Then Sunday the young crowd commandeered "rigs" for ten
couples, and went for a long drive through the inviting spring sun-
shine. Afterward they all returned to the Langworthys' for supper.
It was great fun for the older people, too. Mrs. Langworthy and
the Colonel loved having all that young life about the house. But
it told on Amy. At ten o'clock in the evening she was stealing away
from the guests when Bill followed her into the hall.
"Miss Amy, will you do something for me?" he demanded.
"Anything I can, of course."
"Well, that luncheon we had the other day in that empty dining-
room was too much for my spirits. Bess tells me the other hotel has
more cheerful accommodations."
"Yes, oh, yes! Indeed they are."
"Then won't you lunch with Bess and me at the other hotel to-

cousin to make one of the party this time?"
"She could just as well as not," said Amy, audaciously for her.
"Why don't you ask her to-night?"
"I have asked her. She turned me down."
"I do wish she hadn't. Hortense takes life too hard, I'm afraid."
"She goes over to her uncle's factory every day, doesn't she? Miss
Amy, we really must put a spoke in that wheel. Men ought to work;
we may not like to, but we know that it's our lot in life. But women
ought to wear pretty clothes and go to parties. If they won't do that
much for us, what incentive has a man to keep on working?"
This chimed right in with Amy's own secret objections to having
Hortense go over to the factory every day. Moreover, it confirmed
her impression that Bill Jerome was much the nicest young man
220
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
who had ever made the Langworthy house his headquarters. He
included all the older ladies of the family in his plans. To be sure
he was doing it in order to ingratiate himself with the family. But
he showed brains and breeding.
He also showed, of course, how important he considered it to get
on the right side of Bess's relatives. Bess was young to be married;
and New York was too far from Mount Royal.
However, there would be time enough later to think of those in-

Hortense was firm about not lunching with them. Bill drew out
all her objections-Amy supposed in order to combat them. But he

tangled Hortense in a web of "Busy all day-that isn't the time any-
how that I'd choose to amuse myself," he suddenly switched the
invitation from luncheon to dinner.
Without being fearfully rude, Hortense couldn't turn down the
amended invitation. She accepted reluctantly, but she accepted.
It wasn't until Amy had started upstairs afterward that she real-
ized Bill had planned to leave Mount Royal Monday evening. Now
his departure would have to be postponed a second time.
The dinner-party was a great success. Hortense's consent had been
wrung from her, but she entered whole-heartedly into the festivity.
Mrs. Langworthy was in capital spirits, too; she could always make
a party go. Their table was a center of merriment in the old dining-
room of the Mount Royal Hotel.
Amy was conscious of eyes on them: eyes that would have em-
barrassed her if she had not been so well supported. But surrounded
this way by her whole family, she thought proudly, "Let people look!
They're looking at something." A family handsome, prosperous, well-
dressed, a family moreover that got on so splendidly together: that
was a sight for this town, for any town.
Of course the story of this party would be all over Mount Royal
to-morrow. In a sense it directed too much attention on Bess. Not
too much, however, if Bill went ahead as Amy now expected him
to. Bess, too, expected it. Bess was radiant to-night.
Next day she reported that Bill wanted to entertain them all again
AMY
221
on Wednesday evening. He had discovered that there was a private
dining-room at the Manning Arms; he wanted them to try a dinner
there.
"It's a little risky, isn't it?" said Amy. "I mean, we had such a
good time last night. When anybody tries to duplicate a successful
party, especially so soon-"
"It's Bill's risk," interrupted Bess. "He was careful to say, Aunt
Amy, that he wanted us all to come."
They all went. But when they got back to the house about ten
o'clock, Amy went straight upstairs.
She was tired from so much gaiety; and anyhow she wasn't anx-
ious to have Bill consult her again. Amy was by no means the social
head of the family. Looking back now, she couldn't for the life of
her see why Bill had ever decided to work his arrangements through
her.
Returning to her room after a warm bath, she was conscious that
everything was quiet downstairs. She arranged the light, slipped into
bed, opened a book. She didn't want to go to sleep before Dora
came into the room; to be roused after she had dropped off always
meant hours of wakefulness for Amy.
She was half asleep, however, when she heard light feet in the
hall. One of the girls must have come upstairs. Amy rubbed her
eyes and looked at the clock. Looking at it again after what had
seemed to her a long time, she couldn't see that the hands had moved
forward.
She mustn't allow herself to get into fussy habits. She got out of
bed and walked around the room. Then once more she opened her
book. This time, sure enough, she succeeded in getting interested.
She had read for some time when the door opened. Amy glanced
up, smiled at Dora, and closed her book. Dora would want to talk
over the evening. Well, it had been a nice evening. Amy could say
that truthfully.
Dora didn't return Amy's smile. She looked bewildered, and Dora
was seldom bewildered. She viewed the follies of the world either
tolerantly or mischievously. But after she had closed the door care-
fully she advanced across the room with an air of importance, as
if she had news to communicate.
Amy was amused. "Get it out, Dora. It isn't much of a surprise,
222
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
but if you'll tell me at once, I'll try to act suitably impressed."
"It isn't a surprise?" Dora's eyebrows shot up, and she gave Amy

"I don't know a thing," asserted Amy. "But give me three guesses.

"You need it."

Dora sat down on her bed and drew her knees up until she could

accepted-yet. And he did propose, but it was to the wrong girl."
"To the wrong girl?" Amy's startled voice sounded like a shout
in her own ears.
"Well, not to the wrong girl, exactly. In fact, he's showed more
judgment than I gave him credit for. Say, to the unexpected girl.
He has just asked Hortense to marry him."

III
HERE was very little sleep for Amy that night. Dora's news
was astonishing, though to be sure Bill Jerome had given
hints enough, if anybody had interpreted them properly.
Hortense came in to pay "the aunts" a bedtime visit. Without a
word Amy stretched out both arms to her. Hortense sat down on
the bed and buried her face on Amy's shoulder.
When she looked up again, the radiance in her face made Amy
want to cry. It excited Dora. "You do make a success of everything
you undertake," she said admiringly. "He's the only boy I've ever
seen around this house who made me wish that I was twenty years
younger."
"I'm just as well pleased that you're not," said Hortense. "But I
haven't accepted him yet. After all, I scarcely know him."
"You knew him the minute you laid eyes on him. Come on now,
didn't you?"
"I tried not to think too much about him," Hortense almost whis-
pered. Then with shining eyes she went on, "He says he will do
anything to get me. He spoke of that character in the Old Testament
-you know, the one who served seven years for a wife. Said he'd
wash buggies down at the store for seven years to get me, if he
had to."
"That was a nice loverlike speech," said Dora. "But there isn't
any need for his washing buggies anywhere, is there? I mean, the
Jeromes are well off?'
"Very well off. They are nice people, too, he says; he's sure I'll
like them. And he likes all my family. Aunt Dora, there doesn't
seem to be any catch anywhere. Yet surely there must be. This is
just too good to be true."
Yes, it was too good to be true. Amy, lying awake hour after hour,
223

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
thought over and over that there was one stumbling-block so patent
to all of them that none of the three had dared mention it.
There was no reason why Hortense should forego a radiant future.
She couldn't just hand the young man back to Bess as if he were a
length of dress material on which the younger girl had set her heart.
But Amy was afraid that Bess had made a terrific mistake, which
was bound to embarrass every one else as well as cause herself a

have to be acquired at the cost of misery to some one else?
Bill was to come over in the morning; he had parted from Hor-

breakfasted almost together. Even Retta put by her tray earlier than
usual; and the Colonel telephoned out to the factory that he wouldn't
be there until late.
Bess refused any breakfast except a cup of coffee; she was in a
savage humor. "Let's all go into the sitting-room while Ida clears
away," suggested Dora, picking up her own cup. "We don't want
our news to trickle through to the servants just yet."
Amy would have given a good deal for the courage to run right
out of the house and not come back all day. She found this atmos-
phere insupportable. Yet she must stay, must try to help if she could.
She sat looking down at her clenched hands, not sure that she fully
understood the situation, perplexed as to what any of them could
do about it.
Artie strolled to the piano and began with affected nonchalance
to pick out an air. The Colonel sat miserably silent, watching Bess.
Hortense fingered a magazine on the table.
But Dora was a direct sort of person. Moreover, she would cheer-
fully have gone to the stake for Hortense; Bess's wrath seemed to
Dora a small thing to face, if she could help her darling to hap-
piness.
"This is awkward for all of us," she said. "You've been put in a
false position, Bess-and I can't see that it has been your fault, either.
But the remedy lies in your own hands. If you hang onto your
temper and keep your mouth shut, nobody is going to be a bit the
wiser about what has happened."
Mrs. Langworthy came in at that moment. "I'm sure Bess is going
AMY

to do the right thing," she said. "She and Bill have never been any-

"That's easy for you to say," snapped Bess. "And Aunt Dora says

"We know-whatever it is we know," muttered the Colonel. "But
outside of the family-"
"Inside of the family is plenty," interrupted Bess. "You're the

plenty. Don't fool yourself that they haven't."
Hortense turned to face her. "Just what do you want me to do,

"What do I want you to do? You might have thought of that

"A laughing-stock? Oh, Bess!"
"You have. You know you have. And that after all the years
you've lived in this house, and I've always been told I must be nice

little girl should!"
"That's enough of that sort of talk, Bess," said Mrs. Langworthy

"I don't want to hurt anybody," said Hortense slowly. "I can give
Bill up, of course. He's coming here this morning. Dora can meet
him, and tell him it's all a mistake."
"But it isn't a mistake," muttered Artie to the keyboard.
"Oh, don't mind me!" said Bess. "Who am I to be considered?"
Amy looked up suddenly. She had been sitting there in anguish;
now all at once her heart lightened. Louder than she generally spoke,
and very clearly, she said, "You don't love him, Bess."
None of the rest of them had dared approach that point. Amy had
not only spoken out; she was right in her divination. Bess's vanity
was cruelly mortified: it was bad enough to be publicly cut out with
a desirable young man, much worse when the successful girl was the
half-eclipsed Hortense. But Bess did not love him.
She shot at Amy a look of absolute hatred. Then she turned and
ran from the room. They heard her hasty feet on the stairs, and a
226
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
minute later her own door slammed so hard that the house shook
on its foundations.
Artie turned away from the piano and grinned. "That's all right.
She'll cry her eyes out. But she'll come to herself later on and see
that there's nothing she can do except make the best of things."
"I'm not so sure about that," said Hortense in a troubled tone.
"I'm not sure there isn't something I can do, at least."
But at that moment Bill rang the bell. The minute he strolled into
the sitting-room Amy saw that Hortense's fate was sealed. Hortense
had waited all these years for love; but love had come to her now
with full hands, and would brook no denial.
Bill fought, indeed, against even delay. "I'm thirty-two years old
and my own master. Hortense and I can be married as soon as we
get a license and things. I'll take her back to New York with me;
and if she doesn't want to stay with my family, we can go to a hotel
until we find a place to live."
On that footing they argued all morning. Bill carried Hortense
and Amy and Dora off downtown for lunch; on their return, the
dispute was still going on.
He and Hortense would break into rapturous plans. They could
look for a house in New York as soon as they arrived; or they could
let that go while Bill arranged to be away from his business all sum-
mer. Then they could go to Europe for three months, and find a house
in New York in the fall.
So far they would proceed in unison, discovering with mounting
bliss that he liked all the things she liked, that he was three years
older than she-just the right amount-that he had always liked the
name Hortense, and how well it sounded with Jerome for a surname!
Then abruptly Hortense would fall into a different mood. They
mustn't be married in haste: he knew the proverb. She wanted to stay
on at the factory until the Colonel got his automobile into successful
operation. She didn't see how she could be married anyhow without
at least a few weeks to get her things in order.
When they returned to the house, Amy made tea for them. Dora
had enjoyed the day thoroughly; but after three cups of tea, she
decided that the dispute had been going on long enough.
"May I say just a word?" she asked. "I'm practically Hortense's
next of kin, you know."
AMY
227
"And I haven't even asked your permission to marry her!" ex-
claimed Bill. "I'm not quite sure I understood the relationship busi-
ness; but I was going to speak to the Colonel to-night."
"Speak to him if you want to," said Dora. "That's just a form.
He'll tell you that anything which suits Hortense is all right with
him. And I think we all agree, Hortense is the one to be suited?"
"Of course," said Bill, making a snatch at Hortense's hand.
"Then you go back to New York, Bill, and break the news to your
family," went on Dora. "Let Hortense have a month or two to get
ready. Then in June, have a proper wedding with all the trimmings."
"Is Hortense the kind of girl who cares much about trimmings?"
asked Bill.
"She may think she isn't. But it's nice afterward to be able to look
back, Bill, and remember your wedding-day as something special and
lovely."
"She's right," said Hortense suddenly.
"Yes, she's right," agreed Bill. But he looked a little puzzled. Dora
was such a charming spinster, he couldn't imagine her any other
way. But how could a chronic spinster know about things like look-
ing back on your wedding-day? How could she know even about
looking forward to it, as he was now looking forward to his?
Amy realized how Dora knew. But she didn't venture to say a
word to Dora afterward about long-past dreams and foregone hap-
piness. She didn't dare, even, to admire too pointedly the skill with
which Dora had waited until just the right moment, and then stepped
in and settled everything.
Because everything now appeared to be settled. Bill stayed three
days longer; but he gradually resigned himself to "a proper wedding
in June." He and Hortense bought the biggest solitaire diamond in
Cordiner & Morse's, and warned Mrs. Cordiner not to say a word
about their purchase. Mrs. Langworthy began to plan Hortense's
trousseau. The Colonel offered to give her her wedding.
He'd have given her a car, too, if he hadn't known that she'd
have to get out and push the thing. He had fallen into a vein of
jocularity about his business difficulties. Ruin staring him in the face
was less disconcerting, he held, than the chaos which greeted him
at home these days.
Anyhow, ruin never stared him in the face very long. Charley

Manning had turned up most opportunely in Mount Royal, had

The Colonel was hiring a graduate engineer now-something he

suffered with her.
Bill went back to New York and wrote Hortense twice a day.
Hortense shopped almost every afternoon with Mrs. Langworthy.
Between shopping-trips she stood to be fitted. Two dressmakers
held forth in her room every day now, and the whir of the sewing-
machine could be heard from morning to night.
Their presence at the family table annoyed the Colonel. "I can't
enjoy my meals with those two hatchet-faces at the table," he growled
to Amy. "Retta says she can't insult old Miss Peabody by making
her eat with the servants, and of course Mrs. Fisher's husband used
to be the Methodist minister here twenty years ago. But just watch-
ing Mrs. Fisher crook her little finger when she drinks her tea is
driving me to the point where I can't be responsible for my actions."
Retta would have said, "Don't watch her, then." But Amy herself
had a feeling that dressmakers carried too much gossip. Bess wasn't
a bit careful about what she said in front of them. Didn't she realize
that in a situation which if it were noised abroad would interest all
Mount Royal, it was just as well to have even Miss Peabody and
Mrs. Fisher on your side?
Amy arranged to have meals carried up to the two. It would save
them time, she said, now that they were so busy.
Presently she made tea for them every afternoon; and later on

she carried them soup in the middle of the morning. Their strength

The first week in May, Bill came back to Mount Royal for a visit.
He stayed at the Manning Arms, but he managed to interfere ter-
ribly with Hortense's fittings.
He entertained her downtown, and drove her about the country

of his ideas in coming here was to have the engagement publicly
announced during his stay. But also he felt a little more comfortable

He took pains to be cordial to Bess. He had brought her a message
from his sister, and a box of candy of a kind she had always liked
in New York. If Amy had had her way, she would have concealed
from Bess the fact that he also brought gifts to her and to Dora and
Mrs. Langworthy. There was no use of making the child feel bad,
especially now that she was making a real effort to smooth things

Bess was much brighter and more agreeable during the first five
days of Bill's visit. One evening she even got him to herself for a
few minutes while Hortense was dressing, and chatted to him gaily
and amusingly. Amy, hearing them laugh together, smiled with
relief.
She had forgotten how Bess overestimated any attention to herself.
Dora hadn't forgotten.
"She'll be calling him 'Poor Bill' the next thing we know," Dora

turned him down to begin with, and Hortense is his second choice."
"Perhaps that will be just as well," said Amy. "So long as she
behaves beautifully in front of other people-"
"She is improving," Dora agreed. "She has come to herself, and
knows she's licked."
They bolstered each other in that cheerful opinion. But they had

Bess had refused to admit to herself that she was licked. The

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
engagement wasn't announced; and that the announcement hung

Bess's information was faulty, but her spirit was stubborn. She

off his feet. He should be given a chance to declare himself.
The Colonel flatly refused to "speak to Bill." Bess burst into tears.
"You're all against me. You're against him, too. You can't see what's

Bill was puzzled at first. Mrs. Langworthy was straightforward,
polite, terse. It was Amy who heard their every sentence in an agony

Bill regretted if there had been any misunderstanding. He hadn't

asked Amy just above a whisper.
"I'll speak to her myself," said Mrs. Langworthy. "While I'm
doing it, I wish you'd attend to an errand for me downtown. I
want to send a note to Miss Pringle at the Beacon office."
Amy carried down to the Society Editor of the Mount Royal
Beacon Retta's signed statement that "Colonel and Mrs. Samuel
Langworthy announce the engagement of their niece Hortense
Gibson to Mr. William Stewart Jerome."
Bess didn't come down to supper that night. At the table Hor-
tense wore her engagement ring publicly for the first time.
"I suppose there's always a lot of turmoil in a family when the
girls get to the marrying age," Dora whispered to Amy.
Amy did not answer. She was too downcast for speech.
Only a few weeks ago she had been so happy for Hortense. But
now it seemed to her that the old peaceful days in that household
were gone forever. How good those days had been she never appreci-
ated until they vanished.
She wondered whether, after Hortense left them, Dora would take
Hortense's room. Dora had always wanted a room to herself, though
Dora never wanted anything for herself half so keenly as she wanted
things for Hortense.
Now, however, there would be nothing to stand in the way of her
232
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
having a separate room, and being as furiously tidy in it as she liked,
and keeping the light on all night if she wanted to. She had often
kept Amy awake, had sometimes made her feel crowded and domi-
nated. But at the mere idea that their joint tenure was threatened,
Amy felt unbearably lonely and bleak.

IV
ORTENSE was to be married the third week in June at the
pretty little Episcopal church which the Langworthys helped
to support but seldom attended. Mrs. Langworthy would have gone
to church oftener, she said, if the family could have rented a pew
the way they did back in Kentucky. She had never learned to feel
really at home in a church where Sunday after Sunday you were
seated by an usher. But the Millses were all communicants; and in
the case of anything like a wedding, that made it so much nicer than
if they had had to approach the fold as outsiders.
The wedding itself would be at noon: "high noon," the Mount
Royal Beacon was careful to say. At one o'clock the wedding-party
were to breakfast at the Langworthy house, and at three there would
be a reception.
"That's quite a lot of fuss," Dora remarked to Amy, "over such a
simple natural act as getting married."
The organization of the wedding-party presented difficulties. All
the girls who had made their débuts the same year as Hortense were
already married. Hortense didn't "want a lot of married women
chasing up the aisle ahead of" her; on the other hand she hesitated
to ask only younger girls to be her bridesmaids.
"I want Bess to be my maid-of-honor, of course," she said. "Won't
a maid-of-honor be enough even for a church wedding?"
"Certainly not," said Artie. "You're cheating the ushers if you
don't have pretty bridesmaids for them to beau around during the
festivities."
Bess demurred about being maid-of-honor. "I don't think Hor-
tense really wants me. She feels she has to ask me."
"You can't hold things up," said Mrs. Langworthy. "Miss Pringle
233
234
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
was out here this morning asking for the names of the wedding-

"Let's begin with the ushers," suggested Artie.
"All right. You can be usher number one yourself," Hortense de-

"We'll ask Doug by all means," said Hortense. "I didn't know he

from Albany to be his best man, and I believe said cousin is in his

By the time they had decided on a sixth usher, Bess was com-
mitted. She never would have let the wedding proceed without her,
Amy felt sure. But she objected to each suggestion of a bridesmaid;
she didn't like the color scheme Hortense had chosen for the brides-
maids' dresses, nor the way they were to be made. Hortense was
tall, and looked well in long straight things. But she needn't sup-
pose that just because she was getting married other people were
going to make guys of themselves in dresses which looked like
nightgowns.
The bridesmaid difficulty was solved piecemeal. Hortense insisted
on asking Agnes Chase because she was sorry for her. Then to keep
Agnes from guessing that that was why she had been asked, Hor-
tense included Agnes's older and very attractive sister Marie. Alice
Barnes and Hattie Weston came between Hortense and Bess in age;
they would fit in well enough. Trixie Moore, an old school friend
with whom Hortense still corresponded, wrote that she would be
"thrilled" to be a bridesmaid. Bess insisted on the inclusion of
Marguerite Carpenter, though Marguerite was not a special friend of
Hortense, nor even of Bess herself; the Carpenters were right at the
heart of the Prospect Avenue set.

The day the invitations were mailed the "pre-nuptial festivities"
began. Amy remembered very well that when Retta was married, a
bride was supposed to remain "in seclusion" from the time the invi-
tations were issued until the day of the wedding itself.
But times had changed. There were four bridesmaids' parties for
Hortense. Amy and Dora gave her a luncheon at the Mount Royal
Hotel. Amy had felt that "entertaining downtown" was a second-
class sort of hospitality, but Dora decided that there was a limit be-
yond which even the Langworthy resources could not be strained;
and the maid-of-honor's luncheon for the bride would have to be
given at the house.
The matrons who had been débutantes with Hortense took their
turns. The Carpenters decided to give a small dance for the ushers
and the bridesmaids the day after Trixie Moore reached Mount
Royal. Trixie stayed with the Chase girls; and in addition to their
luncheon for Hortense, Marie and Agnes gave an afternoon party
for Trixie.
"I believe there isn't a house in Mount Royal that isn't being up-
set for Hortense's wedding," said Artie proudly.
Hortense had been too busy this past year to have many new
clothes made. For this round of entertaining she was compelled to
borrow from her trousseau, though Amy had always supposed that
a trousseau was inviolate until after the wedding. But, at any rate,
Hortense wore nothing except new clothes these days; and Dora
took her breakfast to her room every morning. The long months
when Hortense had faithfully risen to the sound of an alarm clock
had receded into a past which was already beginning to seem distant.
Miss Peabody and Mrs. Fisher stayed until ten every night now,
sewing like mad. Bess's maid-of-honor dress was being made down-
town. But the Colonel had insisted that Dora and Amy, as well as
Mrs. Langworthy, blossom out for the wedding.
Dora chose a demure pearl gray, leaving lavender for Mrs. Lang-
worthy. Against her better judgment, Amy was talked into buying
a soft taffeta of an apricot shade. She didn't know where she could
ever wear such a dress after the wedding. But the Colonel was pay-
ing for all this new finery; and he announced that he wanted "the
Mills girls to look their best, and show up these young snips who
think everybody will be staring at them."

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Mrs. Langworthy was the hub around which all the wedding
plans revolved. It was always difficult for her to get started on her
entertaining; but when the occasion sought her out, she loved doing
things on a grand scale. She looked extremely tired these days;
Amy could not remember ever seeing Retta look tired before. But
from morning to bedtime she whirled like a gigantic humming top;
she was much more excited about the wedding plans than Hortense
herself.
The weeks of frantic entertaining, indeed, reduced Hortense to
a trancelike state. When Bill and his cousin from Albany, Milton
Stewart, arrived in Mount Royal two days before the wedding, she
whispered against Bill's necktie, "I couldn't go through all this a
second time even for you, darling. If I have to walk up to just one
more luncheon-table and see five forks on one side of my plate and
six spoons on the other, I shall simply go into hysterics."
But Bill's presence restored her courage; she decided she was too
tired even to pay attention to her own nerves. That evening Milton
Stewart entertained the whole bridal party downtown; and full in
the face of six forks and seven spoons, Hortense said gaily, "Cousin
Milton, why don't you come back in a few weeks and marry Bess?
Then we can have this fun all over."
They had had the wedding rehearsal on the day Bill arrived. The
day before the wedding was reserved for the two most important
parties. Bess was giving a luncheon for Hortense, and Bill would
entertain the ushers and best man at a "bachelor dinner" where the
bride's health was to be proposed in champagne he had brought
with him from New York.
Hortense had said to Bess, "Of course the aunts will sit down with
us at your luncheon, Bess?"
"Of course not," said Dora. "This is a luncheon just for the bridal
party."
"But if Hortense wants you, Auntie Dora?" said Bess.
"I certainly do want you." Hortense's lip quivered a little; she
was remembering that the very next day she was going far away
from Dora, and it might be a long time before they sat down to the
table together again.
"That will make eleven, though. It would unbalance your table,"
objected Amy. Privately she had decided to get out of this party if she

could. The undercurrents of which she was conscious had rather

Even in that house of handsome table appointments and beautiful

which Hortense herself had painted a year ago with no idea that
they would ever be used for their present purpose, each carried a

Amy expressed her admiration. She was gratified, too, to note that
her own place was set with her back to the light. Not that anybody
would be looking at her to-day; but she hated to think how she must

Amy had lived under this roof during the tense months before

The first carriage-load of guests arrived: the Chase girls and Trixie
Moore. Bess came running down the stairs to greet them.
Hortense followed more slowly. From these very stairs to-morrow
she would toss her wedding bouquet among these girls. She, Hor-
tense, who had been trying so desperately of late years to adapt
herself to a background position, was the center of her little world
from now until the moment when she was to bid it farewell.
The chattering, giggling group gathered in the parlor. Dora made
one of them easily.
Amy sat down next to Agnes Chase. A moment later she reflected
that this was one of the things which had made Agnes what she
was: too much careful consideration from, older women. You might
as well tell her right out that she was a little peculiar.
Oh, dear, youth was a very difficult season! Her own age was
much better, as Dora had pointed out a few minutes ago. And
Hortense was now embarking on the best years of her life.
Amy must keep thinking how happy Hortense was going to be:
must never let her mind wander ahead to to-morrow night and
next week, when Hortense would be gone from them. It is always
easier to be the one who goes. That had never been Amy's lot in
life. Of course, it never would be. But this wasn't any kind of thing
to be thinking at a party.
They went out to the dining-room presently. There were "Oh's"
and "Ah's" over the table. Hortense's place-cards were duly admired.
How many times in the last two years had Hortense's place-cards
served to start off the conversation at a luncheon-table in Mount
AMY
241
Royal! If Hortense ever painted any more cards, it would be for
her own table.
Amy hoped Bill would encourage Hortense to keep on with her
painting. He appreciated her music, of course; but sometimes hus-
bands were less encouraging than they might be. Hortense would
get a chance to hear some very good music in New York. Amy
would have liked more good music than she ever got a chance to hear.
Did things like that count heavily in a life? They weren't supposed
to; but you couldn't help noticing.
Under the influence of a bountiful luncheon Amy relaxed a little.
All these past days when Hortense had been learning to dread the
sight of a set table (and Bess had actually been sick one night from
too much party), Amy had been too distracted to eat. She managed
to do very well with her lobster mousse and her frogs' legs, how-
ever; and she enjoyed the fruit salad which had been so prettily
arranged on Retta's newest salad plates.
Ida brought in a great cake for Hortense to cut. Just as she was
setting it down the door from the parlor into the hall blew open
and admitted a draft which was disturbing even on a June day. The
curtains in the dining-room blew sharply against the screens, and
in the parlor a fan fell from a table.
Amy tried to catch Ida's eye, but she was intent on the cake.
Louise had baked a thimble and a ring and a coin in it; Ida wanted
to report back in the kitchen just which guest had drawn which

Amy slipped from the table and went across the parlor to shut
the door. Then she saw why it had blown open just then. Dr. Simpson
was letting himself out at the front door.
He had never before had to let himself out in that house. With
the speed of panic, Amy slipped out of the parlor and followed him.
When she returned, no one had missed her. Dr. Simpson had been
most encouraging. "Mrs. Langworthy is tired out, Miss Amy. She
has reached the age where she ought to be a little careful of herself.
I'm giving her something to get her through the big day to-morrow.
After that, I'll have to enlist your help to make her rest."
Retta was tired out, of course. She had showed sound sense in
calling the doctor before she got down sick. Dr. Simpson was in
that house twenty times a year, to prescribe a gargle for somebody,
242
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
or to suggest soda mint after meals. He had been most encouraging,
really. But when Amy tried to take a drink of water, the glass
clicked against her teeth.
She didn't know what would become of them all if anything hap-
pened to Retta. For years Retta had been the mainstay of everybody
under that roof.
How strangely she had looked at Bess in this very room a few
hours ago! Amy had surprised that look without interpreting it at
the time. Now she realized that Retta was frightened. The very
thought of Retta's fear made Amy feel queer and dizzy.

RTIE MILLS, glancing out of the corners of his eyes to be sure

and perennial larkspur. The congregation rustled to its feet; and
the other ushers advanced with perfect timing, thanks to the fact
that they had a good leader.
It was a great moment for Artie when he headed the procession
at Hortense's wedding. By asking him to be chief usher, Hortense
had also disposed of the perplexing question of who was to give her
away.
When Colonel Langworthy had performed that duty, he went to
stand beside the women of the family in the front pew. Mrs. Lang-
worthy was crying softly.
"Oh, Sam!" she whispered. "If my sister Margaret could have lived
to see this!"
Next to the middle aisle Dora stood with her head high. “Hor-
tense belonged to me," she was thinking fiercely. "I'll never say a
word to Colonel about it. It's not his fault the Church is so stupid
about women. But if there was any giving away to be done, Hortense
was mine to give."
Between Mrs. Langworthy and Dora, Amy was moved by the
beauty of the scene. But it seemed to her far away and unreal, like
a wedding in a play.
Two of the bridesmaids wore pink, two pale blue, two a delicate
green; they all carried armfuls of long-stemmed roses. Bess, in deeper
pink, was easily the prettiest of the attendants, as well as the most
self-assured. She advanced to take Hortense's bouquet, restored it at

"Why, it's all over!" thought Amy with a sense of shock. "We've

There was still the breakfast to be got through, of course, and
the reception. But from the moment when they got home from the

Hortense had wanted to pay for her own wedding; she could sell
a bond, now that there was no longer any special need for hanging

"Retta wouldn't think of allowing such a thing. We'd give you your

The Chicago caterer who had proved so satisfactory for Bess's

Ida was upstairs to look after the guests' wraps. Louise had stationed

The wedding-party had breakfasted at the dining-table, drawn

strangers around her makes me feel as if they were stealing Hortense

Mrs. Langworthy herself was the gayest of the gay. Amy never

before to write a series of jingles on the members of the wedding-

were passed around with the coffee. Led by Artie, the party sang
through the rhymes three times. Retta laughed until the tears ran
down her cheeks.
During the reception she stood in the line just beyond the bride
and groom. Ordinarily Retta could endure just about so much stand-
ing, in line or out of it; then she looked for a chair.
But to-day when she left the newly married couple to shift for
themselves, she circulated through all the rooms and talked to every-
body. When the party began to overflow to the veranda and the
lawn, she followed it out. Then she returned to make a second
circuit of the dining-room, the library, the sitting-room where Amy
was asking for the three-hundredth time, "Have you been served
yet?"
In the old dining-room behind the sitting-room Bess sat sur-

own; and to be sure there were enough of the rest of them to attend
to the guests. Mrs. Langworthy beamed on her. Bess had been at
her charming best to-day, and it was nice to feel that bygones were
bygones.

pled and slipped to the floor.
The Colonel came rushing in, looking as if the house had fallen.
Amy glanced up from where she was bending over Retta. "It's

"She doesn't want one. Don't upset her by insisting."

drew Amy into the hall with him. "Amy, she really is all right? It's
just excitement, I'm sure it is. And of course she's been getting her-

He was almost too eager for reassurance; and after all he was the
woman's husband, and supposed to keep his wits about him. Some-

"I'm afraid there's something really wrong with Retta," she said
sharply. "Dora has noticed it too. She hasn't seemed just like herself
lately."

"But nobody has said a word to me about it!"

they seemed very distant to Amy in their comfortable taking things

"Much better," said Amy firmly.

"She has always had wonderful health," Amy agreed.
"A big strong woman!" he repeated. He stood looking down at
Amy; then in a different tone he added, "She would make two of
you this minute, Amy."
He looked embarrassed when he heard his own words, as if he
hadn't actually intended to say them. Amy shrank away from him.
It wasn't the reference to her own fragility which had wounded her.
It was something beyond that: something she was quite unwilling
to put a name to.

ORTENSE and Bill had gone to Bar Harbor for their honey-
moon. "We may go to Europe another summer," Hortense
wrote. "But right now we're anxious to get back to New York and
begin housekeeping."
Bill left her in July long enough to rent a house which his father
had found for them: a house in the East Twenties, with double
drawing-rooms on the first floor.
"Now where would that put her dining-room?" Mrs. Langworthy
looked up from the letter to ask. She held that the best and largest
room in a house should always be used as the dining-room.
"In the basement, probably," said Bess. "Many of the nicest houses
in New York have basement dining-rooms. She sounds as if they
were flying high. It's funny to think of Hortense with an establish-
ment like that, isn't it?"
"Hortense appreciates nice things," said Dora. "In fact, she's the
most luxury-loving pup I ever saw in all my born days. But I agree
with you, Bess, there's a queer side to their setting up in such style.
It's a funny idea to tie yourself down to a lot of chairs and tables
when you begin a new life-especially when they might have gone
to Europe instead."
"Why don't you go somewhere yourself?" asked the Colonel.
"Retta needs a rest. You and she might go together on a trip. What
say, Dora?"
Mrs. Langworthy refused to budge from the house. She could
rest better at home than anywhere else, she said. But Dora took Bess
for the month of August to a summer resort on a Wisconsin lake.
The family all acted as if they ought to make up to Bess for some-
thing.
The day after Bess and Dora left, Amy found out that her sur-

mises were correct. For months Mrs. Langworthy had suspected

In the midst of the wedding preparations she was alarmed by new

Retta put off the consultation from week to week; she insisted she
was "feeling better." Then one night she was prostrated by pain;
she had had no sharp pain before.
The Colonel was alarmed and took the situation in hand. In two

"But there's no hurry," she said. "I think I'll wait until the weather

"And when Dora knows, Bess will have to know," said Mrs. Lang-

The Colonel yielded; to lug Bess into an argument was always to

would not get better if they let her alone. Sick people often recov-
ered in the most surprising way; but this modern fad for operations
was claiming fresh victims daily.
Only last winter Dora had summarized the official explanations
after the successive deaths in hospital of three people they all knew.
"The verdict always seems to be, 'The operation was successful, but
the patient died of the shock.' I don't understand what doctors mean
by a 'successful' operation. The funeral costs the family just as much
as if the patient had died of the original ailment."
Anyhow, the decision rested with Retta. Dr. Simpson agreed that

a few weeks' delay could do no harm, unless her malady took an

Jason looked pityingly at Amy. He was often sorry for "the aunts."

The Colonel interrupted to find out how much Retta had told

Primarily she was worried. "You worry so systematically," teased

now. For a year or so after the operation she will have to be pretty

Preparation for the parties was a distraction, Amy acknowledged.
The Langworthy house always seemed most truly itself when it was

The Colonel liked the bustle, too. He hadn't half finished paying

"Retta is going to get herself all tired out," he confided to Amy.

Amy was always being lugged in to get somebody to see reason;

Hortense when it comes to a task like writing invitations. The rest

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
too small. Before long that is going to be our principal trouble.

The Colonel's moods of unreasoning optimism always abashed

Yet for all Amy knew to the contrary, he might do just that. With

That is, she was supposed to get along with the flowers from the

Bess carried each bouquet in turn. She danced every dance; Julia
Wiley was out of the way now, and Agnes Chase had refused her

257
the Cordiners. Miss Pringle could remember when Mrs. Cordiner

Mrs. Langworthy remembered how at Hortense's reception she
had disgraced the family by fainting in front of most of these very

ready to bring a chair or a glass of water at the first sign of ex-

She had not been so light-hearted in years as she felt this after-

her worrying for her. Amy was a goose. A darling, to be sure, in
fact quite the nicest member of the family. But still a goose. One
of those lookers-on at life who manage to take it harder than the
actual contestants: Amy had been a peculiarly sheltered woman,
and that gave her plenty of time to distress herself about other peo-
ple's troubles.
When the last guest had left, the family gathered in the dining-
room. Mrs. Langworthy tucked away two or three helpings of every-
thing. She supposed they would keep her short at the hospital; prob-
ably she wouldn't like their cooking, either. Anyhow, there was no
time like the present.
Artie wandered forth presently to gloat over his grapes. The Colo-
nel turned his back on the ladies so that he could blow his cigar
smoke out of the window.
All the afternoon's harvest of gossip had been threshed over, and
the success of the party commented on from every angle. Bess began
to talk about the new clothes she would need for fall.
Perhaps that was her idea of cheering up her mother; she wanted
to sound as if the success of Retta's business was assured. But Amy,
who had been blessedly half numb for the last three days, woke to
258
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
shocked surprise that Bess could even think about clothes at a time

The last barrier between them and the dreaded future was down
now. To-morrow right after dinner Retta was to drive over to the
hospital; the operation was set for eight the following morning.
Eight o'clock on Friday-and this was Wednesday evening! Retta
looked so happy, so well, so substantial in her handsome brocade
dress. And thirty-six hours from now Retta wouldn't be the center
of this happy circle, wouldn't be a woman at all, really: she'd be
simply something the doctors were working on, a-what was her
own horrid word for it? -a "carcass."
Amy left her chair and went to stand beside the Colonel. "It's
going to be splendid when everything comes out together," she mur-

coming out marvelously."
He looked up gratefully; he had had more than one reason for

That was little enough to manage, Amy thought bitterly. If she
could have believed the right thing herself, instead of simply knowing
what it was and giving it utterance, she would have been entitled to
real respect.
But her private sentiment was that all this entertaining had a hor-
ribly valedictory flavor. The very fact that Retta herself was so cheer-
ful struck Amy now as all wrong. If Retta had ceased even partially
to dread what lay ahead of her, her sharp impulsive clasp on life was
weakening. Retta had always been a person who snatched what she
wanted.
Years ago, and once too often, Retta had snatched. Even to herself
Amy had never admitted the true inwardness of that transaction.
But if anything were to happen to Retta now, Amy would be doubly
cheated.
So many times she had said the right thing, and tried to feel it.
So many times she had felt instead this same deadly weariness surg-
ing over her. Yet she had never been so weary as she was to-night.
She would gladly have changed places with Retta to-morrow-and
not from pure love of Retta, either. For too many years Amy had
AMY
259
been a looker-on at a game which was only a sickening disappoint-
ment.
She still had to write to Hortense to-night, however. The letter
must go to-morrow morning, in order to arrive in New York soon
after their Friday's telegram. Amy had agreed with Dora that deliv-
ery of the letter need not precede the telegram, so long as they could
clear their skirts by wiring, "Letter on the way."
Perhaps that arrangement was false kindness. Kindness so often is
false. But Amy could not relinquish a lifetime's habits just because
of her deadly distrust. She was writing at Dora's behest; but she
would write that letter herself, so that Dora need not write it.

VII
HE "new" hospital had been built about five years before,
through the generosity of private citizens. The committee who
raised the subscriptions had also passed on the plans; and a good
many Mount Royal doctors thought the layout eccentric and imprac-
tical, though it was modeled on a famous hospital in the East. Four
separate three-story structures were connected only by corridors; in
each unit there was a public ward on the first floor, with private
rooms above it. The building was hard to heat and costly to clean;
and the internes walked additional miles every day through the
drafty corridors.
The public entrance was in the second building from the right.
Off the main hall opened the office, the dispensary, and a small
waiting-room. The hall itself was paved with squares of white and
black marble, and the walls were frescoed in panels; each panel
painting showed an urn full of flowers.
"A Roman villa," Dora commented when the Langworthy party
entered on Friday morning. "A Roman villa out of Pompeii, I sup-
pose. Or maybe it's really a Pompeian villa out of Bulwer Lytton."
"They brought an Italian artist all the way from New York to do
those frescoes," Artie informed her.
"So I heard," said Dora. "But I can't see that they're a bit better
than the panels Hortense used to paint."
The panels Hortense had painted for the space above the mantle
in Mrs. Langworthy's room had never been mounted; they still stood
in a row waiting for her to make arrangements for their installation.
It flashed across Amy now that she wondered whether they ever
would be mounted.
The feeling of sickness which the thought roused in her must have
showed in her face. She saw Dora look at her anxiously. "It's just the
260
AMY
261
smell," she whispered. "The funny smell they always have around
hospitals."
"Iodoform," said Dora.
"Doc Simpson always smells of iodoform," said Artie. "I think he
bathes in it. Old Doc Ryan back home used to smell of the stables.
That was preferable, if you ask me."
"Please, Uncle Artie!" said Bess.
The Colonel, who had been interviewing an interne, joined the
rest of them. "They took her to the operating-room at eight," he
reported. "What was her idea in not letting any of us see her this
morning, Amy?"
"She was afraid we might make her nervous," said Amy.
As a matter of fact, Mrs. Langworthy had wanted Dora to be with
her that morning before she went to the operating-room. But when
she mentioned it, the Colonel acted hurt because she asked for her
sister instead of her husband. So Retta had hastily altered her request.
She didn't need anybody, she said; they must all have their night's
sleep out, and eat a proper breakfast.
The Colonel, alone in his bedroom for the first time in many years,
had scarcely slept at all. At six he roused the household; the landau
was at the door before seven. But Amy had purposely delayed the
hour of their departure.
The Colonel had been walking on tiptoe ever since they entered
the hospital. He thought he was bearing up bravely; but his every
movement betrayed an anxiety bordering on panic.
"You and Artie might as well go outdoors and smoke a cigar,"
Dora suggested crisply.
"I don't care for a cigar just now," said the Colonel.
"I wish I had learned to smoke," said Dora. "It must be a help
sometimes."
"You did smoke one of Papa's cigars once, when you were about
seventeen," said Amy. "I remember watching you do it. I was hor-
rified."
"First I've ever heard of that," commented Artie. "Didn't it make
you sick, Dora?"
"I rather liked it," said Dora. "But I was sitting opposite a mirror
while I smoked; and I could see how silly I looked."
"Those were great days, when you girls were all young together,"
262
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
said the Colonel. "I remember the first time I was ever in your house.
I had never seen four such pretty creatures before."
He stopped his tiptoe pacing. The recollection of those old days
had soothed him; at this distance the past seemed so safe and warm
and comfortable. The Colonel never remembered unpleasant details,
unless they happened to strike him as humorous.
"Young men always went into spasms about my sisters," said

"As a matter of fact," said Dora, "you must have been the only

Ordinarily when the Colonel thought of those old days in Ken-

present: indeed, they led quite pointedly up to Bess. To-day Bess
didn't seem to him as important as usual.
She was holding onto herself very well, however. In the landau
she had insisted on sitting in the front seat with her father, who had
taken the reins himself to save space. She had been persistently cheer-
ful and impersonal. Of course she had set herself to play a part to-

had been talking to her after breakfast, too. Bess would always take
more from Dora than from Amy.
Now she said, "Everybody knows that women age faster than men
AMY
263
do, Uncle Artie." She fluttered her eyelashes at him, and Artie

The Colonel tiptoed out into the hall, found a nurse, made sure
once more that he would be notified as soon as there was anything
to learn. Returning, he tried to slip back once more into the memory
of those golden days when his generation had all been young to-

past so vividly that it had managed to contradict the uncomfortable
present.
On the threshold of recollection, however, he was met by an
uneasy misgiving and doubt, powerful but vague. It was as if there
were something he wished to remember, and couldn't quite recap-
ture. Did other people remember their past completely and at will?
His seemed to come to him in shimmering fragments.
Yet he had a sensation now an impression so strong that it was
almost a physical reaction that it was frightfully important for him
to grasp this elusive something. In disgust he shook himself free of
the queer compulsion. How could he bother his head with dim forms
out of the past when this very minute, somewhere above his head,
the issue of life or death was shaping for Retta?
"I think I will go outdoors," he said. "Any of you want to come
with me?"
Artie went with him; Artie was bent on cheering him up. After
five minutes of Artie's cheer, the Colonel gave him a cigar and sent
him to smoke it over near where the horses were tied. He himself
lingered on the steps of the main building; he was unwilling to
move even a few rods from the news he was waiting for.
It was after nine now. The Colonel had heard gruesome stories of
operations which lasted three and even four hours. He wondered
how the relatives ever managed to live through an ordeal like that.
The patient, of course, didn't know what was going on. That was
an odd idea: to come to yourself with a piece gone out of the day,
and be forever unable to account for it. He didn't suppose, however,
that that hole in time would bother Retta. She wasn't fanciful.
She had always hated pain, though. Women were supposed to
bear pain better than men; but Retta had never succeeded in steeling
264
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
herself even against discomfort. Oh, well, they knew how to make
things easy for the patient nowadays! He recalled an amputation
which he had once seen performed in a field hospital during the
War: they gave the patient a tumblerful of whiskey, and then cut off
his leg. The Army surgeons were as good as any you'd find. After
those amputations the patient often died, though-and no wonder.
Bess came to stand beside him for a moment and report that there
was no word yet. Then she strolled across to where Artie was finish-
ing his cigar. He began at once to talk to her, and Bess responded
gaily. Keeping up her end, of course-and incidentally keeping up
her practice on Artie.
It flashed across the Colonel's mind that it was too bad Artie never
had any money. He would have loved buyings things for Bess; and
of course it would have been fun for Bess to have an uncle whom
she could tap for luxuries. As it was, she too often snubbed poor old
Artie. When you weren't so young any more, people never had the
same respect for you. But they still respected your money.
Older men should always have money. The family had been right in
talking the Colonel out of that idea of retirement to the Soldiers'
Home. He was going to have money in considerable quantity soon.
He'd need it, too. This business of Retta's could be expected to cost
a lot; and already he had unpaid bills enough to pave the way from
his house to the factory.
At ten o'clock Dora came out to reassure him. It was taking longer
than they had expected; but Dr. Simpson had come downstairs just
now to say that they were nearing the end.
The Colonel could just as well have gone indoors then. But he
waited on the steps while Dora went across to speak to Bess and
Artie. So it happened that Amy caught him there. She came noise-
lessly from behind, appearing at his elbow so suddenly that he
started.
"It's all right," she said quickly. "They finished a minute ago."
"They-finished?"
"Yes, it's all over. The operation is a success. But it took longer
than they expected. She has a wonderful heart, or she couldn't have
stood it."
The Colonel stared at her and licked his dry lips. The strain had
AMY
265
gone on so long, he couldn't believe that it was over. It was over, and
the operation had succeeded. He had believed that it would succeed,

"Aren't you going to tell them?" The Colonel jerked his head

"A willow wand like you would have a nice time supporting me in

Amy smiled, too. That joyous smile of Amy's, which began mys-

which were usually veiled so quickly, looked into his now for a full

And all at once he remembered what it was that he had tried to

But the memory returned and would not be denied: returned so

He had been taken home in the first place by Papa, had met all
the Mills girls, had been delighted and somewhat bewildered by their
looks, their gaiety, the mere fact that there were so many of them.
Soon enough, however, they sorted themselves out for his benefit.
Dora was in love with another man; and Margaret, all unconscious
of Alec Gibson in her immediate future, was turning up a coquettish
nose at every new-comer.
Naturally young Colonel Langworthy fell to the lot of bold, black-
eyed Retta, oldest and most dashing of the girls. He was carried
along on the high tide of immediate interest; it never occurred to
266
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
him that in "matters of the heart" a man could be so calculating as
to try and use his judgment.
One evening when he had pretty well settled down to Retta, but
hadn't actually proposed to her, he was shown just before the spring
twilight into what he supposed was an empty drawing-room. But as
he crossed toward the sofa, Amy turned away from the window
where she had been watching the last of the daylight fade. She
looked him straight in the eye; and although her back was now
toward the light, so direct and startling was the impact of her glance
that the Colonel actually thought she had spoken to him.
He stammered, "Wh-what?" Amy's eyelids fell, and she made
no answer.
"I-I thought you spoke," he went on. "I didn't know there was
anybody here."
"I'm just going," she murmured.
"Oh, please don't! I can just as well wait in the hall. Er-I mean,

She shook her head, smiling faintly; she lifted her eyes to his,
waited for him to go on. But to save his life young Sam Langworthy
could not have gone on.
He had never thought of Amy except as the school-girl sister, the
quiet one who always got out of the room when the others began to
giggle and flirt. Now he saw the sweet curve of her young neck, and
marked where, veiling itself in her dress, it ran on into the lines of
her breast. Her quick blood showed through her transparent skin.
There was a faint scent of fresh violets about her; and her thick soft
hair looked as if it would be warm when you touched it.
Sam stood in confusion, the blood pounding in his ears, a queer
dryness in his throat. Then he took one step toward her, though no
definite purpose had yet resolved itself from his bewilderment. But
he had delayed too long. She passed him and almost ran out of the
room.
Afterward he went straight ahead in his courtship of Retta. For
some time he felt embarrassed in Amy's presence; she must think
he had queer manners, and no conversational resources. Then the
awkward little episode was forgotten completely.
To-day of all days, it came back; and to-day he saw the meaning
AMY
267
he had shrunk from acknowledging at the time. It was Amy whom
he had loved in the beginning. It was Amy whom he should have
gone on loving through the years of his vigorous manhood.
Amy had lived all this time in his household, turning from fresh
girlhood to the estate of maiden lady under his imperceptive eyes.
Amy came to him now, kind and sisterly, to tell him that Retta
would recover and come back to be his wife again.
Across the sunny open space in front of the hospital Bess was just

in life to him. Bess, who had always hurt him a little by not being
kinder to Amy. When Bess was a baby, he had rejoiced to feel that
Amy had a special affection for the child. At the time he had not
even paused to wonder why that idea should give him such pleasure.
Dread of having to talk to Bess just now made him find his
tongue. "I'm not going to collapse. But I think I will go indoors and
sit down. You tell Bess the good news."
Indoors he encountered Dr. Simpson, who came at him with de-
tails to which the Colonel listened politely without quite taking them
in. The gist of them was plain enough, however. They had no rea-
son for further anxiety about Retta.
Everything was gained, then everything for which he had been
hoping so desperately only a few minutes ago. The Colonel won-

Dora came in to resolve that perplexity for him. She swept him

actually than it had been all these years; but he had grown used to
staring at it with the dull eyes of custom.

now if I were you. The next hour or two she'll be too sick to see

"Drive? Drive where?" asked the Colonel stupidly.

His family! And in that family, Retta was the central figure.

interest to others. They'd have happy times together once more, now

thirty years ago!
But to-day he didn't want to be alone with Amy. Poor exquisite
Amy, who could have loved so passionately and so enduringly! Life

him.
Amy was being punished for his sins. But enough of that! He
didn't want to be alone with his thoughts to-day. He was lucky to
have Dora to fall back on. Dora sometimes irritated him, but she
was always amusing.
That was a good idea of hers that they might drive out to the

Governor; it would be odd to walk in there at the Home practically
as a stranger. The bitterness of that adjustment might take his mind
off his greater misery.

Nothing went right in this world. But so far he had always had
