I
THEN Hortense was settled in her house in New York, she
wrote to ask whether she might have the big Russian poodle
sent on to her. Officially Czar had always belonged to Hortense. But
that he might some day leave the household which he had entered
as a sprawling puppy had never occurred to the Langworthys. In
his dignified and somewhat rheumatic old age, however, he was now
condemned to life in a big city.
Artie was delegated to take the dog as far as Chicago and bribe
the expressman on the New York train to have a careful eye out for
him. To Dora there was something pathetic in their departure to-
gether: they both had so much of the same air of determined jaun-
tiness.
Artie saw nothing pathetic in his errand. He felt quite important;
and he was delighted to learn that Hortense in New York should
still feel a hankering for some of the familiar features of her life in
Mount Royal.
About a month later, however, the spaniel died. He was much
younger than either Czar or Bob the collie, who had gone the way
of good dogs about a year before. Beppo had been ailing for some
time; and the Colonel was relieved not to have to make the decision
which Dora had urged, and turn the poor thing over to a veterinary
for chloroforming.
Artie was disconsolate. He complained to Amy, "The house just
doesn't seem right without a single dog in it."
"We might get a puppy to amuse Retta," said Amy doubtfully.
But Dora negatived that idea. The house must be kept as quiet
as possible for Retta. Anyhow, Amy, who always assumed the care
of the pet animals, couldn't be bothered right now to train and wait
on a puppy. Retta had had a trained nurse for the first fortnight after
270
ARTIE
271
she came back from the hospital. But when the nurse was dismissed,
much of the care of the invalid fell on Amy.
Dora had taken over the direction of the housekeeping. It ran
more smoothly for her than it had ever run under the superintendence
of Mrs. Langworthy. But the old hearty haphazard way had had its
advantages, if only because they were all used to it. With Retta com-
ing downstairs for only one meal a day, the heart seemed to be gone
out of the household.
Mrs. Langworthy was getting better, however. The Colonel chafed
at the slowness of her recovery, but Artie took pains to make him see
that a slow recovery was probably more certain. Artie didn't think
Amy stressed Retta's gains as she should. Amy took life too seriously.
At first Hortense had had Eaton's send fresh flowers to Retta's
sick-room every day. In November she dropped down to once a
week. That showed something, didn't it? And after Christmas The
Bee met one afternoon in Retta's room; and Retta was tired by the
afternoon's gabble, but visibly in better spirits.
Bess was playing around with Doug Rutherford again. But she
seemed in no hurry to settle down to Doug alone. Indeed, early in the
new year she suggested that she might go to New York for a month
and visit Hortense.
Artie was jubilant over the idea. In his eyes it proved that the rift
between the two girls was completely healed. The Colonel would have
let Bess go; but Dora and Amy were prompt with their vetoes.
"Retta likes to have Bess here. Until Retta is herself again, Bess
ought to stay with her," Dora said decisively.
"Retta will be better when warm weather comes, I'm sure," added
Amy. "She always has felt the cold; and now it seems as if we can't
get the house hot enough for her."
Artie thought of a plan which would get around every objection.
It was a splendid scheme, if he did say so himself. Why shouldn't
Bess take her mother South during the bleakest part of the winter?
That would do them both good.
"I think we might manage it," said the Colonel. "Bess does need a
change."
But Dr. Simpson pronounced that Mrs. Langworthy wasn't yet
strong enough to stand a long journey. He didn't even say he thought
it was a good idea; he just turned thumbs down on it. Artie was left
272
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
to console himself by the reflection that with Bess and Retta both
away, the rest of them would have rattled in that big house.
During that winter the Colonel was away much of the time. The
engine had always been built outside of his factory; now that he
needed a heavier car, the greater part of the car, too, was manu-
factured elsewhere. Artie fell into the habit of going over to the
factory a good deal, to help out in the increasing complexity.
Most of his best ideas the Colonel disregarded; he was actually
snappish, indeed, when Artie asked him why pending the perfection
of the Langworthy car he didn't get the Mount Royal agency for an
established make like the Rambler or the Pope. But Artie answered
the telephone and wrote letters and smoked cigarettes with the en-
gineer. He felt that he was "back in harness again" after his years of
looking around.
"I do like being busy," he said importantly to Amy.
"I think it's fine that you're taking Hortense's place," said Amy.
Hortense's place, indeed! But then, that was all you could expect a
woman like Amy to know about business.
There were no musical evenings now. When the Colonel was at
home, he and Artie often sat late over their cigars in the library. If
he was not smoking with Artie, the Colonel worked at his desk.
Mrs. Langworthy was always established for the night at eight or
half-past. The Colonel had offered to sleep in Hortense's old room,
so that his late retirings and early risings would not disturb her. But
Mrs. Langworthy was indignant at the notion.
"You needn't be turned out of your own room," she said, "just
because Dr. Simpson decided last fall that his house needs a new
roof."
In flashes like that she was quite the old Retta. In Artie's eyes his
sisters hadn't changed anyhow since he could remember. But the
Colonel was different lately: always preoccupied and often short-
tempered.
He didn't sit with Dora and Amy when they came downstairs
after establishing Retta for the night; yet surely he would have been
the better for even that much change from his everlasting desk-work
and too many cigars. He had never cultivated cronies of his own;
now that the Langworthys were not entertaining as a family, he was
practically deserted.
ARTIE
273
He was civil enough if Artie brought in his friends of an evening.
But under the Colonel's eye Artie's friends seemed uneasy. Artie took
to entertaining down in the cellar, where he was closer to his store
of home-made brandy. He kept it under lock and key; but his friends
were welcome to share it with him.
Later that winter, however, he bought a second padlock for his
tool-chest, and started a hoard to which his friends were not welcome.
The Colonel, who had pressed money on Hortense when she went
over to the factory with him, had not been so thoughtful of Artie.
Indeed, when Artie went so far as to give him a hint, he had ex-
plained that later on maybe he could do something for his brother-
in-law, but right now money was short. Artie was still in charge of
the garden and stable, however; and he managed the feed bills and
returned some tools in such a way as to give him a small secret fund.
This money he put into good Kentucky Bourbon, which he locked
up in his tool-chest. He was ashamed of the way he had acquired
that whiskey. Shame and pride united to keep him from touching it.
The family sometimes acted as if they thought he drank. If they
had only known it, he could keep the stuff by him for months and
never touch it. But one of these days, when his conscience had eased
off a little, he intended to have a go at that whiskey; and the party
would be strictly private. There was no good in handing a thing to
other people when you would appreciate it more than they would
anyhow.
The dreariest winter wears somehow through to spring. Days of
bright sunshine began to alternate with days of driving determined
rain. Dora took advantage of Mrs. Langworthy's being out of the way,
and staged such a housecleaning as that house had never seen before.
Every carpet came up and was relaid; day after day curtain-stretchers
appeared in the side yard, and winter garments flapped from a line.
Bess ordered some new clothes downtown. The Colonel came home
triumphantly to say the Langworthy car was a success at last; the
first one would be on the road in another week.
"A week?" said Artie. "I'll bet you a fiver it will take two weeks."
The Colonel covered the bet. Artie borrowed money from Amy, and
repaid it scrupulously when he won. A gentleman really wasn't sup-
posed to bet on a sure thing.
But three weeks after that proud announcement, the new Lang-
274
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
worthy car actually completed a trial run of thirty miles out west
of the town. Artie stayed at the factory all day, and was wild with
excitement.
"I tell you, Colonel," he said when the car puffed triumphantly
back to the floor, "that car has everything it needs, except perhaps a
good name."
"Isn't my name good enough for it?" demanded the Colonel. "I'm
having the sign on my office repainted to-morrow. We'll run the car

a few more cars finished. The new sign is going to say simply 'LANG-
WORTHY AUTOMOBILES."
"Too long!" said Artie.
"You don't expect me to call the car the 'Mills,' do you?"
Artie ignored the jest. "Naming a car isn't like naming a boat.
When you've made a name well known, you have to stick to it.
There's no changing every time you paint. So it's very important to
get the right name in the first place."
"Let me know if you have any good ideas," said the Colonel. He
still seemed to think Artie was being funny.
But Artie said importantly, "Don't have anything done about your
new sign until I've had a chance to think it over."
Inspiration came to him in the middle of the night. "The Worthy!"
That went right back to the Colonel's own name. But how much
shorter and snappier it was! And look at the chances such a name
would give for advertising; "Worthy is the name, and Worthy is the
car." "Worthy the highest praise." "Worthy is sturdy, and sturdy
is swift." There was something wrong with that last adage; it sounded
too much like "The House That Jack Built." But the name itself

On the way over to the factory next day he communicated his

the Colonel pronounced. But he deferred acting on the splendid
suggestion. He was full of his plans for that afternoon.

jected Amy. "It's early days yet."
"Early days?" Artie pricked up his ears. "Hortense has been married
nearly a year. Do you mean she-?"
"She's expecting a baby," said Dora. "I had a letter from her
to-day."
"I wish you'd told me earlier," said Artie. "I'd have put something
about that into my telegram."
"For mercy's sake, Artie!" said the Colonel. He was a modest man;
he had blushed a little when Dora made her bald announcement.
But Artie was excited and exultant. "It's too late for my telegram.
But it's just in time for a second toast. Here's to Hortense's son! Here
he comes, ushering in a new generation."
"He-or even she-will make you a great-uncle," Bess suggested
wickedly.
"And why not, pray?" demanded Artie. "To-day sees a new business
formally under way; so it's most appropriate that the family should
expand with the business. We may be right at the beginning of the
foundation of a new business dynasty, Bess. Our real American
royalty! And here we are in on the ground floor."
"I don't know about a great dynasty," objected the Colonel. "But
it is nice to think of children coming along. If we can manage to
get them home for Thanksgiving and Christmas-I'd enjoy that!"
"No 'if' about it," said Artie. "It's only spring now. Next Christ-
ARTIE
277
mas Hortense will have to come here and bring the baby. We won't
take 'No' for an answer."
Mrs. Langworthy looked queerly at him when he said "next Christ-
mas," and Amy looked at Mrs. Langworthy and then hastily looked
away.
But Artie was in his glory. Everybody had always acknowledged
that Hortense looked like him. Hortense's son would look like her,
of course; everybody knew that sons commonly "took after" their
mothers. A second Artie, in a new and brighter day! Artie would see
to it that that boy of Hortense's didn't make the mistakes his Uncle
Artie had made.
"This is a doubly happy occasion," said the Colonel. "Triply happy!
Retta has been downstairs all the afternoon. Our convalescent is almost
well."
"Everything coming out right, and all at the same time!" agreed
Artie. "They say bad luck never comes singly. But isn't the same
true about good luck? It's funny the proverb-makers never found
that out."
Mrs. Langworthy turned her glass, sloshing the spoonful of wine
in the bottom. "We ought to have broken the bottle on the car," she
said. "That's the proper way to have a christening."
"I suppose you'll want to break a bottle of champagne on Hortense's
baby, too, when they christen him?" scoffed Artie.
So far as he was concerned, though, he wouldn't have minded let-
ting the champagne flow away in rivulets. Just so long as a great
event was signalized, it didn't matter whether you drank or merely
broke a bottle. He felt a little exhilarated now; but he would have
been just as much exhilarated on a cup of coffee.
He had hated having Hortense leave them; yet that break had been
the prelude to a greater glory. He had worried through a dreary winter;
but to-night was reward enough for anything that had gone before.
Things always came out all right in the end, if you waited long
enough and didn't lose your faith. Artie would say that for himself:
he had had a good deal of trouble in his life, but he had never lost
his faith.
II
RTIE felt that his name for the Colonel's product was a real
gift to civilization. His feelings were hurt when his suggestion
AR
wasn't adopted. But he didn't hold that against the automobile.
In fact a great deal of his time that summer went to waiting on the
first completed car, which had been set aside for family use. He
always had to crank five or six times before he got a spark; often, too,
as soon as he got behind the wheel he killed his engine, and the whole
performance had to be repeated.
But he fell into the habit of taking the Colonel to work, so that he
could have the use of the car afterward. He never ventured off the
macadam; but he would drive for hours up and down Prospect
Avenue and Main Street, proudly conscious of the heads which
turned in his direction.
Dora sometimes rode with him, when she had errands downtown.
Bess's idea was to drive herself after she had had Artie crank the car
for her. Artie didn't take kindly to being left behind after he had
done the real work. But he was proud of the fact that Bess was the
first girl in Mount Royal who ever drove a car; and she had better
luck than he when it came to keeping the engine in action.
Mrs. Langworthy refused to ride with Artie; after her one venture
on the day of "the christening," she retreated to the safety of the
landau. It was much easier for her to get into, of course.
She went for a drive two or three times a week. Other afternoons, if
the weather was fine, she sat on the veranda. The Bee met here every
week all summer. The understanding was that later on, when Mrs.
Langworthy grew stronger, the other members would again take
their turns at entertaining.
The new business was under way at last. Three Langworthy cars
were all that could be accommodated in the showroom of the old
278
ARTIE
279
carriage-shop. The rest of the summer's output went to dealers. Two
establishments in Chicago, one in Minneapolis and one in St. Louis,
"had the agency" for the Langworthy car.
"What cars we sell cost us more to manufacture than the dealer is
getting for them," the Colonel complained to Artie. "If the Lang-
worthy ever really catches the public fancy, I'll be ruined before you
can say 'Jack Robinson."
The irony was lost on Artie. Not only did he look forward with
cheerful assurance to business on a big scale; as a step in that direction,
every sale right now was a victory. But the triumphs which he enjoyed
most were those that came closest.
Charley Manning bought a Langworthy, of course; but that might
be considered getting some of his own back. It was much more sig-
nificant when Richard Duane bought a Langworthy; he was a promi-
nent lawyer, and Artie considered him "about the brainiest man in
town." When S. R. Crandall bought one, that proved not only that it
was a good car but that the price was right.
Artie's elation reached a climax when old John Rutherford offered
to buy the business. The Colonel, too, was pleased by the proposition,
though the amount offered was derisory. Perhaps if he had bargained
he could have got more; but he had no idea of selling his business.
However, he was encouraged by the mere fact of the offer.
Probably if John Rutherford had bought the business for a song, he
could have reorganized it and made it pay. But so could the Colonel.
He did not share Artie's easy optimism. Since the time of Retta's
operation, indeed, his world had no longer seemed to him to rest on
its old secure foundations.
There was something very like desperation in the way he threw
himself at his work nowadays. The center of his interest had shifted
from his household to his factory. Many men habitually lived for their
business; but the Colonel had never been one of those men. He could
be, however, now that he had to.
One afternoon when August was almost over, Artie drove Dora
down to Cordiner & Morse's. She wanted to buy some tiny gold pins
to fasten a dress she was making for Hortense's baby.
Artie accompanied her into the store. While Mrs. Cordiner was
waiting on her, he put in the time looking at silver flasks. Frank
Morse waited on him, and was as courteous and pleasant as ever,
280
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
though he had lost a great deal of weight lately and looked wretched.
Presently old Cordiner came shuffling up and began to offer sug-
gestions to his partner. He should have known that Artie had no
serious intention of buying a flask, and couldn't have paid for it any-
how. But he had taken a notion that Morse was not handling the sale
correctly.
Artie was flattered at first by all the attention he was getting. But
when old Cordiner called him "Mr. Patterson" he wasn't quite so well
pleased. And Cordiner didn't stick even to having Artie "Mr. Patter-
son." In the course of the next three minutes he called him by two
other names.
"Why, the old boy's getting childish!" thought Artie indignantly.
Old Cordiner's senility embarrassed him. He could see, too, that it
was embarrassing Frank Morse. Artie tried to break away; but Cor-
diner only became more insistent.
Finally Dora came to the rescue by demanding Artie's advice on
whether she should buy pins connected by fine gold chains or insist
on having them separate. Artie's ideas on the subject of a baby's
wardrobe were vague, but he loved to be consulted. He gave his
opinion at some length, and the pins with chains were chosen.
He had prudently left his engine running, and for once he man-
aged to get away in fine style. He would have hated to kill his engine
and have to fuss with the crank under the bilious gaze that Tom
Holmes was directing on him between rows of ailing watches.
But his triumph with the car could not quite erase the acute dis-
comfort he had experienced in the store. When he had the auto safely
away from the car-tracks and the crowded downtown blocks, he said,
"Dora, old man Cordiner is long past his usefulness, isn't he?"
"Have you just found that out?" said Dora.
"Oh, he's always doddered a good deal! Seems to me he was an old
man when we first came to Mount Royal. But now he's downright
potty. He called me Mr. Patterson, for one thing."
"Which Mr. Patterson?" Dora teased. "Archibald or young Archie?"
Artie carefully steered past a delivery wagon. "Then he called me
Mr. Taylor, and then Mr. Cary. There aren't any Carys in town that
I know of."
"Phew! He's worse than I thought. Well," Dora decided, "that
ARTIE
281
must mean things are getting brighter for Frank Morse and Mrs.
Cordiner. He's kept them waiting too long as it is."
"You think Frank will marry Mrs. Cordiner when the old man
dies?"
"They've been in love for years. That's one reason I suppose why
she makes such a parade of her devotion to Mr. Cordiner. Salve on

"Dora, do you think she and Frank-do you think they-er, haven't

"That wasn't what I meant. I'm afraid they have. Waiting for dead
men's shoes is a ghastlier idea than being lovers, to my way of think-
ing. But don't let on to Retta that I ever said such a thing, or I shall
be sent to bed without my supper."
"It's telling on him. He looks bad. Mrs. Cordiner is the same
dimpled dumpling as ever, though."
"He's a much finer sort of person than she is," said Dora sharply.
"He's too good for her, really. But I suppose he doesn't think so.
Maybe we'd better attend to our driving, though, and stop trying to
adjust other people's lives for them. You nearly ran into that farmer's
wagon just now."
"I wasn't so very near," said Artie. "Still, it's a good thing to be
especially careful with farmers. As a class they don't like autos any
too well, so we must educate them if we ever hope to sell them autos.
You see, a potential customer for a Langworthy is what I look for
everywhere nowadays."
Dora laughed. Well, Artie thought, maybe he had made that sound
like a joke. He liked to be surrounded by smiling faces. Sometimes,
though, it annoyed him when he couldn't make people take him
seriously. Of course he didn't take himself any too seriously. But
right this minute he'd rather be Artie Mills, light both in heart and
in pocket, than a pompous old ass like John Rutherford or William
Reynolds.
Man-and-woman triangles were more interesting anyhow than po-
tential customers. They were also commoner, he decided within the
next few minutes. On his return to the house he found Retta in one
of her blowing-up streaks, with Amy attempting to soothe her.
"Old idiot!" frothed Mrs. Langworthy. "Louise is too good for him.
Why he should think he's a Lothario-!"
282
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Amy explained that Louise was now threatening a move to that
"small farm" of which Jason had often talked. Yesterday she had
caught him kissing Ida, the comely second girl; she wanted to get him
out of harm's way.
"I'm sure it will blow over, though," said Amy. "Of course Ida is a
lot younger and better looking than Louise. But I've just been talking

"I wouldn't let it go at that," said Dora. "Why take any chance of
Louise's developing a grouch? The simplest thing to do is dismiss
Ida."
"Dismiss Ida?" Mrs. Langworthy groaned. "I can't break in a new
second girl right now. I simply can't."
Artie winked at Dora. For nearly a year now Dora had been super-
vising the housekeeping, and had done it more thoroughly than Mrs.
Langworthy ever did. Dora ignored his wink. She wasn't generally
so meek; but she probably figured that sick people shouldn't be
crossed. Retta was getting better right along: Artie could see that.
But her gain was slow.
At the best of times Artie wouldn't have tried to let Retta in on that
interesting theory he and Dora had worked out about Mrs. Cordiner
and Frank Morse. Interesting things were going on around you all
the time: yes, and a lot queerer things than most people supposed.
Dora was in on more than she ever admitted to the family, too. Dora
was a deep one.
How deep Dora was he realized that very next morning. He was
just cranking the car when Dora came dashing out of the house with
her downtown clothes on. "I've just had a telephone call," she said.
"You must take me straight over to the Cordiners'. Then you can
come back for the Colonel."
"Take you to the Cordiners' house? But they'll have left for the
store before we get there."
"Not to-day," said Dora. "She sent for me."
"Mrs. Cordiner sent for you?"
"Yes. Would I save time by going over on the street-car?"
On this hint Artie went back to his cranking. Presently the engine
sputtered into action. Dora took her place beside him, and they
chugged off down the drive.
He waited for further explanation, but none was forthcoming.
ARTIE
283
Dora sat in grim silence, looking straight ahead. Her piquant, pug-
nacious little face wore an expression he couldn't read.
When he turned into the block where the Cordiners lived, she
motioned for him to stop. "You can set me down here. Never mind
driving right to the door."
Artie stopped obediently and reached across her to unfasten the
door of the car. Dora made no movement to alight. She frowned for
a minute; then she made up her mind to something.
"Artie, you're not to come blurting out with this to Retta. But you
might tell Amy, if you can get her aside. This morning Frank
Morse's landlady went to call him at seven, as she always does. Her
knock wasn't answered. The door was locked; she got a neighbor to
put a ladder up to the window. They found him dead."
"So? Then he has been in bad health lately. And we thought it
was worry-and love!"
"It was worry, I guess, at that," said Dora. "But that won't be what
the doctor puts on the death-certificate. He killed himself. His re-
volver was lying there beside him."
"But look here, Dora, a revolver shot-!"
"He may have shot himself early in the evening, when he was alone
in the house. I don't know. Anyhow, nobody heard anything."
"Suicide! He never seemed at all the kind of man to do that. Did
Mrs. Cordiner telephone you the news?"
"Yes. I couldn't see why at first. But Retta is as close a friend as
she has in Mount Royal. Lacking Retta, she appealed to me. They'll
close the store for a day or two, I suppose. But I can see that she
wouldn't want to be in the house all day alone with the old man."
"I can see that, too. Well, you let me know if there's anything I
can do, Dora. I'll be at the factory all morning, and home for dinner
as usual."
"Tell Amy not to expect me until.she sees me. I may not be home
until evening." Dora climbed out of the car and marched up the steps
of the Cordiner house. Mrs. Cordiner let her in; she must have been
watching.
Artie took the Colonel over to the factory. But he couldn't stay there
himself when there was so much excitement in the wind. He drove
back downtown, and joined the little group of loungers in the lobby
of the Mount Royal Hotel.
284
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Artie was not welcome in that hotel generally. But to-day the staff
as well as the hangers-on were too excited to notice his presence.
Through the wide windows they could see Cordiner & Morse's, with
Tom Holmes sitting at his post just as usual. It would take more than
the death of a partner to move him spontaneously, and evidently he
had had no orders to close the store. Rather acidly Artie remarked,
"If anybody had to do that job, I don't see why it couldn't have been
watchmaker Holmes. I always liked Frank Morse."
They had all liked Frank Morse. He was pleasant, kindly, lived
frugally, didn't drink. Everybody was puzzled by his shocking end.
Artie had come for information; but he knew more than the rest
of them. He didn't feel like telling what he knew, either. In disap-
pointment he drifted away.
It was nine that night when Dora reached home; she was driven up
in a buggy, and lingered for a moment to talk to somebody.
Artie was established just inside the sitting-room door, reading a
magazine. Amy gave Dora The Century every year for Christmas,
and Dora gave Amy Scribner's. Dora liked the travel articles in those
magazines, and Amy the short stories. But it was Artie who always
found time to read the serials. He was deep now in a fresh instalment
of Frances Hodgson Burnett, though not so deep that he hadn't
caught the sound of wheels the minute they turned into the drive.
"Sit down, Dora," he said. "You look all in. Can I get you a glass
of water or anything?"
"Make it milk. I haven't had a thing to eat all day." Dora sank into
a chair and pushed her hat off her forehead.
Artie returned with milk, cold chicken, and a dish of raspberries;
the Langworthy refrigerator always yielded something to raiders.
He drew up a small table for the tray. Then he hunted for Dora's
hatpins, and removed her hat.
"Thanks, Artie. You're a good old thing." Dora drew off her gloves
and took a sip of milk.
Artie resumed his chair and reopened his magazine. Over the top
of it he presently caught her eye.
"That wasn't the Cordiner carriage that brought you home," he
ventured.
"It was Dr. Simpson. He was kind enough to give me a lift."
"He'd been making a call at the Cordiners'?".

285
"Yes. I got Mrs. Cordiner to send for him, supposedly to have a
look at her husband. This morning when I got there the old man was

There's nothing a doctor can do for him any more."

Dora, I don't think Frank Morse was as old as I am?"

"That made a nice day for you, Dora. No wonder you persuaded

"The whole thing will be around Mount Royal in a day or two.

"This morning nobody pretended to know anything," Artie could

"Nothing pathetic about that. She couldn't call on anybody better,"

He felt a surge of sympathy for Dora. She was such a midget to
begin with; and she hated scenes, and despised the people who made
them. Yet she would be kindness itself to Mrs. Cordiner, and further-
more she would keep her mouth shut about what went on under her
eyes during these trying days. She had told a little to Artie, of course;
but then Artie's lips were sealed on a confidence.
He found the situation pleasantly exciting, however. He was sorry
ARTIE
287
for Frank, and still sorrier for Mrs. Cordiner. But it wasn't every
evening that Artie had such a tidbit to mull over. Lately the evenings
in this house had been altogether too quiet to please him.
He hunted for the Mount Royal Beacon, found it under a magazine
which he had himself laid down, and reread the account of Frank
Morse's suicide. It was a lengthy account, but it was dry; it dwelt on
Mr. Morse's being taken into the firm for which he had gone to work
"as a youth," and said no reason was known "for his act."
"Cordiner & Morse advertise in the Beacon," thought Artie.
Why had Dora urged him to keep as much as possible of this story
away from Mrs. Langworthy? Mrs. Cordiner was Retta's friend
rather than Dora's. Retta would certainly get the whole story when
Mrs. Cordiner resumed attendance at The Bee. Artie didn't suppose
that would be very long to wait, either. Molly Travers had persisted in
absenting herself since her divorce; but you couldn't keep any of the
rest of them away from those sessions. Just lately when Retta hadn't
been able to get to The Bee, she had had The Bee brought to her.
Probably this fall she and Mrs. Cordiner would be rivals for the special
attention of their friends.
Artie lighted a cigarette and lounged down in his easy-chair until
his head rested comfortably against the cushioned back. He was
flattered at the way Dora had sought his alliance to-day. Dora had
always had a way of looking on him as her little brother; from child-
hood on, it was Amy who had been his special companion.
Amy had looked awfully pretty to-night, during the hour when she
had sat in this room between supper and getting Retta to bed. She
had been knitting a little shoe for Hortense's baby. The Millses wore
well; Amy looked young enough to be knitting socks for a baby of
her own. Artie had been dying to say something like that to her; but
he hadn't said it. You didn't, somehow, with Amy.
He was still thinking about Amy when he heard Bess and Doug
Rutherford come up on the veranda. Artie caught the murmur of
their voices without distinguishing the words, and presently Bess
giggled. She had a pretty laugh; heard thus through the summer
night, it seemed to carry with it the very spirit of youth.
Artie's own youth might desert him; but what he lost in his person,
he got back indirectly from other members of his family. They were
a remarkably nice family; he had always known that.
288
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
To-day the tragedy which had struck so near made him realize that
the Langworthys were also a remarkably lucky family. The Colonel
had entered a new business-in a brand-new field, too-at an age
when many men would be thinking about retiring; and look at the
success he was making of it! Retta had had a tough siege; but she
was well on the mend. Dora and Amy both led lives that suited them
to a T. And Bess would hook young Rutherford yet. He might think
he was sly; but he had hung around her too long for his own safety.
Artie chuckled. Then he shivered a little, remembering poor Frank
Morse, for whom a bullet through the head had been the only way
out. And for Mrs. Cordiner there was no way out. She was left tied
to that old hulk, to whom she owed gratitude; but she couldn't pos-
sibly love him.
The Langworthys had no business to be so happy. In a world where
things were allotted none too generously, this family had more than
their share.
M
III
RS. CORDINER returned to the store after Frank Morse's
funeral. Duty to her husband, mingled with dread of gossip,
kept her going for two or three weeks. Then she gave up and went
to bed.
"Nervous prostration," said Dr. Simpson.
"It used to be called 'brain fever," Artie whispered to Dora. “In
the books the heroine always got it when she was disappointed in
love."
"I didn't know you read that kind of books," said Dora.
But after a visit to the store, Artie didn't feel so funny. Old Cordiner
was shuffling about forlornly, trying to "break in" a flippant youth
who up to now had worked in a cigar-store. Sometimes Cordiner
would give the same order two or three times in a row; sometimes in
the middle of what he was saying he would forget to go on. Only
Tom Holmes still sat on his high stool with forty or fifty watches
ticking away close to his bent head. He took the presence of the new
clerk as a personal insult; and since he was never so happy as when
he had a fresh grievance, he looked slightly more alert and less bilious
than usual.
The Bee continued to meet with Mrs. Langworthy. More than
once, indeed, the session had to be held upstairs at her bedside. The
oncoming of cold weather seemed to affect her disastrously.
Artie hated it, too. In the fall he had to drain and jack up his car.
He had come to think of it fondly as "his" car. Sometimes of an after-
noon he would pay a visit to it in the stable, and walk around it, and
try to console himself by thinking that after all another spring wasn't
so far off.
Now that he didn't need to drive the Colonel over, Artie didn't
go to the factory so often himself. He was beginning to find the factory
289
290
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
depressing, anyhow. Several unsold cars had been returned by the
agencies; and to get ready money the Colonel had once more negoti-
ated a loan at "the Reynolds bank," and had again put up his cher-
ished transit stock as collateral.
"That gives them the edge on Charley Manning," he muttered to
Artie. "He lent me money just on my note. I've paid him his interest,
of course. But I hate the idea that he isn't secured when those old
skinflints are."
"There's no mortgage on the factory, is there?" Artie encouraged
him. "Nor on the house?"
"I'll never mortgage the house. I might better close out the business.
At my time of life I have to be able to feel that there's something be-
longing to me." All at once the Colonel chuckled. "I get a pension
too, you know, for that bullet hole in my right leg. It isn't much of
a pension; but it will buy my tobacco if I ever have to enter the
Soldiers' Home."
The Colonel was like that: he had once hoped to go out to the
Soldiers' Home as Governor, and now he was making jokes about
entering as an inmate. He did nothing to check the household ex-
penditures, either; time enough to stint the family when he had to.
If the business ultimately succeeded, there would be plenty of money
for everybody. If it failed, paring cheese in the kitchen couldn't avert
disaster.
When he saw Mrs. Langworthy settling toward another house-
bound winter, indeed, he began to urge a Southern trip. This year
Dr. Simpson agreed that she might go if she took one of her sisters
with her; he didn't feel that she was yet in condition to go away
without some responsible person. The omens pointed to Dora.
"If I go anywhere," said Dora, "I'd like to go to New York and be
with Hortense when her baby is born."
"You can just as well do it for all of me," said Mrs. Langworthy.
"When people have their health they can enjoy going places and
seeing things. But until I get more strength back, home is still the best
place for me."
After a moment she went on, "Dora ought to take Bess for a trip in
my place. It's dull for the child, having me half-sick all the time."
Bess protested that she wouldn't stir a step until her mother was
ARTIE
291
stronger. But the Colonel seized on the idea with enthusiasm. "How
about it, Dode?" he demanded.
Dora shook her head. But he urged, "Come now, Dode! You've
been planning European trips for the last three years or more. Why
shouldn't you take Bess with you, and see how those plans work out
in practice? Bess has never been abroad. It would be an important
part of her education."
"I'm educated enough for the present," said Bess. But she couldn't
keep the eagerness out of her voice.
Dora wheeled on the Colonel. "I've a good mind to take you at
your word. Do you know the real reason why I've fooled around so
long with guide-books and time-tables? I can speak a little French,
too, and enough German to get by in a railway station or a boarding-
house. If it were possible for me to do exactly what I wanted to do,
I'd guide parties through Europe. I could do it, too. I know I could."
"I believe you could," he agreed. "Anyhow, you might start with
Bess. Practise on her."
"When Mamma is better, we'll consider it," said Bess.
But Mrs. Langworthy refused to take "No" for an answer. Bess
could help out on the expenses of the trip, for one thing. She could
sell her horse, which she didn't use much in the winter anyhow.
Then with Bess and Dora out of the house, the rest of them could
get along for a while without a second girl; she was really afraid
she'd have to let Ida go to keep peace in the family.
The day after Thanksgiving she actually got her sister and daugh-
ter off to Europe. The horse was sold, too; but Ida stayed on. Louise
said sullenly that she guessed she could get along for the present;
then Ida could stay here and help keep things going when she and
Jason went on the farm.
Retta kept more and more to her bedroom. The auxiliary furnace
which heated the addition wasn't working any too well this winter;
but most of the time a fire of cannel coal was kept blazing in the
grate, and the room was so hot that even Retta was comfortable.
Sometimes of an evening the four of them played whist in the
bedroom: Artie and the Colonel were paired against Mrs. Lang-
worthy and Amy. Mrs. Langworthy was the best player of the four;
but up to now she had never showed any special interest in the game.
This winter, however, she grew keen about it. Over cocoa at the
292
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
end of an evening she would discuss various hands; sometimes she
thought them over at night and talked about them the next day.
"It's nice she can interest herself in whist," Artie said to Amy.
"She used to be interested in so many things," said Amy wistfully.
"Amy, you don't think Retta's getting worse?"
"Of course not," said Amy hastily. "Oh, no, I didn't mean to
imply that!"
Artie wouldn't have Retta get worse. He gleaned hopefully for
every sign of improvement. The mere fact that she had let Bess and
Dora go so far away was surely proof enough that she believed her-
self to be gaining.
But the way Amy took things made him uneasy. He couldn't make
out whether she knew more than she was telling. Perhaps, as Dora
had sometimes hinted, Amy simply liked to worry. He never had
been able to make Amy out. Oh, well, this winter wouldn't last for-
ever! And he could always get something out of Dora, once she was
back on deck.
He spent a good deal of time now maturing his wine and waiting
on his brandy-still; his little hoard of whiskey remained untouched
in his tool-chest. Sometimes of an afternoon he entertained one or
two of his old cronies in the little room behind the sitting-room.
The door was carefully closed; and there was seldom any other
company nowadays to collide with Artie's.
He took to walking downtown in the afternoon, too. Really it was
a relief to have things not quite so pressing at the factory; for the last
year or two Artie had half forgotten what his fellow-citizens looked
like.
Swinging his stick jauntily, he would proceed first to Eaton's and
get a white flower for his buttonhole. That generally took a quarter
of an hour. Then he would parade up Main Street, being careful
always to wave a hand at the loungers in the lobby of the Mount
Royal. Out of the corner of his eye he would be well aware that Tom
Holmes saw him and resented his progress. To an old grouch like
Tom Holmes, it must always be painful to see a young fellow enjoy
life as Artie enjoyed it.
"A young fellow" was what he still considered himself. When he
was away from his sisters and from the household he always got
younger and younger. By the time he reached the Manning Arms,
ARTIE
293
he could whirl through the lobby in a blaze of youth and speed. He
didn't care to linger: those fellows who hung around hotel lobbies
were always fat and logy. Artie was slim and active. Lounging led
to drinking, anyhow; he had seen that years ago, and had veered
off. It didn't do to let your drinking become a habit; but the habit
was what cut deeper than the drinking. Artie could take it or let it
alone.
One sunny afternoon he was whirling through the lobby of the
Manning Arms when he heard his name called. He turned to find
himself greeted by Doug Rutherford.
"Where are you going in such a hurry?" demanded Doug.
It sounded like an invitation to gossip. Artie was too wary to accept
outright. After all, he was Bess's uncle; and this afternoon he hap-
pened to be in possession of information which it might be worth
Doug's while to have.
"I have to meet a man." Artie looked at his watch, then went on
importantly, "But not until half-past four. How come you're here
yourself, Doug? At this hour I should think you'd be in the bank."
"I have to see about a room for one of our Eastern customers,"
said Doug. "Wait until I speak to them at the desk, and then we'll
have a drink. That is, if you can spare the time."
Doug went to the desk, and Artie sat down in a capacious arm-
chair and tapped with his stick against his well-polished shoe. The
shoe had been half-soled, but the leather glowed with repeated rub-
bings. New shoes never had that fine glossy patina, like the finish
you sometimes saw on old furniture. Artie didn't like things which
looked too grossly new; he thought they were vulgar.
Doug came back presently and ushered him into the huge bar-
room, which was wainscoted with slabs of porphyry. Porphyry was
the "purple" in which the Roman emperors took good care to be
born. Dora had found that fact in a book while the Manning Arms
was being built, and had gleefully reported it to Artie. She would
have a lot of things to tell when she got back from her European
trip. He would enjoy listening to her, too. When Dora was in the
humor she was as interesting as reading a book, and far less trouble.
Artie settled back in his chair, threw open his overcoat, and looked
about him. A waiter placed on their table a plate of tiny hot but-
294
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
tered rolls and a platter of baked ham, sliced sausage and cubes of
cheese. Hot rolls were always served to patrons at this hour in the

was brought in fresh every day from Charley Manning's country-

Mount Royal understood how to make a julep.
"What are you having, Mr. Mills?" asked Doug. "Whiskey and

"Whiskey straight," said Artie.
Doug took the same. His father must be out of town, or he'd never

"I heard from her a week ago. Her letter then said that she was

"Oh, yes! They sailed. They had winter crossings both ways. But

When Artie's second whiskey was dispatched, he condescended to

"I know," said Doug, grinning a little at the formal allusion to
Hortense. "Didn't Bess go with her?"
"Bess went to the older Jeromes', to stay a few days with her

"She's prettier than Hortense ever was," said Doug. "Or at least,
she's much livelier. I always thought Hortense was stiff."
The third whiskey made Artie dignified; he didn't care to discuss

295
his nieces with fellows in a bar-room. He beckoned to the waiter and

Doug was a round behind him, but that was all right. Artie took

much reform nowadays," he proclaimed. "When I first came to

there was some fool piece of railroad legislation brought up down

just offered a special train and all expenses paid, and took a lot of
legislators for a nice trip to New Orleans. That broke the quorum,
d'you see?"
"I see," said Doug.
"Senator Roberts was in the charge of the party. He wasn't United

weren't in the Legislature. The railroads didn't boggle at expense,
so long as they got what they wanted. It was a great trip. Ever been
in New Orleans, Doug?"
Doug had never been in New Orleans. Artie told him all about
that city. It was just as well to brush up on his recollections of the
trip, anyhow. He could use them on Dora one of these days in case
she proved too informative about Europe.
Artie had a feeling that he was being very crafty. His cunning
showed of course that he wasn't drinking too much. Doug wanted

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
bar had a tendency to assume a double outline; but that must be

He needn't have worried about the check. Doug paid it. He also

Oh, well! Perhaps Doug hadn't noticed.
Artie walked with careful dignity west and then north. Then his
pace quickened. He began to chuckle to himself.
He would have good news for Bess when she got home. It paid a
girl to have an uncle who could meet people well: an uncle who was
sociable yet discreet. Doug had showed unmistakable interest in the
time of Bess's return. And Artie had led him on very nicely with
that story of the young man Bess had met on the boat. For all he
knew to the contrary, she might have met a dozen young men on
the boat. Or it might have been an Adamless Eden. But Artie knew
how to make out a story.
Amy came running from the sitting-room when she heard him at

looked at him doubtfully.
Artie stood very steady, with his feet a little apart. "You tell your
news. Then I'll tell mine."
"A telegram just came from Dora."
Amy could never talk fast enough for Artie. He guessed her good
news. "The baby is here? Hortense's little son!"
ARTIE
297
"It's a girl," said Amy. "A lovely little girl. And they're both doing
well. Artie, isn't that splendid?"
"I'd have preferred a boy," said Artie. "Still, with a family like
ours the chances were all against it. We do run to girls. I'm tickled

Retta smiled over the news, indeed. But it was the Colonel who
was exultant. The appearance of a new generation made a man
realize that the world wouldn't stop when he and his contemporaries
were done for. In some obscure way, the Colonel found that thought
enlivening.
That night at the card-table he insisted on opening the last bottle
of the good champagne. This was the most suitable occasion to finish
it; it had been bought for the wedding of the new baby's parents.
The champagne warmed up the embers of the whiskey. Still Artie
didn't think Amy need have looked at him quite so hard. He would
always have drunk wine if he could have afforded it. It wasn't his
fault that he was a poor man.
He did Amy an injustice, however. After the new baby's health
had been drunk, and then Hortense's and Bill's, she emptied her glass
into Artie's. And later that same evening, when she had got Retta
to bed, she came to his door for an instant.
"It's been a happy evening, hasn't it, Art?" she said. "Something
for us always to remember."
Artie, who had changed to a velveteen smoking-jacket and was
having a last cigarette, came over to the door and kissed her on the
cheek. Amy patted his arm and started to turn away. Then she came
back.
"I haven't yet heard the news that you promised me this after-
noon," she said. "It got snowed under. But I haven't forgotten."
The news that Doug Rutherford had asked about Bess didn't seem
nearly so important now as it had at half-past five. Artie related it,
felt it go flat, and wound up lamely, "Bess will enjoy hearing it,
anyhow. It'll be nice when she and Dora are back, won't it? I sup-
pose that will be pretty soon now. I like it much better when we're
all together."
"Yes," said Amy slowly, we have had some good times-all
together."
298
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
An icy chill struck to Artie's heart. Amy was speaking as if their
good times together were all in the past.
He was stone sober now; and the nightmare of the day Retta was
operated on came rushing back over him. The real nightmare, indeed,
was yet to come. Those things went on, and got worse. And from
the past you could sometimes judge the future all too clearly.
"Amy," he whispered, "you don't think there'll have to be a second
operation?"
"Oh, no!" said Amy. "I'm quite positive that there won't have to
be any second operation."
But she didn't look at him when she said it. Artie knew what she
was thinking. He shuddered as if somebody had walked over his
grave. But it wasn't his grave that somebody was walking over. Artie
knew he would live to be an old man. Heaven send he never would
be an old man alone in the world.

I
ESS LANGWORTHY returned to Mount Royal in March.
She made the trip from New York alone; Dora was staying a
few weeks longer with Hortense.
The European sojourn had been a great success. Aunt Dora had
been a little too keen for cathedrals when Bess would have preferred
to shop; and the way she soaked in information as if she liked it was
sometimes almost revolting. But she was always excellent company;
and she dealt with arrangements and emergencies so capably and
cheerfully that her traveling companion was left carefree. Seeing her
so much against new backgrounds, Bess began to realize that she
would have liked Dora even if she were now meeting her as a
stranger.
Bess had long been vaguely aware that the Langworthys were more
agreeable than the relatives of most of her friends. But certain fea-
tures of life at home had always galled her: Artie drinking in the
grape-arbor with his disreputable friends, Hortense messing around
with paints and smelling the house up, dinner at noon instead of at
seven in the evening the way the Jeromes had it, and above all, those
family musical evenings which Papa was so fond of.
But she was twenty-two now, and no longer at the family's mercy
the way she had been when she was younger. Anyhow, she had a
different perspective on the family. She wouldn't always be with
them.
In her Pullman state-room, with a five-pound box of candy open
beside her and a dozen American Beauty roses doing their best to
drown out the odor of cinders, Bess tasted the luxury that she loved.
She watched the Palisades run by. She rang for the porter and gave
him superfluous orders; Bill Jerome had tipped the porter liberally
299
300
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
when he put her on the train, and nothing was too good for her if
it was in the Negro's power to get.
Bess was going to have colored servants when she had a household
of her own. She would have fresh hothouse flowers in the rooms
every day, and open fires lighted every afternoon. She would have a
lady's maid, too-why not? It was so much easier for a woman to
look pretty when she had a good maid; and it would be Bess's duty
to her husband to look as pretty as possible.
What had been an intermittent dream grew presently to seem like
reality: she was going back only to visit her family, and would
shortly resume her life apart from them. Once or twice she came to
earth sharply and remembered that she had nothing very definite
ahead, and that it would be hard to settle down in Mount Royal
after her exciting winter. Then she consoled herself that at any rate
this was a thousand miles ahead of her return from school almost
three years ago. She was a finished product now, and highly capable
of making her own way with people.
She hoped they wouldn't think it necessary to have anybody meet
her in Chicago. They didn't, and that was a relief. But long before
her local train reached Mount Royal, she was beginning to look
eagerly out of the window. One after another she recognized the
towns she had known all her life; and her eyes sparkled and the
carnation color rose in her cheeks with the excitement of her home-
coming.
She had expected her father to meet the train. Instead, she found
Aunt Amy waiting for her; just behind Amy were Uncle Artie and
Doug Rutherford. The sight of Doug was gratifying. Bess had pur-
posely kept him short of news while she was away; evidently her
treatment had worked.
She smiled brilliantly at the three of them. Amy kissed her rather
solemnly. Bess had never forgiven Amy for the way she had acted
when things grew tense over Bill Jerome. Still, to-day she was glad
to see even Amy.
"Where's Papa?" she demanded. "And how's Mamma?"
"The Colonel's in Minneapolis on business," answered Artie. "He
expected to be back to-day, but he's been detained."
Jason was waiting with the old surrey for her baggage. Doug was
driving his own car; it didn't look half so well as the Langworthy
BESS
301
which Artie had out for the first time that season. Viewing Artie
behind the wheel, and the station-master obsequiously cranking
under his fussy direction, Bess reflected that Artie had found his
proper niche in life at last.
His handclasp had been a little shaky, though; and for a minute
when he first saw her, Bess had been afraid that he was going to
burst out crying. He had aged a good deal this past winter. Amy
looked tired, too, though the March wind had brought color to her
cheeks. She overwhelmed Bess with questions about Dora and
Hortense and the baby.
Bess leaned past her to wave farewell to Doug. Then she resigned
herself to give bulletins: Dora was fine, Hortense was fine, Bill was
grumbling that he was no longer of any importance in his house, but
actually he loved it. Hortense had wanted to name the baby for Dora,
and Dora had insisted on naming it "Margaret." It was a great deal
like any other baby, so far as Bess could see. Old Czar had recog-
nized Bess right away. He was a remarkable dog. Last fall Hor-
tense's mother-in-law had insulted her by saying, "You won't think
so much of your dog after your baby comes." "The very idea!" Hor-
tense had sputtered to Dora. "Czar is my dog. I'd think just the same
of him if I had fifty children."
"I miss Czar," said Amy.
"Well, now that the babies and the dogs and the fond new parents
are all disposed of," said Bess, "perhaps you wouldn't mind telling
me how my mother is?"
"We have a nurse for her again," said Amy. "Your father thought
that would be simpler, with you coming home and all."
Bess felt a stab of anxiety. "She isn't worse?"
"She's about the same," said Amy. "But Dr. Simpson has ordered
opiates for her once or twice lately, and of course I couldn't manage
that."
"Hortense with fifty children!" Artie said over his shoulder. “I
hope they wouldn't all be girls."
"Fifty girls and fifty boys would make Hortense a nice family,"
said Bess. "Like what-you-may-call-her in Shakespeare."
"Hecuba. She was in Homer before she was in Shakespeare," said
Amy. "I sometimes wish I had had a sounder education, Bess. It
must help a lot as a person grows older."

Bess groaned. "That would make two of you, then. Aunt Amy,

there was a new line-up at home, Bess half wished that Dora had

Mrs. Langworthy had insisted on being properly dressed to receive

to the window.
When Bess actually came rushing in at the door, Mrs. Langworthy
put out her arms and half rose. Bess was enfolded in her embrace and
hugged her heartily. She had always cared more for her mother than
for any one else on earth; and now her sense of homecoming cen-
tered in Mrs. Langworthy, and she was really thrilled to be at home.
She sat for a long time holding her mother's hand and chattering.
Mrs. Langworthy was quiet, but blissfully happy. Amy, who had
nodded to the nurse to go out, sat watching them across the room.
Moments like this made up to Retta for a lot, of course. You
couldn't really comprehend such a thing unless you had it in your
own life, Amy supposed. It didn't make you any happier, however,
to see what you had missed.
Mrs. Langworthy insisted on coming downstairs for supper. She
didn't want Bess told that she hadn't appeared at the family table
lately. She had given in to Dr. Simpson's babying long enough, any-
how; a little more and he would have succeeded in persuading her
that she was really sick.
She was lively enough at the supper-table, though she ate almost
nothing. After supper, however, the excitement and exertion told on
her. She went to bed almost immediately; and Bess heard the nurse
telephoning the drug-store.
It was a queer, strained evening. The Colonel got home about nine
o'clock. He was thrilled to see his darling; but hardly was the greet-
ing over when Bess saw that he, too, was unlike himself. He paced
the floor nervously: he who never used to stir out of his chair if he
could help it. Twice he excused himself and went upstairs "to see
how your mother is getting along." The third time he started, Amy
intercepted him.
Yet he and Amy scarcely spoke to each other. It almost looked as
if they avoided staying in the same room together. That was odd;
they used to get on so well. It didn't pay, really, to have Amy at the
304
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
head of things. She never would have usurped power; but when it
had been by chance bestowed on her, she didn't handle it gracefully.
During the next few days Bess was freshly conscious of cross-
currents in the house: was conscious of them without understanding
them. The atmosphere made her uneasy. Once all she had asked of
her family was that they give her her own way. Now she found it
hard to avail herself of such freedom as was offered her.
Doug Rutherford was very attentive these days. He had finally
perceived that Bess was not only a girl to amuse himself with: she
was necessary to his happiness. A dozen times in the fortnight after
her return he trembled on the brink of a definite declaration. Any
one of those times, if Bess had helped him a little, the great result
would have been achieved.
She liked him better than she had ever liked any other man. Love
as Hortense understood it-and perhaps as the aunts understood it,
but of that Bess wasn't so sure was not in the younger girl's nature.
But Doug's presence excited her, and his absence worried her. The
idea of marrying a youth she had known all her life was a little tame.
But the Rutherford social position and financial standing were un-
assailable. Moreover, in accepting Doug, Bess would show all her
acquaintances that what they had perhaps considered her earlier
failures were only her far-sighted way of keeping the coast clear for
the man she really wanted.
Yet still she held him off. Everything else was blurred to her vision
by the fact which forced itself steadily on her unwilling perception:
her mother was steadily sinking.
They all saw it. They all refused to admit it to one another. But as
the weather grew warmer, Retta never left her room, and seldom
her bed. Much of the time she lay drugged into a coma.
Yet they all fought the inevitable conclusion. The Colonel was
away much of the time; when he was in Mount Royal he always
brought work home and spent solitary evenings shut up in the
library. Uncle Artie was garrulous and fussy. He took to drinking
now in earnest. Amy devoted most of her time to Artie. She relieved
the nurse, too; but the res of them saw less and less of her.
"Why doesn't Aunt Dora come home?" Bess would wake in the
night and think, if Aunt Dora were only here, things would be dif-
BESS
305
ferent. Even with Mamma sick as she was, Aunt Dora could make
the rest of them behave themselves.
But when she spoke to her father about sending for Dora, he
refused curtly. This was the first time in Bess's life that he had ever
been short with her. She was furious at first; then she saw it was
principally anxiety over her mother that made him cross.
Bess felt that it was no good asking Aunt Amy for anything; Amy
had always grudged her what she wanted. Artie had no standing in
the household. Bess bided her time. One morning when her mother
was conscious, and more comfortable than usual, she spoke to her
about Aunt Dora.
"Dora's well off where she is," said Mrs. Langworthy. "It's a
change for her, and she was always a great one for change. Let her
stay until she's ready to come home."
But they weren't writing Dora the truth about Mrs. Langworthy's
condition. They couldn't be, or Dora wouldn't have stayed away for
any consideration. It was the same when they talked to one another.
They all kept up the pitiful pretense that there was hope; they tried
to make small plans for the future, they spoke of this or that which
might be done "when Retta gets stronger."
Sometimes Bess was half convinced that there was hope. When
she wrote to Aunt Dora indeed and she wrote three or four times
in the next few weeks-she chattered along about her own doings,
and regularly succeeded in writing herself into a cheerful mood.
After all, you couldn't be too blunt in a letter.
One warm May evening Doug brought Bess home from a little
party at Marguerite Carpenter's. He steered around the island in the
drive before he stopped his car. "Don't go in yet," he pleaded.
"You're home now. There's no reason why you should be in any
hurry."
Bess sat down in the old swing that still hung between the two
tall trees on "the island." They were in the shadow of the house; but
toward the street the bridal-wreath bushes lifted long plumes of
white in the light of a full moon, and the perfume of opening lilacs
came to them on the night wind.
Doug stood looking down at her; she could feel his gaze, though
she kept her own eyes lowered. She pushed the swing gently to and
fro, and hummed the refrain of the song she had sung at Mar-
306
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
guerite's. She had flirted all evening with another youth; but every-
body assumed nowadays that Doug would take her home from a
party. He had rushed her off abruptly, so that they wouldn't have
to take another couple in the car with them. But for all that, he had
scarcely said a word on the way; he had stared straight ahead of him
and seemed to be mulling something over.
"Let's get back in the car," he said abruptly. "We can drive out in
the country and look at the moon. This is too good a night to waste."
"Certainly not," said Bess. "If I'm not indoors by twelve, I shall
hear from my Aunt Amy."
Doug glanced up at the house; all the windows on this side were
dark. He lifted her off the swing and pressed her so close to him
that she could feel his heart beat. His lips came fumbling for hers.
Bess liked being kissed. But Doug acted as if he didn't quite know
what he was doing. His strangeness made her uncomfortable. She
drew back her head presently and looked at him. His eyes were
closed; and in the peculiar half-light his face looked ghastly.
Still without opening his eyes, he felt for her lips. "I do love you,
Bess," he muttered. "I do love you."
It was the moment of her triumph; and yes, she had fully expected
to triumph. But she hadn't expected it to be quite like this.
Instinct warned her to hold him off. She got one hand free and
rested it on his breast. Then she laughed; that pretty tinkling laugh
of hers had helped her over difficult moments before.
"What of it?" she said. "You say you love me, but you don't
sound pleased."
"Oh, don't talk!" He put a hand behind her head. Now indeed
he did look at her, looked so long and steadily that Bess's eyelids
fluttered and fell. On a long sigh, her lips met his once more.
It seemed a long time later that he stirred; he kept an arm about
her while he seated himself in the swing and drew her down on
his knee. "Such a little armful you are!" he whispered. "How can
anything so small be so sweet?"
Bess lay relaxed with her head on his shoulder. For a few minutes
she had been simply feeling. But now her mind began to race.
She would be married in the fall, if Mamma were stronger by
that time. Perhaps a quiet wedding would be in better taste; but it
BESS
307
mustn't be too quiet, for the Rutherfords would have to be shown
how well the Langworthys did things.
She would ask Hortense to come on from New York and be her
matron-of-honor-unless to be sure Hortense had by that time again
reached the place where she wouldn't want to put herself on exhi-
bition at a wedding. Bess didn't intend to go having children right
off. Eventually, of course, it was the best thing to do, especially if
a man began to lose interest. But there were too many other things
she wanted to do first: travel and entertain, and wear the sleek
furs and beautiful jewels that are suitable for a young matron. Oh,
she would show this town a thing or two! None of the other young
married women here had ever taken hold of the social situation as
Bess intended to take hold of it.
Doug had fought his feeling for her. All the good that had done
him! It had only riveted his chains. She had been clever, too, in the
way she had encouraged his rivals. As a husband perhaps he would
need a good deal of managing; but she intended to manage him.
There would be no giving him his head the way Mamma had
always done with Papa. Why, Bess wouldn't be here to-night if she
hadn't succeeded in putting a stop to that nonsense about moving
out to the Soldiers' Home!
"You love me, Bess?" Doug whispered against her hair. "Say you
love me. Let me hear you say it!"
"Doesn't it look to you as if I did?" Bess laughed again.
But his question had brought her to herself. No good in letting
him feel too sure of her! Mamma's training had been right in that
respect: let the man do it all, and he'll think only the more of you
for making him go all the way.
She pushed away his restraining arm and slipped to her feet. He
clung to her and pled: she mustn't go just yet, he wasn't going to
let her go.
But Bess was resolute. "It's getting late. Somebody may come
looking for me. Doug, we must be sensible. I don't want my family to
get it in for you."
He muttered something about families being a nuisance. Secretly
Bess agreed with him. But she was the picture of demureness when
she led him around to the front steps.
In the shelter of the veranda he enfolded her again. For a moment
308
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
then Bess didn't want to let him go. She was dismayed by the

All at once the light in the hall was turned on from above. Down
the stairs came Amy in her dressing-gown. In the sitting-room the
clock was striking once. That must mean it was half-past twelve.
She would hear from Aunt Amy now!
But Amy spoke only a casual word of caution. "Have you put the
bolt on the door, Bess? If not, never mind. I'll see to it." She walked
past her niece and made straight for the telephone, which was be-
hind the stairs.
Bess heard her give Dr. Simpson's number. With a foot on the
lowest step, she paused. Calling the doctor at this hour of the night!
Mamma must be worse.
Amy came back from the telephone and answered the question
which Bess hesitated to put into words. "Your mother is restless. The
nurse gave her morphine. But instead of quieting her it seemed to
make her worse. And so thirsty! She drinks and drinks, but she
can't seem to quench her thirst."
"Can I see her?" whispered Bess.
"Better not just now, honey. It would only agitate her. You run
along and get to bed. I'll call you if we need you."
Amy drew a chair from the sitting-room and sat down in front
of the door to wait for the doctor. Overcome by a sudden weariness
that made each stair seem mountain-high, Bess dragged herself to
her room and undressed.
Lying in the darkness with her door open, she heard Amy and
Dr. Simpson come upstairs and enter her mother's room. For the
minute that that door was open she heard a high excited cackle
BESS
309
like a person trying to talk through a thunder-clap. That wasn't
her mother, surely?
She wanted her mother to-night, wanted her suddenly, desperately.
She compelled herself to lie quiet until she heard Dr. Simpson leave.
Then she got up and stole down the hall. Everything was quiet now.
But why did it have to happen like this? And to-night of all nights!
Bess closed her door and went back to bed. Deliberately now she
tried to think of Doug. But how could she think of him without
planning for their future? And what plans could she make while
everything hung in this hideous uncertainty?
She slept presently, and dreamed of her lover: disquieting dreams,
in which something she could not put a name to seemed to stand
always between them, keeping them apart. About dawn she woke.
The first thing she remembered was that the hour when she came
in was actually half-past one. And Aunt Amy had never even no-
ticed! That told the whole story, right there.
She lay wondering whether Doug would telephone her this morn-
ing, or send a note, or simply appear at the house and claim her
evening. It would be strange to hear his voice over the telephone
to-day and to realize that although it was the same voice she had
heard so many times, to-day it was ringing up the curtain on the
real drama of their lives.
But the thought left her cold. She bathed and dressed with more
than usual care. Brushing and coiling her lovely dark hair, she faced
her own reflection in the glass and tried to realize that it was now
the reflection of an “engaged girl," a girl who had succeeded in life,
a girl to whom was opening the very future that she had so long
dreamed of.
Over the certainty of that future she smiled a little. But directly
after breakfast she asked Artie to drive her downtown.
She went directly to the telegraph office. On her own responsibility
she sent Dora the curt message: MAMMA SINKING COME AT ONCE.

THEN Dora reached Mount Royal Mrs. Langworthy
better. Bess, watching the meeting of the sisters, felt sheep-

When Dora rose to leave the room Mrs. Langworthy said, "I'm
glad you're back, honey. We needed waking up."
"There's nothing I like doing better than waking people up,"
answered Dora, making one of her monkey-faces.
But just outside the door she turned to Bess and kissed her im-
pulsively. Such demonstrativeness from Dora told the whole story.
Bess clung to her for a minute; then she rushed to her own room,
so that her mother would not be disturbed by the sobs she could no
longer control.
For three days the rally continued. Bess swung to feverish hope.
She could not imagine life without her mother; even Doug would
not make up to her for the loss of that sustaining presence. Surely,
then, her mother would be spared to her.
But this was the last flare-up of vitality. All through June Mrs.
Langworthy lay for the most part under morphine. Sometimes she
would open her eyes, and know them all. Once she said clearly to
Bess, "I seem to sleep so much. Stupid, isn't it?"
"Then she doesn't know!" thought Bess. "Oh, I'm glad, I'm glad
she doesn't know!"
But Mrs. Langworthy knew; she was simply trying to ease things
for her beloved child. One night early in July, she suddenly whis-
pered to Dora, "I won't see the baby. I'm sorry." Those were the
last words she ever spoke. She died the next day.
Her death threw Bess into a state of bubbling hysteria. Amy, who

hadn't had an unbroken night's sleep in months, went suddenly

"You're not going to have that child wear mourning?" he de-

Hortense had gained weight; and her matronly outlines were ex-
ceedingly becoming to her. She moved, spoke, smiled with new

"We're a handsome family," teased Bess. But she revised her in-

Plans for her marriage were still indefinite. Doug hadn't even
asked the Colonel to sanction their engagement. Mrs. Langworthy's

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
death had interrupted the course which events would normally have

Bess would never have chosen this special time to fall in love.
But after Hortense and Bill left, and the household fell back once
more into routine, she felt that she could begin to look ahead again.
Doug took her out almost every evening for a walk or a drive.
He liked to get her away from the house; and indeed Bess agreed
with him that the Langworthy house was a doleful place that sum-

for a while at the Chases' or the Pattersons'. She could scarcely get
him even to drop in at the downtown drug-store which was famous
for its ice-cream sodas. All the young people from Prospect Avenue
went there in summer; Bess loved the glare, and the clinking of
glasses, and hearing herself hailed from nearly every table when
she entered.
"It wouldn't look right when you're in mourning," Doug insisted.
He was using her mourning as an excuse to keep her to himself,
to get her off in the dark and kiss her. She grew to dread their
drives. He stirred her senses; yet there was something raw and a
little disgusting in setting out so primly under the eyes of the aunts,
and knowing all the time that an hour later he would stop his car
in a lonely spot and slip from under the steering-wheel, and take
her on his knees for an hour of uninterrupted love-making.
He had always been drawn to her more strongly after an absence.
Bess considered going off now for a visit to Hortense; considered
it, and decided against it. Things had changed since her last absence;
she had him now, and she intended to keep him. But he acted too
sure of her. Something had to be done about that.
No formal entertaining was possible just then; Bess herself would
have been the first to decry it. She didn't want to encourage attentions
from other young men, either. Wasn't she now practically the same
as a married woman? But there was one thing she could do, and
did. She began to ask other girls in of an evening, two or three at
a time.
"Can't you see enough of those gigglers during the daytime?"
asked the Colonel irritably.

Artie was drowning his sorrows pretty steadily, it seemed to Bess.

Artie wasn't drinking, he would follow Amy around the house

wasted this summer. Her fine hands were transparent, the delicate

Dora was back at her guide-books, revising her notes from her

"Go ahead and have your friends here whenever you like," the

To go out of mourning would indeed be to force Doug's hand.
But not for the world would Bess have admitted that the aunts had
any priority in grief. She had lost her mother, they only a sister.
Once or twice lately Dora had actually spoken of "Retta" as if she
were still alive. Spoken of her casually, as if she were carrying on
the thread which had been so finally snapped!

316
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
he who used to indulge her slightest whim and laugh at her worst

He missed her mother. Probably he was worried about business.

Happy here? She was acutely unhappy here, and not only because

Dora had laughed when she reported all this. She had seemed to
think it was very bright of her to effect a truce in the dispute that
had worried Mamma so. Whose house was this anyhow, when you
came right down to it?
Oh, but they could keep this house and welcome! She was leaving
here very soon. The minute she told them she was leaving, they
would do their best to atone for their present neglect and usurpa-
tion. She intended to have that small but sweet revenge without
further delay.

elegantly, "you've been paying attentions to me ever since I was out

Anyhow, it would be fun to be the center of so much attention. She

She had uttered the words in mere temper and pique. But when

"Well, I've nothing of my own. No property-no money, even.

Bess had been hurt before. Now she was bewildered. "But Doug,

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"I'm not defending my father, Bess. But he has me at his mercy.

It flashed through Bess's mind that Bill Jerome had said he would

going better another year, that will make a difference, you know.
And in the end I can get around my father, I'm sure. Let him get
used to the idea little by little and take out his wrath in sputtering.
Then he won't be likely to do anything drastic."
"Drive me home," said Bess. "And for Heaven's sake don't talk
any more. I've seen a side of you to-night that I never saw before."
"I'm sorry if I've offended you. But perhaps it's just as well for
you to know the worst about me if you're going to marry me."
"That's a pretty big 'if," said Bess.
Her good-night kiss was cold; and when he pressed her to "think
it over" and see him again to-morrow evening, she shook her head
impatiently. She knew what happened to those girls who "went stead-
ily" with the same man for years without marrying him. Eventually
BESS
319
he saw a new face which he liked better; and for the years when
he had kept other men away from her, the girl had no redress.
The shelter of her own home had never been so grateful to her
as it was to-night. Though a great deal had happened this evening-
too much to happen in any one evening-it was still only nine o'clock.
Lights were burning in the hall and parlor; probably somebody had
thought that Bess would be bringing her young man into the par-
lor. And across the hall in the sitting-room, Dora at her book shared
a light with Amy over her embroidery.
"Where's Papa?" asked Bess.
He was in the library, they told her. Passing around the stairs to
the library door, Bess realized that since her mother died the Colonel
had had many solitary evenings.
"Working hard?" she called into the library.
"Oh, no! Come in." The Colonel looked up from his papers with
an eager smile.
All her life he had been smiling at her that way. With him it
had always been, "Come in, Bess." How could she ever have dreamed
of deserting him for people who had to ask for time in order to get
used to the very idea of tolerating her?
She perched on the arm of his chair, rumpled his hair, tweaked
his nose. He had always been so scrupulous about his appearance
that Bess had loved to take liberties with his neatness.
It struck her though that lately he hadn't been quite as tidy as
usual. He sometimes wore his piqué vests three days now; and
to-night he had on a collar which was a little frayed. Mamma used
to keep him up to the mark, of course; she had been far more care-
ful about his appearance than about her own. With a shade of anger
Bess decided that it was never going to be that way in her household.
But how he must miss her mother, daily and hourly! An aging
man left alone in the world: not quite alone, to be sure, but then
he might better have been. He had plenty of liabilities still liabili-
ties instead of family ties. Why, if Bess were to leave him, there'd
be no one remaining in the household except those persons whom
Mrs. Palmer had once acidly described as "the Colonel's poor rela-
tions." They weren't quite that, of course. But you wouldn't ever
catch one of the Millses offering to leave this comfortable shelter.
She slipped from the arm of the chair to his knee. She chattered
320
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
about getting another dog one of these days. Presently she went in
search of the old cat and lifted it to his desk, and sat stroking it while
she talked to her father.
She was consciously being charming to him. And why not? She
intended to make this the first of many similar evenings: to be a
companion to him in his loneliness, a reward for the care he had
been bestowing on so many people through so many years. She
could make up to him for everything.
She would do just that, even if she had to devote the rest of her
life to him. Yet all the while, even though she petted and expanded
her new and solemn purpose, she was conscious of a tickling at the
back of her mind.
It was the challenge of something she hadn't understood, and
must somehow manage to understand. There was something in
Doug's attitude which baffled her. Until she had the clue, she must
continue to hunt for it.
