III
T came to her in the middle of the night. Doug had given him-
self away when he said, "If your father's business gets better
another year, that will make a difference."
That there was any possible fault to be found with her position
gave Bess a nasty shock. Without being precisely members of the
Prospect Avenue "set," the Langworthys had always moved freely
in and out of it. Mrs. Langworthy had never made a life work of
her entertaining, the way some of the Prospect Avenue matrons
did. But when the Langworthys did entertain, the Rutherfords were
generally among their guests. At the time Retta died, Doug's mother
and father were both in her debt.
If the shoe pinched, the pinch must be financial. That her father
was not a rich man Bess had always known. But as far back as she
could remember, their scale of living had been comfortable and in
some respects even lavish. Sometimes when she had mentioned things
she needed to Bess there was no difference between desiring a thing
and needing it-the Colonel had demurred for a minute. But in
the end he always said, "Oh, go ahead and get it! I can manage."
And he sometimes counseled, "Never try to save money on your
luxuries. That's not the place to economize."
The big zinc fortunes led the town. Then came the banks and
the manufacturing plants. The Colonel was always listed as a "manu-
facturer," though he had a retail business, too. Not that a queer old
curmudgeon like John Rutherford would have dreamed of objecting
to a man because he was "in trade": old John rated people on their
cash standing.
But to have money come in here to be compelled to stop and
weigh herself against money-was an appalling idea. Girls weren't
supposed to have anything to do with such considerations. Bess
321
322
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
had been brought up to take it for granted that a girl lived at home
with Papa until she was married; then automatically responsibility
for meeting her needs was shifted from father to husband. She had
made out very well with father. Why on the threshold of her new
existence must she be brought rudely up against considerations she
had never expected to have to think about?
She would never have dreamed of marrying a man for his money
if she hadn't cared for the man himself. But she was very fond of
Doug. Doug poor might not have attracted her so much as Doug in
the direct line of succession to the First National Bank; but then,
Doug wasn't poor.
Doug had everything she wanted. These past weeks she had said
to herself over and over, "I'm so glad I've been steered safely past
the little love affairs which haven't really meant a thing to me."
She had said it, and she had believed it. Roger and Bob and Bill
had been nothing but the preliminaries to Doug, and to the triumph
she had always craved.
Now the triumph was going flat on her hands. She had told Doug
she intended to break off her engagement. When her temper cooled,
she realized that a rupture with him was no solution. Doug would
eventually marry some other girl, and she might have to put up
with what the old saying called "a crooked stick." She might even,
if she walked too far through the woods, pick up no stick at all;
she might turn into a pallid pitiful spinster like Aunt Amy, or chase
trifles and try to make herself believe they were important, the way
Dora did.
She wished she could get some facts on her family's position to
confront Doug with. There was no good going to her father for
information; the Colonel held rigidly to the conviction that the
women of his family had no call to bother themselves with such
things. If Bess tried to speak to him on financial matters, he would
leap to the conclusion that there was some luxury or indulgence
she wanted; in the end she would be better off by a new horse or
a trip East, but she wouldn't know a thing more than she did now.
Artie didn't know half as much as he pretended. Anyhow, she
could hardly bring herself to look at Artie these days, so deep was
her disgust with him and all his ways. She thought of a letter to
Hortense; but, after all, Hortense had been out of touch with the
BESS
323
situation too long. What a wedding the Colonel had given Hor-
tense, by the way! Did that look as if the Langworthys were on the
road to the poorhouse?
Eventually she fell back on Dora. "I've wondered about that my-

success he hoped it would be. But then, as nearly as I can make out,
no new business makes money the way the founders hope it will.
Lots of business men owe money right along, too. I don't mean
bills; I mean notes at the bank, and things like that. So it's hard
to say just where they stand."
"But a rich man, Auntie Dora-surely all the town knows which
the rich men are?"
"Oh, yes! But rich men are never so rich as people think they are.
Prestige counts for a lot, here and everywhere else. If it weren't
for my naturally prying disposition, I'd be impressed, too."
Bess refused to let the subject be changed. "Papa must have made
some money," she argued. "And I don't think he buys stock in gold
mines and things like that, does he?"
"He and Artie made the most wonderful fishing reel on the mar-
ket," said Dora. "The only trouble was that the thing didn't sell.
Colonel's buggies were always too good; they lasted so well that
nobody ever got around to buying a second one. The Langworthy
car is very good, too; so now I suppose he is due to lose money on
that. But I don't know what you and I can do about it, unless we
decide to get along without a second girl."
Dora was just being funny. She had picked up a lot of stray bits
of information which were news to Bess, but she drew no sensible
conclusions from what she had learned. Indeed, she seemed to enjoy
perplexing herself as well as other people.
But then, even Dora wasn't quite herself these days. When Bess
was leaving the room Dora said wistfully, "After all, money usually
takes care of itself, honey. So long as we're all well, everything else
can take care of itself." She was thinking of Mamma, of course.
When was Mamma out of Bess's mind, night or day? Oh, why
did this have to happen just now? Bess had never in her life needed
her mother as she needed her now. And to think that never again
would she have that warmth and cheerfulness to go back to! Mamma
had been shrewd in her way; often Bess had felt the edge of her
324
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
candid disapproval when Papa would have swallowed the bait and
never even have known that he swallowed it. But Mamma had always
been Bess's refuge when things looked really black.
And now to think that never again-never-never-never-! Bess
couldn't reconcile herself to that idea of "never." It was put up to
her afresh twenty times a day; and still she rebelled against it.
She would be better off out of this house. What she needed now
was a fresh start. A fresh start, and an eager welcome! She had
always been so sure that when she finally bestowed her hand, her
new family would receive her as a choice acquisition. "Our new
daughter," she had imagined them saying. "Our son's bride. We
all love her."
But in actual experience she found herself baffled and evaded;
she was hidden by the very person who should have been proud to
parade her. He wanted her to wait until his family should forget
their worst objections to her. Bess knew better than that. What good
would waiting do-unless, to be sure, the Colonel should happen to
make a fortune overnight?
Yet she was sure Doug really loved her. And now that she realized
what the difficulty was, she was able to recede somewhat from the
position in which he had placed her.
Her new white mourning-the first ever seen in Mount Royal-
helped her to recover her self-esteem. To her own eyes mourning
white was quite different from bridal white, or from the piqué skirt
and casual blouse which she used to wear so much for tennis. When
she donned it she swung back to something of the feeling she used
to have during a brief attack of religious fervor in her teens, when
she had begged her first black dress, and had worn it and a silver
cross every Sunday to Morning Prayer. It made her feel consciously
and securely "good."
Amy wore one or the other of her mourning frocks when she
happened to think of it. After Mrs. Langworthy's funeral, Dora had
definitely laid black aside. "I miss Retta, and I shall always miss
her," she said. "But I don't see any sense in making yourself feel
any worse than you have to."
But Bess, in her severe white dresses and her flat white hat with
a narrow black band around it, seemed to herself not only respectful
to her mother's memory, but inviolate in her person. If she went out
BESS
325
alone of an evening now with Doug, it was only to the downtown
drug-store for sodas. Sooner or later, anyhow, the aunts would have
put a stop to those solitary drives. But Bess didn't need Aunt Amy
to teach her propriety.
Last summer, when Mrs. Langworthy was still able to get down to
the veranda on fine afternoons, a hammock had been swung for
her there. Bess had Jason put the hammock up again now. There
of an evening she sat decorously propped with pillows, her feet al-
ways on the veranda floor. Doug sat opposite her in a chair so long
as there was danger of anybody's coming out of the house to greet
him.
The moment when he crossed to her side always filled Bess with
a strange delicious excitement. Their first kiss, too, thrilled and dis-
turbed her. But later on when she lay in the shelter of his arms, she
felt calm, protected, safe. The security of his presence banished for
the time her doubt and bewilderment. With a sigh of contentment
she thought, this must be what marriage was like.
In September they had a spell of hot weather which did not lighten
even at night. Changing before supper, so that Doug would find
her fresh and cool, Bess laid aside her high-necked mourning dresses.
She slipped into a white skirt, turned her blouse in at the neck,
rolled the sleeves in precise folds above her elbows. Her skin was
still smooth and dry after her bath. She felt her own remoteness
from ordinary sweltering humanity.
She felt it afresh later on when the hammock creaked under Doug's
weight. His arms were moist; and although he had undoubtedly
had a bath just as recently as she had, there was a slight odor of
sweat about him. Bess's nose wrinkled fastidiously. But of course she
couldn't expect him to refrain from kissing her just because it was
a warm night.
He was breathing hard against the side of her face. Bess pulled
her head down until it rested on his shoulder, and closed her eyes.
It had been a tedious day, with Amy shut up all afternoon in her
own room and Artie and Dora going for a ride "to cool off." Bess
had refused to go with them; she suspected that Artie would stick
as usual to the downtown streets, heat or no heat. There was no
company about the place for Bess, and nothing for them to do if
there had been company. The tennis-court hadn't even been marked

this year. But another summer, if Bess were still in this house, every-

"I'm not a woman who allows liberties. You ought to know that

"I'm not so sure about that." An abyss seemed to yawn at her

a single thing to improve our position. Honestly now, have you?"

But was she going to meet a series of crises from now on, tide

not. That was probably the way Jason carried on his affair with

of her mother's friends seemed to get themselves so often "in trouble";
even a hired girl should know better, it seemed to her. Well, now
she could guess. But she didn't want any seamy knowledge. She
wanted peace, security, a life arranged on her own terms.
She glanced toward Doug. He was staring down at the floor. Those
last words of hers had sounded like an ultimatum. She didn't want
to lose him. But she didn't intend to take back a single word. After
all, the difficulty was of his making. Let him find the way out.
The pause lengthened unbearably. Bess had a good mind to get

little, and it was after all your own business rather than other peo-

She could expect nothing at Amy's hands, now or ever, except
severe judgment. Amy had repelled her confidence. But that served
Bess right for offering it. What did Amy know about love, anyhow?
Bess had found some of its lessons distasteful at the moment. But
now that the episode was over, she looked back at it with a certain
pride. She was right in not letting Doug go to lengths that no nice
girl would tolerate. But he was right, too, in reminding her that he
was man and she woman. That was the great fundamental fact on
which Bess had to build.
B
IV
ESS had often pictured her wedding. The excitement, pro-
longed for weeks and constantly growing more intense, would
reach a thrilling climax in the solemn hush of "the ceremony."
She would be deliciously important; all the other girls always envied
a bride. And well they might: the eyes of her world were fixed right
then on a woman who had triumphed.
The dream had steadily grown more precise. It had started in early
childhood, when she saw herself sweeping up a church aisle with
a long white satin train dragging behind her. In those dreams she
never got as far as the altar, and it did not so much as occur to her
to wonder who would be waiting for her there.
With the coming of adolescence her interest had widened to in-
clude the groom. In church during the sermon she would open her
prayer-book surreptitiously and read over the marriage service, in-
serting opposite her own the name of any boy who happened to
attract her at the moment. Since Hortense was married Bess had
concentrated on the idea of having a wedding more splendid than
Hortense's: eight bridesmaids instead of six, a veil of real lace which
would presently become an heirloom, a vested choir imported from
Chicago to lead the procession and sing the wedding-march.
All that she renounced for Doug, but not without a struggle. At
first she had fought the idea of an elopement. But anything was
better than continuing strain and conflict; and anyhow, if she gave
in on such an important point, that would surely secure her a tighter
hold over Doug. Renouncing her wedding plans and keeping her
family in the dark ought to show him as nothing else could how
much she loved him.
The night before she was to meet him in Chicago she lay sobbing
in the darkness, consumed by loneliness for her mother. If Mamma
330
BESS
331
had lived, all this would never have happened to Bess. Now that
Mamma was gone, what difference did anything make?
She took the noon train into Chicago. Doug had gone by an earlier
train and secured the license. He met her at the station in Chicago,
and they were driven straight to a church.
Doug had bought her a huge bouquet, and had brought along as
witnesses two Chicago friends who were sworn to secrecy. They
were both strangers to Bess. Facing a strange clergyman in an empty
church, she handled herself with a dignity which was born of
apathy. It seemed to her as if some one else were being married;
her last moments as Bess Langworthy trickled dully away, bringing
her neither excitement nor triumph.
After they left the church Doug telegraphed to his father and to
Colonel Langworthy. Then he and Bess boarded the night train for
Minneapolis. Doug was taking no chances of being followed and
found.
Dinner was served to them in their state-room on the train. Bess
scarcely tasted her food, though she drank a glass of claret and some
coffee. After the table was removed Doug said to her, "Shan't we
sit out in the car for a few minutes while the porter makes up our
state-room?"
Other passengers were reading, yawning, playing cards. The train
thundered along through the night; Bess wouldn't have been sur-
prised to have it plunge somehow into a void, and engulf her in
blank oblivion. But everything went on just as it always did in a
Pullman. (Only lately she had still thought child-fashion that riding
in a Pullman was an exciting treat!) She knew Doug was watching
her; but she stared steadily at her own image in the window, an
image moving specterlike along a country-side that slipped steadily
by in the darkness.
Presently the porter came to tell Doug that their state-room was
ready. Doug held the door open for her.
"I'm going down to the smoker for a while," he said. "I'll be
back as soon as I have my cigar."
This was how they managed such things, then! Bess had often
wondered about the first night. It seemed awfully crude just to begin
undressing in front of a man to whom you had always carefully
forbidden any familiarity beyond what a nice girl might properly
332
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
allow her fiancé. But of course when Doug came back now he would
find her already in her night things and dressing-gown. She won-
dered dully how men found out the way they were supposed to
behave on this occasion. Did other men tell them?
Her bag and dressing-case lay ready to her hand. Mechanically Bess
got out her lacy new nightgown, her little morocco slippers, a be-
ribboned dressing-gown which she had bought in New York and
had never worn in Mount Royal. She took off her hat and dress and
hung the dress up carefully, remembering to turn the shields out to
air just as she always did.
"It's my wedding-dress," she muttered aloud. "My wedding-dress!"
Then a deadly weariness overcame her. She hadn't slept much
last night, and the day had been exhausting. She couldn't even un-
dress until she had rested for a few minutes. Why, she had had her
hat on since before noon!
She stumbled to the bed and lay down across it. She had just
strength to pull a blanket over her bare shoulders. Then she sank
into oblivion. The last thing she remembered was the swaying of a
light as the train jolted forward.
The next thing she knew she heard a man's voice close to her.
Without opening her eyes she wondered why anybody should think
fit to reprove her for not fastening her door. "No doors are ever
locked in this house." Hadn't she heard her father say that more
than once?
Then suddenly she recognized the voice. She sat up and drew
away; she looked at Doug in astonishment, almost in horror. But
Doug was bending over to unbutton her shoes for her. "You poor
darling!" he whispered. "Just tired out, aren't you?"
Gradually Bess came to herself. Why, this was this was his night,
the night to which he must have been looking forward while she
averted her eyes from it. He found her sleepy, sullen, a disappoint-
ment. He mustn't find her a disappointment. Not to-night, or ever.
But especially not to-night.
"Reach me my dressing-gown, please," said Bess. While his back
was turned she had a minute to gather herself together. She yawned,
stretched, kicked off her shoes. Then erect in dressing-gown and
slippers, she found the answer.
She took out her hair-pins, and the glossy dark coil fell about her
BESS
333
shoulders. She smiled and lifted her face to his. "Isn't it about time
that you kissed Bess Rutherford?" she whispered. "The one in the
church didn't count."
They stayed in Minneapolis for a week. They put up at the best
hotel, and had champagne with their dinner every night. They
drove out to Minnehaha Falls and the Falls of St. Anthony, and in-
spected the University of Minnesota and the flour mills. Between
sight-seeing tours Bess indulged in a round of shopping. She had
brought only the one bag with her, so that she needed nearly every-
thing. Doug went with her, his check-book in a handy pocket. It
wasn't the honeymoon Bess had dreamed of. Still, on the whole it
did very well.
There were times when she was fairly afraid of him, times when
he didn't seem like Doug at all, but like some demanding changeling
that had somehow acquired strange rights over her. Did all girls
find these first days so difficult? You had been trained to be a little
iceberg; then suddenly you were a wife, and a wife's duty was to
be compliant.
Afterward he was always very sweet with her; and before the
week was out, Bess caught herself looking scornfully at girls in
hotel lobbies and on the streets. She was a married woman now,
and knew things that they didn't. She was a married woman; and
because she had never had a proper engagement ring, she was al-
ready wondering when Doug would buy her a cabochon sapphire-
or perhaps a ruby-to keep her wedding-ring from looking so lonely
and poverty-stricken. She thought diamonds a little vulgar; but
"cabochons" were the newest thing, and on the other hand rubies,
though expensive, were naturally becoming to dark women.
In spite of its perplexities, she hated to see the week draw to a
close. She would have disliked its dwindling, even if she hadn't
dreaded the return to Mount Royal. A honeymoon was a time set
apart; after it was over, you had again to face the plain prose of
daily life.
She hadn't planned much about what their arrangements would
be when they reached home. She intended to have her own way in
the things that really mattered. But what was the good of getting
married if you had to take the responsibility of thinking out your
steps in advance? The worries of readjustment were Doug's affair
334
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
-her husband's. "My husband," she said, and licked her lips a little.
On a crisp October evening they boarded a train for the return
trip. Bess felt years older than the girl who had come up here only
a week ago: very much wiser, and yes, very much happier. It sur-
prised her sometimes to find how happy she really was. Since Mamma
died, Bess had almost forgotten how to be happy.
They would stay with the Rutherfords, Doug told her, but only
for a short time. Eventually he expected his father to build a house
for them. Meanwhile, they could find something to rent. Bess looked
forward eagerly to buying things for a house of her own. She would
get good furniture, something that could be used later, too, when
Doug's father built for them. It never paid to buy cheap stuff.
Their welcome at the Rutherfords' was worse than Bess had ex-
pected. Doug's mother was plaintive and tearful. Old John was grim.
"This isn't any of my choosing," he announced to his new daughter-
in-law. "But I suppose I'll just have to make the best of it."
At the Langworthys' Bess was received with open arms. Amy
cried over her. Artie made a lot of silly jokes and cackled at them.
Dora promptly came down with a wedding-present: a dozen bouillon
cups in silver standards.
"But Auntie Dora, they're just too nice for the likes of me!" Bess
protested in surprise and gratification.
"They cost me half a year's income, as you could easily find out
by pricing them at Cordiner & Morse's," Dora acknowledged. "But
I thought it might be just as well to show the Rutherfords that
Doug hadn't married into the poor-house."
Only the Colonel proved unreasonable. He thought Bess should
have told him in advance what she planned to do. He had never
forbidden her anything that her heart was set on, had he? Then
surely he had earned her confidence in this most important matter.
Bess explained that a big wedding was out of the question so
soon after Mamma's death, and Doug had simply been trying to
save bother. (Doug-my husband: he must take the responsibility
now for whatever either of us does.)
The Colonel wasn't convinced, but he shifted his ground. She
and Doug must come here to live. There was plenty of room for
them. The Colonel could move into Hortense's old room; Bess and
BESS
335
Doug could have his, and could refurnish Bess's old room as their
private sitting-room.
This didn't suit Bess's plans at all. She hated living at the Ruther-
fords'; but it was a post of vantage. With the young couple under-
foot constantly, old John must presently make up his mind what
he was going to do for them. No matter how cruelly it hurt him,
he would finally have to do something handsome; he stood that
much in awe of public opinion.
Anyhow, the Langworthy house seemed strange to Bess. It was
the only home she had known before her marriage; but already it
had ceased to appear like home. She had viewed it with a critical
eye even when she came home from school. Now she saw it as even
more of an oddity: a house in the wrong part of town, a house built
a little at a time, heated in a makeshift way, and with all the bath-
rooms miles from a bedroom.
It was a house packed with curiosities, too-with curiosities, and
with plain litter: Hortense's painted panels, Mamma's triumphs of
needlework, Dora's guitar, the old magazines in which Uncle Artie
was reading a serial, the heaps of business papers which the Colonel
was always leaving in conspicuous places so that he'd remember to
take them with him to the office.
And how shabby everything had grown lately! The walls, which
used to be repapered every other year, hadn't been touched for a long
time. The sitting-room rug was worn down almost to the back. The
long lace curtains which hung in all the downstairs windows needed
renewing. In the parlor and sitting-room they had been turned end
for end, so that the pattern was wrong-side up; and the curtains in
the dining-room were pushed back as far as they would go, "to get
the view," Dora said, but also to conceal the darns.
"I love coming home and seeing you," said Bess. "I'd lose that fun
if I lived here."
Under her glibness lay a genuine pang. The house wasn't the same
without Mamma. It never would be the same again.
And the way the aunts lorded it now was shown afresh when it
came to handing over to Bess such silver and linen as she might use
in her own household. "You'll want some of your mother's things,
Bess," said Amy. And Dora promptly began to plan out what it
would be nice for Bess to have.
336
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Oh, they were generous enough! Bess got all the newest linen, the

She and Doug came to the Langworthys for Thanksgiving dinner.

Alexander Kirkland, one of the weaknies: men in town. had died

337
The house boasted a servants' hall, a drawing-room furnished like

The deed was signed in May, and in June Bess and Doug moved in.
Bess was thrilled at the magnificence which was now hers. She had
already got over the idea that this house, or any house, could be too
pretentious for her important young self.
But she soon found that a wife's duty includes other things beside
what she had once heard her mother sum up in the words, "I don't
believe in making so much fuss about-er-sex. It's there, and we just
have to make the best of it." Running a large house on Doug's salary
presented difficulties; and at present there was no hope of getting
more money out of John Rutherford. For years to come he would
groan at the thought of how much he had paid for the young people's
house; he wasn't at all bashful about reminding them of it right to
their faces.
Yet what was the good of having a large house-what was the
good even of being a young married woman, and perhaps the prettiest
one in Mount Royal-unless you entertained suitably? Before her first
summer in the Kirkland house was over, Bess had put her hand to
tasks which as a girl she would have scorned. She had to pretend,
however, that she never did anything more strenuous than give orders.
She learned to descend the imposing staircase and greet her guests
with a suitable smile and small-talk, even when she was out of breath
from her recent desperate race up the back stairs to get rid of her
apron and powder her nose after she heard the door-bell ring.
In September, however, things were made easier by her taking over
Ida, who was really a well-trained servant. Aunt Dora thought the

winter; and if Ida went to Bess, Louise's great grievance would be

The Colonel offered her the old team, too. By alternating Nellie

But Doug refused to take such old horses even as a gift. "Your

Bess passed the refusal on. But that winter the Colonel used the
street-car. He sent the horses out to a farm, pensioning them in their
old age. He gave Bess a Langworthy car. After all, she had been the
first woman in Mount Royal ever to drive an automobile. When the
worst of the winter was over, she found her new car very useful. She
always managed to get somebody to crank it for her.
Both Thanksgiving and Christmas this year she and Doug went
to the Rutherfords' for the entire day. But both times she was careful
to ask the Langworthys to her house for dinner on the following
Sunday.
One sparkling afternoon in January she encountered Artie in

to carry on the business these days. Mrs. Cordiner was no better, and
old Cordiner was now almost helpless.
Artie's cordiality was undiminished, though Bess had never for-

Bess was furious. Didn't she always invite Aunt Dora and Aunt
Amy when she gave a big tea? They generally came: Dora very trim
and demure, with a twinkle in her eye which suggested that she found
the proceedings somehow amusing, Amy always a little ill at ease.
Papa, of course, had never forgiven Bess for the circumstances of her
marriage. She couldn't very well come right out and tell him that it
was on his account she had had to do things that way.
He had paid his note at the bank, Doug told her; he would have
been welcome in her house any time he had chosen to come. But the
family ought to realize-even Artie ought to realize that with all
BESS
339
she had on her hands, Bess couldn't everlastingly be running out to
the old Langworthy house just for an hour's talk with them. Anyhow,
people didn't make those drop-in visits any more the way they used
to; life was becoming too complicated.
The center of her life had shifted; the Langworthys were now only
on the fringe of her interests. Why couldn't they accept that fact, and
not let it bother them? In the Colonel's eyes she was still a little girl
-his little girl. His refusal to see her any other way was touching,
if you were in the humor for being touched; but it was also exasper-
ating.
After she had cooled off a little, however, Bess took Artie's advice.
One evening when Doug was out of town, she drove herself out to the
Langworthys'. They had a pleasant hour all together; Bess did most
of the talking, but then Bess had the most to tell.
As she might have foreseen, however, the visit led Papa to believe
that she intended to "drop in at home" right along. But Doug was
seldom away; and when he was at home, he preferred to put in their
evenings in more exciting ways than by listening to the old Lang-
worthy family jokes.
Oh, Bess was sorry that they felt she had seceded from the clan!
She was only leading her own life, and leading it very capably and
creditably. Perhaps even in doing the best thing for herself she had
in the long run done the best thing for the rest of them, too.
Doug had more than once expressed surprise that the auto factory
was still going. Those four aging people were all dependent on it.
In time Bess might have to offer one or more of them a home. The
Rutherford fortunes were solid as a rock, even if Bess didn't get much
of the Rutherford money to spend.
If she ever had to come to the rescue, she would do so graciously
and gladly. Meanwhile, they could not lead her life for her. They
ought not to expect it.
With Papa alone she could have dealt, however, if the rest of them
had been out of the way. There was no chance of their getting out of
the way, of course. Probably the Colonel was the only person in
Mount Royal who did not realize that his immediate family was
now altogether gone from him: his life was dedicated exclusively to
the service of his in-laws.
BOOK EIGHT
THE COLONEL
WHEN
I
HEN Mrs. Langworthy died the Colonel missed her so
keenly that at first he could scarcely come to terms with the
thought that his life must go on day after day without her. Every-
thing was now so changed and his own house so strange to him.
His homecomings were something to be hurried through. At the
family table, which used to be so jolly and often so noisy, the talk
was perfunctory. Of an evening he would shut himself up in the
library with his papers. But however steadfastly he tried to work, he
could never quite lose consciousness of the empty shell about him;
and he would linger hour after hour, even when he was bone-weary,
putting off the moment when he must ascend to his desolate bed-
room.
The curious maladjustment at the heart of their relation had
abruptly ceased to matter. With Retta he had lived the best years of
his life. Whatever else changed or fluctuated, Retta was always there.
Time after time in those first lonely weeks it seemed to him that
Retta was still there: that she was in the next room or upstairs or
heckling Jason in the garden.
Once or twice he actually left his chair and snatched at a door-
knob, certain that if he only moved fast enough he could catch her.
Then his hand would fumble and a cold sweat break out on his
forehead. Over him rushed desolation more overwhelming than he
had known at the actual moment when she left them all. "Dead,"
you said. "Dead," just like that; and you thought you accepted the
fact. It took time to make you realize that she was actually gone, her
hearty laugh silenced, her tasks unfinished, her rich vitality wiped
out as completely as if it had never existed.
Even in that first summer, however, the shock of her death began
to wear off. Those last cruel weeks slipped from his memory; they
340
THE COLONEL
341
were better forgotten. The last years began to dim a little, too. More
and more he thought of Retta as a pretty young matron. Often, too,
he remembered her as the dashing belle she had been when he first
met her. That recollection had been blurred for many years; now
strangely it reasserted itself, and the gay image consoled oftener than
it mocked him.
There was no such soothing aftermath to Bess's marriage. The
curly-haired little girl who used to look up into his face so trustingly.
-sometimes so mischievously-had been betrayed by the woman
who walked over coolly into the camp of the enemy.
Oh, he forgave her marriage! When he had got over his first fury
and hurt, he forgave even her elopement. Her heart had long been
set on Doug Rutherford; the Colonel granted her Doug, just as he
would have granted her the moon on a silver platter if that had been
what her heart was set on. He didn't like Doug especially; but then,
he wouldn't have liked any young man who had managed to run
off with his precious Bess.
What galled him was the way that Bess had settled down to a life
from which the Colonel and the Millses were practically excluded.
The situation was difficult to begin with: the Colonel knew that old
John Rutherford considered him anything but a solid success in busi-
ness. But he did his best to clear the way there.
He began by paying off his note at the Rutherford bank. In order
to do that he had to sell his cherished stock in the transit company.
He let it go with a queer sense of guilt; he had always intended that
stock for Retta's widow's mite, and the fact that Retta wouldn't need
it now was only an additional mockery.
In later transactions he kept clear of the Rutherfords, though too
many of his transactions were on the wrong side of the ledger. To
keep going he had mortgaged his factory at the State bank. That
following winter he mortgaged his house. He had always sworn to
himself that that was one thing he would never do: sooner than
mortgage his homestead he would close out his business, and live
somehow in the old house on his pension.
But interest on his notes had to be met; and a business cannot be
closed down simply on a boast. He was now asking for the Lang-
worthy car as much as it cost him to build. That was only common
sense, surely; but the high price narrowed his market. He had stood
342
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
out against making an inferior car, which was what his engineer had
advised him to do. He didn't want his name on any makeshift. The
Langworthy car must be-and was something to be proud of. But
the very excellence of the car stood in the way of further sales to old
customers; the Langworthy just didn't wear out. Yet every time he
sold a car the Colonel's spirits rose in the old hopeful way. He would
beat his difficulties yet. He had to beat them.
There were times, too, when his domestic situation seemed not
only tolerable but satisfactory. Dora and Amy kept the house run-
ning on the old lines. When Ida went to Bess's, Dora took over the
second-floor work and Amy kept the lower rooms in order; Louise
waited on table in addition to her cooking. The famous peas and
purple corn appeared on the table in due season. In summer there
were always fresh flowers about the house. Winter evenings there
was often a fire of cannel coal in the library. On the Colonel's invi-
tation Dora would bring her book in here, and Amy her embroidery.
Artie sat with them, too, oftener than he used to. With the family
circle so much smaller, Artie clung to it more closely.
On the rare occasions when Bess joined them she was affectionate
with her father, charming to the aunts. The Colonel reproached
himself with being an old fogy; to old fogies any change seemed a
change for the worse. But as a matter of fact, after fundamental
changes they were all settling down toward a new order.
What store he already set by the new order he learned in the fall,
when Retta had been dead more than a year. One night beside the
fire in the library Dora reported to him that Bess had invited her to
come and live at the Douglas Rutherfords'.
"Dode! She asked you to leave Amy and come and live with her?"
The Colonel was thunder-struck.
"It sounded that way. But of course I've no idea of leaving Amy.
I told Bess so; and whatever she had meant to begin with, she

"But Dode, you wouldn't seriously consider such a thing?"

"But you're busy enough right here. You're too busy. I was think-

She never used to have a hard time keeping busy. What had become

"It seems that I am outvoted," said Dora smoothly. "Oh, well,

The Colonel regarded the whole crazy scheme as definitely in the

He shouldn't have spoken to Amy like that. Amy was always

She shrank away from him now, her eyes wide above pale cheeks.

Under her glance the Colonel remembered once more what he was

I'm going to be mean enough to make you say it in so many words."
"You're the salt of the earth, Cull," said Artie heartily.
"What you ask is much less than you should ask," said Dora. "I'm
sorry I even brought up the subject, Sam. I should have known better."
There was a pause while they waited for the other member of
the family to speak. The Colonel had said all he could say.
But Artie was bound by no such scruples. "Amy!" he cried impa-
tiently. "You're keeping Colonel waiting. What the devil is the
matter with you lately?"
THE COLONEL
347
"I've had a lot of time to think lately." Amy's clear gaze rested
for a moment on Artie, with an uncomfortable effect of seeing right
through him. "I've come to the conclusion that we all take too much
for granted. You and I, Artie-yes, and Dora, too. For years now
we Millses have gone on taking everything for granted. Isn't it about
time we stopped and thought things over?"
Artie looked distressed. The Colonel was completely bewildered.
He had been aware for some time of the strangeness of his own
attitude toward Amy. Indeed, since Retta died he had consciously
avoided Amy around the house; where she was concerned he had a
bad conscience. But the last thing that he wanted in the world was
to hurt her. Had he made her feel that she was unwelcome here in
her own home?
Dora was exasperated. "What is there that takes so much thinking
over?" she demanded. "The Colonel simply wants to know what
he can depend on. As for our taking too much for granted, yes, per-
haps we do. But the business of the world couldn't go on for a single
day if people didn't take a great deal for granted."
"Amy's right," said the Colonel abruptly. "That is, she's talking
on my side of the fence, if she only knew it. I want everybody to
stop taking things for granted. I want a definite understanding."
"What understanding?" asked Dora.
"I want you and Amy both to promise me that you will always
regard this house as your home."
"I do, anyhow," said Dora. "So it's little enough to promise. And
thank you, Sam."
"Thank you, Dode," said the Colonel.
Amy lifted her eyes to the Colonel's and drew an audible breath.
Then she reached out impulsively and took his hand. Her fingers
were cold, but her clasp was firm and strong. It was like Amy to go
beyond words when she was really won over. She was never one to
talk.
Immense relief surged over the Colonel. He had actually feared
that they were going to abandon him; and almost worse than the
threat of desertion was the fog of misunderstanding which had
seemed to be creeping over them all. It was gone now: gone finally,
and he was safe from its recurrence.
He shook Amy's hand heartily. Then he stood up and reached in
348
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
his pocket for his keys. "There isn't any of Bill's champagne left," he
said. "But I think the occasion really demands a toast. There's still
some port."
Sipping port together, they celebrated a reunion as poignant as if
they had actually been separated in the flesh. Dora was as merry as a
little cricket. She blamed herself for dallying with Bess's suggestion;
but Dora never wasted energy in self-reproach. Instead, she did her
best to make the evening seem festive.
She presently allowed a second glass of port to be poured for her.
Artie had already had three. It hadn't occurred to anybody as worthy
of remark that no promise had been demanded from Artie. Least
of all did it disturb Artie. His fate might be bound up in that of his
sisters; but Artie never glimpsed himself as a useless and rather
pitiful appendage. He thought of himself as the girls' natural pro-
tector.
The impulse to help them over their difficulties, to do their think-
ing for them, to shield their maiden modesty under his masculine
ægis, grew stronger as he mellowed with the wine. Beginning on
the fourth glass, he had a really brilliant idea. It was funny they'd
none of them thought of this for themselves; but he could put the
solution up to them in a few well-chosen words.
"Listen a minute, Cull," he said. "Here's the right answer. Why
don't you marry Dora or Amy?"
The Colonel looked aghast. Amy stretched out an impulsive hand
to move the bottle farther from Artie, who had been helping him-
self after the first glass. But Dora laughed.
"Marry Dora or Amy!" she echoed. "Oh, Artie! That's brilliant.
Just as if you were offering him a choice of vegetables. Will you have
peas or string beans, Colonel? Artie wants to know."
Artie defended himself. "I couldn't very well say 'marry Dora
and Amy,' could I?"
Dora laughed again. Amy put up a hand to shield her face from
the fire; her cheeks were burning red.
The Colonel guffawed once or twice; but he didn't feel much like
laughing. Artie had rushed in where angels might well fear to tread.
No real harm was done, of course; his sisters would consider the
source. But at that moment the Colonel could cheerfully have
strangled his brother-in-law.

II
HE idea of the Colonel's marrying Dora was so supremely ridic-
ulous that when he had cooled down a little he was genuinely
amused by it. But the suggestion that he might marry Amy embar-
rassed him so much that he avoided the family table for three days,
and made business a pretext for lunching and dining at the Manning
Arms. Then he went out of town for a week. Amy probably needed
that respite even more than he did.
To marry Amy now-frail, exquisite Amy! Why, in all the years
that she had lived under his roof, he had never once spoken to Amy
if he happened to meet her in the upper hall in her dressing-gown!
The idea of Amy's living in the ordinary married relation to any
man struck him as unthinkable and somehow just a little repellent.
If he had married Amy years ago, when his choice unwarily slid
by her, that would have been vastly different. Their life together
would have been one of those things a man scarcely dared dream
about; such perfection mocked the dull every-day round of what
most people called happiness.
Yet if he had married Amy in the beginning, he would never have
had all those full and satisfactory years with Retta. Retta had been a
splendid wife, far beyond any other wife of his acquaintance. Even
now, the warm memory of Retta sustained him in his solitude.
If he had married Amy he wouldn't have had Bess, either: his
idolized Bess, to whose faults he had always been deliberately blind,
and whose partial estrangement from him now was a hurt that
wouldn't bear thinking of. And if you came right down to it, under
that other arrangement he wouldn't have had all these years of Amy
close at hand yet somehow intangible, Amy beautiful in her remote-
ness, Amy who had during all these middle-aged years retained for
349
350
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
him the haunting magic of a strain of music heard in the distance
and echoed faintly through passing time.
It was all most perplexing. The Colonel was not an introspective
man. He was not even an especially shrewd observer; his easy opti-
mism combined with his native kindness to prevent his seeing things
as he often feared they really were. When his emotions were in-
volved his judgment was always clouded; and when his loyalties
were threatened, he was capable even of deliberate distortion.
But right now he didn't know what to think. There was no simple
solution of a difficulty which, though not especially complex to begin
with, had had time to send out shoots which in their turn had rooted
themselves.
Yet he found himself unable to put the idea summarily out of his
mind. Because he had made a mistake in the distant past, must he
go on forever living with the fruits of his mistake? He had years of
life ahead of him. Of course he had never acknowledged his error
when the acknowledgment could hurt Retta. But now Retta had
been dead a year and a half. His remarriage would not even seem
disrespectful to her memory.
His interests had always centered in his household; he openly
despised those men who centered life in their offices. Artie had been
right in insinuating that the Colonel needed "a real home again."
Damn it! Was Artie right about all those intimate matters, and the
rest of them wrong? Artie was a nice one to recommend marriage,
anyhow: one of those chronic bachelors who didn't care how many
risks other people took.
The Colonel didn't even dare to put the solution up to Amy.
Amy's threat of flight had terrified him. To lose her altogether was
a prospect so bleak that he simply could not risk it.
He tried to console himself by reckoning up how much was still
left of the good life which had once been all-sufficient. That it was
shrinking at all alarmed him. For so many years time had been
bringing him things that he naturally expected it to keep right on
along the same line. Without reasoning it out closely, he had always
taken it for granted that life is a steady progression and accretion.
Consequently he was bewildered and baffled now that time, instead
of bringing him things, was beginning to take them away. He
fought the idea that time might continue to take things away from
THE COLONEL
351
him. Life should always be on the up-curve. If you slipped back a
little, you made good your lapse and advanced beyond it. That was
so, surely. It had to be so.
That winter he was encouraged about Bess. There, at least, he could
help straighten matters out. He was her father, and it was high time
they were both reminded of the relationship. Though he would
sooner have faced a firing-squad than have appeared to run after a
Rutherford, he took his courage in both hands and dropped in at
her house one evening.
Bess was delighted to see him, and inquired minutely about the
household and the aunts. Doug took him into the dining-room for a
whiskey-and-soda. They both urged him to come again whenever he
could. They weren't often at home in the evening unless they gave
a party. But if he could let them know in advance, they'd be glad to
stay at home on purpose to visit with him.
The Colonel preferred his own home in the evening. He tele-
phoned to ask Bess and Doug to come over the next week. They
came, stayed an hour, appeared to enjoy themselves. He wished they
wouldn't always wait for him to make the advances. But there! He
mustn't allow himself to get notions. Because the younglings were
out of the nest was no reason why you should try to work up a
grievance against them.
Toward Amy, of course, he couldn't take the initiative. He hoped
Amy had forgotten that unlucky speech of Artie's as completely as
she had ignored it. He was afraid, though, that Amy never forgot
anything.
If he had actually married Amy when they were both young, what
consideration he would have had to practise toward her! Living
with her would have fined him down and drawn him to a point:
would have made him much sharper mentally, too. Or would it?
People didn't change in essentials no matter what lives they lived.
More likely he would have hurt Amy a dozen times a day and never
have known the difference.
It would have been a narrow and exclusive relation. Amy never
could have headed a large and miscellaneous household as Retta
did. A rich, warm, haphazard life, with spurts of vigorous impa-
tience and spasms of hearty laughter: that was Retta's way. It never
could have been Amy's. With Amy the Colonel would have known
352
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
a bliss beyond his experience, even beyond his imaginings; but he
would have paid for it with hours of pain and humiliation.
Ah, but the bliss would have been worth the pain! To see Amy
flower in the sunshine of an early marriage: Amy with a baby's head
against her breast, Amy with her lips growing warm and her clear
eyes misty with passion-the blood rushed to his head at the thought.
Amy as she might have been was the one thing that could have
made his own life vitally significant.
Well, it was the one thing he had missed. He wasn't sure even
that Amy could ever have cared for him as she had it in her to care
for some man. Indeed, he ought to hope that she looked on him with
something like indifference; any other possibility must have meant
acute suffering for her all these years. Surely he hadn't that heavy
guilt on his soul. Surely Amy, paling in the shadows, had no idea
what she had missed. There at least he could take the heavier share
of the punishment. He had altogether too clear an idea.
Well, they would patch along now as best they might. Dora at
least was a satisfaction to him. Perhaps right now he was doing Dora
an injustice, holding her back from some wider horizon for which
she yearned.
But he didn't think so. Along toward the end of the winter Dora
told him that Hortense was expecting another baby in the fall. Dora
had no old-maid notions: she wanted to be on hand when that baby
was born. And she told the Colonel what she wanted; she didn't

going to Hortense's when she felt her presence there was desirable.
All in all, the Colonel felt quite easy in his mind about Dora.
She couldn't have left the Langworthy household that spring even if

"It's too lonesome around the house without Mrs. Langworthy,"

Certainly he was sincere in his regret. The sun may parch and blister
you; but when the sun is withdrawn, you can't help feeling chilly.

Dora had done more harm than good. Amy's shrinking sensitive-

would have been so pleased and excited; and he had had little

She was remorseful for having been so impatient of a little embar-

wear for Retta. But Retta had died lingeringly, after a full and
happy life. Poor Artie had never achieved much, anyhow; and at
the end he was snuffed out shockingly and rather ridiculously.
The story of the accident had to be pieced together; there were no
witnesses. Driving himself in the old Langworthy car, Artie had for
once ventured out of town. On the railroad track east of Mount
Royal he had managed to kill his engine on a crossing where there
was neither gate nor watchman. The four o'clock out from Chicago
358
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
was almost due. Artie, getting out hastily to crank, had left the car
in gear.
He was run over by his own car. When the train passed five min-
utes later, a brakeman saw his body lying beside the track.
It was just the sort of accident which, if it had happened to some
one else, would have provided Artie with conversation for a week.
Gossip about his carelessness was tinged on the whole with regret.
He died owing small sums of money all over Mount Royal. But the
general verdict was that he had never done harm to a living soul.

III
RTIE'S death overwhelmed Amy. She had been his protector
ever since they were children. He was her only brother; and
his death left her and Dora the sole survivors of their immediate
family.
The Colonel broke the news to her. They had sent for him as soon
as the tragedy was discovered. Dora was away when the Colonel
reached the house. In his grief and sympathy he forgot to go softly with
Amy. He took the stricken creature in his arms; and Artie's death
accomplished what his hints had been powerless to bring about.
Amy was not only grief-stricken over Artie: it seemed to her that
her whole world was falling to pieces. For the moment she could
make no effort to hold it together. Indeed, her long years of repression
had dammed up in her a tide of feeling which broke loose now and
overwhelmed her.
When she gathered herself together a little, it was to face a changed
world. To the Colonel she was no longer a remote and nunlike
creature, whom he might love but scarcely dared to look at. She had
lain sobbing in his arms, had clung to him for comfort, had listened
to the words of endearment which broke from him in spite of himself.
At the moment he had felt nothing beyond pity and sympathy. But
Amy had betrayed herself. The warm sweet feeling of her lingered
with him for hours. He hated taking this special means toward his
end. But toward this end any means at all were justified. Amy had
turned to him for shelter from her grief. That shelter must be hers in
all the loneliness to come.
He spoke to her the evening after the funeral. Artie had been
buried in the Langworthy lot next to Retta. Bess and Doug came in
after supper to pay a little visit. When they left, Dora went upstairs
to begin the dreary task of going over Artie's things.
359
360
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Amy made a move to follow Dora, but the Colonel held her back.
A new masterfulness had come over him in the last few days. He took
Amy into the library, settled her in a big chair, touched a match to
the fire which lay ready in the grate. A hard rain was making the
evening chilly, and Amy always felt the cold.
"Dora is leaving for Hortense's in about two months," he said.
"She'd have plenty of time later to get on with those things upstairs.
She must be very tired to-night. But it's best not to interfere with
what she thinks she wants to do."
"I didn't know she had made her plans already," said Amy list-
lessly.
"Yes. She told me about them before supper. She intends to be gone
for some time. Indeed, I've a feeling that she'll never be back here
just as she used to be. She was always itching to be off, you know.
I don't really see how we have managed to hold her as long as we
have."
"She hasn't told me anything."
"Does she need to?" He stood with his back to the crackling fire,
looking down at Amy so frail and weary in her black dress. "The
old order is pretty well gone, Amy."
"Yes. It's it's cruel to watch. Retta and now Artie-"
"Don't look back, Amy. Look forward. I don't want to hurry you.
But we've already wasted too much time. It isn't ours to waste. After
to-night, we must begin to plan ahead."
"Don't!" whispered Amy under her breath. "Don't!"
Speech was the worst waste of all; Amy had always been so sparing
with her own words. Other people's words had too often wounded
Amy, and must once more. He had to put a pin through this poor
struggling butterfly, or even now she might escape him.
"Before Dora leaves," he said quietly, "you and I are going to be
married."
"Oh, no! Never!" cried Amy.
"Oh, yes we are, sweetheart! Unless of course you prefer to live with
me in sin. I shouldn't mind living in sin with you, Amy. But it might
make talk."
"But Colonel, I-"
"Colonel is a good title, Amy. But my name is Sam."
THE COLONEL
361
"I can't call you that. I can't can't live with you as a wife does.

"Look me in the eye," said the Colonel. "I insist. Look at me, Amy.
That's better. Amy Mills, I've loved you most of my life, and you're

very disagreeable if I have to. But if you'll only put out a hand to me-

He was pleading with her as he had never pleaded when he was a
young lover. But Amy shrank away from him. A log in the fireplace

He followed her into the hall, heard her round the stairs and begin
the ascent. Should he follow her now? There was no one upstairs but
Dora. He dreaded postponement. But no, he couldn't be cruel to her.
Only that afternoon she had seen her brother buried. Now and
always, he must remember to be very gentle with Amy.
Half-way up the flight, he heard her quick steps falter. She, too,
had remembered the desolation upstairs: the doors now permanently
closed in a house that had once teemed with life and rung with gaiety.
For a moment she clung to the banister, giddy and a little sick. Then
she turned and came slowly down to him.
"I think it's wrong," she whispered against his cheek. "But oh, my
darling, you want it! I'd walk barefoot through hell for you. What
does a little wrong matter to me now?"
She was like flame in his arms. He was a man, and had been
through a man's usual experiences. But he had never guessed that
love could be like this.
Yet now he must have assurance, when a minute ago he would
have been immeasurably grateful for the mere fact. "I want it, Amy,
of course I do. But I'm not taking anything unless you want it, too.
You're sorry for the old man. You're used to sacrificing yourself.
Is that all you mean? Come on, now, out with it! Don't keep me
waiting."
He had his assurance back in the library, while the fire died down to
coals and later to ashes. After her whispered words of love Amy sat
silent for the most part, too moved for speech really, and perhaps too
spent. He ought to have let her go sooner, ought to have realized that
362
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
in this one day she had seen her old life definitely end, and the new
one begin.
But she seemed to have passed through ghastly weariness to a
strange new peace; and he had a feeling that she was as unwilling
as he to see this special evening end. They knew so many things that
they needn't put into words, he and she; after their many years of
never daring to say what they meant, they had passed to complete
and perfect understanding.
And it would always be like this for them henceforward. Always!
They would have time enough to get used to their happiness. Yet in
the very strangeness lay a special charm. Himself and Amy. Oh, if
only this one night could have gone on forever!
In the cool light of next morning they told Dora. "Nice to have it
settled," said Dora contentedly. "You'll want to be married quietly,
of course, on account of our mourning. But I wish you'd pick out
something you'd really like for a wedding-present, Amy. I must have
given several hundred wedding-presents in my time. But this one I
shall really enjoy giving."
The Colonel faced the future without misgivings. Yes, without a
single misgiving. But of course, in the case of a marriage at mature
years, there were always complications.
There was the task of informing Bess, for instance. The Colonel
shrank from that. After all, Retta had been Bess's mother. He
couldn't expect Bess to see eye to eye with him in the matter of his
second marriage.
Amy took the job off his hands. "I'll speak to Bess myself," she
told him firmly. Queer to see Amy making decisions this way: queer,
but very delightful. Amy had suddenly come out of the shadows:
had ceased to be a looker-on at life, and was already assuming the
responsibilities of her new rôle.
Every day now when he got home from the office, Amy was waiting
for him in the sitting-room or the library; her tea-table was always
ready and the kettle boiling. The Colonel didn't care much for after-
noon tea. But the knowledge that somebody was waiting to welcome
him warmed his whole day. And to have that somebody Amy-it was
just too good to be true!
After a week of Amy's welcomes he came in one afternoon to find
the tea-table ready, but with Dora in attendance. "Amy will be home
THE COLONEL
363
almost any time," she informed him. "But she asked me to give you
your tea if she was a little late."
"Amy is trying to spoil me," said the Colonel. But he couldn't keep
the gratification out of his voice. Only a few months ago he had
simply come home. Every day now his homecoming was a ceremony.
He accepted his tea and a wafer-thin sandwich. "Is Amy shopping?"
he asked.
"She had a fitting at the dressmaker's, I think. Mrs. Fisher is sewing
for Amy at her own house."
"A good notion that. Amy's?"
"Amy's. Her work was begun last Monday. She wanted to be mar-
ried in gray, but I talked her out of that."
"Is she going to wear white? I'm not very well up on such things,
but I'd like her to wear white."
"She's having several dresses made," said Dora. "I mustn't talk out
of turn. But perhaps she wouldn't mind your knowing that there is a
white one among them."
The Colonel beamed, scalded his throat with hot tea, took another
sandwich. Then he became aware that Dora had glanced more than
once at the door. It wasn't like her to show nervousness because some-
body didn't appear on the dot.
"Where else was Amy going beside to the dressmaker's?" he asked.
"She said something about stopping for a minute to see Bess."
"Oh! Well, I suppose they got to talking."
"I suppose so," said Dora.
He wasn't afraid of how Bess might receive Amy's news. Of course
he wasn't! Now if he had been proposing to marry some stranger-
but Amy had been living right here in the house when Bess was born.
Half of Bess's blood was Amy's, too; when you came to think of it,
a mother's sister was extraordinarily close kin. Bess could be the most
gracious person on earth; surely she would be gracious to Amy now.
People always tended to be very kind to Amy.
He argued himself into something like equanimity. Just the same
he was both relieved and anxious when he heard Amy at the door.
She came in smiling, her cheeks pink from the outdoor air, her
eyes shining. "All right?" asked Dora pointedly.
"All over. She's been told." Amy leaned over to kiss the Colonel's
forehead. "Sit still," she commanded him. "You must be tired."
364
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
He wasn't tired. He watched Amy accept a cup of tea, saw her seat
herself with one beautiful easy motion. So many women simply fell
into a chair! Amy had always been graceful, but now she moved with
new confidence and poise. Simply to watch her made him happy.
But he couldn't just go on watching her. He had to ask, "What
did Bess say to your news?"
"Oh, she didn't say much!"
"Was she surprised?" asked Dora.
"She certainly was. Most people are disappointed when they come
out with the news that they are going to be married. It's exactly what
their friends have expected, and nobody turns a hair. But Bess didn't
expect this. Why should she? Or for that matter why should anybody?
Bess couldn't be any more startled than I was myself."
Dora chuckled. "A good idea went begging a long time. People
are funny, aren't they?"
Amy and the Colonel were to be married the first of September.
Amy suggested that they might go to the rectory and have just Dora
and the rector's wife for witnesses. But the Colonel negatived that.
"One elopement in the family is enough," he said. "We'll be mar-
ried here in the house. We'll ask Bess and Doug over for the cere-
mony, too. I'm not going to have any hole-and-corner business about
this."
Amy consented; but she thought he meant that they would just
stand up in the sitting-room and be married. Surely that was only
good taste under the circumstances? Artie would have been dead a
bare two months on the day that she was married. Whenever she
thought of Artie she was remorseful at being so happy.
But then Dora had to be reckoned with; and she didn't hold with
remorse. "Family only" weddings were always weepy little affairs at
the best; and under these special circumstances, a private wedding
would be horribly gloomy.
She bullied Amy into agreement, and then she went to work. First
she arranged with Jason and Louise to come back from the farm for
three days, clean the house thoroughly, and plan a proper wedding-
breakfast. Then she asked what remained of The Bee to attend the
ceremony. Finally, to take the curse off having so many women
present, she rounded up Senator Roberts and Captain Duquesne. The
THE COLONEL
365
Colonel was bringing in his engineer to act as his best man. They
would make out very nicely.
Dora was to leave for the East the day after the wedding. Her
preparations for departure were all made well in advance. The day
of the wedding itself she was free to devote to Amy.
Dora woke soon after dawn and looked across to where Amy lay
still asleep, one arm thrown up above her head, her face turned away
from the light. So many mornings, scattered through so many years,
Dora on waking had looked over toward Amy's bed the first thing!
It was strange to think that she would never do the like again. Dora
had urged this marriage on; she had believed it would be the finest
thing in life for Amy. But now a sickening doubt swept over her.
"Let Amy be happy!" she whispered through set teeth. "Oh, please,
please, let Amy be happy! She never has been, and she needs it so. It
doesn't matter about the rest of us. Only let Amy be happy!"
In the bathroom ten minutes later she fumed over a dripping faucet.
This old house was falling to pieces; even if you were at it all the time,
you couldn't keep it up properly. Dora would be glad to be out and
away from it. In helping on the necessary readjustments she had said
one word for the Colonel and Amy, and two for herself. Well, that
was the sensible way to act in a selfish world. She didn't know what
had possessed her to be so sentimental just now: to mutter a speech
which was more than half a prayer-she who hadn't prayed since she
became a conscientious agnostic at the age of eleven!
"A little more," she thought derisively, "and I'll be turning into the
kind of old maid who weeps at weddings!"
She went down to cut flowers before the sun was on them. Jason had
produced a kind of order in the garden these last three days; but it
was still a very faint echo of what it had been in Retta's time. That
scheme of uniting all the beds in the side yard had never gone through,
either. Dora supposed that now it never would.
She gathered all the lingering September blossoms: snapdragons,
asters, zinnias, love-in-a-mist. She was in the kitchen arranging them
when Louise descended to get breakfast, just as if she still belonged
here.
"Hattie will answer the door to-day," Dora told Louise. "You'll be
busy enough in the kitchen. But mind you and Jason come in for the
ceremony."
366
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
The service was read in the little formal parlor. Mrs. Meade had
come with the rector. Mrs. Chase brought Agnes and Geraldine. Molly
Travers was there; beside her stood Mrs. Palmer, imposing in velvet
and jet. Even Mrs. Cordiner came, a thin wreck of a woman with
haunted eyes, whom Dora would scarcely have recognized if she had
met her on the street. Mr. Cordiner never left the house nowadays.
The business was being sold to strangers from Chicago.
The room was full when Amy came downstairs. Dora was waiting
for her in the hall. She had not prevailed on Amy to wear white; but
the blue of Amy's frock matched her eyes and she carried an armful
of roses which trembled a little as she advanced under all the eyes
that were upon her.
The Rev. John Warren Meade read the service impressively, in his
clergyman's voice and with his Episcopal accent. The second he had
finished he descended to his natural tone, shook Amy's hand, and said,
"I wish you all happiness, Mrs. Langworthy." Being the first to call a
bride by her married name was one of the privileges of his calling.
He did it very nicely.
Senator Roberts was not so restrained; he kissed the bride. Then
Captain Duquesne kissed her. It was odd to see Amy saluted by those
strange men. Dora would have said in advance that Amy wouldn't
like it, even at her wedding. But Amy was too excited and exalted
to be disturbed by anything.
The wedding-breakfast was served at three tables in the dining-
room. Everything went off smoothly, and Louise had done herself
credit on the fried chicken and the big bride's cake. They were still
at the table when Hortense's telegram of congratulation was brought
in. Hortense had sent a handsome wedding-present; but she wanted
some word from her to arrive on the day itself.
In the sitting-room after breakfast they all stood around for a few
minutes and talked. The Colonel and Amy hadn't planned any
wedding-trip, so their departure would not give a signal for the party
to break up.
It was actually Bess and Doug who were the first to leave. Bess had
been very quiet all day. There were tears in her eyes when she took
leave of Dora. Poor child, these proceedings were hard on her! There
were times when Dora felt that she wasn't inclined to be quite just
to Bess.
THE COLONEL
367
Doug slipped something into the Colonel's hand when he left. A
minute later the Colonel found a chance to show it to Dora.
"Saves having a lot of useless silver or cut glass around the house,"
he grunted. "Still a check is an odd wedding-present, isn't it? Looks
as if he were quite sure I could use the money."
"Bank it in Amy's name," said Dora quickly. "She's awfully vague
about money, Colonel. You'll have to force it on her or she'll be
going around without shoes. She needs a boss as well as a protector."
There were other wedding-presents on display in the little room
behind the sitting-room. Dora found the Colonel gloating over them
after the last guest had gone.
"Sometimes people are so horrid about second marriages," he re-
