Amy hasn't so many friends, if you consider just numbers. But those
she has evidently know how to appreciate her."
It flashed across Dora's mind that these were all Retta's friends,
these people who had been so kind to Amy. Amy had no intimates.
She had always gone along to Retta's parties; but she was a typical
lone-hander. Dora held in her own case that if you were not married
you were all the freer to make friends for yourself. But Amy had
never seemed to feel that way about it.
The Colonel stood smiling down at Mrs. Travers's Wedgewood
loving-cup and Mrs. Cordiner's silver pitcher and Mrs. Palmer's ugly
but expensive Royal Worcester vase. His hair was iron-gray, and not
prematurely; he was heavy, there were deep furrows in his face. He
was years older than Dora. But at that minute he looked like a boy
bewildered by all-consuming youthful bliss. Dora felt a sudden ache
of pity for him.
Being Dora, she took refuge in concreteness. "What time is it,
Colonel? A carriage is coming for me at four-a borrowed carriage;
and I don't like to keep somebody else's coachman waiting."
"Why, Dode, where are you going? I thought you weren't leaving
until to-morrow."
"I'm leaving Mount Royal in the morning. But I'm going for the
night to Mrs. Chase's."
"But, Dode, why? Surely your own home is good enough for you
one more night?"
Dora laughed. "Don't be a goose. Hush! Here's Amy."
368
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Amy had just come from superintending the clearing away in the
dining-room. Already she was mistress in her own house.
"Come upstairs with me while I get my hat on," said Dora.
"Colonel, will you ask Jason to get my bags? They're all ready in
Hortense's room. My trunk can go in the morning."
In the sisters' room Dora busied herself with a few last directions.
She hated partings; she wished this one were over.
In a sense it was over: over before it had begun. Amy was gone
from her irrevocably. You had to take those things; they were a part
of life-an ever-increasing part as you grew older. Dora had steeled
herself to the wrench of losing Amy. She had resolved to leave early,
and to leave smiling.
But she had trusted herself too far. Nobody had seen Dora cry since
she left the old home back in Kentucky. "Old maids who weep at
weddings" had always been one of her favorite objects of derision.
If she had cried when Retta died, or Artie, it had been behind a
closed door, so that no one was any the wiser.
What broke down her resolution now was the look on Amy's face.
Dora found herself unable to confront the spectacle of Amy's
happiness.
Because, of course, this marriage was all for the best, and the Colonel
was a man in a thousand, and Amy deserved every good thing that
could come to her. But not even Amy had a right to look as happy
as she looked to-day. Nowhere in this world could such happiness
last.
WHE
IV
THEN winter closed in on them, the Colonel and Amy took to
spending their evenings in the spacious bedroom that occu-
pied the entire second floor of "the addition." It was a room which
Amy had never willingly entered while Retta shared it with the
Colonel.
She had shrunk at first from the idea of succeeding Retta quite so
pointedly. But aside from "the aunts" room, which must be kept
unoccupied against Dora's return, this was the only bedroom fur-
nished for double occupancy. The Colonel took for granted that Amy
would share it with him. She couldn't protest without making it sound
as if she were ashamed of her marriage.
Anyhow, her discomfort was short-lived. In a few weeks the room
ceased to look as if Retta had ever lived there. The old cheerful litter
-what Dora used to call "Retta's unfinished business"-was all cleared
away. The wide lounge on which Retta had "rested" during her
invalid days was banished. Hortense's flower panels, which stood
about for years waiting to be installed, were neatly fixed above the
mantle.
In the course of time Amy made other changes. She brought in
plants to stand on the window-sills. She installed a shelf of books;
Amy always liked to have her favorite books close at hand, and few
of hers had ever been shelved downstairs. She brought her tea-table
up here, and the biggest and brightest lamp from the sitting-room.
Of a winter evening the room was remarkably snug and cozy, with
a fire of cannel coal burning in the grate and the old cat dozing before
it. The buxom Hattie could bank the furnace fires early and retire
to the third story, where she cooked herself over a small gas-heater.
This was an unusually severe winter; at meal-times the dining-room
was generally chilly and uninviting. The Colonel and Amy stressed
369
370
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
to each other the superior comfort of an upstairs sitting-room and an
open fire. Not for worlds would either of them have admitted that
their new system was desirable because it saved coal.
Retrenchment in the household and worry in his business ceased to
matter to the Colonel the moment he set foot over that sheltered
threshold. He was fairly bewildered by the strange and exalted hap-
piness which he experienced with Amy. Sometimes of a morning he
would wake early and lie looking out of the window to where the
distant solitary elm made a lacy pattern of bare boughs against the
winter sky. For the first minute he would be almost afraid to turn
and look at Amy, for fear that he might have dreamed the enchant-
ment and she wasn't there after all.
Then he would deliberately put off looking, for the sheer joy of
having it still ahead of him: that first glimpse at the molded beauty
of her sleeping face, the sweetness of the moment when she began to
stir, her smile of recognition when her eyes sought and found his, the
rapture of her lips advancing for his kiss.
To begin with he had been worried by the idea of Amy's having
to meet contacts which might seem coarse to her delicacy. Retta's
bridal tremors had hurt and puzzled him. To be sure, Retta had got
bravely over her reserves and had often demanded from him more
than he had to give. This business was only a part of married life;
for all the propriety of her spoken code, Retta had made it seem too
important.
But Amy's delicacy might take fright at simple nature; or so the
Colonel in his ignorance supposed. Charley Manning, a man of wide
and possibly sinister experience, could have told him better. From a
distance Amy had always fascinated Charley Manning. He saw at a
glance that the exquisite remote creature was secretly all athrill with
sexual feeling. It was no part of Charley's destiny to waken that sleep-
ing beauty; but although he was of an extravagant nature in many
ways, this particular waste he privately deplored.
The Colonel, who had no such faculties for judgment, failed par-
tially to credit the facts even when they became apparent. Amy un-
folded to him as naturally as a flower unfolds to the sunshine. He
chilled her sometimes by forgetting that his very presence meant
shelter and strength to her. Sometimes he wounded himself by lapsing
into the dread that he wasn't young enough for a new love; he was
THE COLONEL
371
tantalized by memories that meant nothing in the world to Amy.
This cloud on the horizon, however, was no bigger than a man's
hand; and there was no drawback at all to the exquisiteness of having
Amy constantly beside him during the long winter evenings.
He had always hated the way she bent to her embroidery as if it
were a task done under threat of punishment. He had always derided,
too, Retta's contention that she could "work and listen at the same
time." Or say Retta could do it; that didn't make the custom binding
on Amy. Twice, perhaps three times, he asked Amy to lay down her
work while he talked to her. After that she never tried to get on
with her embroidery when he was there.
She found plenty of employment for their evenings together. She
taught him backgammon, a game which he had more than once
refused to learn from Retta. She read aloud to him some of the books
which he had never found time for; indeed, because he felt that the
Millses were born readers and he was not, he had always been in-
clined to pooh-pooh books. Now he listened with growing interest to
Ivanhoe, to Treasure Island, to Stanley's How I Found Livingstone.
Amy had her own ideas about what a man ought to like; she hit the
Colonel's taste neatly enough.
Before they settled down to their book or their game, she often had
a letter to read to him. So much of the family was in the East now
that letters had become an important part of every-day life.
Hortense's new baby was a boy: that boy whom poor Artie had so
desired to see in the family. Dora was delighted with the baby; but she
preferred the little girl, who was now walking, talking, daily develop-
ing new traits of curiosity and mischief. It took Amy to be genuinely
thrilled about a baby. Watching Amy's bright-eyed musings over some
detail of nursing or naps, the Colonel would swear softly to himself.
Amy had been cheated; yes, and he had had a hand in cheating her.
Perhaps he would live to hear her reproaches; at the bottom of his
heart he believed that when a woman began to grow old, nothing
really mattered to her except her children.
Much talk of absent kin seemed to bring them closer. "People never
can be far away from you-not really far away-when you love them
and think of them and remember that they are thinking of you,"
Amy claimed.
She talked about Artie, too, hesitatingly at first, then with a sense
372
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
of reconciliation to what had happened. Her verdict was, "Artie never
could have endured being old."
Less often but consistently she spoke of Retta. Sometimes she de-
liberately shaped her speech to bring back Retta in her pleasant
matronly thirties: a Retta the Colonel, too, enjoyed recalling. Retta
had reached her best about that time. The age which saw the peak
of anybody's looks depended on the individual. Bess, though always
pretty, had never since been quite so completely a picture as she
had been when she was four or five.
Amy herself was at her loveliest in this enchanted winter. The
delicate dusting of gray on her hair was like the bloom on ripe fruit.
Her skin had never lost the porcelain fineness of youth; and when she
was excited or amused, quick color came and went in her cheeks. Her
slim body was firmer than it had been, yet at the same time suppler.
Amy would never be fat; but she no longer looked as if she would
break at a touch.
She had once been a little careless about her clothes, too: never
untidy or in bad taste, her toilets had been indifferent to effect. Now
she dressed deliberately to please the Colonel. Her colors were brighter,
her frocks fitted tighter about the bosom. Even when the two of them
were alone she rustled with hidden silks; around the house she wore
patent leather slippers with square cut-steel buckles, which reminded
the Colonel of shoes in the "Colonial" costume plays which were so
popular about that time.
They went to the theater rather often, sitting well down in front
because the Colonel sometimes fancied lately that he was growing
a little hard of hearing. Well, yes, and because he wanted to be seen
with his lovely new wife. In the eyes of Mount Royal, the Colonel's
union with Amy must look like a marriage of convenience. In a sense
the Colonel hoped it did. But that was no reason for acting as if he
were ashamed of Amy.
After the theater, indeed, he paraded her instead of going tamely
home to yawn over sandwiches and cocoa in their own kitchen. He
made a habit of taking her to the dining-room of the Manning Arms
for a chafing-dish and a bottle of wine.
After-theater supper was the one meal at which that hotel shone.
In the jerky elevator which carried them up seven stories through an
openwork metal shaft, the Langworthys regularly encountered people
THE COLONEL
373
whom they knew. In the dining-room they found music, a special
menu, new pink-shaded lights on every table. Charley Manning had
finally hit on something at which his hotel could succeed. He lost so
little by running those after-theater sessions that he counted it the
same as making money.
One evening when they had been to see The Prisoner of Zenda
(with a road company, but Amy wept during the last act, and the
Colonel chuckled gleefully when the lights went up on her red eyes),
they walked the three blocks to the Manning Arms, and went up
in the elevator with Roger Willett, who had once been "Poor Roger!"
to Bess, and his wife, the former Elsie Patterson. Amy was wearing
a tiny toque trimmed with forget-me-nots, and the huge marten scarf
which the Colonel had given her for Christmas.
"How bride-y she looks!" the Colonel heard Elsie whisper to her
husband. Getting out of the elevator, the Colonel strutted a little.
Elsie and Roger went directly to a large table where Arch Patterson
and his pretty wife were already seated. There, too, with their backs
toward the door, were Doug and Bess Rutherford.
The Colonel and Amy went to a small table in a corner. When he
had seated Amy he happened to look toward the larger table; he saw
Elsie say something to Bess, and glance in his direction as she spoke.
Bess shrugged. But Arch Patterson came over and asked, "Won't
you and Mrs. Langworthy join us, Colonel? We're waiting for the
Paul Reynoldses. It will be just a nice party."
Amy started to say, "Oh, you young people won't want to bother
with us!" But the Colonel, who was still on his feet, seized her arm.
When a man married a woman who was quite a bit younger than he,
it was better for him to come down to her level than to expect her to
be decorous with him.
Amy was cordially greeted, and a place was made for her between
Arch Patterson and Roger Willett. The Colonel moved toward the
chair next to Bess, but Elsie said gaily, "That won't do, Colonel!
Relatives mustn't sit together." She took him beside her.
Mrs. Reynolds presently took the chair at his left. The Colonel
didn't care especially for Mrs. Paul Reynolds, who came from Detroit
and made no bones about considering Mount Royal considerably be-
neath her. But the talk was general. It was gay young talk with a
lot of laughter in it, and the men took turns in ordering champagne.
374
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
The Colonel enjoyed himself thoroughly. Their party was the last to
leave the dining-room. Roger drove him and Amy home.
Roger Willett had bought a Detroit car and was trying the heroic
experiment of running it all winter. If that could be done with a
Langworthy, now- But the Colonel switched his mind resolutely
from all questions of business. He could hear Amy chatting to Elsie in
the tonneau; and more than once Amy laughed aloud. His heart
leaped at the sound. In all the years before he married her, he never
remembered hearing Amy laugh.
When they were alone in their bedroom, however, Amy was sud-
denly silent. "Tired, darling?" asked the Colonel.
"A little," said Amy. "I'm not used to quite such late hours, you
know."
She rallied herself and began to talk about the play. She was putting
her hat away in a box, hanging her scarf on a padded hanger, blowing
into her gloves before she laid them on her dresser. Every intimate
detail, every quick sure movement, spoke to him of their happiness.
Yet he realized that under the surface Amy was not simply tired
to-night: she was worried.
The Colonel could guess only too easily what was bothering her.
There was something he had wanted to ask Amy for months, but
had shrunk from putting into words. It wasn't fair, though, always to
wait for Amy to break the ice. In the middle of something she was
saying he put in abruptly, "Bess was pretty quiet to-night, for her."
"She looked very lovely."
"Um! But she didn't put herself out to be cordial to her relatives."
"Why should she? It was a young people's party. We were really
intruders."
"They were all married people," objected the Colonel. "In spite of
her airs, that Mrs. Reynolds will never see forty again. She's older
than he is, if you ask me."
"Oh, I don't think so!" said Amy. "She just isn't very attractive."
"Well, that's a nuisance for all of them. I've never had any but
good-looking women in my family. I wouldn't know how to treat
the other kind."
"Now you're paying compliments," said Amy with a faint smile.
To be sure, it was still a little strange that he should be paying
compliments to Amy.
THE COLONEL
375
He was tempted to sink satisfied into the bliss of the hour and the
surroundings. But his uneasiness persisted; and, after all, he had to
guard his happiness. He must deserve happiness if he wanted to

He lit a cigar while Amy began to take down her hair. Then he
came out with it. "Amy, how many times has Bess been over to see
you this winter?"
Amy kept her face toward her mirror; but she said distinctly, "She
hasn't been over here. Don't you think I should have told you if she
had?"
"I thought so. But I wanted to be sure."
"She's on the go constantly, entertaining and being entertained. And
running that big house is quite an undertaking for a girl without
much experience of housekeeping and managing servants."
"You always have a good word for everybody, don't you?" snorted
the Colonel. "How many times have you been to see Bess?"
"Well, I've been there."
"How many times?"
"Let me see! Three, I think."
"Three times! And she has never once-?"
"I suppose I should have waited after I'd called there the first time,"
Amy broke in. "But on your account I didn't want to stick to any
hard-and-fast rule."
"My account be-! I beg your pardon, Amy. But I don't want you
going there again until Bess gives some sign of recognizing that you're
on earth."
"You needn't get excited," said Amy. "I discovered that I was
returning too many of my own calls. So I stopped."
"Well, let's say no more about it," said the Colonel. He couldn't
very well scold Amy for the fact that Bess had never set foot in the
Langworthy house since the day of Amy's wedding.
But he didn't propose to let the matter rest. The very next afternoon
he telephoned to be sure Bess was at home. Then he walked up Pros-
pect Avenue to have it out with her.
Bess was affectionate enough to him; she had never discarded the
pretty little tricks with which she used to cajole the Colonel when she
was a little girl. But as soon as he brought up the object of his call,
she stiffened.
376
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"I'm good friends with Amy," she said. "But you can't expect me
to hang around a house where I'm favored every minute with the
sight of a stranger in my mother's place."
"A stranger!" scoffed the Colonel. "Your very own Aunt Amy, a
stranger!"
"Oh, I know Amy is a little tin god to you now," said Bess bitterly.
"It would be amusing if it weren't so disgusting, the way you dote on
Amy."
"Bess, be careful! That's no way to talk."
"I'm not saying anything. It was you who brought the subject up.
But I can't help noticing things. You never took my mother around
the way you take Amy."
"What's that? What's that? When you were a little girl, I escorted
your mother to twenty or thirty damn dinner-parties every winter.
I remember that, if you don't. I used to drag the whole push of you
into Chicago in the days when I had the money. And I always put in
an appearance when your mother entertained; I used even to drop in
for a minute at a ladies' party."
"I've no doubt you thought you were fine to her." Bess's full lips
narrowed obstinately. "Just the same you never took her around the
way you do Amy."
"But even if that were true, Bess, is it such a life-and-death matter?"
"It's nothing to me, of course. You're welcome to run your life to
suit yourself. All I ask is the same privilege."
He was brought face to face with the incredible fact: Bess was
jealous of Amy. There had been bad blood between them, he knew,
when Amy had sided against Bess in the matter of Bill Jerome. But
Bess's own mother had sided against her then, and even more ef-
fectively. Anyhow, that was all over and done with years ago.
But the truth was that Bess was of a jealous nature to begin with.
She hadn't got that either from her mother or from him; but she
had always been that way.
The Colonel was never too good at straight vision; and in the case
of his cherished daughter, an only child born after ten years of wed-
lock, he had always preferred not to see too much. But for a minute
now he saw things that he would have given half the remainder of his
life to be able to ignore.
It was useless to tell himself that women were always unkind to
THE COLONEL
377
other women. He had been appalled sometimes by things overheard
in his domestic circle; but Amy was always kind to everybody. Amy,
Amy! How he always came back to Amy these days! Yet in the
bitterness of this hour he felt that even Amy couldn't make up to him
for the loss of Bess.
He made a last desperate grasp at reconciliation; and he humbled
himself to do it. "Won't you come over just once, Bess, some evening
when I'm sure to be at home? Bring Doug with you or not, as you
like. Surely that isn't too much to ask? I'm your father, after all; and
I'm not so young as I used to be."
"There's no need to be so tragic about it," said Bess. "Why should
you insist on any set program of visits? It's so much simpler if you
come here."
It was just too simple; it would mean that he condoned Bess's
snubbing of Amy. That was the final word between him and Bess.
He set his jaw grimly and left her house. When the front door closed
behind him, he felt that it was closed to him forever.
He walked for a long time before he turned toward home. His lame
leg, which ordinarily bothered him only on damp days, was aching
desperately when he finally dragged himself up his own front steps.
He moved like an old man, and with reason: for the first time in a
long life, he now tasted unrelieved bitterness.
The hall was dark, and the chill of the house struck to his bones.
Probably Amy was waiting for him upstairs. For the moment he
didn't have the strength to ascend to her. He turned on a light, looked
mechanically to see whether the Beacon had been delivered, took off
his heavy coat and laid it across the back of a chair. Then he sank
down on the other chair and sat there shivering and stupefied.
He must have been sitting there for some time when through the
fog of his mind a bright picture struck clear: steep stairs and a long
dark corridor, and at the end of it a door opening to warmth and
light. It was a minute before he recognized that that was what ac-
tually lay ahead of him.
That was what he came home to every day now, that warmth and
welcome, that security and harmony. That was what he would come
home to every day all the rest of his life. It was always waiting for
him; and by God, he had earned it! Yet even in the face of that bright
vision he thought drearily, "We have just enough of the good things
378
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
to tease us on." He had been ecstatically happy this past winter. Such
happiness was too good to last. And even while it endured, it came
and went; and there were moments when even the memory of bliss
grew dim and misty.
That evening when they sat beside their glowing fire, Amy fetched
Dora's banjo. Laughing at herself for the chords she picked out on it,
she sang to him in her sweet small voice: sang "In the Gloaming" and
"Dublin Bay" and "Tenting on the Old Camp Ground." And pres
ently she laid down her banjo and slipped to the rug at his feet; her
tired sweet head rested against his knee, and her hand stole up to
search for his.
He had more happiness to-night than many men have in a lifetime.
But perfect as it was, it was a small happiness in a great world. He
and Amy were shut in here with their warmth and brightness. She
had remembered even to bring him the music which he might have
missed lately. But in the great cold rooms downstairs the piano, which
used to be the center of so many family gatherings, had not been
touched for months. It stood closed and out of tune; and memories
like ghosts hovered over its silent keys.
W
HEN the first shock wore off, his native optimism reasserted
itself. He couldn't rest in the belief that Bess was permanently
estranged from him. Yet there were no further measures that he could
take to bring her to her senses. He thought of a dozen schemes, and
discarded each in turn.
He must restrain his impatience, and let time do its healing work.
If he gave her plenty of time, if he didn't make any rash move, Bess
would presently "come to her stomach." That was the phrase Retta
had always used in prescribing for Bess's tantrums when she was a
child. This was nothing but a tantrum on a large scale. Bess couldn't
really hate Amy. No one could.
Anyhow, Bess's attitude toward her father would change if she
ever became a parent herself. In the natural course of events that
should happen one of these days, if only because Bess loved attention.
When she realized how important Hortense was with her children,
that would give her a hint. It had always irked Bess if Hortense had
anything that she didn't have.
Moreover, there would be no surer way to solidify her position with
the Rutherfords than to present them with an heir. It was odd if old
John hadn't already begun to dun her. The Colonel didn't give any
credit to Dora's fantastic idea that the family was running out. Bess
herself hadn't been born until her parents had been married almost
ten years.
When his grandchildren appeared, he wasn't going to have them
call Amy "Grandma." Amy would love them, though. As soon as
Bess saw how Amy loved those babies, she would be compelled to
soften. When Bess herself was a baby, Amy's attachment for her had
been poignant and touching. Amy must sometimes think of that even
now. She never spoke of it.

380
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Even in their present intimacy Amy didn't talk much to the
Colonel. She could express herself with a look, a gesture, with a kiss
light as thistledown, but warm and lingering. He had never so
much as glimpsed the bliss of really being loved by a woman until
he was kissed by Amy.
But speech was not her medium. Or perhaps she didn't get a
chance. The Colonel had always talked a lot to Retta, but with Retta
he had felt the wisdom of keeping some things back. In a sense, of
course, Amy needed shielding more than Retta had: it would never
do, for instance, to worry Amy about his damn business.
But whatever wild opinion or casual conjecture he might drop in
front of Amy, he need never fear that it would be used against him.
So he talked endlessly to her; and although he did not always speak
of what was worrying him, he regularly succeeded in talking him-
self cheerful.
With the oncoming of spring, too, everything assumed a brighter
complexion. Business picked up. The whole house was open for
occupation once more. Amy took to having their meals served in
the little room behind the sitting-room; it had been the original
dining-room when the house was built. This was a great improve-
ment on eating in the vast "addition" dining-room, where just the
two of them at one end of the big table had looked lost and forlorn.
Jason came in for a few days to start the vegetable-garden. While
he was there, he "took a lick at the flowers." The buxom Hattie,
though noisy and not very tidy, was a cheerful and willing servant.
Nobody had cared to ride again in the car which killed Artie; it
stood in a corner of the stable, just where it had been placed at the
end of that fatal day. But a new Langworthy carried the Colonel
back and forth to business. On Sundays he even ventured to take
Amy driving in the country. Punctures were still a nuisance; he
seldom got through a Sunday afternoon without having to change a
tire. But the new engine worked very well.
The general improvement reached a peak the day they met Dora
at the station. She had written that she was coming "for a long visit."
The Colonel would take that "visit" business up with her later. The
important thing was to get her here.
Her hair was a silver white now, but her clothes and appointments
were daintier and more distinctive than ever. She gave a rapid
THE COLONEL
381
glance at the two of them; then she grinned impishly. She was so

She stood on tiptoe to kiss first Amy and then the Colonel. Dora

well as business was picking up.
When she was established in the bedroom that they had had re-

dog, a cocker puppy which bore considerable resemblance to the
lamented Beppo.
She would stand no nonsense from Bess, either. As soon as she got
her possessions well settled and the packing wrinkles pressed out of
her clothes, she and Amy gave a big luncheon-party. They borrowed
Ida to wait on table, and Bess came early to help with the flowers.
Bess herself gave a big tea for Dora. That launched her on a whole
stream of entertainment. Prospect Avenue matrons who had scarcely
noticed Dora's existence before her lengthy sojourn away from
Mount Royal now sought her as "guest of honor" for their parties.
Amy was drawn into the whirl, of course. Five or six times a week
little Miss Pringle scribbled for the Beacon, "-in honor of Miss Dora
Mills. The guests included Mrs. S. H. Langworthy-" Reading those
items in the Society Column, the Colonel felt that the final approval
of public recognition had been set upon his marriage. Up to now it
had always seemed a little strange-indeed it had seemed a private
and personal matter that "Mrs. S. H. Langworthy" actually meant
Amy.
It soon became clear that Dora, rushing about to luncheons and
teas and answering the telephone a dozen times a day, had in view
another objective beside being amiable to hostesses and stirring up a
little excitement for Amy. That next fall Dora was to achieve her
great ambition; she had made connections with a new travel agency
in New York, and she was definitely committed to conducting at
least one trip for them. She was drumming up trade in Mount
Royal.

"I plan to start with the mammas," she explained. "Then when

She intended to come back here in the future only as a guest. Well,
she was an enlivening guest. No house could be gloomy-or even
quiet-with Dora and a new puppy both in it.
He was dashed, however, when two or three days after that first
big luncheon-party Amy told him that she wanted to sleep in her
old room that night. She and Dora didn't get much time to them-
selves, and they had such a lot to talk over.
Bedtime conferences were natural enough between sisters. Any-
how, the boarding-school girl came out now and then in every
woman. You couldn't tell him women grew up and men didn't. All
women loved to chatter sometimes, in relaxation and freedom, after
the lights were out. Besides, Dora and Amy had roomed together for
years, and must now and then get homesick for the old times. He
hated to spare Amy for even one night. But then, he mustn't be
selfish.
The night's excursion was repeated at intervals, however; and
gradually the intervals drew closer together. Surely any husband was
entitled to lodge a protest. But a blunt protest to Amy was not to be
thought of, and his hints about breakfast-hours and extra work for
Hattie were met and answered on their own merits.
Only lately he had been pluming himself on the way he could say
anything in the world to Amy. Now here came this most important
matter on which he dared not venture to speak his mind. It was not
merely that he felt awkward about putting such intimate matters
THE COLONEL
383
into words; he was tied by a consideration which he hated to

If she would leave a husband for a sister, even temporarily, that

must be crowding fifty; but physically she was a woman of thirty.
Once he had been timid about approaching her as a husband, fear-
ful that he might shock or offend her. Apparently the shoe was on
the other foot. He had failed to interest her.
He tried to get his mind on other and brighter subjects. The sum-

matters were shaping for the best?
Bess stayed only a few minutes after he got home; but in coming
at all she had made the one concession he asked for. The Colonel

He looked to see his own glow reflected in Amy. But at the supper-

During the evening she was silent, even for her; and at bedtime that

He was more than hurt now. He was frightened.
If all she wanted was a change or respite, couldn't she have said so?
Dora was due to start back East the first of September; Amy could
have gone with her for company on the trip, have paid Hortense a

but thinking that she must be enjoying herself, he would have tried

Was she simply shy about acting the married woman in front of

passage of the weeks should have made her better instead of worse.

not only outspoken but mischievous in her acceptance of the basic

Dora watched him in silence for a moment; pity and understand-

He had rallied to meet the situation. He was compelled, of course,
to tell Amy that Bess was a trouble-maker, that he was disappointed
in her, that he realized they were better off to see nothing of her, at
least for the present.
Oh, Amy did the rest! The reconciliation seemed complete. Yet it
was with a sinking heart that he saw Dora off the next day.
Why should he be afraid to be left alone with Amy? The two of
them had been perfectly happy all that first enchanted winter. It was
outsiders who had made all the trouble.
But he had lost his old sense of security in life. Somehow now he
was conscious of a deadly internal weariness-he who used to face
with perfect self-confidence and zest the day and whatever it brought
forth. All his life he had been accustomed to planning and doing for
other people. Now he would have liked to find a quiet nook, and
learn to smoke a pipe, and sit for a long time without having to plan
or do anything.
But of course that was nonsense. He was getting on his own nerves.
Small wonder then if he sometimes got on Amy's! He was bound a
THE COLONEL
387
little too closely to his past. The old house, the familiar town, the ever-
lasting family! He had never before had that feeling of walking in a
treadmill. Some men had it all their lives, and got so used to it that
they appeared not to mind. He was spoiled, that was all. He had had
too many years of a big family all happy together; but he couldn't
expect time to stand still for him.
It was strange though that he should have lost heart just now, when
a life far better than the old was actually opening out to him. Rich
years lay ahead for him and Amy; but why should he ever have sup-
posed that he could have that sunlit future unless he adjusted to it,
guarded it, made himself worthy of it? You couldn't just lay down
a lump sum to assure a life's happiness; happiness had to be earned
day by day.
All life had seemed to lie within his grasp that evening-only a little
over a year ago-when Amy had come back to him so beautifully to
acknowledge her love. Amy had gone to sleep in his arms on winter
nights, and had wakened only to seek his kiss. He could never love
Amy enough-not half enough. Their first year together was sufficient
in itself to reward a lifetime's striving. But their first year was over
now, was definitely dead and done with. And in the recollection even
that first year was now a little tarnished.

VI
IS distrust of his own happiness wore off when Amy was back
with him again. But things were not the same that second
winter. Before long it began to be manifest that the difference was in
Amy.
Now that the first excitement of her marriage had worn off, she had
too much time to think. The full household, as it had for so long
existed, had often overwhelmed her; but its dissolution had left be-
hind an echoing emptiness. Nobody came here to call, even; and
outside of her evenings at the theater with the Colonel, she seldom left
the house except for solitary walks.
The few women of her own generation whom she knew and liked
-or at least knew well enough to be used to them-could offer her
no distraction. Mrs. Travers had rallied from the shock of her di-
vorce, and was devoting herself to her business interests; during the
day she was never available. Mr. Cordiner died that winter; Mrs.
Cordiner, set free at last when freedom could mean nothing to her,
shut herself up in a mourning so close that it amounted to suttee.
Mrs. Chase was always cordial to Amy when they happened to meet;
but Mrs. Chase was busy marrying off two daughters, adjusting the
world for the daughter who did not marry. Mrs. Palmer stayed in
Chicago most of the time to be near her son; his dissipation had
finally landed him in a sanitarium, and there were grim rumors about
his condition.
Amy had rallied indeed from what Bess said to her; her super-
sensitiveness could not go on forever getting the better of her judg-
ment. She knew the Colonel loved her. But to build a life on love
alone-ah, that was difficult! It would have been difficult even if the
two of them had not been involved in a position essentially false.
The Colonel could simplify matters in his own mind. She supposed
388
THE COLONEL
389
that he did, and that for him the picture was: he loved Amy, he had
married her, and the past simply didn't matter.
But to Amy the past mattered very much indeed. More and more
as time went on did she see herself as a woman who had married her
sister's husband: married him after years spent in his household, when
she had managed to endure only by ignoring her love. She should
have gone on ignoring it, if she hoped for peace in the end.
Of all this she dared not breathe a word to the Colonel. He couldn't
understand; he might only laugh at her for splitting hairs. But
whether he laughed or swore, or held his tongue with conscious pa-
tience, her confidences would distress him. She had no right to distress
him: she was his one refuge in a world which wasn't treating him
any too well.
She lost weight and color, and fell back into her old habit of sleep-
ing badly. Amy's light slumber, so sweet and childlike, had struck
the Colonel as very touching; he had never realized that such pro-
found peace was a new experience in her life.
This second winter he began to be aware that although she went
to sleep almost as readily as ever, she had begun regularly to wake
after an hour or so, and to lie awake a long time. At first he used to
kiss her, to chafe her arms gently, to hold her cold hands against his
body.
Then he began to fear that he was only annoying her. Before he
moved he took to whispering, "Amy, are you awake?" Often she
would fail to answer; yet he knew by her breathing that she was
awake. She didn't want him to know it; she preferred those dark
hours of solitude to any comfort he had to offer.
Gradually he, too, began to lie awake, rigid beside her in a long
silence. It was the silence of complete estrangement, worse between
them than a naked sword.
He felt himself to blame for not having been a better lover. In
their lamplit evenings together he tried to make it up to her. If she
couldn't love him as he had hoped, she might at least grow very
fond of him.
But he made the mistake of being gentle and brotherly with her.
Amy was a passionate woman; and under his gentleness she froze.
The old conflict began to have its way with her once more; and the

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
dogs of conscience were ravenous from having been ignored through
that one enchanted winter.
Her health began to be definitely bad; she was worried by symp-
toms which she tried to keep from him. Here at least the Colonel
knew what to do. He made her stay in bed, and he sent for Dr.
Simpson.
Amy had always kept Dr. Simpson at a distance, refusing to sink
the man she didn't like in the physician whom she on the whole
trusted. Now for the first time Dr. Simpson had a chance to make
something like a thorough examination.
The result amazed the doctor himself. "These delicate-looking
women!" he growled to the Colonel. "She'll live not only to see you
and me underground: she'll bury Bess and Hortense. That is, she
will if she stops worrying. Worry is slow poison to a woman like
that; sometimes not so slow, either."
"What's she worrying about?" demanded the Colonel anxiously.
"You tell me. Or no, never mind! Take it up with her. There's
nothing on earth the matter with her but nerves. Nerves are beyond
a doctor. I could give her a tonic, of course. But that wouldn't do
her any good-unless it happened to amuse her."
The Colonel had been thinking restlessly, Could Amy possibly be
worrying over the damn business? "Business" and "worry" had
come to be synonymous terms in his own life, just as Artie had
surmised they were in most men's. But Amy knew nothing of that
side of his life.
He brushed aside the thought, and caught at the hint in the doc-
tor's last words. "I don't think she gets enough amusement. Wouldn't
she be better off if she had a complete change?"
"Of course she would be. Anybody is. Change of scenery and
change of society: that's better than any tonic you can get in a
bottle."
They had been talking in the lower hall. Before he returned to
Amy in the bedroom, the Colonel spent a frantic half-hour figuring
in the library.
He approached her with his proposition all thought out. March
was a dreary month in Mount Royal. Amy must go to New York
and spend at least a month with Hortense.
THE COLONEL
391
Amy said she didn't feel up to such a trip. But she had brightened
at the mention of Hortense.
"Two babies!" teased the Colonel. "They know Dode by this time
as well as they know their own mother. And you've never even seen
'em."
"Oh, Colonel!" Amy sprang from the support of her pillows and
leaned eagerly toward him. "Couldn't they come here? Hortense has
never paid a real visit to her old home, never since she was married.
Let's have them here! Then you, too, can see them."
Beholding her suddenly alert and eager, he managed to ignore
the way she had involuntarily called him "Colonel." Retta had al-
ways called him "Sam." Dora, too, said "Sam" when they were
engaged in one of their mysterious plots against-or in behalf of-
other people's peace and dignity. Amy had never been at ease with
his first name; but during their married life she had got around her
awkwardness by substituting the endearments that thrilled and
charmed him when he heard them from her lips.
"Colonel," she said now. But perhaps that was partly because she
was thinking of Hortense, and with that thought had slipped natu-
rally back into the ways of the old time when they were all to-
gether. She wouldn't go off to sleep in Hortense's room, though, the
way she had in Dora's; he'd attend to that. Even that might be
almost as good as the way things had been going lately; but he
didn't feel called upon to let her make the experiment.
Amy and he both wrote to Hortense that night; their letters went
East in the same envelop. The Colonel wrote a second letter from
the office; and into that private communication he put some hint of
how disturbed he was. The trip would be something of an under-
taking for Hortense with two small children, he realized. "But if
you can't bring the children," he wound up, "for God's sake come
alone."
For three days he was on edge over the outcome. Amy was ex-
cited and eager, deciding which bedrooms would be most convenient
for her guests, planning to hire extra help in the house, counting up
the old friends whom Hortense would surely want to see. The very
idea of the visit had benefited her. But if she were to be disappointed
now, she might sink back farther than ever.
He needn't have worried so; he might have known he could count
392
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
on Hortense. The third evening a Special Delivery letter reached the
house. "I've only been waiting to be asked," Hortense wrote. "But
I can't start until next week, or possibly the week after. Mrs. Jerome
needs new clothes for Mount Royal; and the offspring, too, must

take time. Some days I wonder whether I shall ever be caught up
on my shopping."
A second letter stated that she was bringing along a nurse for the
children. Dora, who would have been of great use on the journey
if she had been available, was conducting a second tour.
"But it's time I learned to be responsible for my own children,
even on a Pullman," Hortense went on. "Margaret is a darling, but
Billie already shows signs of being a little devil. It's the Mills in
him. Aunt Amy, can you look up some Jersey milk for the children?
And you won't think me too fussy if I ask you to have all their
drinking water boiled?"
How richly human and satisfying it all was! A young mother was
making careful arrangements for her children. Amy was having
Artie's bed moved into Hortense's room, so that she could take the
little girl in there with her. "The aunts" old room would do nicely
for the little boy and the nurse. They wouldn't need Bess's room;
but it was opened anyhow and cleaned. The Langworthy house was
itself again in the bustle of preparation.
Hortense arrived on a blustering day in the middle of March. It
had been a fatiguing trip; the nurse was sulky, the children were
fussy, the baggage was complicated. Hortense had an eye to every-
thing, even while she was kissing the Colonel's cheek and hugging
Amy. Her hat was a little askew; there was a smear of soot on her
chin. But she was radiant.
Amy insisted on getting into the tonneau of the Langworthy with
the nurse and the children. Hortense must sit beside the Colonel, so
that he could point out landmarks and changes.
There had been a good many changes in the past four years. Hor-
tense smiled on most of them. Only once did she grow serious. She
looked for a long minute at the chairs in the windows of the Mount
Royal Hotel, and the Colonel knew she was thinking of Artie-
poor old Artie, who used to hug himself because people sometimes
remarked that Hortense looked like him.
THE COLONEL
393
Then abruptly she began to speak of Cordiner & Morse's, now
being modernized by the new owners. "Everything is different there
except old Tom Holmes and his rows of watches. While they were
about it I wish they had swept him out, too. There he sits year after
year, enjoying other people's bad luck. Colonel, I sometimes have a
feeling that when we're all dead and gone he'll still be sitting there."
"Sitting there scowling, with charity toward none, with malice
toward all," chuckled the Colonel. "As a matter of fact, 'Tense, I
don't think he ever did anybody a particle of real harm. But he
always did make chills run up and down your back, didn't he?
When you were a child, I think you actually believed the old duffer
was Father Time himself."
Tom Holmes was left outside with every other worry at the mo-
ment of homecoming. The children were bathed, and the nurse gave
them their supper in front of an open fire in the library. The Colonel
sat gloating over the picture they made in their tiny nightgowns and
rosy bare feet while Amy and Hortense ranged upstairs and down.
Hortense must go into every room, must renew her impressions of
how the house used to look, must note with interest and comment
with approval on every change Amy had made. When she saw her
panels mounted at last in the big bedroom, she stopped short and
kissed Amy.
The three of them at supper in the big dining-room filled it with
cheerful chatter, though the great dining-table had been shoved into
a corner and they ate at a small one. The meal was worthy of the
old days; Hortense ate until she had to loosen her belt. Amy's new
second girl waited on table fairly well; once when she made a slip,
Amy caught the Colonel's eye and smiled.
Loath to leave the pleasant scene, the Colonel lighted his after-
supper cigar right at the table. "Tense," he said, "you always were
a handsome girl. But since you've had children you're the prettiest
thing I ever saw in my life, except perhaps Amy."
"I suppose it is partly the children," agreed Hortense. "But they
go along with the rest. Don't you see, Colonel, I now have everything
that makes life worth living?"
"The children are wonderful, though," said Amy. "You enjoyed
your session with them, didn't you, Colonel? You always like little
children."

"They're the rewards of a well-spent life." The subject drew the
Colonel on to pronounce a name he hadn't intended to mention:

Hortense looked astonished. "Why, didn't you know? Bess had a
miscarriage in February."
The Colonel was distressed by her blunt announcement; young

company. He supposed Amy would be embarrassed, too.
But for once Amy sounded properly like a married woman. "I

"Not known, even to her own father?" protested Hortense.
"Not known, especially to her own family," said Amy steadily.
"It is humiliating for her. You're no judge of such a matter, Hor-

Hortense saw that Amy was signaling to her to let the subject
drop. What Amy may have said to her afterward about the rift in
the family he never learned. Anyhow, Hortense was a merciful
woman. Two days after her arrival in Mount Royal she went to see
Bess, but she went alone. The Colonel could not help wondering
whether Bess would have been as considerate if the circumstances had
been reversed.
Hortense had little need to parade her children elsewhere in
Mount Royal. Amy paraded them.
She had hired a baby-carriage from Kortebein's; the nurse wheeled
Billie, and Amy took the little girl by the hand. Every fine afternoon
the cortège proceeded down the street. Sometimes they called on
Amy's acquaintances; sometimes they merely walked until the little
girl was tired and had to be put in the buggy with the baby. The
Colonel often came home early, simply for the pleasure of seeing
Amy bring her charges in.
Meanwhile, Hortense was lunching, tea-ing, calling: picking up
old friendships, getting caught up on the news. This was the kind
of triumphant return of which she must have dreamed in the days
when rigid spinstership seemed to be drawing in inevitably upon
her. But those dreams had vanished with the days that produced
them. Hortense was so happy in her marriage that it was hard for
THE COLONEL
395
her to believe she had ever needed to console herself with imaginings.
She had come to stay a month. She stayed six weeks. During the
last fortnight she reported more than once that Bill was "yammer-
ing" for her to come home. "He'll appreciate me when he does get
me back," she concluded. "But let him wait. It will do him good."
The time came, however, when she had to make her plans for re-
turning. Amy, too, had at length to face the idea. When Hortense
finally set the day for her departure, the Colonel could see the shad-
ows beginning to close in once more about Amy.
Perhaps Hortense saw something, too. Still, he mustn't let him-
self grow suspicious. Full as her life was, Hortense would always
find room in it for "the aunts"; it was surely only natural for her
to suggest that Amy go back to New York with her. Amy was so
much better now that the trip would do her good; and in New
York she could have a fortnight with Dora before that intrepid con-
ductor gathered her school-girl charges together for the summer
flight to Europe.
Amy rejected the idea at first; but it was plain that her refusal
was reluctant. The Colonel pressed her. He explained that he ex-
pected to be so busy through the spring, he would miss her less now
than later. He even half-promised to go down and get her when her
visit was over; he hadn't been in New York since he was a very
young man, and for once he would like to make a trip which wasn't
on business.
She hesitated. Was he making her think that he was trying to
get rid of her? He had to take that risk. He ceased to argue against
her reluctance, and began to assume that she was going. He was
turning into a diplomatist in his old age. Once his own communica-
tion had been Yea or Nay; and he had expected nothing beyond that
from other people.
The day that he started downtown to get Hortense's tickets, Amy
yielded. It was none too soon. At his office he was met by appalling
news. His first thought was, "This settles it. The jig really is up
now." Swift on the heels of that came the reflection, "I'm not going
to say a word to Amy about it. Bad news isn't made any better by
running home with it the first thing."
He had to resort to subterfuge to keep Amy from finding out;
he even had to purloin the Beacon, which carried the bad news
396
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
flaring on the front page the next night but one. Hortense helped
him loyally; she accepted at its face value his statement that he saw
no reason for worrying Amy until he had some idea himself of
whether this thing which had been sprung upon him meant com-
plete disaster or only ordinary business annoyance.
He had been sure he could rely on Hortense; but the effectiveness
of her measures went beyond anything that he himself would have
attempted. She secured the immediate dismissal of the second girl,
so that the servants wouldn't be overheard gossiping to each other.
She kept Amy from walks which might have led to chance encoun-
ters with acquaintances: had her play with the children in the yard
on the two fine days, and amuse them by the fireside when it hap-
pened to rain. When Mrs. Chase came in the last afternoon to say
good-by, Hortense simply apologized for not calling Amy from her
packing.
She didn't draw a long breath for three days; and a good night's
sleep was only something to look forward to after she got home.
But she steered his scheme safely past all hazards. On the appointed
day the Colonel saw the party off; and the bustle and excitement of
their departure suggested nothing beyond holiday-making.
Amy clung to him on the station platform. Right at that last mo-
ment reluctance almost overcame her. He could have swept aside
all their arrangements and taken her home with him. In the empty
days to come he would still have the memory of that unwilling de-
parture, of Amy's misted eyes and clinging hands.
He tore himself away from her when the train stopped. He
watched for her last wave from the window; he even lingered to see
the train disappear in the distance. It is bad luck to watch any one
out of sight; but sometimes it may be the one alleviation in a world
grown desolate.
When he turned away at length, it was to face a crisis where a
stout heart was the least thing demanded of him: his experience
and resources were being put to a crucial test. The disaster was
only three days old; but already it seemed to him that he had lived
with it a long time, and that before it burst upon him he had seen
it coming.
As a matter of fact, he had always known that such a thing might
happen, but he had never had the slightest actual belief that it would.
THE COLONEL
397
He wasn't important enough, surely, to be snuffed out summarily.
Yet snuffing out was just what this threat amounted to. The De-
troit firm with which Eugene and Paul Reynolds were connected
by marriage had sued the Colonel for half a million dollars for
infringement of patent. They might just as well have sued him for
ten million. If they made out a case, the Colonel wouldn't have car-
fare left.

VII
HERE followed a week of frenzied consultations with engi-
neers, with lawyers, with the State bank, which held his notes.
The Colonel was quite sure that he hadn't infringed any patents;
else why had he been paying a professional engineer all along?
The engineer swore that there had been no infringement.
But this was a question not of facts but of law; the Colonel learned
to his sorrow that the two things may be quite different. Richard
Duane, whom he retained to represent him, wasn't too encouraging.
"They can sue a hundred times, on a hundred different points,"
said Duane. "The matter may drag through the courts for years.
Meanwhile, your business suffers. And it will cost a lot to defend a
suit of this kind."
"What else can I do except defend it?" demanded the Colonel.
"Nothing, if they're really out to flatten you. If the Reynoldses
are after your scalp, you're as good as scalped this minute. But I'm
not sure that's really what they're after. The Detroit people are all
tied up with the Reynoldses and the Rutherfords. But of course the
Rutherfords are connections of yours, too."
The Colonel tried not to wince. "That isn't going to do me much
good. In a purely business matter-"
"There is no such thing as a purely business matter," interrupted
Duane. "Don't let anybody ever try to tell you that business isn't
tied up with all sorts of personal motives. But even from a business
standpoint, I'm not just sure of the going. Didn't John Rutherford
once try to buy out your business?"
"I wouldn't put it quite so strong as that. He made me what
he called an offer."
"Well, when he once gets his eye on a thing he never lets up.
We'll play for time, Colonel, and make them give their hand away.
398
THE COLONEL
399
It wouldn't surprise me if the whole thing proved to be simply a
scheme to make you sell out cheap."
"Hell!" exploded the Colonel. "If that's what they want, they
needn't have gone to all this bother. I'd sell out for a baking of
flapjacks. Let them go ahead and lose their money for a change."
But a few weeks later, when an offer actually reached him, his fit
of bad temper was over. The offer came officially from Eugene
Reynolds. "'The voice is Jacob's voice," the Colonel reflected grimly,
"but the hands are the hands of Esau." His fighting spirit reas-
serted itself. After all, his business was his own; if he couldn't sail
the ship he could scuttle it. He refused the offer curtly.
He put a second mortgage on his house to pay Richard Duane.
He had once promised himself to keep the homestead clear, no
matter what happened. Later he had sworn that the first thing he
did must be to pay off that previous mortgage. Well, this was no
time to remember either boasts or oaths.
He continued to make cars; somehow he paid wages and got
credit for materials. The easiest way to get hold of him now would
be to throw him into bankruptcy. It was odd that the Reynoldses
hadn't thought of that.
The summer wore away. Amy wrote to him two or three times
a week: long letters, impersonal, chatty-friendly enough, of course.
He had written her cautiously about the suit. He was both relieved
and disappointed to find how calmly she took his news. Probably
to Amy in New York it all seemed a long way off. That was how he
wanted her to feel about it. Of course it was!
Bess had telephoned to him the week after Amy left; she invited
him to her house for Sunday dinner. The Colonel refused curtly.
He would have preferred peace between him and Bess; but peace
was not to be bought so cheaply.
She telephoned a second time. Then one summer evening she
came driving over, and sat with him on the veranda while he smoked
his cigar.
Hearing her chat lightly on about one thing and another-but
mostly about herself-he was driven to wonder at the strangeness
of familiar things. It seemed perfectly right and natural to sit with
Bess in the old surroundings. But Bess could hardly have made it
plainer that she came only because Amy was away. She as good as
400
THE LANgworthy FAMILY
offered him his choice between them. It was a fantastic choice. How
could Bess suppose for a single minute that he would side in with
her against Amy?
He expected Amy home in the fall. But in September he had a
letter from Hortense. Aunt Amy was much better; but Hortense
was afraid that the harsh Middle-Western winter might undo all
the good so laboriously achieved. Didn't the Colonel want to urge
her to prolong her visit? "I'll invite her independently," Hortense
wound up. "If you think it better, you needn't even let her know
that I have written you."
Had that letter been prompted? Oh, no, surely not! Anyhow, he
mustn't allow himself to get tangled in cobwebs of what might or
might not be; it was far better to stick to the plain common sense
of the situation. Amy would be much more comfortable at Hortense's
this coming winter. The Langworthy house already seemed dreary
on fall evenings. The Colonel himself was dull company, thanks to
the state his mind was in.
Hattie had developed into a good plain cook. She fed him heartily
on meat and potatoes, on squash and cabbage and pie. But she simply
didn't see dirt; and after all there wasn't much incentive to clean
rooms which were never used. The Colonel's bedroom at least was
swept out now and then; and he even ordered her to wash the win-
dows in the library, where he now took his solitary meals before
the fireplace where only a few months before-already it seemed
like years he had watched Hortense's children eat their first sup-
per beneath his roof.
He was glad to get back into his dark gray fall suit; his summer
vests were too manifest an extravagance. His blue serge had grown
so shiny that he wore it now only while the other was being pressed.
When winter settled down in earnest, indeed, he often didn't bother
to have his gray suit pressed. It seemed finicky to bother about such
things when the grocer carried him only because he was an old
customer, and the butcher had grown so surly that Hattie no longer
dared to complain about the delivery of tough meat.
The Colonel had not been able to pay his quarterly interest on
Charley Manning's notes. Charley's office had not called his atten-
tion to the omission; that was characteristic. But he had vaguely
expected to hear from Charley when news of the lawsuit became
THE COLONEL
401
public. In all the ensuing months he had had no word. Apparently
his enterprise was now too desperate even for Charley Manning.
When Christmas came Bess once more invited him to dinner.
That would have meant dining with the elder Rutherfords, too. It
was a relief to be able to tell her that he had already accepted an
invitation from Mrs. Chase.
Under that hospitable roof he spent a cheerful enough holiday.
But how strange it was to be spending Christmas under any roof
except his own! He had been glad enough to give Hattie the day
off, and save the expense of a Christmas dinner. That was a nice
way for any Langworthy to approach a holiday!
This sort of thing couldn't be allowed to go on. In January he
steeled himself to do what he had vowed a dozen times that he
would never do. The impossibility of ordering any more coal until
he had paid his bill, the difficulty of finding even Hattie's six dollars
a week, left him no choice except to put his pride in his pocket.
He went up to ask Charley Manning for one more loan.
It transpired that Charley had left Mount Royal the day after
Christmas. He enjoyed the heat of an Illinois summer; but now
the Riviera beckoned him. He had "gone to gamble at Monte Carlo"
was the way the gossips put it. As a matter of fact, he preferred
Nice to Monte Carlo, and he took his choicest gambles right at home.
In his spacious if rather shabby office, Minnie Knowles queened
it alone and enjoyed her importance. "Mr. Manning won't be back
for months," she said crisply. "I never know when to expect him.
But it won't be soon. He has been in Mount Royal more than usual
this past year. But when he goes away his plans are always in-
definite."
The Colonel was driven to hint, "Surely he must leave things in
charge of some one. I mean, he can't just walk away and expect his
business interests to to hibernate until he comes back."
"He has his interests thoroughly organized. They run in his ab-
sence just the same as when he's here."
"But what would you do if an emergency came up? Cable him?"
"An emergency?" Her tone implied that the Manning interests
were not subject to emergencies; even to hint at such a thing was
a sort of sacrilege.
In desperation the Colonel tried again. "He never closes his

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
house, does he? I remember his telling me once that he hated the

"I pay his household bills, of course. I-ah-" Her feeling of im-

She liked Colonel Langworthy, so far as she could like any one.
Her shrewd eyes took in his frayed cuffs and the wrinkles across

held him up to Charley Manning, who for all his opulence tended
-"like all bachelors!" thought Mrs. Knowles scornfully-tended

She was here to get rid of beggars; her job was her religion. Still
she had her personal sympathies, though they were faint.
"If there's anything I can do for you-" she brought out.
It was half an offer of help; but it was as good a way as any of
putting an end to the conversation. The Colonel wasn't asking
any help from women. Perhaps it was just as well, anyhow, that
his call had failed to net him anything. He knew more about Charley
Manning's pensioners than Mrs. Knowles thought he did; he didn't
aspire to join those ranks. Not yet, anyhow.
He would have enjoyed a half-hour's talk with Charley Manning,
however. That old sinner generally made out better than the unco'
guid who sniffed at him. The moral eluded the Colonel; but Char-
ley's society always braced him up.
However, the very feeling of doing something a little different
had much the same effect. He had been on the treadmill so long
that he had fairly forgotten it was possible to step off. Yet he had
lived many years before he ever embarked on the manufacture of
automobiles; he had not only got along, but had earned a comfort-
able living.
Just before embarking on this luckless enterprise, indeed, he had
been on the point of starting on an entirely different course of life.
Mightn't it still be possible to make a clean break?
"There's always the Soldiers' Home!" he had sometimes said when
THE COLONEL
403
he felt particularly desperate. But wasn't that conceivably more than

The very day after his visit to Charley Manning's office he came

Major!" he snapped. "What you been doing with yourself lately?
I haven't seen you in a dog's age."
"I'm not usually downtown at this time of day," explained the
Colonel.
"Don't come to meetings at all any more, do you?"
"Well, I have a young wife now, you know. She doesn't like to
be left alone in the evening." That was a pretty casual way to refer
to Amy. But it was just as well to scotch any rumors that she was
spending a long time in the East.
"H'm! But maybe you're wiser to keep out of Loyal Legion poli-

"That isn't what you used to tell me. Come to think of it, though,

"He's up at the Manning Arms right now. I don't know what

"That doesn't sound likely. The Local Manager doesn't get any

"No, and I don't see how he can steal much. I give you the story
for what it's worth, Major. But sometimes a man turns honest
when he gets old. Stranger things have happened."
Strange things were happening every day. And after all, this
new idea of the Colonel's wasn't so strange. He had once been nomi-
nated for office because he failed to seek it. If he let the authorities
know that he had changed his mind, that might help.
404
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Just as well, though, it might hinder. He mustn't let his bad luck
make him superstitious. Meeting Captain Duquesne in the street
didn't mean that the finger of Fate was pointing. It meant merely
that the Colonel's watch had wound on and on last night, and this
afternoon Tom Holmes had grudgingly admitted that for once a
layman was right in suggesting that the mainspring must be broken.
The next afternoon the Colonel hired a livery horse and buggy;
retail credit was still fatally easy for him to get. Even if his car had
not been laid up for the winter, it would have been mired on the
road leading to the Soldiers' Home. But the Colonel enjoyed the
drive out there: the horse's hoofs splashing in the mud, the front
wheels cutting a neat pattern through it, the wind stinging his face
a little, but not harshly.
The Home looked very comfortable to him to-day. The buildings
seemed to draw closer together now that the leaves were off the trees.
The billiard-hall was full at this hour in the afternoon, and so was
the library; and the cheerful hum of talk over beer-mugs reached
him even through the closed windows of the canteen. Winter made
the country less engaging in his eyes; but after all this settlement
was self-sufficient. Snug in his own quarters (with heat and light
furnished) a man needed only the right companionship to be very
well contented.
The Colonel drove around the grounds, and then dropped in to
call on the new Governor. He seemed very well established; but
the Colonel's ambition no longer flew so high. There were other
positions in the institution; and although few officers ever resigned
themselves out of a Soldiers' Home, in the course of time one or
other of them must die.
He returned the horse to the livery-stable and ate a substantial
supper at the Manning Arms. He had hoped that Senator Roberts
might come into the dining-room; he would have preferred their
encounter to seem like chance. The Senator did not appear, however.
The Colonel returned to the lobby and sent up his name.
He was admitted to a room which looked and smelled as if a
political caucus had been going on there; but for the moment the
Senator was alone. He produced cigars and ordered up drinks. He
was too astute a politician not to treat this as a purely social call;
it was left for the Colonel to break the ice.
THE COLONEL
405
The Colonel made his opening without undue finesse. "I was out
to see Governor Cox this afternoon. Think there's any chance you'll
need a successor for him one of these days?"
"I wish there was," said Senator Roberts. "I don't like him. But
he's pretty well entrenched. He used to be National Commander of
the Loyal Legion; and he's been shrewd about repairing his fences."
"The Treasurer is a civilian, of course," hinted the Colonel.
"Yes, but I'm afraid he stays. Government accounts aren't a thing
you can learn in a minute. Anybody can be Governor of a Soldiers'
Home, but good Treasurers are scarce."
"I can believe that," said the Colonel earnestly. He had never even
kept his own books; right now anything financial struck him as a
nightmare.
Senator Roberts eyed him through a fresh cloud of smoke. "The
Adjutant is beginning to get past his work, if that interests you."
"Oh, I suppose anybody can be Adjutant?"
"You or I or John Smith could be, after a week's practice. They're
not making any immediate change, so far as I know. But in the
course of a year or so, a change is bound to come."
"I'm tied up myself at present," said the Colonel.
He knew Senator Roberts must have heard of his lawsuit; but
he gave his own version of the story at some length. The Senator
listened with interest; he had a connoisseur's appreciation of any-
thing that savored of chicanery. The Soldiers' Home was not men-
tioned again; but when the Colonel left at ten o'clock he felt that
they had reached an understanding.
His luck was beginning to change. The very next day he received
a check for three hundred dollars on what he had long considered
a bad bill.
Three hundred dollars wouldn't go far toward paying his debts.
But it would be a neat sum to hand over to Amy. The Colonel sent
her the check, and suggested that she put it to good use by joining
Dora's next tour to Europe. Amy had never been to Europe, and
now was her chance.
404
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
Just as well, though, it might hinder. He mustn't let his bad luck
make him superstitious. Meeting Captain Duquesne in the street
didn't mean that the finger of Fate was pointing. It meant merely
that the Colonel's watch had wound on and on last night, and this
afternoon Tom Holmes had grudgingly admitted that for once a
layman was right in suggesting that the mainspring must be broken.
The next afternoon the Colonel hired a livery horse and buggy;
retail credit was still fatally easy for him to get. Even if his car had
not been laid up for the winter, it would have been mired on the
road leading to the Soldiers' Home. But the Colonel enjoyed the
drive out there: the horse's hoofs splashing in the mud, the front
wheels cutting a neat pattern through it, the wind stinging his face
a little, but not harshly.
The Home looked very comfortable to him to-day. The buildings
seemed to draw closer together now that the leaves were off the trees.
The billiard-hall was full at this hour in the afternoon, and so was
the library; and the cheerful hum of talk over beer-mugs reached
him even through the closed windows of the canteen. Winter made
the country less engaging in his eyes; but after all this settlement
was self-sufficient. Snug in his own quarters (with heat and light
furnished) a man needed only the right companionship to be very
well contented.
