AM
VIII
MY didn't go to Europe in the summer; she came home instead.
She looked charming, and she seemed delighted to be with
him again. He had been a fool to attach such importance to a passing
spell of nerves; Amy had her ups and downs, like everybody else.
She was interested in his new plans for the future: not eager at
all-the whole thing was still vague to both of them but quite
acquiescent. He broached the subject one Sunday afternoon when he
took her out to the Soldiers' Home and drove her around the Ad-
jutant's quarters.
"Do you think you could ever be happy in a little tucked-up place
like that?" he wanted to know.
"It doesn't look tucked-up; there must be seven or eight rooms."
"Perhaps I meant 'tucked-in.' It's near the Quartermaster's and
the Commissary's quarters-much closer than any house is to us in
town."
"I'd get used to that, I'm sure," said Amy. "I don't think I'd ever
try bringing Hattie out to the Home, though. She carries on even
with the milkman, I've just found out; and I don't think age would
be any barrier to her favors."
"I don't like that very much, even in town," said the Colonel. "But
we can't let her go just now. I owe her more than a month's wages."
When the words were out he bit his tongue; he was afraid Amy
might offer to pay those wages herself. Most of his three hundred
dollars was still in a bank in New York.
But Amy was guileless about money. A certain sum had been set
aside to pay something on her trip to Europe; therefore in her eyes
it couldn't be diverted for wages. She intended to make that trip in
the fall; she planned to sell one of her bonds to raise the rest of the
money. Dora's enthusiasm had at last infected her; she talked eagerly
406
THE COLONEL
407
and often of the sights she expected to see and the reading she had
done in preparation.
He was glad on the whole that she was to be away again for a
time. Ahead in the fall loomed something which would have dis-
tressed her too keenly if it had gone on close to her and she had had
to face day by day its effect on him. He had to be careful, how-
ever, not to let slip any hint of that bad black business. Amy would
have shrunk from the pleasantest trip if he had allowed it to assume
in her eyes the look of leaving a sinking ship.
He got her away just in time. He had vowed that he would make
a success of his factory or else close it up. He kept his word. The
first of October he closed it up. The building went on mortgage,
the fixtures and stock to his creditors. The Langworthy car was now
only a memory, except for the few specimens which were still for
sale in his downtown show-room.
His retail business he kept; but it wasn't worth much. His orphan
cars could be sold only at a tremendous loss. Perhaps he could have
got the agency for another car; but it scarcely seemed worth trying.
All his plans for the future centered in snug retreat with Amy to a
"tucked-up" place at the Soldiers' Home.
Roberts was running for the United States Senate again: Captain
Duquesne had been off in his information there. But everything
else was proceeding according to plan. The Colonel suspected that
there was bad blood between the Senator and the Local Manager,
whose special pet was the new Governor Cox. Yet the Local Manager
had been approached successfully; and once he was squared, the ap-
pointment as Adjutant was as good as in the Colonel's pocket.
The old Adjutant resigned, to take effect the first of January. His
clerk could carry on for a few weeks; the interregnum would not
be long. On the first of February, the Colonel locked the door of his
retail establishment for the last time.
It was a melancholy day. The Colonel hadn't given the retail store
much attention for the last few years; but this had been his original
business, and had carried his name before the town ever since he
had settled here. The thought of the Langworthy sign coming down
was defeat in itself. Snug retirement might be all very well once you
were used to it. To-day it looked like a long step toward the final
snugness of the grave.
408
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
At that he minded it less for himself than he did for poor old
Miss Ames. She had been but humdrum help; in a few months Hor-
tense had learned more of the business as a whole than Miss Ames
had acquired in many years. But she had spent the best part of her
life over his books; now when she was too old to get another job,
he was forced to let her go.
He would have pensioned her if there had been any possible way
to do it. As it was he managed to dig up a hundred dollars for
her. Her gratitude over the gift was pathetic. If you expect nothing
of life, perhaps a hundred dollars does look like a good deal.
If you expect nothing of life, you get nothing. Yet he himself had
expected so much; and at this moment he wasn't greatly better off
than poor old Miss Ames.
Through the beginning of a sleet storm he walked toward the
center of town. He was dying to drop in at the Mount Royal Hotel
for a drink; and the way the cold was penetrating his bones, a drink
would have been justified. But he fought down his longing stub-
bornly. A social glass was all very well; but drinking to drown
your misery was only the beginning of another sort of misery.
He got on the street-car and was disagreeably conscious of the
odor of wet clothes-yes, and of wet feet. Some day they'd get auto-
mobiles so perfected that a man wouldn't have to lay up his car
for the winter. Some day; but that day would arrive too late to do
Sam Langworthy any good.
Walking up from the street-car, his footsteps lagged. Disagreeable
as the weather was, he hated to go in the house. Yet under the gray
slant of sleet the long-planned, only half-completed flower-garden
told another tale of desolation. And the tennis-court hadn't even
been marked in three years.
He remembered the distant days when they used to have family
games sometimes late on a Saturday afternoon. The Colonel and
Bess generally played against Artie and Hortense; Hortense was so
good that they really had to handicap her with Artie. At one time
Dora had wielded a mean racket for such a small person; but she
gave it up rather suddenly. Retta had never played, nor had Amy.
The time came when Bess preferred to play with young men;
and they always wanted the court just when the family wanted it,
too. The Colonel had been disgusted with the way Bess never thought
THE COLONEL
409
of enlarging her tennis party to include, say, Hortense or Artie.
Later on she had found it convenient sometimes to include Hor-
tense; but her father should have insisted on a different arrangement
right from the beginning. He had always taken the wrong course
with Bess. Always! He was reaping the reward now of a forbearance
which had been nothing but weakness.
The thought of Bess drove him into the shelter of the house.
There he was met by fresh trouble. Hattie heard him enter and came
to report that the furnace which heated the dining-room wing-
and his bedroom-was broken. There was no cannel coal, either.
Should she try to make a fire in his bedroom grate with the coal
which was used for the kitchen range?
There was no point to driving a willing horse to death. Hattie
might be all that Amy thought she was; but against heavy odds
she was doing her best to make him comfortable.
"This part of the house doesn't seem very warm either," he com-
mented, slipping his dripping umbrella into the great jar which used
to hold so many umbrellas, not to speak of parasols and an occa-
sional tennis-racket. Now his own umbrella could have had the jar
to itself if Hattie hadn't rescued it.
"I'll dry this in the kitchen," she explained. "I got a nice fire there.
I took the dog out there, too. He never would have nothing to do
with me when Mrs. Langworthy was here, nor Miss Dora. But he
follows me real well now. I guess he's lonesome."
"The kitchen's a good place for him," said the Colonel. "I may be
driven out there myself; it's an awful day. But first I'm going to
have a look at that broken furnace. It's too late to get a workman
to-day, and we don't want the pipes to freeze."
He found that the furnace grates had both been broken, doubtless
by too vigorous shaking to get rid of clinkers. If he could get the
grates out, he could save a little money by taking them downtown
himself. He had never been in the least handy about the house; even
now Hattie ran the furnaces. But it was a relief to have a problem
that he could literally get his hands on; he had been forced to deal
so many times, and so awkwardly, with intangibles.
A quarter of an hour's fumbling with the grates led him to re-
flect grimly that it was a good thing he would have the services of
a houseman at the Soldiers' Home; he really didn't seem to get
410
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
the knack of this kind of thing. But perhaps if he had a better screw-
driver he could make more headway. Anyhow, he hated to go up-
stairs and admit defeat.
The quest for a better screw-driver led him to Artie's tool-chest.
So far as he knew the chest had never been opened since Artie's
death. But the little room which Artie had partitioned off stood wide
open now, and the key lay on the tool-chest. Doubtless Dora had
found it when she sorted out his things; but even Dora's courage
had failed before the disposal was completed.
The Colonel wasn't feeling very sentimental about Artie; in fact
it seemed to him that poor old Artie was pretty well out of a mis-
erable world. Perhaps, though, he had left some decent tools behind
him. The key grated horribly; but at length the rusty lock yielded.
The Colonel threw back the lid and stood staring in amazement
at Artie's secret hoard.
It was incredible, but the facts were there: Artie had died in pos-
session of five full quarts of bonded whiskey. Where he had ever
got it to begin with was a mystery-unless, came the voice of sus-
picion, the Colonel had himself paid for it under the alias of "garden
hose" or "fertilizer." How he had ever managed to hang onto it
was stranger still. Probably he had been saving it to give himself
a real treat some day. Then he had been cut off without ever reaching
the day. Even a life like Artie's held possibilities that were never
realized.
That whiskey now came like an answer to prayer. He had fought
off the temptation to stop in a bar-room on his way home. But not
to take the drink he wanted would be merely silly now. The Colonel
helped himself to one bottle, and locked up the chest. This time
he was careful to put the key in his pocket. With the bottle under his
arm, he clambered the basement stairs, and reported to Hattie that
he hadn't had any success with the furnace.
"Then you just better eat your supper right here in the kitchen,"
she counseled. "I got lamb chops to broil, and it's going to spoil
them if you take 'em into a cold room."
The kitchen was not merely warm but hot. It smelled a little of
ironing and of new bread. Hattie made good bread, and sometimes
she treated him to pie. The cakes which had been such a specialty
of the house in Louise's day, however, were only a memory now.
THE COLONEL
411
Along toward the end of her reign, indeed, Louise had often com-
plained that with the family so small now she couldn't get a cake
eaten up, even with three of them still busy in the kitchen.
He poured himself two fingers of whiskey, and drank a little water
afterward. Then he virtuously recorked the bottle and set it well
back on the table. Hattie had just covered the table with a clean
red-and-white checkered cloth. She was untidy enough about the
rest of the house; but here in the kitchen she really had things
rather inviting.
She uncovered a fire which had died nicely down to red coals.
From the wire broiler ascended an appetizing column of smoke. It
was cozy here. After the day he'd had, even lamb chops in the
kitchen were something to be grateful for. Probably Amy would be
a little scandalized, though, if he ever told her he had come out here
to eat his supper.
He had had a letter from Amy that very morning. In the turmoil
he hadn't half enjoyed it. Yet it had been an interesting letter: page
after page of thin foreign paper covered with Amy's precise and
rather decorative script. She told him a lot about Italy, which she
was enjoying more than any other country she had yet seen. It was
splendid of her to write such a long letter, when she had to take
the time from either her sight-seeing or her rest. He hoped Dora
was making her rest properly. Amy never knew when she had had
enough.
They'd lead a quiet life when they got out to the Soldiers' Home.
He mustn't let it get too quiet, however; some men forgot that
after a woman was married she still might enjoy a little entertain-
ment. He must manage an evening at the theater for her as often
as possible; and he must see to it that she had plenty of pretty
clothes.
His salary would be small; but of course having his quarters too
would help a lot. Perhaps he could get his pension increased; the
Government was paying larger pensions right along to Civil War
veterans. The Republican Party had one eye on the polls when they
shoved through all those pension bills; he didn't quite like the way
that public money was being used for private ends. But yes, he
would apply for a bigger pension; if necessary, he'd pull wires to
get it. He would do more than that for Amy.
412
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"Your chops are ready, Colonel," said Hattie at his elbow. He
started. He had forgotten she was there.
Glancing around he saw that she had set only one place. He had
never been a great stickler for formality; anyhow he hated eating
alone. "You sit down with me," he invited. "Lamb chops mustn't
be kept waiting."
She said, "Oh, no! I couldn't think of that." But even while she
spoke she was putting more chops to broil. He didn't have to coax
very hard.
He hadn't realized how hungry he was. Chops, fresh bread and
coffee: it made a satisfying meal, without frills or distraction. It
was cozy here with his back to the range. Indeed, heat and repletion
were making him a little drowsy.
Unluckily, just as he finished his second cup of coffee the thought
flashed across him, what would Bess say if she could see him eating
in the kitchen with the servant?
He tried to thrust the idea from him. What did it matter what
Bess might say? When her word might have made a difference, she
had been conspicuously silent. During these last trying weeks he
had never once heard from her.
She knew nothing of the Soldiers' Home project; she must suppose
that when the Langworthy sign came down from the old building,
that ended the Colonel. But through her he was a connection of the
Rutherfords. Stingy as they were, surely if they had been properly
approached they would have put up a few thousands to save the
Colonel from open failure. He would have starved sooner than ask
them for help. But Bess might have managed it for the credit of
the name she had been born to.
She was no longer a Langworthy, however, in name or in heart.
She had gone over to the Rutherfords, lock, stock, and barrel. The
first shock of her defection had bewildered him. But lately he had
had a fatally clear light on Bess, and had made out what for so many
years he had purposely avoided seeing. A horde of little jealousies
pricked her every day and every hour; and a deep and abiding jeal-
ousy had separated her from the woman who should have been
closest to her after her mother's death. She hadn't lifted a finger to
save the Colonel, largely because his safety would have benefited
Amy.
THE COLONEL
413
He didn't want to see these things so clearly. Indeed he didn't
want to think about them at all. But how to stop thinking? Perhaps
a drink would help a little. It couldn't hurt, anyhow, now that he
had eaten his supper.
He had a drink, and poured another. Catching Hattie's eye on
the bottle as he set it down, he felt discourteous to be drinking before
her this way.
"Get another glass, and have some," he invited. "It's good whiskey;
it keeps the cold out."
She brought the glass quickly, before he had a chance to change
his mind. But when she had downed the whiskey, she pretended
to cough. "It's awful strong," she protested.
The Colonel grinned. The girl wasn't consciously hypocritical;
this was simply her idea of good manners. Of course it wouldn't do
to make a habit of this sort of thing; but once in a way, you might
learn a lot about the people who worked for you, and whom you
generally thought of only in connection with their work.
His amusement combined with the whiskey to make him feel
better. He lighted a cigar and watched the smoke drift lazily in the
warm air. He felt the dog against his foot, too. He had never devel-
oped quite the same affection for this puppy Dora bought on her
last visit here-not quite the same affection that he had had for the
older dogs. Still it seemed natural to have a dog lying against his
feet. He nudged Carlo. Carlo was a silly name, but the Langworthys
had long ago run out of good names for their dogs, and the Colonel
had never liked to follow the fashion some people did of naming a
new dog for a dead one.
Carlo settled himself more firmly, and presently the Colonel nudged
him again. Hattie must have fed the dog before supper, or he
wouldn't have lain so quiet with chop bones on the table. The
Colonel nudged more gently, not to disturb Carlo's after-supper nap.
The next moment Carlo pushed open the swinging door from
the pantry and came sauntering over to the table and sniffing. The
Colonel stared in amazement. Then he was acutely embarrassed.
"I thought the dog was under the table," he explained. "I was
poking him when he wasn't there at all. Was that you I was poking,
Hattie? I'm sure I hadn't any idea. I beg your pardon."
Hattie giggled. "Oh, it's all right!" His apology amused her; but
414
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
obviously she didn't believe him. That she should think what she
did think, and still not be offended, showed what a cheap sort of
person she really was.
Yet for her own class of man Hattie would be rather attractive.
The milkman, Amy had said. Probably the iceman, too, and very
likely the delivery-boys. Alone in the house all day, she had the
field open to her.
It was none of his business, however; or rather there was no sense
in making a fuss when he had no way of enforcing his orders. But
Amy had been right about not taking Hattie with them to the Sol-
diers' Home. When they moved out there, he thought he'd have
Amy look for a widow about fifty. A neat dry sort of person: some
one who would wear a uniform and cap when she waited on table
at the luncheon-parties which Amy would be giving to the other
officers' wives. Modest as his establishment would be, he wanted
everything nice.
He'd have one more drink, and then he'd get to bed after his
hard day. He had the drink, but when it was finished he was un-
willing to move. Presently he dozed a little. He had never got into
the habit of dozing in his chair. He must be getting old.
When he roused himself Hattie had almost finished clearing the
table. She poured the drinks now: poured for the two of them. Glass
in hand, she moved her chair around beside the Colonel's. He felt
her snuggle against his shoulder; half consciously he put his arm
around her.
This was the sort of thing Artie used to do sometimes. Poor old
Artie, driven after a barren life to snatch what comfort he could!
Comfort, that was the idea: something warm and soft to hold onto.
The Colonel realized that he was slightly drunk. He hadn't been
drunk since the War. A thing like that didn't matter once in a way;
the danger lay in letting it get to be a habit.
He felt a deep and sleepy content. His worries no longer bothered
him. And after all why should they? His worries were as good as
over. It had been a bitter business to plow through. But his future
would be secure; and in the good days that were to begin so soon,
his content would only be increased by the remembrance of all his
anxiety and distress.
The whiskey was beginning to wear off. He put Hattie away from
THE COLONEL
415
him and got clumsily to his feet. Walking over toward the stove,
he stretched himself and yawned luxuriously in the heat.
"It's a great thing to be warm, isn't it?" he said with a smile. "I
hate to think how cold that room of mine is going to be to-night."
Hattie had followed him, and was leaning against his shoulder.
"My room is nice and warm," she whispered. "I've had the gas-heater
going since five c'olock."
"Yes, I'm glad we had that heater put in for you," said the Colonel.
"I'd hate to think of your being cold."
He meant exactly what he had said. He was used to doing for
other people; and it pleased him to think that even in his reduced
circumstances he hadn't made anybody miserable except himself.
He drew away from her a little, looking down at her kindly. He
must see about getting her a new place when he left here. In the
right sort of place Hattie would prove a good worker.
He had always drawn more loyalty than he was entitled to. Foggily
he tried to put something of his gratitude into terms that she could
understand. While he was still groping for words, Hattie rubbed
against him like a cat.
"Putting in heat for me," she whispered, "you didn't know what
a good turn you done yourself. Not then, you didn't. Did you?"
At the look of invitation on her face, he recoiled in horror. Her
greedy eyes, her smile of solicitation: they could mean but one thing.
And he had actually led her to believe that the suggestion would
be grateful to him! Perhaps she even thought it was what he ex-
pected of her.
He couldn't blame her too much. After all, he had long known
what she was like. He even managed to be courteous to her.
"I'm sorry," he said. "I still have an hour's work ahead of me. I
brought home some papers. You run along to bed and don't bother
with the dishes. I want to get an early start in the morning. I'd like
breakfast at seven."
But alone in the cold bedroom where Amy's plants were drooping
in the window-sills, he made no excuses to himself. Poverty didn't
matter so much, nor failure, nor even loneliness. But now his self-
respect had gone on the pyre. With that gone, everything was indeed
lost. He never would have believed that he could sink so low.
A
IX
FTER a restless and miserable night, his common sense reas-
serted itself. Most of the men he knew-respectable family
men, too-would make nothing of such an episode. Indeed, they
would probably be rather tickled by it. After all, nothing had hap-
pened. A slut of a servant had made advances to him, and he had
put her firmly in her place. Best ignore the matter completely, though
it would be a long time before he forgot it.
He had plenty to occupy himself with. He'd drive out to the Sol-
diers' Home to-day and start right in on his arrangements for moving
there. The old Adjutant had been gone since the first of the year;
the Colonel's appointment must come through very shortly. He'd
turn his attention resolutely to the future, and ignore these last few
weeks of nightmare.
It was a wretched day, combining the chill of winter with the dis-
comfort which passes in the Middle West under the name of "Spring."
A driving rain from the northeast was in his face all the way out;
and the livery-stable had assigned him an old horse which he hadn't
the heart to push. The buggy had side-curtains of a sort, and he had
a tarpaulin over his knees. But by the time he had completed the
four-mile drive he was wet around the edges and chilled to the
marrow.
At the Soldiers' Home, however, he found everything most cheer-
ing. Governor Cox received him cordially and sent an orderly to
drive the horse into the Home stables. There was always plenty of
"help" around here. It promised an easy life.
The Governor took him into the Adjutant's office. Three clerks
in the outer room-veterans all of them-grew very busy on his
entrance. The Governor led him through into the private office
which was to be his.
416
THE COLONEL
417
A big desk, a swivel chair, two arm-chairs, a safe, tall varnished
cabinets for papers: it was a comfortable room, heated by steam.
Left here by himself for a few minutes, the Colonel sat down in
the swivel chair, looked out of the windows, smoked a cigar. He
had windows both to west and south; one of them had a prospect
through the trees to the big pond where in summer a pair of swans
floated, and rowboats-free to the old soldiers could be rented by
visitors for five cents an hour. The other windows showed him wind-
ing paths, pleasant drives, the library with its lighted windows glow-
ing pleasantly on this gray day, and suggesting the hundreds of
volumes which he would have time to read in this leisured place.
Soon now he would be coming into this quiet room every day,
to work a few hours and then go home to Amy. No worry, no
strain, no creditors! When the day's work was over, you shut the
office door upon it. At last he was entering on the sort of life he
should have switched to years ago.
His cigar and a quarter of an hour's rest had already given him
the feel of the office. He was eager to get to work. He went into the
outer room and asked the senior clerk to show him something of
the routine.
The senior clerk-his name was Mitchell, and he had a red nose
-was none too lucid in his explanations. But poring over the papers
by himself, the Colonel discovered with delight that he could get
the hang of them well enough. He was still happily absorbed at half-
past eleven, when Mitchell came to tell him he could "mess at the
Nickel Plate" until his own quarters were ready.
"The Nickel Plate" was a small mess for clerks, the Lieutenant
of the Guard, and others whose rank or hours made it unsuitable for
them to eat at the huge mess in the main dining-room. They paid
a little toward their fare, and its superiority had won it a nickname
which suggested shine if not precious metal. The dinner was an ex-
cellent lamb stew, accompanied by hot corn bread and huge sour
pickles, and followed by rice pudding. It was not a meal on which
to base a heavy afternoon's work; but then office hours at the Home
were over at four o'clock.
The Colonel, placid and indeed torpid, was smoking a two-o'clock
cigar in his private office when Governor Cox came in. "The after-
noon mail has just got here," he announced. "I always have mine
418
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
sorted out first. Here's something from Headquarters that will in-
terest you."
On the Colonel's desk he laid out his appointment as Adjutant.
It was all settled, then; and by a pleasant coincidence his appoint-
ment had come the very first day that he could give the position his
attention.
"I think this demands a drink," said Governor Cox.
The Colonel was not exactly in need of a drink; in fact in his
present relief and happiness he felt drinking rather a comedown.
But he couldn't refuse hospitality; and he found Soldiers' Home hos-
pitality was pleasant, easy-going, and slow-paced. He was taken to
the Governor's quarters, was introduced to the Governor's wife, met
the Governor's two daughters, a blooming young lady in her twenties
and a saucy chit of fifteen whom the elder sister was supposed to
be teaching.
Mrs. Cox sat down with the two men in the dining-room, asked
all about Amy, suggested that she might give a reception to intro-
duce the Langworthys to the other officers' families. The older daugh-
ter came to bring them some choice cheese which was kept under
lock and key upstairs. The younger burst in to exhibit a litter of
kittens.
It was almost four o'clock when the Colonel managed to break
away: too late for the Governor to go back to his office, as he ac-
knowledged with a grin. But the Colonel returned to his niche, in
spite of the Governor's suggestion that his horse could just as well
be driven up here to the house. The new officer wasn't in the habit
of quitting at four. If his work proved as light as other people's
seemed to be around here, he might have to take to billiards or get
up a table of whist, to find a way of filling in the late afternoon
hours.
He had sat down with the door to the outer office open. He heard
the sound of desks slamming, and the door into the hall banged
behind departing clerks. He supposed they all went; he was standing
looking out of the window toward the distant pond when he heard
a cough on the threshold behind him.
The senior clerk stood there, looking as if he had something to
say. The Colonel started to offer him a cigar, thought better of it,
and seated himself at his desk. "Yes, Mitchell. What is it?" he asked.

419
"I didn't know as you'd be back here again this afternoon," said

"Nobody has the combination but the old Adjutant. You can

The Adjutant ranked as a Captain in the Soldiers' Home. It would
be hard for the Colonel to get used to being addressed that way.
Perhaps people might slip into the courtesy of giving him the higher
title. His uniform would bear a Captain's shoulder-straps, anyhow.
He must see about ordering a uniform right away; the Adjutant
had to do a good deal of inspecting, and he couldn't go about that
in civilian clothes.
"Very well." The Colonel folded the paper and put it in his breast
pocket. "Now what is it, Mitchell?"
"Senator Roberts wants to see you, Captain. He left word with

"I can do better than that. I'll stop on my way home. It's a nasty
day, and when I'm once in the house I won't be anxious to go out

"That's all, Captain. The Senator telephoned this afternoon while

"I'm always glad to see Senator Roberts," said the Colonel. "Thank

He didn't like the look in that fellow's eye. Even delivering so
simple a message, he had appeared sly and shifty. But it wouldn't
do to start in with a prejudice against a subordinate. People some-
times got blamed for slyness when they were simply embarrassed.
In his buggy the Colonel wondered why Mitchell hadn't simply
told Senator Roberts where to reach him. There was a telephone in
the hall of the office building; surely he remembered seeing one, too,
in the Governor's sitting-room.
Oh, there probably wasn't any "why" worth considering! When you
were out for a position that you knew other people were after, too,
you always saw a sharpshooter behind every mullein stalk. But all that
420
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
business was over now; his appointment was warm in his pocket. The

Word came down presently that the Senator would see him now.
The Colonel was taken up to a spacious suite on the second floor;
he wondered a little who was paying for it, the Party or the tax-

Colonel knew by sight, a court bailiff named Hagerty. He looked
crestfallen, but he tried to hang on just a minute longer. An office-

portant enough to be conciliated.
"Poor devil!" thought the Colonel. Only yesterday he had been feel-
ing sorry for himself; but yesterday seemed very far away.
Senator Roberts shooed Hagerty out of the door and motioned the
Colonel to a chair. He himself stood and frowned down at some

the too-cordial smile of a politician whose eye is always on the next
election. But now he let the silence drag on until the Colonel grew
uncomfortable.
"Mitchell, the Adjutant's clerk, told me you wanted to see me."
That was repeating the message he had sent up from the desk half an

at the Home getting acquainted. I think I'm going to like it there."
"I'm sure you will." The Senator sat down, crossed his legs,
drummed on the table. "You realize of course that I jerked wires to
get you the appointment?"
"It came to-day. I'm properly grateful for it."
THE COLONEL
421
"You should be. As things stand now I haven't the pull with the
Board of Managers that I'd like to have. They're so damn pure they
don't ask any favors, so they think they can look down their noses

"Well, the Soldiers' Homes are supposed to be kept out of politics,

"Are they? That'll do to put in a Fourth of July speech. But

"Somebody in particular you mean?"
"This fellow Cox. The present Governor. I want him let out. More

The Colonel was increasingly uneasy, remembering his pleasant

"No, but I want him to wreck himself. I think he's doing it, you

"It ought to be easy enough to prove if it's true. If he's taking

"There isn't any safeguard that can't be got around if you can

THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
But the Colonel hadn't come this long road in order to turn spy

The Colonel mopped his forehead. Sudden relief made him giddy.
A precipice had yawned at his feet. But a man who stood his ground
was respected even in a hot-bed of chicanery. And, by the way, there
was no room for Mitchell in any office the Colonel ran; his first guess
about that fellow had been right.
"I suppose it's just as well to have an understanding right from the
beginning," he said slowly. "I feel that I can be of use out at the

spent my whole life dealing with them instead of with vehicles and
machinery."
"Yes, you're clever with people. Now shan't we have a drink?" The
Senator rang for a boy. "What's yours? Rye or Bourbon?"
It was over quickly enough, there at the end. But the Colonel's
throat was parched, and his game leg ached. He stirred uneasily in

Most people wouldn't have believed that Senator Roberts would
yield so readily. But the Colonel, qualifying his whiskey liberally with
THE COLONEL
423
soda water, looked at the Senator with a friendly eye. Even politicians
weren't as black as they were painted.
He downed his drink and refused a second. He was getting up to go

"Very well. She's in Europe, you know. She must be about ready

"She'll enjoy the peace and comfort out at the Soldiers' Home. On

The Colonel smiled. It pleased him to have anybody recognize his
responsibility for Amy; the Senator's words brought close his picture

for a moment he felt as if he were actually living in it.
Then all at once the floor seemed to give way beneath his feet.
Senator Roberts wasn't thinking of Amy, except so far as Amy's
existence tightened his own hold on the Colonel. It was a hold re-
enforced all around, and Senator Roberts fancied it unbreakable. He
hadn't yielded an inch. Saying "White" when he meant "Black," he
assumed, of course, that the Colonel understood him and had simply
answered in kind.
Signed over body and soul to a life of spying and tale-bearing: that
was how the Senator pictured him. Doing the rotten work with pious
protests on his lips, to be sure; but he had his living to make and a
wife to support.
And who knew whether he would actually have the strength to
stand out day by day and month after month, when it would be so
easy to make a concession here and another concession there? Even
if he tried to stick the thing out on a decent basis-oh, they could get
his scalp easily enough! The very feeling that they were after his scalp
would indeed be enough to ruin his dear-bought sense of security.

this way. Drawing his appointment out of his pocket, the Colonel
felt as if he were drawing his heart out of his body. But his voice was
steady, though his hand shook a little.
"I suppose it wouldn't do any good to tear this thing up? The

Senator Roberts thought he was bluffing, of course. Well, he'd soon

The Colonel had already considered applying for an increased
pension. This offer came to him probably as the price of his silence.
Well, he couldn't look another gift horse in the mouth just yet. Any-
how, the irony was so obvious it amused him even now.
"Think you could get me an additional twenty dollars a month?"
he demanded. "For Brevet Lieutenant-Colonel Langworthy, with the
thanks of a grateful country!"
He had barely energy to get his letter into the mail-box in the lobby.
He felt as if he had run five miles to do it.
Senator Roberts had come downstairs with him, incredulous up to
the very end. "I wish you wouldn't do it," he said when the letter was
half-way into the slot. Then it was done. That much was settled. It
was all he could settle now.
He had turned toward the door, and Senator Roberts was walking
back to the elevators, when John Rutherford once more crossed the

THE COLONEL
425
Their business couldn't possibly concern the Colonel. It was dirty
business, he felt sure. But there went two successful men.
He had shivered earlier in the afternoon on seeing old Rutherford;
but then he could dismiss the idea that sight brought with it. Now
the prospect stared him in the face. His pension, even if they increased
it one of these days-and those things all took time-wouldn't be
enough for him to live on alone, aside from the question of supporting
Amy. His most likely future was a grudged bedroom at Bess's, and
a plate at her table.
He couldn't face the crowd and the smells in the street-car; and to
telephone for a livery-rig was just too vast an undertaking. In the
street he looked about for Dan Mason's hack; but it was undoubtedly
standing in front of the Mount Royal Hotel, several blocks farther
downtown. The Colonel turned up the collar of his overcoat, unfurled
his umbrella, and stepped forth into the rain.
The drenching walk home, though it seemed to last a long time,
made no clear impression on his mind. The next thing he realized, he
was letting himself in at his own front door. The house was chilly;
there was even a faint mustiness in the air. Would this house ever sell
for the full amount of the mortgages? To put it in order again would
take a substantial sum. What purchaser who could spend that much
would consider buying on this side of town, which had been ruined
by cheap building?
It would be warm in the kitchen, but he wasn't going into the
kitchen. He turned on all the lights in the library and sat down to
write a letter to Amy.
Poor Amy, still in her land of enchantment: what a homecoming
he was providing for her! But it would be better to have the bad news
meet her in New York. Then she could stay at Hortense's until she
and the Colonel got their affairs straightened out. -Or perhaps until
they acknowledged that now at length their affairs were beyond all
straightening.
Hattie came in to say that the grates had been repaired, but she had
let the furnace fire go down to save coal. Didn't he want to have his
supper in the kitchen? The Colonel, not looking up from his paper,
said he didn't want any supper.
She took that for what she thought it was worth, and brought a
tray to his desk. Corned beef and cabbage, hot biscuits, pie: the sight
426
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
and smell of it made him a little sick. He played with the food, but
succeeded in eating very little. For some reason she had neglected to
make him any coffee. He was thirsty if not hungry. He drank three
or four glasses of water, and felt colder than ever. Half-drenched as
he was, it was foolish to sit in this cold room. But he didn't want to
go upstairs and change his clothes until he finished his letter.
Hattie grew worried and tried to build a fire in the grate. She had
a hard time getting the small coal to kindle; her clatter distracted
him. When the fire finally blazed up, the chimney was wet. Smoke
filled the room. The Colonel's eyes smarted, and he began to cough.
The smoke finally drove him to bed with his letter to Amy still
unfinished. Well, he would have time enough in the morning to write
to Amy.
Time enough! From now on he would have nothing much but
time. He had expected to go out to the Soldiers' Home bright and
early to-morrow. But he wouldn't be going there to-morrow or any
other day. And his factory was in the hands of strangers, and the retail
store closed for good.
At least he could lie abed late. When he was rested, perhaps things
wouldn't seem quite so hopeless. Surely there was some way out even
now, if he held on and didn't give up.
Had he been altogether wise to make such a definite break with
Senator Roberts? Probably not. All his life he had been impulsive,
hopeful-yes, and imprudent. Yet he didn't quite see where he could
have done differently.
It wouldn't have made much difference anyhow in the long run.
Everybody had to fight a losing fight: it was lucky young people
never realized what lay ahead of them. The important thing, of
course, was not to whine: you lost because you must, but you needn't
let yourself be disgruntled. That was clear to him even now; but
somehow it didn't seem terribly important. He was so tired that
nothing could seem important any longer. So tired, and so terribly
alone.
HE
X
E had barely time to stretch out in bed before he fell into a sleep
that was half a stupor. His last conscious thought was a dim
gratitude that in spite of all the things that Fate could do to a man,
sleep was still left to him.
But some time in the heart of the night he woke shivering. Luckily,
in that climate a bedroom was always provided with winter comforts;
and the Colonel had never fallen in with the new-fangled idea which
Dora had tried to talk up to him, that a bedroom window should be
opened at night, summer and winter alike. He struggled into his
heavy woolen bath-robe, pulled the covers tight about him, drew up
an extra blanket.
Still his teeth continued to chatter and his limbs to quake. He was
so cold that he didn't believe he would ever be warm again. Years
ago in the army he had often slept on the ground, between two
blankets if he managed to pool resources with a comrade, all too fre-
quently with but one blanket to do duty both below and above him,
and sometimes in the rain at that. Yet he had never been so cold as
he was right now. Were the old fogies right, after all, when they
claimed that the seasons were changing? He used to think that was
a very funny assertion.
Through the fogginess in his head he at length realized that he was
definitely having a chill: the sort of thing that led to grippe or even to
pneumonia. He needed a fire in the grate, a hot-water bag, some of
Artie's whiskey made into a toddy.
He couldn't get those things for himself, in that chilly house at that
unearthly hour, without growing colder-and colder-and colder. He
must have help. Yet to summon help he would have to rise in the icy
air, stumble down a corridor and then another corridor, open a door
that would let down upon him fresh drafts from the attic. It must
427
428
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
have taken him an hour to steel himself for the endeavor. He was at
last driven to effort by sheer panic. He didn't want to die now,
scattered and shrunk as his hopes and plans were. He wouldn't die
-he simply wouldn't until he had forced life to give him back
some of his own.
At the foot of the third-floor stairs he stood leaning against the wall
and called to Hattie. He called and coughed, and got his breath and
called again.
He couldn't make her hear. She had her door closed, of course;
perhaps on such a cold night she slept with the blankets pulled over
her head. To climb the stairs to her door was out of the question. It
never occurred to him that even Hattie must see he was a sick man;
since the night before last, to ascend those stairs implied only one
thing.
He got back to bed somehow. Toward dawn he fell into a light
feverish sleep. He was wakened by a stabbing pain in his chest.
Mere incredulity kept him quiet for a time. It was impossible that
the next breath should hurt as the last one had. Yet with every breath
the pain recurred. This was curious. It was very curious. How did
people get along when it hurt them even to breathe?
Hattie came presently to see why he wasn't down for breakfast.
Had he ever been afraid of her and of what she might think? Hattie
was a good girl, but she was stupid. The trouble with people like her
was that, no matter how simple the emergency, you always had to
do their thinking for them. He scarcely recognized his own voice in
the feeble croak that came from his dry lips. But he told her quite
distinctly to send for Dr. Simpson.
Her message must have been urgent; Dr. Simpson was there in
half an hour. He showed no alarm; after a little listening at the
Colonel's chest, he began to give orders.
He stayed nearly an hour. It was a comfort to have him there.
Under his ministrations the Colonel croaked, "Anything much-the
matter with me?"
"A neglected cold is the mischief, especially at this time of year,"
snapped the doctor. "I don't believe you've been eating properly,
either. A man without a woman to take care of him never shows any
sense."
A woman to take care of him! Did that mean Amy? But he had
THE COLONEL
429
always taken care of Amy; it was that way around. He'd taken care
of more women than Doc Simpson had. But not so many wives: Doc
had the jump on him there. Three women in succession had opened
the fullest intimacy to this fussy bald man with the funny little round
paunch. To keep up with Doc Simpson the Colonel would have had
to marry Dora in between Retta and Amy. Marry the whole Mills
family! That was a funny idea. He must remember to tell that to
Dora sometime.
More comfortable now, he dozed a little. Dr. Simpson came back at
noon. He was working hard to earn his money. He must be surer
than the Colonel was that his bill would eventually be paid.
The Colonel dozed again, and wakened to find the late afternoon
sunlight across his bed. He remembered once years ago when Retta
had taken him in Chicago to a Three Hour service on Good Friday-
a papistical innovation which the Rev. John Warren Meade had never
dared to attempt in Mount Royal. The day had been overcast, and
mournful music wailed while a clergyman with no surplice over his
cassock moved back and forth in front of a bare altar.
It had all gone on a long time; the three hours seemed more like
ten. But at the very end, when the clergyman knelt on the altar steps,
the sun had come suddenly through the clouds, and a tinted ray
rested on his bowed head. That was somehow very moving. It couldn't
have been arranged that way, and the Colonel had been impressed by
a vague feeling that it must mean something.
The sunlight to-day meant something, too. He couldn't explain; but
surely if he called it to people's attention they would understand for
themselves. Some one was standing on the side of his bed away from
the windows. It was a woman, he knew by the voice; he heard her
say, "He's waking."
It was a familiar voice, yet one he hadn't heard lately. There were
so many familiar voices he hadn't heard lately; he was confused.
He'd have to turn his head and look at her. Thanks to the way the
doctor had him swathed, it was an effort to turn his head.
Little by little, however, he managed it. Then, blinking the sunlight
out of his eyes, he stared in amazement. How did Retta get here?
It was Retta, indeed. He guessed he ought to know. But what made
her so young? And she was quite evidently expecting a baby. But he
430
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
knew who that baby would be. How could he possibly know that
beforehand? It was all very confusing.
She smiled; and he saw with relief that it wasn't Retta at all. It
was Bess. They'd have a good laugh one of these days when he told
her that there for half a minute he had failed to recognize his own
daughter. He had never thought Bess looked much like Retta. Her
present condition seemed to intensify the resemblance. "Motherly,"
people used to call Retta. Well, Retta had been a good mother. It
wasn't her fault things had turned out so badly.
He managed to say, "I'm better now." Dr. Simpson came from the
foot of the bed and poked a thermometer into his mouth.
Dr. Simpson must have sent for Bess. From time to time doctors
had to make a lot of decisions for other people. But why did women
always hanker to hang around a sick-bed? Bess had left him alone
to face things much worse than a neglected cold.
She was taking hold now as if the situation belonged to her. "I
want to take you home with me, Papa," she said. "They're getting a
room ready for you now. It's a nice room. You will be very com-
fortable there."
The Colonel couldn't talk past the thermometer, but he shook his
head violently. Luckily Dr. Simpson backed him up. "You can't
move him in this state. We'll have to make him comfortable right
here. I'd expected to have a nurse by this time, but the town is full of
pneumonia. I won't take just any old nurse; we may have to send to
Chicago. Good nursing will mean a lot to him."
Removal of the thermometer allowed the Colonel to ask, "Have
I got pneumonia, Doc?"
"It would serve you quite right if you had," said the doctor cheer-
fully. "Now don't talk. I'm going to put another pillow under your
head, and then we'll see whether you can get down a little soup. Lord,
your beard is scratchy! You men with heavy beards look like bums
if you go without shaving a single day."
Was it only yesterday that the Colonel had shaved and dressed and
gone about his business like the rest of the world? It seemed a very
long time ago. He wouldn't have wanted to get up now even if they
had let him. It was an effort to lift his head for an extra pillow. He
just wanted to lie quiet-lie quiet and rest-rest for a long, long time.
He could have rested if it hadn't been for the breath that hurt him so.
THE COLONEL
431
He'd soon be all right again, however, once he got rid of this tightness
in his chest.
It was worse the next day, when he began to cough. They wrapped
his body in towels, wet towels pinned tight about him. The towels
fidgeted him. Other things seemed to slip away from him when he
tried to sense them. But he was only too conscious of the towels, and
of the slops some one was always trying to make him swallow.
Dimly, as if it were happening at a great distance, he was aware that
the room was being made more habitable. A cannel coal fire burned
night and day. The room was very clean, too; and some one had
picked up and put out of sight the clothes which he had left lying
on a chair. That was tidier, of course; but he missed the promise of
clothes laid ready for to-morrow. It made him feel that he was in for
quite a siege.
When he refused chicken broth, and beef tea, and other food fit
only for babies, they began giving him port wine. He always had liked
port. This was good port, too. Somebody must have raised some
money somewhere.
Perhaps his transit stock had brought in a substantial dividend. He
thought his transit stock had been lost in the general ruin. But perhaps
he was mistaken. He had feared so many calamities that he had grown
to believe some of them had actually happened.
He had even thought at one time that the Langworthy car was a
failure and had been given up. Yet here he was now driving one day
after day. A very beautiful car it was, too; but the steering-wheel hurt
his chest sometimes.
When he was tired of this car he meant to turn it over to Senator
Roberts. The Senator had played him some kind of dirty trick; he
couldn't just remember what it was. But there was no sense in cherish-
ing a grudge. Even Bess had forgiven him for-for whatever it was
that he had done to her.
Only he hadn't done anything, really. And Bess hadn't held any-
thing against him. All his troubles had occurred in a fever dream,
back there when he had pneumonia. He was successful and happy
now, and very proud. Why shouldn't he be proud, when he had won
through every fight he was engaged in, and triumphed over all his
enemies? People kept crowding into the room to congratulate him,
and he received their good wishes complacently. He wished though
432
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
that his new engineer didn't look quite so much like Dr. Simpson.
He was getting tired of the very idea of Doc Simpson.
Dr. Simpson himself was there one night. The Colonel distinctly
heard him say, "I think we'd better send for Amy." The Colonel
chuckled. Dr. Simpson always had liked Amy; this was just his way
of getting her on the ground. He needn't have called her "Amy,"
though. She was Mrs. Langworthy now. She had been for a long
time. Surely even Doc Simpson must have heard of that.
Driving was beginning to tire him. He was glad when the car went
away in the middle of the night, and he was back in his own bed.
He had to cough, and a person in a trained nurse's cap poked a nasty
little basin under his chin.
He took a sharp look at her, and recognized her. She was the nurse
they had had for Retta. What was she doing here now? And why was
she at him? Didn't Retta need her any more?
No, he remembered that, too. Retta got well and went away. Amy
went away, too. They were talking of sending for her. Perhaps if Amy
came back they wouldn't think they had to have this strange woman
here. She was trying now to grab the steering-wheel out of his hands.
He hated her. He wouldn't put up with much more nonsense from
her.
All at once he laughed, and had to cough again. He'd got it straight
at last. The nurse was taking care of him and a nice time he must
have given her. Even with that hot poker turning in his chest, he
wouldn't have behaved so to Amy. But so long as they insisted on
sending for somebody, weren't they making a mistake in not sending
for Dora? Dora was a better nurse than Amy, any day.
H
XI
E wakened with his fever gone. He wasn't sick any more, only
infinitely weak and weary.
Somewhere beyond the foot of the bed he heard Bess say, "But I
thought you told me the pneumonia was over."
The nurse answered, "His lungs are clear. It's the state of his heart
that is worrying the doctor now."
"Then he's still very ill?" asked Bess.
"Very ill," agreed the nurse.
Bess had always said "ill" when she meant "sick." It was one of
her little elegancies-one, by the way, which had infuriated her
mother. But why must she use it about the Colonel now?
He couldn't by so much as the flicker of an eyelid let them know
that he heard what they said. Heard it, and understood it. "Very ill,"
indeed! If they'd just go away and stop bothering him, he'd show
them yet.
At first he was angry. Then he smiled inwardly at his own cunning.
He was overhearing things he wasn't supposed to hear. He'd have a
fine time one of these days teasing them about the careless way they
had talked in his presence, as if he were too stupid to understand
plain English. Actually his head was quite clear now, and his hearing
abnormally acute. He just didn't want to move.
Later in that same day he managed to get his eyes open. Above him
bent a vivid eager face, which broke into a smile when it saw his
flicker of recognition. Hortense! How did Hortense get here?
He hadn't energy to utter the question; but she divined it. "I've
been here three days, Colonel. Dr. Simpson telegraphed to my house
to let Aunt Amy know about you. She had only just sailed, so I didn't
wait for her. I sent the children to my mother-in-law's, and got on a
train."
433
434
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
"Didn't mean to make so much trouble," he whispered faintly.
Hortense patted his hand. "It hasn't been much fun for you yourself,
has it? But you're better now. You're so much better!"
Bess came to the other side of the bed. "I've been here every day,
Papa. But you haven't known any of us."
She was mistaken; he had known her. He was glad to have her
here. Yet why did she come every day now, after staying away so
long? His "illness" was a mere accident. Still, if Bess could ignore
the issue between them, even in a sick-bed reconciliation, that was
all right with him, too.
Bess moved away again and began to talk to the nurse. They seemed
to have struck up a great friendship; he could hear them gossiping
about this one and that one. He was satisfied to be left to Hortense.
Hortense knew what he wanted to hear about.
"Aunt Amy's ship docks to-day," she said. "Bill is to meet her at
the pier and put her on her train. She'll be here day after to-morrow.
That isn't too long to wait, is it?"
He found voice to whisper, "I'll be better then."
"Of course you will," said Hortense. "Much better. It's too bad Aunt
Amy couldn't get here sooner. But at least she won't even know
you've been sick until she gets word that you're better. That's some-
thing, isn't it?"
Now that he knew Amy was coming, he was quite satisfied. Amy
was coming, and the pain and suffocation were gone. How heavenly
it was to lie here with the pain gone: to lie here in the warmth and
drowse, and feel so safe and at peace!
Only they wouldn't leave him in peace. Even when they weren't
at him, there were always at least two of them in the room. They
withdrew to the other side of the room and kept each other company.
Because they talked under their breaths, they thought he wouldn't
be any the wiser. But right along he got things that normally would
have been too low-pitched and far away for him to hear.
That afternoon he heard Bess say to the nurse, “I heard yesterday
that Mrs. Cordiner was no better. I wonder why they don't take her
to a sanitarium."
"Nobody can make her go, if she doesn't want to," objected the
nurse. "She's just as well off at home, anyhow. She has the best of
care."
THE COLONEL
435
"But she hasn't been really right for a long time," Bess protested.
"Her nurses have had to keep their carbolic out of her way; and all
her food is given to her cut up, so that she won't be trusted with a
knife."
"Yes, her money doesn't do her much good now, nor that big
house," agreed the nurse. "It's acute melancholia. Maybe it's just as
well she hasn't any family. It would be hard on them to see her the
way she is now."
"She's the last person I'd ever have thought such a thing would
happen to," said Bess. "She always seemed so sure of herself. Mamma
liked her. I'm afraid I never did."
"Mamma liked her!" How many of Retta's old friends had gone
downstream in the last few years! Tragedy had closed in on the
little group of her intimates, marking one after another of them for
its own.
Perhaps the same thing would prove true of any other circle, if you
watched it over the course of the years. Perhaps tragedy closed in on
everybody, if you just gave it time enough.
Young people treated time not only carelessly but confidently, feel-
ing that it was on their side. For so many years the Colonel had felt
that way himself! He remembered once long ago when Dora had
pointed out to him in a book the Latin words, "Tempus edax rerum."
"Time the eater-up of things," she translated for him. Dora knew
languages.
The Colonel had looked at her in blank incomprehension. The
Latin might be venerable, but it was wrong. Time might eat up your
clothes or your furniture, might put wrinkles in your face and steal
away your hair. But all the while Time was bringing you things: a
wife, children, money, grandchildren. You got more than you let
slip.
He knew better now. So many of his own difficulties were due
simply to Time; time passing on, and passing him by.
The mention of Mrs. Cordiner had brought to his mind the recol-
lection of Cordiner & Morse's, where he had gone on so many oc-
casions to buy gifts for his dear ones. It was all changed now: old
Cordiner dead, Frank Morse dead, Mrs. Cordiner worse than dead.
Only Tom Holmes survived from the good old days: Tom Holmes
sitting behind his rows of ticking watches, measuring out time to his
436
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
fellow-townsmen, grudging them the minutes that went to make up
their life-span.
The idea of Tom Holmes was dreadfully disturbing. That night
through his broken slumbers the Colonel felt that Tom Holmes was
somehow responsible for all the trouble he had scowlingly witnessed.
When Colonel Langworthy dozed he could hear the rows of watches
ticking time away, ticking life away. Toward dawn he began to fight
sleep in order to get away from the ticking of those watches.
During the day the ticking caught up with him, anyhow. He tried
to complain of it to Hortense. She had stooped over him in the kindly
morning light to say, "Aunty Amy is on the train now. We've had a
telegram. If she makes her connection in Chicago, she will be here by
this time to-morrow."
"Ticking," he managed to say. "Ticking!"
For once Hortense misunderstood. She had them take the clock
out of the bedroom.
"That fussiness is a good sign in sick people," he heard Bess say
complacently.
The ticking in his ears grew louder and louder. Toward afternoon
he began to have difficulty with his breathing. That was strange, when
his pneumonia was gone. Dr. Simpson had them prop him up on
pillows. Hortense looked at him with manifest distress, though when
she caught him watching her, she managed to smile at him.
"Amy's coming," she whispered encouragingly. "Dora couldn't
leave New York right away, of course. Amy is alone on the train. It's
an anxious journey for her. You must rest all you can, so that you'll
look your best when she gets here."
He succeeded in getting a hand up to his chin: a week's growth of
beard wouldn't leave him looking his best for Amy or for anybody
else.
"Oh, I spoke to the doctor about that!" said Hortense quickly. "I
thought we might have a barber in to shave you. But Dr. Simpson
said we'd better not bother you. Aunt Amy won't mind your beard,
anyhow. It makes you look-rather impressive."
With his fever-cracked lips and sunken cheeks, he must look pretty
awful. But perhaps Amy wouldn't mind. She had seemed glad enough
to see him the last time she was here. But he always wanted to make
an effort for Amy.
THE COLONEL
437
During that next night his eagerness gradually deserted him. He
began to dread her coming. The watches ticked louder and faster,
louder and faster; he couldn't get away from them. They were
bringing Amy closer, but he didn't want her to come back and find
him like this.
She must come back some day, he supposed; this was her home,
too. But he didn't want her to come back in confusion and doubt, the
way she had come since their first perfect winter-the way she had
been underneath even during that one winter, he was beginning to
fear now.
He wanted her in perfect possession and understanding, not
wretchedly and with a divided heart, as a man must have even Amy
on this dull earth. It wasn't Amy who was to blame. It was life. He
had grown very sick of life and the way it is doled out to us poor
humans.
Yet when morning came at last-the morning of the day that was
to bring Amy-he found himself alert once more, and quite calm.
They cleaned him up and changed his bed, and he drank a little milk
to please them. Then he sat propped with pillows and watched the
door. The door of his room and hers: the door through which Amy
would presently come to him.
The ticking of the watches had ceased rather suddenly. In the quiet
of this remote upstairs room he began to listen for something else.
At first he didn't know quite what it was. Then with a leap of the
heart he realized, it was the music of bugles that he was listening for.
He had always loved the bugle-calls. Best of them he loved the one
that rounded out the day. He resented their calling it "Taps"; even
the habit of years had not reconciled him to a word so abrupt and
unbeautiful. In his own mind the call went to words that it seemed
to find for itself; and the last words, borne on slow mellow notes,
were always "Go to sleep."
Out at the Soldiers' Home they sounded all the bugle-calls. There
he could have gone to sleep every night to the same notes which were
the final honor they paid a soldier when they laid him down for his
last long rest. Retta had spoken once of not liking the idea that people
must be haunted every night by the last moments of a funeral. But
Retta had got hold of the wrong end of the stick. It wasn't that sleep
meant death; it was that death meant sleep.
438
THE LANGWORTHY FAMILY
A long time had passed since he had slept restfully and peacefully.
That must be why this notion was haunting him now. Because of
course the nearest bugle-calls were four miles away; and it was
morning anyhow, no time for Taps.
Hortense came to report that she had telephoned the station, and
they said Amy's train was on time. Bess was to meet her at the station.
Amy would rather have had Hortense, perhaps; but Hortense seemed
reluctant to stir from the Colonel's room. He had made arrangements
for them all so long that the habit was hard to break; but of course
it was pleasant to feel that Bess was now kinder even to Amy.
The strain of watching tired him at length; and he couldn't get his
eyes open even when he heard Amy's quick footfalls in the hall. So
after all he missed her entrance. When he actually lifted his weary
eyelids, she was standing beside the bed.
She knelt and put her arms around him; her cool cheek rested
against his forehead. He sank against her a little. Fragile Amy was
stronger now than he was. He must be careful, though, not to lean
too heavily. She was holding him so tight she might hurt herself.
She let him go at last. But it was only to establish herself so that she
could stay with him. Hortense carried off her hat and cloak, and
brought her a cup of tea.
He remembered how when they had tea together during the last
weeks before they were married, once or twice Amy had given him the
first spoonful out of her cup. She wouldn't think of doing that to-day,
of course. She'd be surprised if she knew he even remembered such a
silly little thing. But silly little things like that mattered even in the
final sum!
He had the final sum now. Everything was right. It was beautifully
and completely right, as it had never been before. There was no fly in
the ointment now. Everything was perfect at last.
He closed his eyes for a moment, to shut out even the sight of Amy
in the chair at his bedside. Then he must open them quickly, to make
sure she was actually there.
Opening them he surprised a strange look on her face. Then at
length he understood. He had seen men die on the battle-field, had
seen his own father die, and Retta. He would never have believed that
his own death could come so close before he recognized it.
His worn-out heart was helping him to an easy end. His head was
THE COLONEL
439
strangely clear. He didn't want to come back: come back to more
trouble, when everything was perfect here toward the end. But he did
want just a little longer, to make plain to the rest of them all that was
now manifest to him.
Whatever he had experienced so far he could accept. It was just
that he was tired. Tired, so tired; and sleep was slow in coming, and
the bugle-calls were so far away that he should never hear them now.
He moved his hand a little, and Amy's met it. He must stay here
long enough to tell her that he understood everything, that he wasn't
disgruntled, that the bitterness had all passed from him. In just a
minute now he could collect his strength, and tell her.
He felt his fingers relax in hers, though her clasp was now so tight
that it seemed she meant to hold him against any break. Yes, she
could hold him. She could help him tell her. In a minute now, he
could tell Amy everything.
The windows darkened suddenly, as if some one had drawn heavy
curtains over them. Yet through the darkness he could hear what he
was longing for. It was strange, yet very lovely, that at this moment of
his acceptance he should hear a bugle-call beginning. He even heard
Amy give a stifled cry as the bugle reached its last soft notes, "Go
to sleep."
